# To the Headquarters of the Russian-Manchurian Army.

{p. [10]}

{p. [11]}

## To Petersburg.

*November 1904.*

Would an able doctor be satisfied with his choice of profession if he were permitted all his life only to work at the dissecting table or to write prescriptions for "hypothetical" illnesses? An engineer who only draws plans, but never sees the product of his calculations rise from the ground? A composer who writes notes side by side according to all the rules of thoroughbass and counterpoint, without ever hearing the acoustic effect of his works? — No! — They, like all other creators, must see tangibly useful results emerge from the labour of their minds and hands; their diligence must translate into an immediately perceptible success, diminished by no criticism, so that they may believe in themselves and their fellow men may believe in them.

With an officer in peacetime, it is another matter. He must be the greatest idealist—and at the same time the greatest optimist—if he is not to lose faith in his work.

He works for a greater whole, in which the individual parts he has honed soon lose themselves again in the mass—he must blindly believe that the method and instruments by and with which he works are the best—for the only real touchstone, an actual conflict, he never gets to see. As a reward for his pains he has his *own inner conviction* and——*recognition*.

Kriegsspiel, tactical exercise rides, and Kriegsspiel once again. Here and there, however, the cannons have been roaring for years, now in the land of the Boers, now among the Chinese, a war here, an uprising in the colonies there, offering the officers of the country concerned a welcome opportunity to experience actual warfare and at the same time to cast a glance out into the wide {p. 4} world. — Yet we are permitted to read of all this only in the newspaper, and must quietly pull along at the old cart in the ever-same, rutted tracks, in which only now and then do the crushed stones laid in our path change their shape. What officer would not be struck by the thought of why he of all people should remain stuck in one place, why he of all people should not at least once see that for which he was trained—war? "Yes, my dear friend," people answered me, "if you were not an active-duty officer, the matter could probably be arranged more easily, but as it is, it will hardly do! — It sounds somewhat paradoxical, but it is true and quite natural. How do you actually imagine it? Do you want to march with the Russians as a Cossack, or enlist with the Japanese? Or do you believe that in a war one can simply go and watch, just as at a manoeuvre?"

I was still far from having settled these questions in my mind when, on a gloomy November day, I bought my ticket to Petersburg at the Vienna Nordbahnhof.

Now I was finally sitting in the carriage; passport, certificates, a stack of letters of introduction in my pocket—of the latter one can never have enough in Russia, so I was told. What work, what running about, what begging and pleading it had been! Having to endure the eternal head-shaking and shrugging of shoulders! Well, you will probably get as far as Petersburg, most had said to me by way of farewell—only a single, easily ignited journalist offered to administer my future laurels.

Despite all these dim prospects of reaching the theatre of war in any acceptable capacity, an outfit and other equipment nevertheless had to be procured for the purpose. What headaches—and money—that had cost! Not too much and not too little—not too expensive and not too cheap—and not a single soul who could have given any halfway practical advice. Will you drag your trunks with you everywhere? Will there be a somewhere or a something over there in Manchuria where you can securely deposit part of your luggage? Or might you also be able to procure what you lack over there?

{p. 5}

A sound principle in such undertakings is: better too much than too little. Sending things back or, in the worst case, leaving them behind is always easier than having missing items sent on. In cases where transporting all one's luggage is uncertain, it is advantageous to divide it among several small trunks. Whoever cannot remember what each individual one contains should draw up an inventory of them.

It was advisable to procure everything at home, since in Russia even furs and galoshes are far more expensive than with us.

Photographic supplies, too, should be taken along from a firm known to be reputable; people are all too fond of foisting old plates and spoiled chemicals onto a stranger, whereby then—as chance usually has it—precisely the best shots are ruined.

My quite deficient knowledge of the Russian language still had to be supplemented, several books and papers on East Asia and the present war read, and several most humble letters written to high-ranking personages in Petersburg. An orderly person also makes arrangements in the event that he should never return.

All that, thank God, now lay behind me; I had even succeeded in stowing the trunks and bags—seven in number—in the compartment—and now, with God's help, we could be on our way!

I know no more magnificent feeling than setting out on a great journey. When the propeller begins to turn in the mighty colossus of a ship as it steers majestically away from the mole toward the open sea, or the comfortable Pullman carriage glides slowly along the platform, my heart fills with so much joy, so much longing for what is distant, for the unknown...

The train rumbles over the points, hurrying out of the suburbs of Vienna; the Giant Ferris Wheel still waves from the Prater, and then the train races in wild haste across the Marchfeld—and with it, far, far ahead into the indefinite, the uncertain, races my imagination.

Thoughts weave and entwine with one another until they turn into dreams; smoothly the springs sway and {p. 6} the wheels hum their lullaby. Thus it goes on for a few hours—then a jolt, the screech of the brakes, wakes us—the train comes to a halt. Outside, deep night; at the station of Granica, brilliant white, deep snow.

“Pozhaluysta, pasport!” “Passport, please!” The first word on Russian soil. Here, a passport is as much a part of a person as his soul. The police officer and customs officials are extremely polite; even the anxiety that they might charge duty on my two cameras once again is happily past. A large, elegant dining room, dignified calm, everything proceeds slowly, deliberately—confidently, so to speak. For the first time I feast on the famous Russian “zakuski,” delicacies laid out in lavish variety at the buffet.

They contribute to the favorable impression that I feel upon first stepping onto sacred Russian soil—just as I had expected, for at heart I had always been a sincere Russophile. Surely these were merely the slanders of a malicious press that sought to undermine Russia's prestige. To me, its military failures to date—though admittedly lasting rather long by now—were explainable by the exposed location of the theatre of war and other strategic reasons; I did not doubt that the tide must turn before long—only not too soon, so that I might still arrive in time!

My table neighbor—in his case the inspection of passport and baggage had taken somewhat longer, for he was a Jew—asked me about the purpose of my journey. “Why didn't you go to the Japanese instead?” he remarked. “Things won't go well for you among our people.” “Better than things apparently went for you in Kishinev,” I replied angrily, disturbed in my illusions, and went to the ticket office to purchase my seat reservation to Warsaw. Beside the counter stood a large tin with a red cross—a collection box for the surviving dependents of those fallen in the war. When I had dropped into the box the change from the ticket that the cashier slid across the counter to me, I felt that I too had now contributed my mite to the costs of the war—and was worthy to continue the journey.

{p. [15]}

## Riga.

*Early December 1904.*

The detour to Riga would hardly have squared with my impatience to reach Mukden as quickly as possible, had I not had dear relatives there. From them I hoped to receive many a useful piece of advice and an unvarnished account of the mood in Russia regarding the war at that time.

For at home it had been so difficult to form an accurate judgment of the course of the war from the newspapers. Aware that our entire press was anti-Russian, I was firmly convinced that the Russian cause had to be faring much better than could be assumed from the endless calamitous reports that were trumpeted to the world distorted and full of malicious glee.

My optimism suffered its first jolt here. I spoke with men whose word I could believe—among them wholesale suppliers to the navy and the railways. Corruption was said to be most blatant in the purchasing and acceptance commissions of the navy. The name of an Admiral M. was mentioned to me, who had issued bribery schedules arranged in downright tabular form for the various deliveries! In return, however, the contractor was permitted to deliver only fifteen thousand pieces instead of thirty thousand, and to split the remainder with the head of the acceptance commission.

Another gentleman had supplied railway frogs to a line in the Urals, I believe for four rubles apiece.

A year later, he bought back the very same goods from the officials for a few kopecks, only to sell them back to the government {p. 8} another year later at the original price! Railway construction in Russia must indeed be expensive—or seen another way: how immensely rich this country must be to be able to build so many railways under such a system!

However much was stolen in Russian railway construction, Russia’s rail network, including its Asian possessions, is the most magnificent in any country. Nowhere do we find conceptions so gigantic and at the same time so purposeful as here. The construction of its monumental railways across Siberia, Transbaikalia, and Manchuria to the ocean, and of that from Orenburg to Samarkand toward the Afghan and Persian borders, are not only technical masterpieces that moreover reveal a strong, resolute policy, but Russia has also rendered a great service to the entire civilized world through these projects, bringing both Europe and America closer to the previously closed interior of Asia. This was always one of those considerations that heightened my sympathy for Russia.

Riga is a splendid city, so completely different from other Russian cities. Its inhabitants are mostly Germans. They all sincerely longed for the ultimate victory of Russian arms—most believed in it, too; they all had a stake in the events in the Far East.

Few parts of the tsarist empire were struck as painfully by the casualty lists after the first engagements as the Baltic provinces; not only is a large part of the naval crews drawn from them, but, following old traditions, the nobility of these lands has its second sons serve in the army or as naval officers. The conspicuously large number of German names that became well known among Russian army and naval commanders in the course of the war bears eloquent witness to this. Midshipman von Schlippe, son of the Marshal of the Nobility of Volhynia, whom I visited, is one of the seven officers who survived the catastrophe of the battleship *Petropavlovsk*; his account, coming from an authentic source, will certainly still command interest today.

“An unfavorable factor,” von Schlippe recounted, “for the Port Arthur fleet was the narrow harbor entrance, which at {p. 9} that time was passable for larger ships for barely two hours before and after high tide. Our bold Admiral Makarov sought to make up for this disadvantage by personally taking command of small squadrons and going out to meet the superior enemy, with the intention of drawing the Japanese onto Russian mines through feigned maneuvers. The adversary, for his part, tried to obstruct our wide-open roadstead with mines, which, however, mostly failed thanks to the vigilance of our coastal searchlights and torpedo craft; a great many Japanese boats were ‘bagged’ in the process, and their mines rendered harmless.

“On the night of March 30–31—it was around the time of the Russian Easter—our torpedo craft had once again clashed with Japanese minelayers. The latter, six ships in all, succeeded in cutting off and surrounding the torpedo boat *Strashny*. Admiral Makarov immediately launched an attack on the Japanese with those ships that were initially able to clear the harbor entrance, but he could no longer prevent the *Strashny* from being sent to the bottom before our eyes, whereupon the enemy, declining battle with us, escaped our fire thanks to their high speed. Soon, however, they had joined up with significant forces and confronted us with superior numbers. Already 15 miles from the harbor, the Russian side had to take into account the risk of being cut off from port, from which no further reinforcements could be expected due to the onset of low tide. Admiral Makarov ordered a retreat, by which, however, he simultaneously attempted to lure the enemy onto our mines. The Japanese did not follow us this time, and soon only the smoke from their funnels betrayed their presence. Unmolested, we steamed back to Port Arthur: first four cruisers, then *Pallada* and *Petropavlovsk*.”

It had been a hard day for us; we had been on our feet since early morning and had eaten nothing yet. I felt hungry and went into the officers' mess, where the Easter table was still set. As I entered, I saw the Admiral standing quietly and pensively in the room. Around the table stood seven or eight officers, drinking tea and discussing {p. 10} the events of the day. Tired as I was, I sat down on a sofa; next to me sat an engineer officer.

We were two miles from the port; on deck, preparations were already being made to drop anchor. Suddenly, I felt as if the sofa I was sitting on were falling through the floor, and immediately afterwards the colossal ship shuddered and groaned in every joint.

“A mine!” flashed through my mind at once, and I ran up onto the deck. The deck swayed and buckled beneath my feet. On the way to the tween deck, a second terrible shock—our own mine magazine had exploded. Hot billows of smoke poured into the upper officers' quarters; red flames leaped toward me from the battery deck. Groping, I found the second companionway, where a crowd, already unrecognizable to me through the smoke and fire fumes, was crammed before a half-open battle hatch. I pushed my way through and gave the order to open the hatch from the outside.

Now I could breathe and see again. The sight was terrible. The entire ship was listing to port, the propeller thrashing in the air. A mighty column of fire rose from the spar deck and swept, hissing and crackling, across the upper deck, which was already rearing skyward. Masts, funnels, everything crashed down in confusion. I remember seeing Grand Duke Cyril on the conning bridge. I looked around for a lifebelt, but saw none; next to me was the accommodation ladder, and I lowered myself down to the water. Our priest was already battling the waves; a few times he spun around in a circle, then a vortex dragged him down into the depths; immediately afterwards, a wave swept me far away. Foaming water washed over me; the instinct to survive gave me strength, but rapid blows from hard objects in quick succession to my head knocked me senseless for a moment. Suddenly it grew lighter; I felt myself carried to the surface and drifted upon the foaming waters. The waves grew calmer; I looked around, but in vain: the proud 'Petropavlovsk' had vanished. Beams, pieces of wreckage, an overturned boat to which several men were clinging—that was all there was to be seen.

I slipped several of the floating pieces of wood under {p. 11} my arm and tried to pull off my shoes, but soon I felt violent pain and my strength waned. I remember seeing the Grand Duke beside me again at that moment; he was wearing an overcoat and cap and clinging to a shattered steam launch. My body began to grow numb; every second seemed an eternity to me; for fifteen minutes I had to keep myself afloat like that, then the cutter 'Gaidamak' took me to the torpedo boat 'Bezshumny', where first aid was administered to me. Debris had severely injured my head, particularly my ear. Taken ashore, I had breakfast in the railway carriage of the Grand Duke, who had suffered severe burns to his hands and feet. The next day I was still able to attend the memorial service, but then I collapsed. After being bedridden for two months, I was brought unconscious to Harbin, to the Moscow branch of the Red Cross, where cranial surgery was performed on me.”

To my question as to how, despite the Russian precautionary measures described at the outset, the Japanese had managed to lay that fateful mine in the immediate vicinity of the harbour, Herr von Schlippe remarked to me that this could only have happened during the engagement of the torpedo boats that had likewise already been discussed.

Our conversation naturally turned also to the Baltic Fleet. Its potential future pivot point, Vladivostok, is by no means impracticable in winter for the entrance and exit of large ships, as is generally assumed. The sea itself is ice-free, and the ice in the harbour is never so thick that the fleet's icebreakers could not establish communication with the open sea.

At that time, the articles by “Captain Klado” were creating a great sensation both in Russia and in the foreign press. The Second Squadron, which, having finally put to sea after months of trials, had immediately made a tragicomic laughingstock of itself at Hull, was at that moment still pinned down in Vigo and Tangier, and already people were speaking of a third squadron.

This squadron will—and indeed must—depart, so it was said, if {p. 12} Russia does not want to have its entire naval power destroyed piecemeal in the Far East. People here had already arrived at the realization that Rozhestvensky must confront Togo not as soon as possible, but *as strongly as possible*. Recent days had proven that he could count only on the forces he brought with him; but according to a table drawn up in September of this year by a high authority, this balance of forces stood none too favorably for the Russian admiral. Specifically, according to the compilation—excluding torpedo boats—15 Russian ships faced 33 Japanese, 95,200 tons against 216,000, 703 guns against 941. While this ratio was probably no longer entirely accurate at the time, since several more ships had been assigned to the Baltic Squadron after the month of September, and on the other hand Japan had also lost ships during this period, this circumstance cannot alter the fact that the forces placed under Admiral Rozhestvensky up to that point were bound to be too weak to fight for and secure lasting command of the sea in Japanese waters.

Russia, however, still had sufficient material at its disposal to dispatch another strong auxiliary squadron consisting of 7 battleships, 1 armored gunboat, 4 cruisers, 4 torpedo cruisers, 14 destroyers, 1 mine transport, and 2 hospital ships. Among these was, above all, the battleship *Slava*, a sister ship of the *Orel*, *Borodino*, *Alexander III*, and *Suvorov*, which formed the core of the Second Squadron. How little conviction there was here even in February regarding the necessity of dispatching a second squadron is shown by the fact that when the shipyard where the *Slava* was being built declared itself ready to complete the armored vessel by autumn, this offer was not even given the necessary attention. When, after two months, it was nonetheless found necessary to proceed more energetically with the *Slava*, the incident with the cruiser *Orel* occurred, whose bearings overheated during its first sea trial as a result of sand having been introduced, and which also sank shortly afterward in the harbor of Kronstadt. In order to replace certain damaged engine parts quickly, they were taken from the *Slava*, on which {p. 13} work was then suspended again. For the Third Squadron, the battleships *Imperator Nikolai I* and *Imperator Alexander II* were also available, though certainly not new (1887 and 1889). In Libau, moreover, three modern armored ships equipped with the latest artillery were fitting out, namely the *General-Admiral Apraksin*, *Admiral Ushakov*, and *Admiral Senyavin* (1893–1896). Furthermore, the old battleship *Pyotr Veliky* was also available. Of the cruisers, I would also mention the *Pamyat Azova*, *Vladimir Monomakh* (sister ship of the *Dmitry Donskoy*), and the modern *Admiral Kornilov*. Of the destroyers, six have been under construction for ten years. The firm of Krupp had, as is well known, presented the Russian government with a submarine valued at one and a half million rubles as a gift, and in return had also received an order for five more such boats. As a repair ship, the huge new *Okean*, and as hospital ships, the *Polyarnaya Zvezda* and *Standart* should be mentioned. Owing to their speed, the last three could also be used for reconnaissance, in which case, however, they would have to be placed in the ranks of combatant vessels.

The mission of the Third Squadron depends on the timing of its departure. The favorable outcome would undoubtedly be if it could still enter into action simultaneously with the Second Squadron. In this case, the older ships could cover the better ships of the combined squadrons. They could find a further use in hunting Japanese transport ships. If the two squadrons intervene in events at different times, the mission can only be accomplished if Rozhestvensky succeeds in first winning a partial success over Togo, joining up with the Vladivostok squadron, and postponing the decisive battle until the arrival of the Third Squadron. All speculation regarding the establishment of a base of operations, its location, Togo steaming out as far as Singapore to meet them, etc., is still decidedly premature today. Equally groundless are the rumors that Russia intends to send its Black Sea Fleet to the Far East.

{p. 14}

Apart from the fact that, after the Dogger Bank incident, Russia would hardly be inclined to bring new political trouble upon itself by forcing the Dardanelles, its warships have far too limited an operating radius for such an undertaking and, moreover, are fitted for oil-firing. After all, every day can bring new surprises that may completely alter the situation. One circumstance that had a disruptive effect on the dispatch of the Third Squadron was the shortage of marine engineers.

These were pretty much the data I gathered in Riga and later in Petersburg regarding Russia's naval strength at that time—or, expressed in the style of a reporter: obtained confidentially from a high-ranking personality at the Admiralty.

[Illustration: Vignette with landscape view]

{p. [23]}

## Petersburg.

*December 1904.*

For a week now I have been in the capital of the tsarist empire, which stands under the sign of one of the bloodiest wars of all time—and behold! Nothing, absolutely nothing of the war to be felt! It may well also be the present standstill of operations that has smoothed the waves of agitation, yet they are said not to have run particularly high among the people even during the days of Liaoyang. The theatre of war is many thousands of miles away from the heart of the empire. This alone gives the war and its home front a different character from those cases where the capital is at the same time an operational objective of the enemy. The comparison of Petersburg today with Vienna at the time of the July days of sixty-six, or with Paris during the German advance, can serve as a factor in assessing the political and military situation. In the tsarist empire, *space and time play a different role than with us Western and Central Europeans.* What do the several hundred kilometres from the Yalu to Mukden—taken, of course, as a loss of ground—signify compared to the colossal dimensions of the empire, and what do one or two more years of war mean in the slowly rolling history of Russia! The Russian does not know the proverb “time is money”; yet if patience really brought roses, Providence would long ago have stuck a rose in the buttonhole of every Old Believer.

Everything here runs in its accustomed groove; calm and grave. Were it not for the bright jingling of bells from hundreds upon hundreds of little sledges—Petersburg's peculiar means of transport—ringing out merrily until late at night through the wide {p. 16} streets and across the endlessly vast squares, an ear deafened by the din of our great cities could scarcely believe itself to be in a city of a million people. But let us not content ourselves with strolling the streets; let us look deeper into the soul of the people and investigate what lies hidden behind this almost sluggish-seeming calm.

Already at the border I had noticed beside the ticket window those little collection boxes with the Red Cross; some for the bereaved, others for field ambulances and hospitals. Along the way, as well as here in Petersburg, I find on every tavern table, in every shop a collection point for charitable gifts intended for the orphans of fallen soldiers; neat little tin houses into whose chimney now a kopeck, now even a gold coin drops.

[Illustration: Winter Palace on the Neva.]

And now onward. Let us make our way into a salon of Petersburg high life. Bright girls' voices ring out to greet us; from the adjoining rooms we hear the whirr of sewing machines. The lady of the house, a long pair of shears in her right hand, stands at an enormous cutting table that has been placed in the middle of the state room. All around, blooming youth, at {p. 17} whose cradle it was never sung that they would wield the needle. There is joking, laughing—and sewing for the soldiers in the Far East. After the theatre, gentlemen in tailcoats appear; they too are enlisted in the service of charity and, to the amusement and mockery of the ladies, must try their hand at various menial tasks of the noble tailor's craft. Thus people chat and flirt, speaking of everything except the war; and yet many a piece of white linen that today still glides through lovely hands will in a few months be soaked with red blood!

A few ladies have left the gathering early. In the summer they completed an auxiliary training course and are now performing auxiliary service, partly in hospitals, partly in outpatient clinics for convalescent wounded; that requires being in good time and punctual. “How could all this be otherwise,” the lady of the house told me, “when our own sublime monarch leads the way untiringly with an inspiring example. You must go and see the Winter Palace; then you will understand what the Empress's love was capable of creating for our soldiers.”

A broad marble staircase led me the following day into a glittering foyer. A brief introduction to an officer of the Guards, who requests my card. Footmen in gold-embroidered liveries carry tea trays into the halls. Along the wall lie small and large parcels, sewn into coarse burlap, addressed to Mukden, Harbin, etc. I had kindly been exempted from the ceremonial prescribed for visiting the imperial apartments; I was introduced to Countess M., who conducted me into the halls.

First, a spacious apartment in which several hundred sewing machines are merrily turning. Among other things, handkerchiefs are being hemmed here, printed at the four corners with portraits of the imperial family, and in the centre with maps of the theatre of war showing Port Arthur. In the next hall, four stately ladies preside at large tables; I believe they cut the fabric and then distribute the work to young ladies who look quite charming in their white aprons and sleeve protectors. My complete ignorance of plain needlework unfortunately prevents me from following the factual explanations of my charming {p. 18} companion with proper understanding, yet wherever I look, I see diligent and exceedingly delicate hands busily at work. All linen goods are washed before being packed. We come to a spacious pressing room, where the linens are sorted and stamped. What a mundane domestic activity beneath lavishly gilded chandeliers and marble cornices! We come into a hall where bed linen for hospital trains is being made, then into a department for woollens and warm footwear. Now we enter the Empress's famous winter garden. In the vestibule is a large machine that automatically cuts bandages. Here I also meet several ladies from yesterday's gathering. Their hands are covered in paste; their task here is to pack and seal bandages.

[Illustration: Improvised workroom in the Winter Palace.]

“Is work carried on here every day?” — “Every day from 3 to 7 in the evening, with the exception of Thursdays, when the school children come to us.” — “And who donates the materials for all this?” — “For the most part the Tsarina from her private funds, and partly from the monetary donations received here. Princess Golitsyna performs her duties here, overseeing the receipt and acknowledging of incoming sums.”

“About how many ladies would you say work in the Winter Palace every day?” — “At present about a thousand, but in the summer, when the Empress was here, there may well have been twice as many.” — On the second floor I find several rooms crammed with books and periodicals, old and new, Russian, German, French, just as they arrive and as people {p. 19} bring them in from the street. Reading material for the wounded in the field hospitals. Adjoining this is a room filled with small parcels wrapped in colorful cloth. “Dlya soldat”—“for the soldier” is the simple address. These are gifts from humble folk, which are collected and forwarded postage-free. The Christmas presents already departed last month; every soldier receives a tin box containing sugar, tea, chocolate, tobacco, a pipe, a sewing kit, some linen, smoked meat, and a piece of soap. The entire country contributed to this gift, and in the end every donor enclosed a postcard with their address, asking the recipient to write a few words about how and where he spent Christmas Eve. From here we enter long corridors in which crates are stacked up to the ceiling. These are the gifts from abroad. France, of course, in the lead; Mumm and

[Illustration: Seamstresses in the reception hall of the Winter Palace.]

Roederer are plentifully represented—icing the noble beverage over in Mukden will not be difficult. To my astonishment, I hear that after France, *England* ranks next as the most generous benefactor. Twenty-four large crates from a Miss B. arrived this week, in addition {p. 20} to considerable shipments of port wine. Germany likewise does not lag behind. Via a small spiral staircase we reach the interior of the imperial theater—yet one would scarcely recognize it as such. Rows of seats, boxes, stage, everything covered with crates, bales, or goods not yet packed. It serves as a depot from which loading onto the trains takes place. Two freight trains weekly handle the dispatch of these colossal quantities; transit takes two months, as freight trains must still give way to troop transports. Russia also seems to reciprocate the expressions of sympathy from its neighbors. Whatever cannot be produced domestically is ordered from them; thus I am shown a shipment of woolen shirts from Berlin valued at 26,000 marks. The packing slips affixed to the containers indicate packaging in complete sets, which will greatly simplify distribution on the spot.

“God grant,” says my companion, “that all these gifts may still reach those for whom they are intended.” I must confess that on leaving the Winter Palace, my sympathy for Russia had once again been strongly reinforced.

Outside, thousands of lights flickered through the thick fog; a warm wind from the sea had melted the snow, and foul mud covered the streets. The bright jingling of sleigh bells had fallen silent; grave and taciturn, people went their way. Flags fluttered from every roof; Petersburg was adorning itself for the morrow, for the Tsar’s name day.

My stay in Petersburg was beginning to drag out unpleasantly. Here began my training in patience.—I could not accustom myself so quickly to this *disregard of time* à la russe.

I had delivered my letters of recommendation with more or less success; the people were all courteous and hospitable. Yet all my efforts would have been in vain had our ambassador, His Excellency Baron Lexa von Aehrenthal, not taken an interest in me.

“So you have come after all, even though I had word sent to you that your plan would hardly be feasible!” — {p. 21} “Excellency, I am firmly resolved to go over; if not otherwise, then as a medical orderly!” “Well, perhaps it can be managed by another route after all,” His Excellency remarked; “you did mention that you feel a journalistic vein within you; something might best be done with that.” “Certainly, Excellency,” I replied, happy at the glimmer of hope that seemed to shine from the ambassador’s answer; Lieutenant Colonel Prince Alexander Volkonsky had also advised me in his letters that this was the only possible way.

[Illustration: Red Cross train.]

“I am certain, Excellency,” I continued, “that by working as a journalist I can render many a service to the Russian cause. It is even a principal motive of my journey to refute, on the basis of firsthand observation and my professional expertise, all those spiteful lies that a prejudiced press has disseminated throughout the world about the Russian army and its leaders.”

Baron Aehrenthal did not let the matter rest with the gracious promise of his support, but had the kindness to {p. 22} request his secretary of legation, Count Trauttmansdorff, to proceed that very same day to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs on my behalf.

If I now thought that I would be underway in a few days, this proved only my complete ignorance of that complicated clockwork known as Russian bureaucratism. First of all, I had to gain clarity on the meaning of the word “*zawdra*.” In German, “*zawdra*” would mean nothing more than “tomorrow.” Unsuspecting, I clung to this literal translation and for two weeks presented myself trustingly day after day at the office of the Great General Staff, where the friendly Captain Nikolski assured me most definitely each time that General Kuropatkin’s telegraphic permission would be here zawdra, that I would most certainly receive my propusk zawdra, and could depart zawdra. In the Russian reckoning of time, the difference in dates is probably the slightest distinction; one could most easily grow accustomed to that; the harder thing for us fast-living Western and Central Europeans lies in the far more protracted assessment of the value of time in general.

If the Russian says “*sejcsasz*” = “immediately,” he means at best the next day; but if he says “*zawdra*,” it is the brief designation for something dragging out quite indefinitely, at best from a week upwards.

[Illustration: Transport of a heavy gun.]

{p. 23}

Yet waiting here in Petersburg at the same time as myself were many others. The Hôtel de France was teeming with merchants and industrial magnates from all over the world. One had ships to sell to the Russian government, another cannons, a third tinned provisions, a fourth macaroni, and so on. They were all waiting for the “zawdra” on which their deal was to be concluded. What a life there was in that hotel, a babel of languages—a squandering of money in the hope of future gain! They all hoped—only two groups were certain: the Germans and the French. As I heard, these were said to have owed the conclusion of many an advantageous contract above all to the active support of their military attachés.

In the great industrial competition of our time, in which the marketing of war materiel plays a leading role, a well-sounding name and good outward manners are not enough for a military attaché. Even an exclusively military education, however thorough, does not suffice on its own—*beyond* this, he must have knowledge and understanding that cannot, to be sure, be acquired in the salons of the upper ten thousand.

For Hungary, which offered cartridges and tinned provisions for sale, the presence of the *Hungarian commercial attaché von Podhorszky* was of the greatest advantage, while the *Austrian firm of Skoda* was supported to the utmost by the joint ambassador.

At that time, my observations on the occasion of my daily visits to the War Ministry and the Great General Staff were by no means uninteresting. How many people of all social classes were there, inquiring about the fate of their nearest and dearest in the theatre of war! What anxious hope, grief, and pain could be read in their faces! How many officers shot to cripples did I already see in the anterooms back then; most had come to plead for an increase in their meager pension.

At last—who could describe my delight—my “zawdra” too had seen the dawn. From General Adjutant {p. 24} Kuropatkin an affirmative answer had arrived, a propusk was issued to me, my passport visaed valid for Mukden—nothing stood in the way of my journey to the Russian headquarters any longer. To have requested, waited for, and obtained all this in spite of all the unfavorable prognoses given to me could only enliven my joy, my self-confidence, and my faith in the future all the more.

[Illustration: Entrainment.]

{p. [33]}

[Illustration: Our traveling party through Northern Manchuria.]

{p. [34]}

{p. [35]}

## To Mukden.

*January 1905.*

There is something splendid about traveling. To speed through one and a half continents in flight, to see different regions and different people every day, and with it the refined comfort of the Trans-Siberian Express! I had left Petersburg after long waiting and hoping. It was on the evening of January 3, at a time when newspapers abroad were already carrying editorials on the fall of Port Arthur. Here people still believed that the foreign dispatches had exaggerated this time as well, for only on the following day was the event officially confirmed. In Moscow I was surprised by the fierce tone of the papers. The foreign newspapers, too, had been spared that black stamp which usually covers most of their columns. Was this supposed to signify a nascent freedom of the press, or were the Christmas holidays to blame, during which even the strict censorship grew weary of its duties?

Since Sunday evening I have been rolling toward the Far East, and the time when I shall see European newspapers again lies far ahead of me. From now on, the people themselves will be an open book to me, in which, if one observes attentively, a great deal can be read. The Russian is approachable and talkative. Recent events seem to have loosened his tongue, and he is particularly fond of speaking about them to strangers. The military situation, especially the fall of the fortress, is heatedly discussed, though rarely from a military standpoint, mostly more from a social one. A colorful company always gathers in the dining car. In Colonel K. of the artillery I shall have an excellent traveling companion; he is traveling with me as far as Mukden, where he will join the staff of the {p. 28} Third Army, under General Kaulbars, as chief of transport. An engineer from Vladivostok, who paid his respects to Admiral Rozhestvensky in Vigo, will accompany me as far as Harbin. He describes the admiral as a very calm man, though it seemed to him as if a certain nervousness lay concealed behind his reticence. “We were not mistaken!” he is said to have remark regarding the Hull incident.

Among the gentlemen, a gigantic Caucasian in national dress also catches my eye, for whom even the high doors of the Siberian railway are too low. We have few ladies on board. A merry little grandmother who, back during the Russo-Turkish War, had established a large hospital in Moscow out of her own means. This time the matron has set up a feeding station in Chelyabinsk and a hospital for fifty-six soldiers in Omsk. But entirely independently—she wants nothing to do with the Red Cross! Not only she, but many others as well, are seeking ways to direct charity straight to the right place, bypassing the collection points of the Red Cross, which has lost the trust of poor and rich alike. Much is told about this, as well as about the meager generosity of the wealthy monasteries toward the wounded, likewise about the famous blanket affair that took place between the well-known industrial magnate Savva Morozov and Grand Duke Sergei.

Other great lords who were involved in the timber speculations on the Yalu appear in an equally unfavorable light. People point to their greed and predatory policy as the real cause of Russia’s entanglement in this war. The war does not seem to be popular. Among the fellow passengers there is only one—a long-bearded timber merchant from Irkutsk, a genuine Russian type—who finds good in everything God and the Tsar have done. He crosses himself at every spoonful of soup and before every icon of Mary; he represents, so to speak, the war party.

Yet the day is short, and it is far more worthwhile to look out the window during the few daylight hours in order to imprint the ever-changing scenes upon one’s memory, to which {p. 29} my amateur cameras have so far rarely been able to come to the aid because of snowstorms and fog.

Early Tuesday morning we crossed the Volga bridge near Syzran. A monumental structure resting on twenty-eight piers. We crossed it slowly, taking about ten minutes. At the beginning of the war, Japanese officers wanted to blow it up; the daredevils were caught and hanged. Since then, platforms on which sentries stand have been erected on every pier. At Samara station I was shown a large barracks camp. The city built it for seventy thousand rubles; barely finished, it proved entirely unusable. It is said to have stood empty ever since.

[Illustration: Station in the Urals.]

On Wednesday we crossed the Urals; splendid winter scenery, accompanied by a snowstorm that whipped thin ice through the carriage doors. But we are quite splendidly provided for against such weather. The enclosed vestibule forms a kind of anteroom, from which two well-fitting doors lead into the heated corridor. Thanks to the holidays we have only five first-class passengers, so that each of us occupies a compartment to himself. When the electric table lamp is lit in the evening on the small table upon which the conductor has placed a glass of steaming tea, it sounds quite cozy behind the double-locked window as the storm outside sweeps across the vast snowfields. Then one's thoughts wander into the past and into the misty future. Time for that we have, indeed. With time one can no longer be so particular here anyway. The timetable is drawn up according to Petersburg time, yet our delay varies between five and seven hours. Local time also changes from day to day, so that the only reliable timepiece left is one’s own stomach. My other traveling companions seem to have plenty of time as well. The question {p. 30} of whether we arrive in Irkutsk on Sunday, Monday, or even Tuesday seems to affect them little.

On Wednesday evening we left the beautiful Ural landscapes behind us, and since then we have been travelling through the endless snowfields of Siberia. Snow, snow, and yet more snow, as far as the eye can see, up to the distant horizon where the glittering plain disappears into vague mists. Along the line, snow fences against drifts, now and then a stunted birch copse, a telegraph line leading inland—that is all that interrupts this lifeless monotony. From hour to hour a small station building, buried up to the windows in snow, solitary in the endless expanse.

[Illustration: Buryats.]

The settlements seem for the most part to lie far off the line. I would have imagined the traffic on the line itself to be livelier. Except for an ambulance train which we overtook beyond Ufa, and a transport of recruits coming towards us from Irkutsk, not much of a military nature has been seen so far. Large troop transports are said to have passed through here several weeks ago; {p. 31} people speak of more than a thousand wagons heading east in a single rotation.

A merry life prevailed at the station in Chelyabinsk. Siberian officers going on two months' convalescent leave, before returning to the front. They were quite weather-beaten, and their tall fur caps gave them a martial appearance. I thoroughly enjoyed watching them drink champagne, vodka, and tea wildly mixed together.

[Illustration: Siberian reservists.]

On Thursday we passed Kurgan; it recalls the history of the Decembrists and of Kotzebue, who likewise lived here.

Towards evening we reached Petropavlovsk, which lies right along the railway; there is not much good to say about it. Outside the station, many Tatars—we are already deep in Asia. A huge array of rolling stock had assembled on the sidings, perhaps seventy to a hundred axles in all.

{p. 32}

A short stroll in the open air does one a world of good, especially as it is quite pleasant outside. Since leaving the Urals, there has been no more than five to six degrees of frost; yesterday the express train from Irkutsk even brought word that they had two degrees of warmth there. People console me with the assurance that I shall have ample opportunity yet to get to know the Siberian winter.

This evening we reach the Ob, and then we will travel merrily towards the Russian New Year. How we shall establish the midnight hour, I do not quite know, but this will not dampen our high spirits. Just now there was a knock at my door. The colonel and my neighbour, the engineer, came to ask whether I would care to join in the collective New Year's celebration with the obligatory champagne punch, in which most of the passengers will join together tonight. How good it feels not to have to wander through the world all alone, but to find dear people everywhere, as I have been fortunate enough to do so far. Happy New Year, and joyful hope for the future, however enveloped in a blue haze it may lie before me!

The New Year's celebration was quite cosy. The plump blonde woman from second class, with the Red Cross on her arm, was the only lady. Most of the passengers had alighted towards morning at Taiga. I was rather disappointed by the great primeval forests I had hoped to see. At Mariinsk I awoke to find once again the scene of previous days: a plain with devastated, deforested woodland along the railway line.

Their esteemed colleague at Klyukvennaya does little credit to Russian postmasters. He was unmistakably still fatigued by the mental exertions of New Year's Day—to put it in plain German, too lazy to register my registered letter. First he claimed he could not find the register, then that he could not read my handwriting, then he insisted that he did not know and had no need to know where Budapest, together with Hungary, was located—and finally he claimed that my letter was insufficiently stamped. Outside, the second bell sounded.

I had bought a large sheet of stamps for several roubles, which I held out to him. He tore off the larger {p. 33} half—saying he would stick them on himself so that I would not be delayed—and I was glad that the ink-slinger at least made out the receipt for me.

Lodging a complaint about such things would only be an unnecessary waste of time—far worse things happen here that go unpunished. Where time loses its value, there can be neither punctuality nor order; in the chaos of slackness, the legal entitlement of the individual is lost.

[Illustration: Halt in the snowfield.]

{p. [42]}

## Irkutsk.

A cold, dark night. Outside the railcar, a balalaika and dancing soldiers. Is it joy that they are going off to war, or are they dancing to keep from freezing? We are in Irkutsk. Things will hardly proceed as smoothly and comfortably as before. Above all, how are we to get into the city, across to the other bank of the Angara? In summer, a pontoon bridge leads across the torrential river; it was taken down with the onset of the drifting ice. If the Angara freezes over in severe cold, the matter is once again quite simple. It is less favorable, however, when ice is drifting. Even before reaching Irkutsk, we were told that six people had drowned the day before while crossing. Most reassuring! For the time being, we sit in the small, smoky buffet room waiting for morning, which in these parts dawns very late in winter. From time to time, someone brings news. One moment it is said that the river is frozen over; the cold is at thirty degrees again, after all. Then boatmen appear, offering to take us across immediately for a large sum of money. Another advises waiting for morning to take the steamboat, which might still be running. And so, yawning and shivering, one empties glass after glass of chai, until another batch of passengers arrives from Lake Baikal. Now, however, the small room is packed to suffocating. A motley crowd: officers, Chinese, children, Sisters of the Cross, figures bundled up in tall *tachas*—the shapeless Siberian fur coat—and among them a most peculiar trio; at first glance, it brings to mind Nestroy’s merry brothers in *Lumpazivagabundus* [*The Rogues' Trio*]. In threadbare black overcoats, light little hats on their heads, and without galoshes, they are easily recognized as foreigners. Perhaps German journeymen wanting to head west again. I sit {p. 35} down near them to hear what they are saying. They are railing against the stationmaster, and they may not be so wrong about that. We were twenty hours late getting to Irkutsk. As my ticket from Petersburg to Manchuria Station is only valid for twenty days, I asked the *nachalnik*—as these gentlemen are called in Russian—to certify the delay. He calmly refuses; in wartime, he says, he is not allowed to do such a thing! What that has to do with the war is not clear to me, but one must comply.—The man was simply too lazy to dip his pen into the inkwell!

[Illustration: Irkutsk.]

Morning finally comes. We drive down to the Angara. To my astonishment, the coachman speaks German—I still know this jargon from Galicia.—“How did you come to be here?”—“I was sent away, that is to say exiled, eleven years ago, sir.”—“Did you murder someone?”—“No, sir, only for counterfeit money, sir.”

Down at the icy bank, a struggle with various people who want to haul our trunks into their boats. They lie, saying that the launch will not be running, yet it is already whistling for departure. A wretched, small, very high-built screw steamer. A narrow, icy plank leads steeply over the deep water {p. 36} up to the deck, and people push and shove across it. Things are beginning to get Asiatic, I think to myself. From above, no river is to be seen, nothing but dense fog. With one foot on the railing and the other on the engine, our captain stands, a splendid, daring fellow. With his left hand he points the way to the helmsman; with his right he pulls the steam whistle from time to time. Sometimes he has the engine stopped or even put into reverse when ice floes that are too massive block our way. Thus we wind our way successfully through fog and ice to the other bank. A jolt that sends the trunks rolling across the deck, and we have landed. Only now do I see that the sides of our little vessel are made of nothing but thin wood! “That such a thing is even permitted!” I say, forgetting once again that I am already in Asia.

As I walked through the wide streets with their clean, mostly single-story, though also two- and three-story houses, I had the distinct feeling of being in one of our provincial towns between the Tisza and the Danube. Only the tall, magnificent domed structures of the numerous churches lend the Russian character. The older houses are all of wood, including the elegant “Hotel Metropole.” These buildings are the only typical ones. Gateways, window cornices, and the seldom-missing, usually tasteful veranda all display a mixture of Russian-Chinese ornamentation. A stone triumphal arch in a similar style commemorates the pioneer of the Amur region, Count Muravyov. Besides many inns, there are three large hotels, all of which are packed full. Mostly officers, both healthy and sick; nor is the eternal feminine lacking, making its presence felt in the local *cafés-chantants*. Prices for lodgings and provisions are enormous.

Already along the line over the past few days, the traffic of troop and hospital transports had grown brisker. We had encountered the first major transport of wounded at Achinsk, and it was a very large one indeed; the men were well accommodated, and their care seemed to be irreproachable.

Here in Irkutsk, however, one truly feels that one is drawing nearer to the war. The recruits have only just been called up. In the barracks, in every larger courtyard, even on the street {p. 37} [Illustration: Red Cross feeding station for the sick.]

drills are held. “Na pravo, na levo,” one hears from all sides. Many detachments are still in civilian clothes, wearing only the large, shaggy papakha on their heads. In this principal staging station, many commissariat officers and military officials have their permanent quarters; military doctors for 7,000 sick, officers arriving and departing daily, among them some in the picturesque costume of the Caucasians—all this offers an ever-changing, colorful scene. Even apart from this, Irkutsk, as an entrepôt for Chinese and Siberian trade to the West, enjoys quite lively commerce. At midday and in the evening there is bustling life; in the brightly lit display windows of the department stores, some of which are laid out on a grand scale, are to be found all the goods of European industry, everything fabulously expensive, of course. The only thing here that is excellent and for which one pays nothing is the crystal-clear water of the Angara, which makes up for the lack of good wells.

{p. 38}

Once the stores are locked up at eight o'clock in the evening, however, deep darkness reigns and shady rabble roams through the town. To be sure, night watchmen patrol the streets, striking their sticks against the ground—a reminder of Constantinople—yet cases of late passers-by being stripped of fur coat and purse even on the main streets are by no means rare.

And now a look at one of those places where an attempt is made to heal the many wounds inflicted by the war. It is the hospital built from an endowment by General Kaufmann and bearing the name of the Dowager Empress. The chief physician, a German, receives me and most kindly explains everything to me.

[Illustration: East of Tankhoy.]

The permanent medical establishments begin in Mukden. Formerly there were also splendid hospitals in Liaoyang, which are now in the hands of the Japanese. The largest casualty distribution station is Harbin, with beds for 30,000 sick; from there begins the evacuation to the west and to the east, to Nikolsk, Vladivostok, and Khabarovsk. Before Irkutsk as well, along the entire railway line, {p. 39} there are hospitals, the largest in Chita. Actual hospital trains run as far as Irkutsk; from here, only the evacuation of convalescents or medical patients takes place.

The hospital trains outfitted through the generosity of Their Majesties and of individual princes or corporations are said to be splendidly equipped, whereas those of the Red Cross as well as the military hospital trains consist merely of improvisations from freight and passenger cars respectively.

Things are said to have been dreadful after the days of Liaoyang and Mukden. Often there was barely any straw for bedding; the wounded simply had to be crammed into the cars as quickly as possible, and one was glad to find enough time at the stops to remove those who had died along the way. Now, thank God, it has become quieter, and we can attend to the patients in greater detail. The doctor was wearing a white work coat splattered with fresh blood. “What, do major operations still take place now?”—“They are mostly internal suppurations caused by remaining projectile fragments or bone splinters, which necessitate reopening.” From his purse he took a shrapnel ball that he had just removed from a man’s shoulder blade.

“My colleagues in the field hospitals do not have enough time,” he explained to me, “and here we often face cases where the wounds have scarred over beautifully, but the man complains of terrible internal pain. I extracted this bullet jacket from a patient, for which almost the entire pelvis had to be opened.” The bullet jacket he showed me resembled a coiled spiral, with a piece of the bullet base still jammed into its upper end. What terrible pain the poor man must have suffered with this thing in his bones for two months! We came to the patient, who lay pale and lifeless in bed after enduring the operation. A Red Cross sister was sitting with him. These young ladies—they are exclusively girls from good families—do not belong to any religious order, but have completed a two-year nursing course in Petersburg, Moscow, or other larger Russian cities, after which they are assigned to auxiliary service in the hospitals.

{p. 40}

Usually they receive free board and thirty rubles a month—certainly not much in a country where the ruble has the purchasing power of a crown! In this hospital of the Empress, the young ladies nursing have renounced all remuneration for their services. I could not conceal from the doctor my admiration for his undertaking, far from home, such a task that consumes both physical and moral strength!

In the hospital lay nearly two hundred patients. Most wore the Cross of St. George or another reward for their bravery pinned to their shirts; in exchange, however, they had had to give up their arms or legs. Many amputations had been performed—almost everywhere bullets had struck at long ranges and the impact had been not straight on, but sideways.

"The Japanese bullets—they have the smallest caliber—are almost too humane," the doctor told me, "mostly they pass clean through, the wound heals in a few weeks—but the worst is yet to come! We had a man here,

[Illustration: Station building on the Transbaikal line.] {p. 41} the bullet had entered at the back of his head and come out through the nasal bone; after a few weeks he was cured and asked to be allowed to continue serving as a medical orderly. After several months, without any previous morbid symptoms, the man faints; two days later he was dead." The doctor wanted to show me several more "fine" cases, a shattered jaw, an excised shoulder blade, splintered bones in a knee—I thanked him; I felt an urge to get out into the cold air.

[Illustration: Baikal Station, the summer resort of Irkutsk.]

This was my first foretaste of war. If there were any means, I thought, of making comprehensible in a single spot, in a single image or sound, all the misery, all the pain suffered in just one day across the hundreds upon hundreds of hospitals of Russia and Japan, how ghastly it would have to be! To those good, brave lads up there in the ward, with their honest, simple peasant faces, it would surely be a matter of utter indifference whether Russia has an ice-free port on the Pacific Ocean or not!

{p. 42}

Back at the hotel, I heard the cheerful clinking of glasses. Officers departing for the front were celebrating a merry farewell dinner with pretty Orpheum girls.

I have my seven-day provisions for the journey to Harbin in my rucksack; now we can cross the Angara again and head further east!

[Illustration]

{p. [51]}

## Transbaikal.

More than two weeks on the road, and the journey presses relentlessly onward. I have grown as accustomed to it as the miller to the rumble of his mill, and at night I wake up whenever we come to a halt. Then, from time to time, I also hear the impatient stamping of horses from the railcars on the siding; still transports of train troops, whom we pass. On the whole we make rather slow progress; fifteen to twenty versts an hour, but one must be content even with that.

When we boarded in Irkutsk, our carriage offered the most unbelievable spectacle of filth; fortunately, a colonel-auditor later raised quite a stir, summoned the station master, gendarmes, and whatever other officials there were, after which it was even swept out, and the supposedly broken hot-air heating recovered as well. The lighting has likewise grown worse; yet travel is still quite comfortable. Below, the wheels hum and drone, the carriage sways in an ever-monotonous rhythm; a candle illuminates the worn compartment, from whose walls the wires of a former electric system hang down mournfully. Outside, however, light glimmers and floods in magnificent effects. In bright sunshine we greeted Lake Baikal, and late into the night we were still rolling along its shores. Now there were massive slabs of ice piled atop one another, now broad expanses swept bare by the gale, in which bright moonlight was reflected in a single tone yet with a thousand different shadows—a wondrous silver carpet spread out at the feet of mighty Alps! I regretted not having been able to admire this magnificent panorama in its entirety. How wonderful it would have been to cross the lake with one of the two icebreakers, {p. 44} the *Baikal* or *Angara*, or by sledge! Until recently, the Circum-Baikal Railway was reserved solely for military trains: passenger trains stopped at Baikal Station, from where their passengers made the crossing over the lake in two and four hours, respectively. At Tankhoi, on the eastern shore, another train awaited them. The icebreakers also take on freight wagons. These are pushed into the interior of the giant vessels from high ramps. If one crosses the lake by sledge, an inn set up halfway across warms and refreshes the traveler. The temperature at Lake Baikal is usually very low and sometimes drops well below −40° R; nevertheless, the southern part of the deep lake freezes over only during prolonged, very severe cold. From the train I could clearly see a dark ribbon of open water—the wake of an icebreaker’s latest passage—even though we had twenty-five degrees of frost. For weeks now, passenger trains have been departing once daily direct from Irkutsk to Manchuria. The line of the Circum-Baikal Railway strikes me as a masterpiece of engineering. Cut entirely into the rock, it hugs the lake closely, bridges several small rivers, and passes through forty-eight tunnels, though admittedly short ones. All this was constructed in a relatively

[Illustration: Baikal icebreaker.] {p. 45} short time, for the most part during the war itself, which somewhat excuses the primitive appointment of the stations.

Late in the evening we reached Tankhoi, but for a long time I was still able to follow the ever-changing picture of the glittering ice sheet and its picturesque shores, until at last the moon disappeared behind the dark forests of the mountains. All around, everything seems dead—frozen stiff; only our train puffs and rattles through the silent solitude. From time to time a campfire flickers, in whose glow stands a soldier. His tall black papakha pulled down over his ears, rifle at the order, he lets the train pass. When his relief arrives, he crawls back into the dugout, the entrance of which peeks out behind through the snow.

[Illustration: Baikal landing place.]

The following evening we crossed the Selenga; splendid moonlight once again; the high bridge superstructure cast its long shadows across the sheet of ice, over which the wind sent fine snow trickling like slender rivulets. From there the journey proceeds very slowly; barely ten versts an hour. We reach a wide plateau, which forms a watershed between the Pacific Ocean and the Arctic Ocean. The nights are becoming bitterly cold; in Zavod it was thirty-five degrees below {p. 46} [Illustration: Circum-Baikal Railway.]

zero. From there the country becomes monotonous again: the broad valley plain of a tributary of the Shilka, heavily ravaged forest to the right and left; alongside the railway, a road that seems heavily traveled, the old Siberian trakt. The further east we go, the less snow; last night we passed Chita, and from then on there is scarcely any white to be seen on the mountain slopes.

We know virtually nothing about the war. There are no newspapers, and at the stations one learns little. Yesterday in Chita there were again a few telegrams: Vladivostok must be evacuated by the civilian population, only artisans are permitted to remain there. It was further reported that in Hungary the military is said to have fired on the workers!

How modest the people here are with regard to “news”! At the stations, soldiers sometimes come up to us and ask for finished newspapers. Recently one caught hold of a newspaper page from Tomsk—long out of date—which was then read aloud in front of the carriage. From the {p. 47} window, one saw nothing but a circle of black Cossack caps pressed tightly around the man with the newspaper.

This morning there was finally a splendid subject for a snapshot. About ten Cossacks were standing around a band of honghuzi in front of Karymskaya station. Real, genuine honghuzi, life-sized, one of them even bound! By the time I came with the camera, they had already boarded a third-class carriage along with their escorts. During the journey we paid the band a visit; to my regret it turned out that they were merely Chinese being escorted to the next guard post on account of some brawl or other. Repulsively ugly, but powerfully built figures, almost as disgusting as the numerous Buryats who are now seen in ever greater numbers at the stations.

Tomorrow morning we arrive at Manchuria station. From there we travel on the Chinese railway; hopefully it will be better than the one I am on now. The valley has meanwhile grown narrower, bare mountains on both sides with steep slopes and gullies. We are approaching the Khingan. Hopefully from tomorrow on there will also be more of military interest to see.

[Illustration: Customs inspection at Manchuria station.]

{p. [56]}

## Manchuria Station.

[Illustration: Manchuria station.]

A station scene, colourful and chaotic, such as I have hardly ever seen. The buffet hall wooden and spacious, poorly lit, crammed to the very last crevice with people and trunks. Foul-smelling Chinese, perfumed ladies, gendarmes, porters, generals, all in a wild jumble. Where to put my trunks? Who will watch to ensure they are not stolen from me? When does the train to Harbin leave? etc., questions to which no one can give me an answer. To make matters even worse, two Cossack chieftains are brawling because the staff refuse to give them any more vodka. Left alone, a person would be lost in {p. 49} this turmoil. Unpleasant as the Russian official may be, whether stationmaster or postmaster, the Russians are just as charming as travelling companions. They apologise for all the indignities one has to endure from the authorities, and endeavour through their amiability to make amends for the poor impression made on us by railway and postal officials. With the help of a Red Cross doctor and an officer, I finally manage to get my belongings into safety; as to everything else, complete uncertainty reigns. We are already in the staging zone of the active army; there, for a private individual, all reckoning comes to an end!

Characteristic of the Asian stations was that nobody knew anything. The considerable delays of the trains render any timetable illusory; one arrives—and waits until things move on. So for the time being one has leisure here to look around at one's surroundings. The female element consists mostly of families of Russian officers travelling from Vladivostok or Harbin to Russia. Beside me a darling little girl with a Chinese bonnet on her blond head; she is crying because the dark Cossack chieftain wanted to take her into his arms. Heated argument between chieftain and mama. How little homogeneous the Russian officer corps is! Elegant, intelligent physiognomies, then again people who with us would never be made non-commissioned officers. In this wooden shack, the magnificent Chinese vases that flaunt themselves as centrepieces alongside artificial palm trees have a comical effect. Waiters with greasy hands and white ties. A table, festively set, is being reserved. Fresh guests pour in again. Those can only be Germans! Tall, strapping figures, monocle clamped in eye, impeccably white caps of Prussian cut with the Red Cross upon them. The two chieftains are requested by their comrades to make themselves scarce for a while, because the "Germanic" Red Cross has just arrived. I was never an admirer of Prussia, but in these surroundings the personnel of the German hospital train, soon gathered in full strength at the reserved table, seemed to me like a greeting from the civilized world. These are men, these are women who, wherever they go, cannot fail to command respect. {p. 50} After a modest supper, their chief issues orders. The German hospital train will proceed tomorrow at 8:40—and it departed not a second later! We? The stationmaster thinks tomorrow at noon, the station commandant says tomorrow evening, etc. Each something different. Fortunately, we are allowed to spend the night in a railway carriage. But first we want to have a drink. We are speaking German, and a thoroughly tipsy Livonian sits down with us. He is a railway engineer and tells some choice stories. A stationmaster was recently crushed by a locomotive. Out of boredom he had been drinking vodka. Then, so as not to miss the train, he had lain down to sleep with his ear to the rails. The vodka, however, was stronger than his guardian angel, and the locomotive ran over him! To this opportune acquaintance I owe magnificent photographs that our new friend placed at my disposal the next day: scenes of wartime life. Will they reach their destination unconfiscated, I wonder?

There was, after all, one small reminder of home. At a merchant's shop where I bought provisions for the onward journey, I found a whole spool of red-white-

[Illustration: Russian and Chinese station in Manchuria.] {p. 51} green ribbon. Unfortunately he was a Buryat, and so the origin of our tricolour remains unknown to me. Our train did not leave at 2 o'clock as was initially stated; it is now supposed to leave at 10 o'clock in the evening. Why there are no longer any second-class carriages here, I do not understand. We shall be travelling with a transport of reserve horses. Four days on the hard benches of third class will have little appeal. On the other hand, the trip costs nothing! A peculiar zone tariff.

[Illustration: Troops reading the newspaper.]

{p. [60]}

## Harbin.

[Illustration: Doctors and Red Cross nurses.]

For four days we rolled from Manchuria to Harbin, and in third class at that. Yet it could have been worse, for nowadays one usually travels by *teplushka*, that is, in a heated freight car. The journey will always remain a fond travel memory for me. The car was spacious, well heated, and we soon made ourselves at home in it. Our commander, a reserve officer from Tomsk, refreshed us generously with canned provisions and *shimosa*—a military euphemism {p. 53} for vodka. From Hailar onward, a pretty, cheerful Polish woman took over the duties of housewife; everyone was happy and in good spirits, and so time passed more quickly than we would have liked. The journey across the Greater Khingan offered some scenic charm, and this stretch with its loops, viaducts, and tunnels is certainly quite interesting from an engineering standpoint; otherwise, the landscape seems monotonous and tedious. Bare brown hill ranges, now and then mountains with deep gorges and watercourses, from Tsitsihar onward flat plain, everything lifeless and desolate. Mighty are the Asian rivers, of which we crossed the Nonni and the Sungari here at the end. Before Harbin, for the first time in a long while, I saw well-tilled fields again.

En route one had the opportunity to visit hospitals and barracks. More frequently now one sees the Red Cross flag flying; almost every station accommodates wounded and sick. The military guarding of the railway line is already far stricter here. Next to each station stands the frontier guard barracks, a solid, airy timber structure, usually for fifty men and a few mounted soldiers. Beside the barracks is the alarm signal, a tall pole wrapped with pitch wreaths; on the nearest hill, an observation tower. The Hunhuz raid that I secretly wished for as a travel adventure unfortunately failed to materialize; in general, matters are said not to have been nearly so bad with these frequently recounted Hunhuz attacks on railway trains. For the most part, the fighting in the railcars played out between Hunhuzes and their mortal enemies, the Chinese; nowhere was I able to learn of any attacks by these bands on Russian troops. Nevertheless, the military precautions, especially the outfitting of all bridges for artillery defense, pointed to the possibility of such attacks.

When we crossed the high structure of the Sungari Bridge, Harbin lay before us in bright sunshine; first the harbor with numerous ships still trapped in the ice, behind it a vast sea of houses. We had to wait three hours before our train could enter the station, which was packed full with transport trains, locomotives, and railcars.

The station building surprises by its size, cleanliness, {p. 54} and above all by its modern Secessionist style. The station lies between the harbor and the New Town, to which a broad, straight avenue leads. The favorable impression upon arrival, unfortunately, soon passes. In the vicinity of the station is an ice rink decorated with colorful flags, then a very tasteful building that, intended as a hotel, currently serves as an officers' hospital. Farther along are the Russo-Chinese Bank, a row of wooden barracks, and finally a dainty, wooden Russian church in front of which stand two small muzzle-loading cannons of Chinese origin; those would be all the sights along the roughly two-kilometer route.

Of the many streets, only a few are built up; one of the latter bears the proud name “Sungari Prospekt.” Otherwise, this part of the city is nothing but an endless, hilly waste upon which residential houses, administrative offices, hospitals, and earthen huts are scattered far apart without any order; mostly unfinished, crude, tasteless brick buildings still awaiting their coat of mortar. The imposing, again Secessionist building of the Chinese Railway Administration makes no impression in these repulsive surroundings.

Down in the harbor quarter it is merrier, livelier, even if not exactly more beautiful. Of the 44,000 inhabitants that Harbin counts—not including the garrison—the greater number falls to this part of the city, which has a mixed European-Chinese character. Here one finds the commercial offices of German and Russian firms alongside the stalls of the natives, restaurants, cookshops, a Chinese theater, the Russian opera—today they are performing *La Traviata*—and even a circus. Old Harbin, the purely Chinese quarter, is filthy and of little interest. Before the war, the place maintained a significant trade with foreign countries. Pelts from Northern Manchuria (Hailar) went from here by boat to Germany; likewise, bean cakes, wheat, and pig bristles formed significant export commodities.

The people here are not entirely displeased with the handover of Port Arthur into Japanese hands; it is hoped that Dalny will in future become a commercial center similar to Shanghai and that the hinterland will benefit from this. Perhaps one will {p. [63]} [Illustration: Harbin station.]

{p. [64]}

{p. 57}

in Harbin then also learn more about the outside world than hitherto. Now we are already so very close to the war—yet it is simply impossible to gain even an approximate picture of the events of the last few days from all the confused talk. Even the outbreak of the war, so I am told, became known in Harbin through private reports four days after the first bombardment of Port Arthur—and officially only after another four days! The postal and telegraph services leave much to be desired in the great empire of the Tsar! The foreigner at least is left defenseless against the arbitrariness of the officials in question. More than once it happened to me that they refused to accept a letter, the Herr *nachalnik* simply declaring that he was under no obligation to know where Hungary was! Here in Harbin, the main staging station of the war's rear, a tiny little post office is operating; hitherto it has been so crammed full every day that I have not yet managed to reach the counter. Some regulation of the inn-

[Illustration: Bridge guardhouse on the Sungari.] {p. 58} keeping trade would also have been very necessary. For a wretched room in a wooden shack they charge six to ten rubles a day. All the inns here are bad, but I believe I must especially warn travellers coming to Harbin against the "Hotel Ariante."

The description of my journey to the Russian headquarters would be incomplete were I not to mention the final leg from Harbin to Mukden. Immediately after my arrival, I had registered with the station commandant for the mail train and, three days later, stood on the platform at the appointed hour. I was already rejoicing at the small crowd when I learned that, once again as an exception, the train would depart four hours later. Hitherto there had been so little difference between the rule and the exception, and besides, I am already so well practiced at waiting, that I would not mention these few hours at all had I not whiled them away in pleasant company. A gentleman who knows Budapest and is travelling to his wife, who lives there. To speak of home is a pleasure one only comes to know in a foreign land.

Courteous, like all the Russian officers I had met so far, the station commandant had intended a seat in second class for me and called my name first during the assignment; unfortunately, at that time I was still standing before the closed ticket window, which the cashier opened only after prolonged knocking and shouting from those waiting. Meanwhile, all the seats had been distributed; only a few in third class were still free, and these were just then taken by storm. Anyone left behind here must travel in a *teplushka*. The men shout, the women plead, everyone shoves, squeezes, and pushes toward the narrow carriage door, in which, to make matters worse, a suitcase is wedged. Modesty achieves nothing here! After a brief struggle, I am inside the carriage. No sooner have they forced their way in than people climb like monkeys toward the ceiling; the berths are arranged in three tiers, one above the other, and whoever is at the very top can at least no longer be trampled on. I managed to secure a spot on the *bel étage*, where I immediately stretched out full length as a sign of taking possession. My new acquaintance pushes my suitcases in after me—a handshake: {p. 59} “Give my regards to Andrássy Street!” And we were already rolling.

It was quite cozy. Beside me stood soldiers. I could only see their heads; they were eating something that smelled of garlic. Spurred boots dangled down from the upper tier. In one corner, singing soon began, and the chorus joined in. Where people sing, there settle down in peace, for evil men have no songs, I thought to myself, placed a few handkerchiefs over my mouth and nose as an air filter, and tried to fall asleep.
