The Cossacks were from the Don. They sang a song that resembled the “Körösi kis leány” down to the very last note.

[Illustration: Large military ramp at Harbin station.]

At first the familiar melody delighted me: I tried to make out the lyrics from the medley of voices, but they were completely different from the Hungarian.

With sleep, however, things would no longer go quite right this time. I was excited. After all, we were now heading straight for Mukden. I thought back to that hour when I departed from Vienna. How confident I had been then, how lightly I had estimated all those obstacles that might be thrown in my path along the way. What would I have done if in Petersburg they had flatly refused my request and not granted permission to proceed to the headquarters of the Russian Manchurian Army? I believe I would have gone *all the same*; at least I would have tried it, after all. Now everything was happily {p. 60} behind me. The plank on which I lay was hard, right next to me were the door and the cold window—smoke, fumes, and exhalations to the point of suffocation—but beneath me the wheels hummed and rolled once again, straight toward Mukden. What will it be like there? Will I find our two military attachés? Is the army still near Mukden, or is it perhaps already in victorious advance upon Port Arthur?

My confidence in the Russian army had been renewed. Everything I had seen along the way of soldiers, officers, hospital trains, and hospitals had satisfied me to a high degree. That now and then an officer is drunk, or a soldier has begged from someone under cover of dusk, by no means permits an unfavorable conclusion about the whole. The men were excellently clothed and looked well-nourished. The lads around me, too, who still undauntedly sang “Körösi kis leány,” were splendid fellows, with dashing yet good-natured countenances—only they shouldn’t have sung. The dim candlelight above the carriage door had burned down, the hoarse voices died away in the steady hum and rumble of the rolling wheels, and then all became dark and quiet.

From the other corner of the carriage, a small red lamp glowed through the smoke-filled air. It burned beneath an icon of the Virgin Mary, which one of the Cossacks had nailed up there.

Over the final stretch before Mukden we had advanced quite slowly. At every stop we halted for several hours. Pushing past us back toward the north, however, came train after train, heavily laden with wounded. During the night, the groans and whimpers of those crammed together here in tight quarters could be heard. The battle on the western flank had begun yesterday; they did not seem to have been prepared for such heavy losses. And these here were only the less seriously wounded; those unfit for transport filled the hospitals of Mukden.

The outcome of the struggle was still undecided. I still {p. 61} harbored the hope of being able to accompany the brave Russian soldiers on their triumphant march toward Port Arthur. How astonished I was that few of the Russians shared my confidence. On January 24—we were then halted just outside Mukden—a rumor had spread that Sandepu had already been taken. I still remember a *colonel* who, beaming with joy, burst into our compartment with the words: “On February 1, Kuropatkin will begin the advance on Liaoyang with the whole army!”—“Why, how do you know that?”—“A wounded man just told me!”—Yet such childlike optimism could, only a few hours later, give way to a timidity as yet unjustified; people were already speaking of a rift that was said to have erupted between the commander-in-chief and the commander of the Second Army, General Grippenberg.

More and more wounded, and soon I heard from the southwest the dull rumble of the guns—slow, solemn, it echoed over from the flat Hun River plain, where the Grim Reaper had been reaping his harvest for two days.

Before I speak of the events at Sandepu, I consider it necessary to mention General Mishchenko’s raid. While it is merely an episode that had little influence on the course of the major events, it contains from a military-psychological standpoint such characteristic features that, after studying it, we can more easily explain many later events of this war.

[Illustration: Bridge guard on the Nonni.]

{p. [70]}

{p. [71]}

# General Mishchenko's Raid and the Battle of Sandepu.

{p. [72]}

{p. [73]}

## The Raid on Yingkou.

### Purpose and Plan.

The word "raid" recalls the dashing cavalry general Stuart, and when we hear of a raid by General Mishchenko, we suppose that he must have carried it out just as dashily, or perhaps even more so, than the brave American cavalry leader. For this time it was, after all, a Cossack raid, the first major undertaking of that Russian cavalry—now finally unleashed and so greatly feared in Europe—for whose sake so many permanent cavalry barracks were once built in the beautiful land of Galicia, which today are an even more permanent exile for young cavalry officers.

For the understanding of the non-military reader, let it be explained that a "raid" means an incursion planned on a larger scale by a relatively strong body of cavalry.

Its mission is to inflict damage on the enemy's rear and flank, and possibly to divert his attention from those operations planned against the opponent's front.

The principal element is that of surprise, so that the enemy has no opportunity to take countermeasures; furthermore, the raid must harmonize *in timing* with the main operations directed against the enemy. Thus, once again, the correct choice, or rather seizing, of the *moment* plays a prominent role.

The raid against Yingkou can only be regarded as a partial operation of that great offensive movement upon which the Russians were finally forced to decide after the fall of Port Arthur.

The Japanese had not delayed, immediately after the fall of Port Arthur, in beginning the dispatch of the forces now released here toward the north to their operational army. {p. 66} The task of General-Adjutant Mishchenko could now be to bypass the enemy's left flank, appear by surprise in his rear, and, by destroying the railway line, delay the further transport of the Port Arthur force group, divert the enemy's attention from the front through such a diversion in his rear, and thereby create more favorable conditions of attack for his own army.

Certainly each of these two tasks, which could also be accomplished simultaneously, was worthy of a total effort. Seldom will a cavalry commander find himself in a position to employ his forces so decisively on the further course of the main operations as on this occasion, and such an opportunity, which promised to bring the Russian cavalry its long-awaited laurels, was well suited to awaken full energy and drive—if such were at all to be awakened in him.

[Illustration: Detraining of wounded.]

{p. 67}

The right moment for such a diversion had probably already been somewhat missed again, as the Japanese by this time had already dispatched a good part of their siege army toward the north; nevertheless, the enemy's entire situation—above all his long, vulnerable line of communication to the sea—could still be considered promising.

The account of events that now follows is based on the precise data that I owe to the kindness of the Norwegian General Staff Captain Nyquist, who rode along on the raid at General Mishchenko's headquarters. *Not so the critique of it, for which I alone am responsible here.* Anyone who could not form such a critique for himself needed only to listen to the voices of several senior General Staff officers in Mukden, who discussed just as vehemently the planning of this raid as well as various purely private considerations which, as was so often the case in this war, exerted an ill influence on the execution of the operations.

### Forces.

The *forces subordinated* to Adjutant General Mishchenko consisted of:

Transbaikal-Ural Cossack Division under the personal command of the Adjutant General; deputy, General Abramov: 4 regiments.

Tomsk Cossack Division under General Teleshov: 3 regiments.

Dragoon Brigade, assembled from the 10th and 17th Army Corps ¹), under General Samsonov: 3 regiments. ²)

Caucasian Cossack Brigade under Colonel Prince Obeliani: 2 regiments.

A mounted sapper detachment (2 officers, 30 troopers).

::: notes
¹) *Army Corps* designates the European corps, as distinguished from the Siberian corps.

²) These were the only three dragoon regiments in the theatre of war; all other cavalry consisted of 1st and 2nd line Cossacks.
:::

{p. 68}

Three horse artillery batteries (7·6 *cm*) of six guns each: 3 batteries.

A half-battery (8·4 *cm*) of four guns (old pattern): 1/2 battery.

Four scout detachments (okhotniki) of approximately 150 men each from the 1st Siberian Corps: 4 companies.

A Red Cross medical detachment with 10 Finnish ambulance wagons; in addition, four pack horses per regiment for transporting the wounded and Chinese arbas ¹) acquired along the way for this purpose.

A supply column, consisting of approximately 1,500 mules.

Total: 12 cavalry regiments ²), 3 1/2 batteries, 4 scout detachments, one medical column, and—1,500 *mules for hauling supplies*.

The ammunition column did not march with the troop column, but had remained to the rear with General Kosakovsky's detachment, which consisted of cavalry, infantry, and frontier guard troops, and was intended to serve as a sort of support for General Mishchenko's force.

If we examine the composition of the force designated for the raid, its considerable cavalry strength is particularly striking. Twelve cavalry regiments correspond to three of our cavalry divisions. While such a substantial number of cavalry regiments ought, organisationally, to be provided with a correspondingly strong artillery component—two horse batteries per division can be considered a minimum—here we find only three in total. The addition of a half-battery of heavy 8 1/2 *cm* guns was intended to facilitate the destruction of various objects—it was thus meant to find less a tactical than a technical application.

The allocation of infantry appears sufficient, considering that the Russian dragoon regiments in particular {p. 69} can be employed advantageously as dismounted infantry.

::: notes
¹) Heavy two-wheeled cart with a sturdy wooden axle.

²) Each consisted of six squadrons; only the Verkhneudinsk [Regiment] of five, its sixth having remained in Port Arthur at the time.
:::

*What seems entirely incredible, however, is the taking along of fifteen hundred pack animals to haul provisions, while the ammunition column remained two days' march behind!*

These organisational disparities could hardly have been accidental, for even during the war there would still have been time to provide the cavalry division with adequate artillery, infantry, and administrative units. On this as on many other occasions, however, one will not go astray in assuming that the well-organised Cossack divisions existing already in peacetime, *of which we heard so much before the war*, were in fact mere fables mechanically repeated in the reports arriving annually from Petersburg.

Apart from the existing defective organisation of the Russian cavalry formations, even this ad hoc assembly of the raiding force was an unfavourable omen for carrying out this cavalry expedition.

Whoever burdens himself with fifteen hundred supply mules seems to have little desire to cover daily distances of a hundred kilometres, as Stuart or Hadik demonstrated.

Whoever leaves his ammunition column two days behind hardly intends to engage in a stubborn fight with the enemy.

Whoever wants to destroy a railway in such a way that an army's supply is disrupted by it takes along more than two sapper officers and thirty sappers!

Whatever obstacles might later stand in the way of carrying out this raid and frustrate its success—already *its very conception shows that from the outset it lacked that energy and will for swift action* which, above all, a cavalry commander must possess.

The *primary operational objective* was Yingkou; the *destruction* of the magazines and railway facilities there.

{p. 70}

The secondary operational objective was the railway bridges of Haicheng and Gaizhou (south of the former).

The initial situation on 8 January, the ordered column formation, and the day-by-day advance are illustrated by the sketch on page 73.

### General Concept for the Execution of the Advance.

*Terrain.* It is flat between Xifangtai and Yingkou.

Since the general direction of the roads located here runs west to east, whereas the detachment's direction of advance was north to south, almost the entire march proceeded cross-country over the deeply furrowed, ploughed gaoliang fields, which certainly had an adverse effect on the troops' performance.

The Liaohe, the Hunhe, and their tributaries were still frozen solid; in the vicinity of Yingkou, however, the crust of ice was already so weak that the crossing of the batteries required time-consuming preparations.

The country's wealth of resources is great. *I have never seen a country where every crumb of soil was more intensively utilized for agriculture than in Manchuria.*

The detachment moved through an area hitherto little touched by the war, which makes the provisioning precautions described above appear all the more superfluous.

*Direction of march.* In order to preserve the element of surprise, the Japanese left wing had to be widely outflanked,

[Illustration: Finnish ambulance wagons.] {p. 71} which necessitated crossing the Liaohe twice. To keep the movement concealed, it would have been more advantageous to carry it out entirely west of the Liaohe, but this was not possible, as the southern course of the river—which is already very wide and whose alluvial land is marshy—would have called regaining the left bank into question; thus, after turning the Japanese left wing, the march returned to the shortest route.

*Column formation.* In order to cross the river more quickly, the march was begun in four columns and then continued in three, which actually formed merely a marching checkerboard across the deployment frontage. The heads of the columns were about two versts apart, so that adjacent columns could see each other.

The center column, which General Mishchenko led, had the direction.

*Reconnaissance.* Just as I later had opportunity to observe for myself, here too the *sending out of far-ranging intelligence patrols* was omitted. At daybreak (between 6 and 7 a.m.), however, several officer patrols did go ahead, which as a rule were recalled towards evening.

*Security.* During the march, the center column provides security to the front, the flank columns to the flanks.

During the overnight bivouac, the security troops form a ring around the camp.

The strength of the security troops was one regiment per column; their relief was left to the column commanders.

*Overnight stay.* Bivouac in the intervals between the marching columns.

### The Advance.

*9 January.*

Formation of columns according to the sketch.

*The left column*, consisting of the Tomsk Cossack Division, the Caucasian Cossack Brigade, and a horse artillery battery, was the strongest. To it fell the task of reconnoitring towards the {p. 72} Yingkou–Liaoyang railway line and destroying it if possible. That this railway destruction could not have been meant very seriously is evident from the fact that the Cossack regiments are neither trained nor equipped for such work,¹) while the demolition detachment was attached to the centre column.

Nothing was to be seen of the Japanese on this day. All the more frequently, however, the vanguards encountered detachments of Chunchuses, who opened fire on the approaching column from the villages.

Each time, the Cossacks attempted to attack the enemy entrenched at the edge of the village with the *lava*—that is, a dispersed tactical formation on horseback. Here, *the powerlessness of the mounted soldier against the prepared rifleman showed itself every time*—all the more so here, as the small horses were not fast enough to cover the exposed ground at a rapid pace. Each time the *lava* attack was repulsed, whereupon the Cossacks dismounted and drove off the bandit gangs with a few shots.

In order not to become entangled in time-consuming village fighting, the settlements were always bypassed.

Bivouac near Dawan.

*10 January.*

The columns set out at 7 o'clock in the morning. This late departure time is somewhat explainable by the late daybreak, the extreme cold (20–25° R.), and unfamiliarity with the terrain to be traversed cross-country.

Around 11 o'clock in the morning, the centre column ran into a Japanese half-company that had entrenched itself in a rifle trench west of Qilijie.

Two to three sotnias of the centre column dismounted for a firefight, which the Japanese half-company withstood until after- {p. [81]} [Illustration: Sketch of the raid against Yingkou.]

::: notes
¹) The Cossack regiments have no pioneer platoons and also possess no telegraphic equipment. In the given case, they were provided with explosive charges and railway demolition tools by sapper battalions.
:::

{p. 74}

noon. It then withdrew to the left bank of the Hunhe, where it was received by a half-battalion.

During this opening engagement, the two flank columns had not intervened, although they would have been in a position to cut off the Japanese half-company by a simple wheeling movement.

*Cooperation* between individual groups in combat, particularly the kind arising from the *independent initiative* of one commander or another, is something we almost never find among the Russians.

On the one hand, they considered each other so strong that they believed they could dispense with assistance—the *perpetual reliance on numerical strength!*—on the other hand, they find nothing improper in the *worthless squandering of time*, even when it is as precious as during the execution of a raid. From this arises the lack of energy on the part of the flank column commanders, which failed to awaken even when they saw the centre column, *more than an entire division strong*, held up by *a single Japanese half-company*!

One must understand the nature of the Russians, their soul and train of thought, to be able to explain how it came to pass that these so *splendid* men could achieve so incredibly few successes. A half-company halts the march of a cavalry corps!

The fight against the half-battalion on the left bank of the Hunhe again lasted until evening, at which time the Japanese withdrew towards the railway.

The columns went into camp at 8 o'clock in the evening.

During the engagement, reconnaissance towards Haicheng had been continued, and the patrols reported that the railway bridge there was occupied by infantry and artillery.

11 January.

During the advance, a report reached General Mishchenko that the Japanese were fortifying Niuzhuang.

The Japanese offered their first resistance west of the Taizihe. The reports as well as the private accounts of {p. 75} the engagement that developed here are contradictory. According to one account, Niuzhuang was said to have been occupied only by a Japanese telegraph detachment; according to another, the struggle for the village is said to have lasted until towards evening, with the fire of the batteries attached to the centre and left columns reportedly concentrated on the place.

*12 January.*

After covering half the distance towards Yingkou, a long rest was taken, during which the

### Dispositions for the Attack

were issued.

*Attack Group.* One squadron from each of the twelve cavalry regiments = twelve squadrons dismounted for the firefight. Three dragoon squadrons to cover the horse lines. The four scout detachments, the mining detachment, and all the artillery. For this attack group *no unified commander had been appointed; instead,* each of the sub-units had received its orders directly from the detachment command.

*Railway Demolition Group.* A pioneer detachment, assembled from the squadron pioneers of the twelve regiments. It was to destroy the railway between Yingkou and Dashiqiao.

*Covering Group.* General Mishchenko, with the main body of his entire cavalry, was to cover the attack of the attack group advancing against the Yingkou railway station against possible Japanese counterattacks from Dashiqiao. Consequently, the

### Grouping for the Attack.

*12 January, 5 p.m.*

This attack grouping is certainly quite a peculiar one.

Of the available cavalry, one *twelfth* is designated to carry out the attack proper, but *eleven twelfths* {p. 76} to cover it—that is, for a secondary task. This measure does not appear to have been unjustified, since Yingkou was occupied by only weak forces, while stronger forces were to be expected from Dashiqiao.

Unjustified, on the other hand, is the haphazard throwing together of the attack group from twelve squadrons, each from a different regiment! In vain would one search the textbooks of tactics or army organization to find even the slightest justification for such an arbitrary tearing apart of units.

[Illustration: Transbaikal rifleman.]

It was exclusively *personal*, private motives that guided the commander in such arrangements: trusting in numerical strength, he was certain of his success at Yingkou. Yet the individual regimental commanders were also jealous of the laurels yet to be won—each wanted to have taken part in this victorious action. The relationship of the Russian superior to his subordinates is, while preserving discipline in every respect, an extremely collegial one. Mishchenko was one of those superiors whom his subordinates loved without exception and for whom the men were enthusiastic. By our standards, his good-naturedness and consideration for personal interests often went too far. So too here. In order that he might pin the St. George upon the breast of each of his regimental commanders, every one of the twelve regiments was to have its share in the glorious capture of Yingkou!

Similar motives may have accounted for the failure to place the attack group under a unified commander, the immediate consequence of which was that the mining detachment moved into the firing line alongside the squadrons, both of its officers were shot dead, and the mining detachment performed the demolition work assigned to it later only deficiently.

{p. 77}

To open the engagement, the artillery opened fire at 6 p.m.—again, therefore, too late! The fire, already begun at dusk, could be sustained for half an hour at most; then it was night, and the movements of their own troops could no longer be seen from the artillery position.

As can be seen from the text sketch below, the infantry attack could only begin after their own artillery fire had ceased.

How severely its effect was already impaired by the darkness is shown by the circumstance that, shortly after the start of the action, a Japanese officer departed Yingkou station on a locomotive heading eastward, without the Russian artillery succeeding in hitting the locomotive brought under fire.

[Illustration: Grouping for the attack.]

Just as unhindered at the same time, a train arriving at Yingkou from Dashiqiao was able to bring up approximately a battalion of infantry on open flatcars and detrain them right there.

The Japanese did not succeed, however, in bringing further troop transports from Dashiqiao to Yingkou, as the pioneer detachment assembled from the squadron pioneers had in the meantime destroyed the railway line in question with pyroxylin demolition charges.

It is characteristic of the Russian intelligence and reconnaissance service that at the beginning of the engagement the strength of the enemy {p. 78} in Yingkou was not even approximately known. Weak Japanese forces had ensconced themselves in the small houses lying north of the railway station, from where they offered stubborn resistance to the twelve Russian squadrons slowly advancing.

Toward evening these Japanese detachments withdrew to the station, where the fighting continued until around 10 or 11 o'clock at night, when the Russians took possession of it. The demolition work that was now to be carried out was begun, without, however, any particular damage being inflicted on the Japanese.

There was no plan of the station and its installations on the basis of which the work to be carried out could have been distributed and organized. The two sapper officers had, as mentioned, fallen during the assault, and so the detachments ran about aimlessly in the dark between the buildings, setting fire to a storehouse here or a shed there; to attempt demolitions in this disorder would have posed the greatest danger to their own units.

On the Russian side, no one attempted to penetrate into the town of Yingkou itself—with the exception of the American correspondent Little! He had accompanied the raid and followed the troops to the station. Here, the journalist could not resist the proximity of the telegraph office in the town—during the night he succeeded in making his way into the town, dispatching his telegrams, and in the following days catching up with the Russian detachment on its retreat!

[Illustration: Ammunition wagons.]

{p. 79}

Is it not astonishing that the defenseless journalist penetrates into the town for the sake of his dispatches, while the twelve squadrons must content themselves with insignificant destruction at the railway station—must, for at 11 o’clock at night the order from General Mishchenko to break off the engagement and begin the retreat reached the attacking group. In this campaign things occurred that are so different from what we have learned, and especially from what we assumed of the Russian cavalry, that one had to have been on the spot to be able to believe such a thing!

In the group commanded personally by General Mishchenko, reports had arrived toward afternoon that a Japanese battalion was advancing from Dashiqiao. General Mishchenko sent a regiment of the Caucasian Cavalry Brigade forward against it, which naturally could not withstand the regular infantry attack of the Japanese and was forced to retreat around 10 o’clock in the evening.

One regiment fights, the other eleven stand idle and wait—wait until that one is beaten—then they all retire together, because they do not want to risk suffering still greater losses. This is the typical employment of forces by the Russian generals in small matters as in great, in tactics as in strategy, which alone is to blame for the fact that the Russians, despite numerical superiority and *excellent soldier material*, have never—not once in this campaign—recorded any noteworthy success. Twelve squadrons halt where a lone journalist presses forward, and at 10 o’clock at night General Mishchenko orders his cavalry corps to retreat in the face of a single advancing Japanese infantry battalion!—And how does the general justify this measure? “A more important task awaited him in the general attack on Sandepu, for which he had to arrive in good time and for the sake of which he had to spare his forces!”

Had General Mishchenko not later proved himself personally to be a brave soldier at Sandepu, had he not, braving enemy bullets, been wounded there, one {p. 80} would have to ascribe his measures before Yingkou to a lack of personal courage. But that is not so. Russian generals have rarely lacked personal courage; the casualty lists, headed invariably by Russian colonels and generals, prove this sufficiently. What was always lacking, however, was an understanding of the value of time and of seizing the given moment.

The raid on Yingkou had already as good as succeeded, for there were no significant enemy forces present to prevent the destruction of the town and the threat to the lines of communication of the Japanese field army. The Russian cavalry could have gathered its first laurels here and indirectly secured an outstanding share in the outcome of the Battle of Sandepu. As it was, however, nothing was accomplished at Yingkou because they still wanted to go to Sandepu, and nothing was accomplished at Sandepu because the troops were already exhausted by the arduous march to and from Yingkou.

The Russian losses before Yingkou amounted to 300 wounded and a few dead, the latter being carried along by the retreating troops and buried in the course of the night.

The detachment spent the night of the 12th–13th several versts north of the campsite of the previous day.

### The Retreat.

The engagements of the preceding day had alerted the Japanese, and it was to be expected that they would attack the detachment from the east within a very short time. As General Mishchenko, for the reasons mentioned above, had no intention for the time being of committing to a more serious engagement, he was anxious to cross over to the right bank of the Liaohe as soon as possible—if possible, still south of the mouth of the Hunhe.

Owing to skirmishes dragging on late into the night, the detachment had only found rest in the early morning hours. Consequently, the *departure* on the 13th did not begin until around midday.

{p. 81}

The result of the prior river reconnaissance showed that the ice cover at the southernmost crossing point was already very weak, so that it seemed advisable to assign the artillery of the southern column to the center column.

The Liaohe was reached at 6 o'clock in the evening. The left bank is steep and high here; ramps had to be cut into the hard-frozen ground and the ice sheet itself artificially reinforced, labors which were significantly hampered by the falling darkness. The river crossing lasted six hours, so that the troops once again only came to rest late after midnight.

Nothing was to be seen of the enemy on this day—a further sign that at *Yingkou* one had faced only completely inferior forces.

*14 January.*

The Russians had remained in camp until 8 a.m. No reconnaissance troops had been left behind on the left bank; thus Japanese artillery was able to appear on the opposite bank of the river at the aforementioned hour and open fire on the withdrawing Russian columns. This time the Japanese shot very poorly; for the time being, their fire had no effect at all.

To cover the retreat, two Cossack regiments remained behind with the artillery, whose combined attack soon succeeded in silencing the Japanese batteries and compelling them, as well as the weak Japanese infantry, to retreat.

On the Russian side, thirty men had fallen on this day.

The further retreat of the Russians was not disturbed by the enemy. The transport of the wounded to the rear, in which the Finnish ambulances proved very practical, permitted only a limited marching speed.

With the army, there was already concern for the fate of the detachment, and General Kosakovsky was sent with two Caucasian regiments {p. 82} toward the south to effect a possible disengagement. Kosakovsky advanced to the vicinity of Dawan, where, upon receiving news that General Mishchenko was already approaching safe and sound, he turned back and rejoined the army. Up to the beginning and during the Battle of Sandepu, the Mishchenko group remained on the army's western wing.

[Illustration: Cossack camp.]

{p. [91]}

[Illustration: Cossack with Mongolian horse and Chinese saddle.]

{p. [92]}

{p. [93]}

## The Engagements at Sandepu.

*Late January.*

“Our corps, the Siberians as well as the Eighth and the Rifles, fought bravely; but in the village of Sandepu itself there was a distillery with arm-thick walls that we could not master!”

These words, from the mouth of a Russian general staff colonel, sound somewhat incomprehensible—yet in fact it was so, and had good reason too.

Since its unsuccessful October offensive, the Russian Manchurian Army had stood north of and along the Shahe.

The right wing was formed by the Second Army under Grippenberg, the center by the Third Army under Kaulbars, and the left wing, which was pushed forward into the mountains in a southward hook-like curve, by the First Army under Linevich.

Whether Grippenberg’s relationship with Kuropatkin was already strained before Sandepu, I do not know. I will only state that in the autumn prior, Grippenberg had been designated to carry out an entirely independent task with the army group subordinated to him, and this force, already largely assembled in Jilin, was called the Jilin Army.

An independent operation by this army against the Japanese right flank seems to have been planned and might perhaps also have had prospects of success. Kuropatkin—as they say, in order to preserve the unity of supreme command, while others believe out of jealousy toward Grippenberg—drew him and his corps close to himself even before the October fighting.

In the unfortunate endeavor never to seek success in exploiting the opportune moment, but always {p. 86} again solely in relying on numerical strength, the commander-in-chief had allowed the entire winter to slip by in order to await the troop transports arriving from Europe.

“We must be twice as strong as the Japanese!” a Russian general staff lieutenant colonel, who had just returned from the headquarters of the army commander-in-chief, told us.—Does there not lie in these words, which were certainly an echo from on high, an admission of their own weakness?

To be sure, the Manchurian winter is little favorable for operations. Cold and lack of water significantly limit operational objectives. Here the operational objective lay quite close: the Japanese positions on the opposite bank of the Shahe, which at some points were no further than a thousand paces from our own outposts. It is no fable, but a fact, that Russian regimental bands played in the zemlyanka camps and the Japanese from across the way showed their approval with shouts of “khorosho.”

The climatic conditions at the beginning of the month of January (the fall of Port Arthur) had been no less favorable than at the end of it—yet if considerations of cold and lack of water were decisive for the choice of timing for the attack, then one ought not to have attacked even in February.

Although the time for resuming the offensive had already been postponed far enough, the eventual opening of action seems nevertheless to have corresponded more to Grippenberg’s urging than to the intention of the commander-in-chief. The entire design and execution of the attack points to this.

As in all previous Russian offensive attempts, here again we find the typical recipe for attack:

A wing group is assigned a task—the capture of an attack objective; the entire remainder of the army stands ready during the wing group’s fight—and waits. *Time, as has been said, has no value*, so the main body of the army can wait until the wing group has accomplished its task. Now the enemy, though numerically weak, is far more active than the Russians. He uses the time that {p. 87} the inactivity of his adversary affords him for rapid lateral shifts; he shifts his forces, *skillfully arrayed in depth*, to the threatened wing and suddenly confronts the partial attacks of the Russians here with relative superiority.

He can even dare to significantly weaken his center or the other wing, knowing full well that the Russians invariably fail to employ their numerical superiority—for the Russians were almost always numerically superior to the Japanese—*united and simultaneously*, to achieve a common objective. For this they lacked the proper sense for exploiting the given moment—and especially training in command technique, in which the most meticulous *calculation of time* is, in turn, the fundamental prerequisite.

Thus, here too, the attack on Sandepu appears to us as a purely partial undertaking by General Grippenberg, who was guided by the certainly commendable idea of putting an end to the long operational lull through a successful offensive by the Second Army, and thereby at the same time tying the hoped-for turn in Russian fortunes of war to his own name.

Such an attack, even if somewhat belated in terms of timing, suited the geographical conditions: attacking the Japanese frontally, that is to say a strategic breakthrough, had no prospect of success; the *main attack* had to be directed against one of the two Japanese flanks. The eastern wing stood in the mountains, where a decision was more difficult to bring about and where the Russians, coming from their homeland, were not accustomed to fighting. Thus, the favorable direction of attack for the *opening engagement* of the great general offensive remained that against Sandepu.

To pin the enemy along his entire front through vigorous engagements, possibly through diversions against his flanks and rear, while at the same time attacking and taking the pivot of his left wing—Sandepu—with a relatively superior force—as Gripenberg's army indeed was at the start of this operation—and then: to {p. 88} *roll up the Japanese position line from the left wing toward the right!*

That was the idea—or at least it ought to have been the idea for the resumption of the offensive on the part of the Russians at the end of January.

In actual execution, however, we see here first of all a diversion timed and positioned ineffectively—carried out by General Mishchenko with a cavalry corps against Yingkou. After this caricature of a raid, which has already been discussed in detail, had ended without result, the Second Army's attack against Sandepu began, having been, as it were, announced in advance to the Japanese by the aforementioned cavalry excursion.

[Illustration: Chinese liquor distillery.]

The Second Army fights, bleeds—bleeds to death. The Third and First Armies stand by—waiting—and looking on!

Where is the commander-in-chief here, who should have led the whole unified force to the attack—and to victory? Where is the general staff technique of his army chief of staff, General Sakharov, already known to the world from his exquisite telegrams?

Poor, brave, gallant corps, who stood under fire there at Sandepu again {p. 89} after a murderous one-year campaign, and those of you who, newly arrived from Europe, had to receive your baptism of fire there! For you it was all the same whether it was dissension—or the incompetence of the commanders. Your commander, Adjutant General Gripenberg, could indeed—whether out of wounded pride or because he realized the futility of his efforts under the given circumstances—turn his back on you and go home; whether he acted rightly as a soldier in doing so remains another matter; but you had to pay for this botched game with your blood—uselessly and ingloriously, which is the bitterest thing of all for a soldier!...

And now to a concise account of the events and how it came to pass that the army could not master the liquor distillery.

To carry out the attack against the Japanese left wing, *General Gripenberg* had at his disposal a substantial force, more than sufficient for the assigned task: four corps and a cavalry corps, the latter reinforced by approximately an infantry division.

These forces had been grouped further to the west toward the Hunhe about a week before the start of the attack, so that the western wing groups, advancing straight ahead and pushing through victoriously, should have struck the enemy's line of communications. These troop movements, as well as all other preparations for the attack, appear not to have been noticed by the enemy, for as events showed, he had taken no countermeasures against them. The Chinese had been expelled; those caught spying—or sometimes merely suspected of it—were beheaded, and their wrinkled, yellow severed heads, impaled on poles, adorned many an entrance to a village. Even the military attachés were not allowed to see Gripenberg's cards, and journalists were not permitted to leave Mukden at all at that time.

The attack was planned for January 24. On the evening of the 23rd, the thermometer dropped to −22° R, and during the night it grew even colder.

Among some units the rumor spread that the attack had been postponed on account of this abnormal cold, {p. 90} and several commanders are said to have made representations in this regard as well.

To the cold was added an icily sharp wind from the Hunhe, against which no winter fur coat and no papakha offered protection. The troops of the VIII Army Corps were nonetheless full of enthusiasm. They had arrived fresh from Europe and were to receive their baptism of fire on the morrow.

[Illustration: Chinese border near Dalinor.]

Early on January 24, the *Second Army* was deployed from the right toward the left wing as follows:

*West Group:* General Mishchenko, with roughly the troops that had been placed under his command during the raid on Yingkou, reinforced by the 54th and parts of the 61st Reserve Infantry Division, between the Hunhe and the Chinese border northwest of Panpuosa.

*Center Group:* I Siberian and VIII European Corps north of Heigoutai and at Changtanhenan—that is, echeloned to the left rear of Mishchenko.

*East Group:* The Rifle Corps formed from the 1st, 2nd, and 5th European Rifle Brigades northeast of Sandepu; echeloned to the left rear of this, the X Army Corps.

{p. 91}

If we now believe that these forces, so promising in both numbers and quality—they were mostly European corps, superbly disciplined and well equipped—would launch a unified attack on the morning of the 24th, we are mistaken! On this day nothing more occurred than an advance by General Mishchenko; with his Cossacks on the right bank of the Hunhe in the vicinity of Heigoutai, he encountered weak Japanese cavalry, which fell back—and then he halted! In other words, General Mishchenko's advance, scarcely unleashed before it ground to a halt, had a result that day similar to the general's raid on Yingkou already discussed; it told the Japanese, who were somewhat unprepared here: “Look out, gentlemen, tomorrow we attack you; take your precautions, shift your reserves from the center toward the left wing—you may do so without worry, for you must already know from experience that the Russians with their three armies *never attack at the same time*!”

And so it was, only the “tomorrow” was a “Russian tomorrow”, a *zavtra*—consequently, the entire Japanese left wing was attacked in a unified manner not on the 25th, but only on the 26th.

On the 25th, the “rolling up” of the Japanese front was continued to the extent that the *Mishchenko Group* succeeded in taking possession of Qitaizi, Mamakai, and the ferry at Beidagu, while the *Center Group*, after strenuous fighting, seized the village of Heigoutai on the right bank of the Hun River. The enemy—who could not have been very strong—had entrenched himself along the edge of the village and, as the Russian artillery fired almost exclusively with shrapnel and not with high-explosive shells, held out until evening. With the onset of darkness, the *First Corps (Stackelberg’s Corps*, which since the beginning of the campaign had already lost nearly *one hundred percent* of its original strength!) stormed into the village, where a desperate house-to-house fight lasted until the early morning hours. The actions on these two days, conducted with only fractions of the total force available, resembled mere demonstrations to such a degree that the Japanese—perhaps expecting a {p. 92} main attack against their center—are likely to have shifted their reserves toward Sandepu only on the 26th.

What *precious time* the Russians had again *allowed to slip away* here! It truly seemed as though the Eastern Group and the entire Third and First Manchurian Armies had frozen to the ground during this time!

Always and in all battles the same Russian principle: one flank group begins; once it has accomplished its mission, the next follows, and so on—everything else looks on and waits! But if one of these groups deployed *one after another* encounters insurmountable resistance, the whole enterprise stalls—the individual corps become entangled in actions separated from one another in time and space, the ultimate result of which is always and everywhere retreat!

The bravery and above all the eagerness for battle of the Russian troops during the fighting around Sandepu recalls the days of the heroes of antiquity.

On the 26th, the Japanese were pushed back from all their positions; their efforts had not succeeded in retaking Heigoutai; on the contrary, they were driven back to the Sandepu–Malan-Sumapu line.

Nowhere did the Russians wait for their own artillery to prepare the attack; everywhere they charged—*by battalions*—against the Japanese, who, although always well covered, were numerically significantly weaker and were forced to yield the field each time after bitter close combat.

By the evening hours, the First Siberian and Eighth European Corps had left well over a thousand dead on the field.

Terrible was the plight of the wounded. The temperature had fallen far below –20° R. The few stretcher-bearers were insufficient to transport to the rear all those whose faces and hands were streaming with blood, or who were writhing in agony with a bullet in the belly. Those who were unhurt gave no thought to the dying, but rushed forward, for at last victory seemed to beckon to the Russians—the new, fresh troops from Europe wanted to {p. 93} show that the soldiers of old Russia could still fight and conquer—with their baptism of fire, the tide had to turn! Thus the Eighth Corps stormed on across the frozen fields of Heigoutai toward Sandepu—a handful of snow had to serve many a man as food and drink that day.

Whoever collapsed, struck down by an enemy bullet, simply remained lying there—until the bitter frost came over him and pressed the gaping wounds together with icy fingers; the blood ceased to trickle, the pain froze into lifelessness. Yet even those who reached the advanced dressing station were not yet safe. There were indeed enough doctors there to dress their wounds, but on the two-wheeled cart or on the Chinese *arba* that was to carry them to the main dressing station, the shivering chill came over them once more, penetrating the thick furs and the Chinese camel-hair blankets with which the poor wretches had been covered. Nothing helped here except movement, and so one saw men who could scarcely walk dragging themselves along painfully beside the ambulance wagon. Better that the wound should bleed and burn—the wounded man wished only not to freeze to death.

[Illustration: Chinese arba.]

{p. 94}

The Russians had been lying before Sandepu since afternoon. The village lies toward the south on a gently rising height, which the Japanese had skillfully prepared for tiered fire. The Japanese artillery stood close beside the village. The villages on both sides of Sandepu were burning; these the Russian artillery had taken under fire—Sandepu itself had not, as was said, owing to an error in designating the targets to be shelled on the map!

Here, as in other instances, the Japanese were able to assemble their reserves under cover in the villages, since these were never shelled by the Russians with high-explosive shells, but only with shrapnel; likewise the Japanese could also venture to place their artillery immediately adjacent to settlements. How devastating, by contrast, was the effect of the Japanese Shimose shell in the Russian ranks, and particularly in their batteries! Where the Japanese shell struck, a dense, broad cloud of earth, two storeys high, rose into the air and, lingering aloft for several minutes, shrouded all the horror it had wrought. It was not only the shell fragments, but the earth itself, thrown up as though by a mine, that tore limbs from torsos and severely injured the faces of even those standing farther off. To the whiz of bullets, to that howling and whistling high up in the air, and to the spattering of dirt where rifle bullets or shrapnel balls strike, one becomes accustomed fairly quickly in war, and even the sight of men wounded by these projectiles is bearable. Terrifying, on the other hand, was the effect of the shell on our nerves; each time it was as though a powder mill had blown into the air—and then the many mangled limbs lying about, and bodies crushed by the earth into a black mush!

Toward evening, the fire slackened somewhat on both sides before Sandepu; the attacker took advantage of this lull in the firing to push forward and finally penetrate into the village, whose northern and northwestern parts were occupied by the Russians. Darkness as well as exhaustion put an end to the fighting that night. It was mostly troops of the Eighth Corps who had penetrated into the village.

{p. 95}

The rolling field kitchens had even caught up with some companies, so that warm meals could be cooked here, almost chest to chest with the enemy. Where this was not possible, his old friend the *chaynik* once again provided the Russian with a cup of hot tea, which helped him endure the dread cold until the following morning.

That evening, news of victory in the fall of Sandepu spread through the army and, with the swiftness of long-awaited glad tidings, made its way back to Russia that very night. Unfortunately, the joy was premature.

In the early morning hours of the 27th, the struggle in the narrow lanes of Sandepu began anew. The Russians attempted to advance toward the center of the village from the north and west, but in doing so encountered resistance that could not be overcome either by rifle fire or bayonet charges—a row of buildings that, connected with one another by wire and other obstacles, were heavily manned by the Japanese and prepared for defense. The redoubt in this defensive line was formed by the *chin distillery*, whose broad fluted stone walls and flanking towers, looming over the surrounding houses, enabled the Japanese to bring fire upon the Russian reserves—if one could speak of such at all in the confusion of battle—while their leading ranks dashed themselves to pieces at the foot of the wall.

And where was the Russian artillery? No reproach can be made against it here. Almost the entire Eighth Army Corps, battalion after battalion, had entered the village; Russians and Japanese were fighting in all the alleyways; every shot would have struck friend and foe alike.

Later, during the Battle of Mukden, when I had the opportunity while with the Second Siberian Corps to observe the precise fire of the 2nd East Siberian Mortar Battery, I was reminded once again of this struggle. A few well-aimed shots from a *mortar* or *howitzer battery* would even then have still sufficed to clear the aforementioned obstacles out of the way, since they had already failed to prepare the {p. [104]} [Illustration: Sketch map of the actions at Sandepu.]

{p. 97}

attack the previous day with heavy artillery bombardment.

Thus once again all the strength and all the courage of the Russians were in vain. The successes of the preceding two days had instilled confidence in the bayonet attack among both men and officers. General Kondratovich led his troops forward to the assault at the head of the 1st East Siberian Rifle Division (First Siberian Corps). The general and almost all the officers of the lead battalions fell; more than fifteen hundred men covered the lanes of Sandepu as dead and wounded.

The Japanese received reinforcements in the course of the 27th—the Russians had, after all, allowed them two days' time to bring them up.

The *Rifle Corps* near Santaizi and the *Tenth Army Corps, held back as army reserve, did not enter the engagement on this day*, nor did the elements of the Central Group advancing along the right bank of the Hun River, which had been deployed in an excellent direction of attack threatening the Japanese rear (Sumapu and Niuju), but were probably for their part *waiting* for the fall of Sandepu instead of actively cooperating in taking it.

Only when General Mishchenko’s turning column made itself felt around noon on the 27th from the south against the Japanese left flank, threatening Sandepu itself from the south, did Sumapu fall into Russian hands.

This *indirect assistance, however, came too late*; by that time, Sandepu had already been evacuated by the Russians.

*Adjutant General Kuropatkin*, following the fighting of the Second Army, had likely come to the conviction, in view of the terrible losses that the two days of fighting for the village of Sandepu alone had cost, that further bloodshed, further throwing-in of individual battalions piecemeal, as had now become the fashion, would be futile. {p. 98} On the evening of the 27th, the commander-in-chief—contrary, so it was said, to the representations and entreaties of the commander of the Second Army—issued the order to evacuate Sandepu, which took place during the night of January 27–28.

By that day, however, the Japanese had brought up and closed up their reserves, and now went over to the offensive themselves.

Their most dangerous opponent was General Mishchenko’s group threatening their rear, which was thrown back near Landonggou with considerable losses.

By around noon on the 28th, those Russian forces that had already gained ground southwest of Sandepu were also driven back toward and across the Hun River, where elements of the First and Fifth Siberian Corps held Geijiao and Heigoutai.

On the Russian side, it was only on this day that the Rifle Corps and a brigade of the 10th Army Corps were brought into action; Laobatai and Xiaotaizi were taken by them by storm, so that at this time almost all the places around Sandepu were in Russian hands. Instead of fixing the Japanese forces in Sandepu and energetically pressing the attack—already victoriously begun—further to the east with their own main force, the riflemen fell back to the north again in the evening!

The cardinal objective of Grippenberg's entire offensive—the surprise capture of the Japanese's most vital flank bastion—had been missed!

What had failed against a weak opponent in the first three days was now, after the latter had brought up strong reserves, all the less likely to succeed.

Since this book is concerned less with a meticulous record of all the engagements than with pointing out those psychological factors responsible for the perpetual Russian failures, a description of the further engagements that were fought on the western flank until {p. 99} 20 February is not essential here. In general, these combats resembled all those that had preceded them and all those that were to follow later: facing a uniformly directed Japanese attack stood isolated Russian corps, parcelled out by battalions and companies into village fighting, employed with neither temporal nor spatial unity.

The cold during these days was, to be sure, paralyzing to operations—yet this affected friend and foe alike, and did not prevent the Japanese from achieving fresh successes on this terrain that was already half lost.

The fighting surged back and forth until 2 February, promising victory now to one side, now to the other, and was finally broken off by the Russians on that day, allegedly because Adjutant General Kuropatkin refused to grant further reinforcements for the rifle brigades' repeated attacks on Lidarentun.

*To summarize*: Kuropatkin intends to attack with his three armies; he designates the right-wing army for the attack on Sandepu—the other two armies remain standing idle during this attack.

Grippenberg has three strong groups available for the attack on Sandepu. One attacks, after two days the second group attacks as well, while the third remains standing idle during this attack.—Finally, on the fourth day, the third group also intervenes vigorously and successfully, only to withdraw again the next day without exploiting its successes!

Under such circumstances, it was perhaps entirely justified on the part of the commander-in-chief to refuse the squandering of further forces, since their deployment would by then have come too late anyway.

Whether the blame for the defective attack dispositions of the Second Army lay with Grippenberg or with Kuropatkin, who was in the habit of meddling in the detailed orders of his subordinate commanders, opinions were divided.

Unanimously, however, Grippenberg's conduct in abandoning the {p. 100} army in such difficult circumstances was condemned by all those whom I had occasion to question on the matter.

On 4 February, the Russians stood roughly where they had stood before the battle began; around fifteen thousand men had fallen on their side—and frozen to death.

[Illustration: Frontier Guard Barracks.]

{p. [109]}

# The Lull in Operations until the Battle of Mukden.

{p. [110]}

{p. [111]}

## Life in the Russian Positions.

After Sandepu, all had become quiet once again. Every train arriving from the north brought fresh troops—it was already openly admitted that they had to be *twice as strong* as the Japanese to be able to engage them with any prospect of success. All Mukden was one vast hospital, from which the wounded, as they gradually became fit for transport, were evacuated to the rear. General Mishchenko also lay wounded here.

My first visits in Mukden were to the chief of censorship, Colonel Pestich of the General Staff, and to the now historic railway train in which the military attachés lived. It had been shunted onto a siding next to the station and consisted of two elegant first-class Pullman cars and a dining car, in which the attachés were the guests of General Kuropatkin three times a day.

The eye soon grew accustomed to the colorful hustle and bustle of Mukden, with its constantly shifting scenes of European and Asian local color. Tempting as it would have been to linger here longer in order to penetrate that foreign world unfolding in all its originality, to visit the lama temples, the imperial tombs, and all the other sights of ancient Manchu culture, such studies were, after all, not the main purpose of my being here. No less tempting was the pleasant life in the railway carriage, where, through the kindness of the Russian General Staff officers assigned to the attachés, a half-compartment had been placed at my disposal for my temporary stay. Most of the foreign officers were in Mukden at that time; a few, the so-called "diligent ones"—among them the two Austro-Hungarian {p. 104} attachés—had, however, remained out in the positions, so that I felt quite lonely amid this great international bustle.

Probably never in my life, neither before nor since, have I felt the sentiment of gratitude, joy, and belonging so deeply as on that morning when Lieutenant Colonel Csicserics opened my compartment door and greeted me—a total stranger to him until then—here in a foreign land with that warmth and genuinely fraternal manner that has—not yet died out in our army. How comforting and familiar the comrades' informal "Du" sounded back then, whose true, inner worth we at home are often not even conscious of.

[Illustration: Main street of Mukden.]

With delight I accepted his invitation to accompany him out to the positions the following day, on which occasion we also intended to visit our comrade Captain Count Szeptycki, who lived out in Kouloutsi at the headquarters of General Bilderling (17th Army Corps).

Early in the morning in biting cold—it may have been around 22° R—we rode away from Mukden. Dense clouds of yellow dust whirled across the plain from the direction of the Hunhe. Above us was the magnificent, deep blue sky of the south, and whenever the icy north wind ran out of breath for a brief moment, one could even feel the warm rays of the sun.

{p. 105}

We pass the Mukden bridgehead with its entrenchments and batteries. "Hide your camera," Csicserics warns, "the Russians do not take kindly to amateur photographers." Poor art of light! Too much honor is paid to you when you are credited with vital services in betraying military secrets! I consider the value of photography in espionage to be a fable believed purely out of tradition. Just tell me, after all, what more precise data could an admittedly questionable photograph provide during the reconnaissance of a railway or a fortification than what I inconspicuously enter in my notebook—or perhaps commit to memory for the time being—on the basis of observations made with an expert eye, whether naked or aided? To note how many tracks a station has, how long a loading ramp is, how high an elevator, how many embrasures and the like, it is hardly necessary to photograph the things in question!

Further south we pass the headquarters of the commander-in-chief. With a certain awe I approach the place from which Russia's destiny for times to come will be decided. How little has a commander learned compared to other scholars! What a simple, one might almost say elementary, science are tactics and that bit of strategy compared to medicine, astronomy, and so forth. And yet there has never been anyone in the world—with the exception of the teachers of new religions—be they inventor, statesman, or scholar, who has intervened so forcefully in the machinery of world history, who has so thoroughly redrawn the maps of continents, as the victorious commander with his bit of strategy!

Not far from Mukden is a railway station, Sujiatun. A siding branches off from the main line, and on it stands a short train. Two locomotives, one facing forward, the other backward, and a few elegant Pullman cars. A carpeted flight of steps leads up to the last carriage; two lampposts are driven into the ground before it. All around the train, a large perimeter is likewise enclosed by lampposts, with sentries stationed at its entrances. From here the commander-in-chief directs his {p. 106} armies by electrical wires. The commander speaks a word into the telephone, and hundreds upon hundreds of thousands rise from the trenches, crawl out of the dugouts, leave the hospitals half-healed, to throw themselves upon the enemy. But at that time, the commander did not speak this word into the telephone—nor did he later.

In the village are quartered the headquarters troops belonging to the army high command—Caucasians—and the supply trains. Kuropatkin maintains strict discipline; meticulous order reigns in his headquarters as well as in those of the other staffs. Further on the road to Kouloutsi we ride through several villages. A sad sight. The roofs and walls of the low mud buildings seem to have suffered from fire. The inhabitants are gone, having moved to Mukden. Next to the villages, earthen huts, called *zemlyanki*, have been dug into the ground, offering the troops far warmer shelter than the wretched *fanzas*.

The roads are excellent, and faster than we would have given the little horses credit for, we were in Kouloutsi, where we warmly greeted Szeptycki. He complained that he had already been sitting in his cramped *fanza* for weeks and that things simply would not get moving up at the front. We told him that the day before, General Rennenkampf had passed through Mukden to take over the cavalry regiments of the wounded General Mishchenko over on the right wing. Rennenkampf, whom I had the honour of being introduced to, is a magnificent figure, a genuine cavalry general, and one of those whom one certainly cannot deny quickness of decision and energy in execution. Heedless of danger, he, like his entire staff, has already been wounded repeatedly. Until now he was on the extreme left wing in the mountains, which he was now glad, as he said, to be out of. Yet the plain has its drawbacks too. If the thaw sets in, which this year is likely to happen earlier than usual, one gets miserably stuck in the mud. If the offensive does not come soon, it will hardly be possible down there to make any headway later in the sodden ground. Rennenkampf, however, is certainly the man who knows how to overcome such difficulties.

Szeptycki had spent almost the entire summer with his staff {p. [115]} [Illustration: Rittmeister Spaits, Lieutenant Colonel von Csicserics, Captain Count Szeptycki in Koutoutsi. Photographed by Binder-Kriegelstein.]

{p. [116]}

{p. 109}

The day before in the dining car, Rennenkampf had also mentioned Szeptycki in the most laudatory terms, adding several flattering remarks about the Austro-Hungarian cavalry. It was likely also thanks to his high esteem for our cavalry that he received me so exceedingly warmly and granted me permission to attach myself to one of his Cossack regiments.

Captain Szeptycki had a guest with him, the writer and journalist Binder von Kriegelstein, who had served as an artillery first lieutenant in the Austro-Hungarian and later also in the German army. At that time he was writing for the *Berliner Lokalanzeiger* [Berlin Local Advertiser] and for other German newspapers.

Both were delighted at the news that Rennenkampf was preparing for a raid against Liaoyang. Szeptycki, who had always felt very much at ease with the dashing cavalry general, immediately decided to join him on the extreme right wing, and Binder likewise hoped to be able to take part in the raid.

Life here in the centre must have been quite tedious during the long lull in operations, which had now already lasted for four months. An ineffective cannonade around midday, isolated night-time enterprises by a few raiding parties that were mostly unsuccessful, an occasional shifting or relieving of troops in the positions—such was roughly the character of Russian activity in the centre and on the left wing during the winter months. That very afternoon we rode away from Kouloutsi. Binder-Kriegelstein had taken a few more photographs of us. The picture shows the outer wall of the *fanza* in which Szeptycki lived. It was one of the better ones, and its roof as well as the doorposts had still been spared by the soldiers. The neighbouring huts looked desolate. Whatever was made of wood had long since gone into the cooking fires of the muzhiks, leaving only the bare grey mud walls with the large, gaping square windows, from whose corners there still hung now and then a piece of parchment paper, which in these parts serves in place of windowpanes. Those villages in which higher commands or staffs had been quartered could consider themselves fortunate. There {p. 110} the houses remained undamaged for the time being and were even put into better repair.

In every larger Manchurian settlement there are one or more spacious buildings: a temple with a garden and adjoining priest's quarters, a liquor distillery, pawnshops, or the farmstead of a wealthier Chinese. Each of these buildings is surrounded by a thick, high stone wall, usually fitted with loopholes and flanking towers for defence. The large wooden arched gate of the outer wall, always painted with the

[Illustration: Manchurian fanzas.]

obligatory good and evil god, leads into a smaller forecourt, in which stands a low domestic altar with burning incense; further back in the main courtyard stands the dwelling house—the *fanza*. From the outside it looks quite cosy. Single-storey, the roof thatched with sorghum stalks, wooden wattle walls smeared with mud. The painted door leads into an anteroom, which usually also serves as a kitchen. On either side of it lies a spacious room. The heating is distinctive. The stove is replaced by the *kang*, which extends about two metres wide and half a metre high along both side walls. It is {p. 111} made of solid clay and is usually heated from the anteroom, so that the room is free of smoke and pleasantly warmed. Overnight, however, the *kang* cools down, so that in the morning hours we usually suffered keenly from the cold. The surface of the *kang* is covered with clean, shiny straw mats. It is upon this that the family life of the Chinese takes place. Here the women crouch beside a low square wooden table and embroider or weave, here children are suckled, opium is smoked, here the whole clan sleeps all jumbled together; erotic modesty is foreign to the Chinese.

On the front wall of the room there is invariably a domestic altar with candlesticks and cups in which thin yellow incense sticks are usually kept. On the walls I often found very beautiful ink drawings, such as the one included in this book.

The windows are large and take up almost the entire front wall. Set into the window frame is an outwardly opening wooden lattice, which often displays elaborate carvings. Over this lattice is pasted thick white parchment, into which a tiny glass peek-window is also cut. The parchment would have kept things relatively warm, but it had mostly been damaged by the soldiers, so that the cold air blew through its cracks.

Few of the original inhabitants had remained in the villages. Almost all had moved into Mukden, which around this time harboured some thirty thousand or even more such displaced villagers. For the most part, the Chinese had been expelled by the Russians from the area of the troops and headquarters—a measure that was entirely justified given these people's aptitude for espionage, but which struck the poor Manchus, whom the entire war did not concern in the least, quite bitterly.

Sometimes such a poor devil would sneak out of Mukden back to his village and was glad if he still found his four stakes on the old spot. He could rest easy if a flag was flying over his roof. If it was the Red Cross flag, then a divisional field hospital was housed here, as these were often pushed forward disproportionately far toward the firing line {p. 112}. This also frequently resulted in their suffering from enemy fire, *for which the Japanese could not be blamed*.

The Slavic tricolour signified a staging post. These were mostly occupied by doctors and officers who temporarily had no assignment, that is, whose previous official assignment had ended and who, as a result of the sluggish administration, *waited* for weeks for a new duty posting.

[Illustration: Mongolian felt yurt.]

The Mongolian felt yurt, which has also become naturalised in Manchuria, could be used to good advantage for housing smaller detachments. It keeps much warmer than a tent, even though the latter was always pitched with double walls.

The higher headquarters were marked by yellow flags with inscriptions, and by lanterns at night.

Order and cleanliness distinguished their sectors, just as strict camp discipline seemed to prevail in the other billeting areas as well.

Nowhere was there abundance, let alone luxury, yet everywhere in this zone of the field army the needs of both enlisted men and officers were satisfied.

{p. 113}

The Russian is accustomed to eating well and plentifully, and he did not need to renounce this habit here either. The cooking at the higher staffs was even excellent; on the other hand, they too lacked the comfort of proper cutlery. The Russian is impractical in this regard—portable officers' messes, such as belong to the field kit of every staff in our army and in other armies, were entirely absent there, and it was not uncommon for roast meat with gravy to be served on a tin coaster. This may well also be a consequence of Russia's underdeveloped industry, which is wholly dependent on foreign countries for the procurement of practical travel goods.

Of drinking bouts or even orgies, of which the imagination of journalists so often knew how to report, there was not a trace. For the non-Russian, however, it was unpleasant that vodka and tea were the sole substitutes for beer and wine.

I had already come to know tea during the journey out, but even more so on the theatre of war itself, as an excellent, refreshing, and health-preserving universal beverage, to which far too little attention is unfortunately paid in the other European armies—indeed, there are even regions, such as in Hungary or Austria, where this drink is completely unknown to the common soldier.—

It is no exaggeration to say that the Russian Manchurian Army owes its excellent state of health to tea.

The source of all infectious diseases usually lies in unboiled, bad water.

The very fact that the Russian, accustomed to tea, never drinks ordinary water, but even to quench his thirst drinks only boiled water—that is, tea—provides a universal preventive measure for the army's state of health.

How could the Russian soldier have survived the forty-day transport from Moscow to Mukden had his friend, the *chaynik*—tea kettle—not stood by his side? How many living bacilli would he not have swallowed on the way across all of Asia had they not been rendered harmless in the *kipyatok*—{p. 114} boiling-water cauldrons—at the stations and halts?

And now on the theatre of war itself! What was the poor devil supposed to drink here? *Vodka was strictly forbidden to the enlisted men;* it could be sold to them neither in inns nor at railway stations. *Chin*, the Chinese liquor, a hideous drink that continues to ferment in the stomach for a week after one has slept off the hangover; wine was out of the question, beer too expensive, and unboiled water, finally, the surest breeding ground for every possible disease.

When one considers how difficult it is with us, even during peacetime exercises, to keep the men from drinking tainted water, the advantage for the state of health of an army whose men are accustomed from home never to drink water, but always tea, becomes clearly apparent.

[Illustration: Distribution of tea en route.]

{p. 115}

A fifteen-minute rest was enough for an individual horseman to untie his chaynik from the stirrup and quickly brew himself a warming drink. Most of the men carried compressed tea—they called it Cossack tea—with them; they almost never took sugar with it—the addition of rum is unknown to the Russian tea drinker. “When I drink tea, I drink tea—when I drink spirits, I drink spirits!” the Russian is accustomed to say when expected to spoil the aroma of his favorite drink with liquor—that comes separately later!

Thus, in the *fangzi* of the general staff sections, too, the samovar bubbled all day long—alongside it, there was chatting, smoking of papirosy, and working. Nowhere in the offices were the time and place of duty and relaxation sharply separated from one another—as is indeed hardly easily possible in war anyway. The daily routine lacked that strict formality to which we are accustomed, but on the other hand there was also no trace of that nervousness which has become ingrained among us and which, in a serious emergency, will scarcely be sustainable over the long run.

Another habit peculiar to the Russians, and one they did not want to abandon in war, is staying up late into the night—even when there is no reason whatsoever to do so—and the consequent *sleeping in late until the early hours before noon*—even when it would have been necessary to rise earlier.

Even the men in the zemlyankas rarely crawled out of them before 8 o’clock in the morning. Mind you, though, all that is said here applies only to the period when operations had come to a standstill!

The zemlyankas were spacious, fitted out for fifty to a hundred men, and had earthen stoves. They were dug out by unit, mostly near the villages, in regular lanes—work that had to be finished back in autumn, since later the ground froze as hard as bone.

In the morning, or rather before noon, the battalions assembled in front of their quarters, greeting their commander with a ringing “Pozdravie zhelayem!” Then they drilled. Mostly in close order, less often extended-order combat,

{p. 116}

and the latter mostly in the manner of “assembling individually to the front”—as we were frequently able to observe during such exercises.

Once work is finished, the troops march in with fresh, cheerful singing. Nowhere have I heard soldier songs as melodious as among the Russians, whose metallic voices are as pure as the hearts of these good fellows.

When night falls, the soldiers step outside their zemlyankas, take the papakha from their heads, and sing their evening prayer. Inside the earthen hut, the large kettle with boiling water bubbles, for which they have been given double rations of tea and sugar. Then they can sleep, or perhaps ponder—how long the war will still last. Ponder, though, thank goodness they do not yet do! The zemlyanka is warm, the food good—far better than at home—and they must die sooner or later anyway, if God wills it so.

Comfortable as it was here in the rear, it was by no means so forward on the actual battle line of the positions. These foremost lines, sometimes no more than a few hundred paces from the Japanese outposts, consisted partly of section trenches with covered shelters for entire battalions, partly of entrenchments—here termed redoubts. Four to five hundred paces behind the infantry cover stood the batteries, mostly positioned behind undulations of the terrain for indirect firing.

From the left wing of the Second to beyond the center of the Third Army, drawn back quite far from the foremost line, ran a *mighty artillery line consisting of approximately three hundred heavy fortress guns*, which had been brought here and installed with great effort. They formed a formidable rampart against any potential Japanese breakthrough attempt.

In these infantry and artillery positions, duty was extremely grueling. The biting cold and the never-resting enemy made life quite miserable for the garrison troops. The skirmishing—the Russians called it “perestrelka” (firing back and forth)—went on here all day long.

Relief troops attempting by day to move into {p. 117} the position were immediately brought under fire by the Japanese; even individual horsemen coming from the nearest village were shot at; the Russians, for their part, favored taking Japanese entrenching parties as their targets.

In the zemlyankas, tales were told of daring feats by this or that Russian soldier; one was said to have succeeded in bringing home a plump little pig that was roaming between the opposing battle lines; on another occasion, three men were said to have wagered that they could capture a herd of horses which a Japanese patrol had led to the Shahe to water, and so on.

The fact is that, as a result of this ceaselessly waged petty warfare, there were newly wounded men every day. —

For the correspondent here in the positions—when he was granted access to them—there was an abundant field of observation. If he knew how to talk with the soldiers—the men are trusting and talkative—he could sit with them in the evening in their dug-out and listen to their conversations. “How well off the Japanese over there must be,” they said, “they always have schnapps, for every time they come at us, they are drunk.”

“And how well our prisoners over there are said to be doing!”

“Chefoo”—so they called a Chinese interpreter—“showed us letters from Russia, and it was in the newspaper too: the Japanese have had a large ship of cabbage brought in so that they can give our men borsch. Vasily Alexandrovich showed us the letter—the same man who was left behind near Linjiapu last week along with four others from the scouting detachment. But he wasn’t killed; he surrendered along with the others—Chefoo told us—and the Japanese brought them to their general, who presented each of them with a ruble; Chefoo found out on that very same day.” “We’ll see Chefoo hanging from a tree yet,” another said. “Fyodor Maksimovich”—that was their captain—“beat him the day before yesterday when we came back, and threatened that he would strike him dead if he ever showed his face among us again.”
