More difficult than in the positions was monitoring the men’s intercourse with the Chinese {p. 118} at the railway stations. Here, where individual troop trains often stood at a station for days on end, Japanese emissaries had the best opportunity to mingle among the loitering men.

[Illustration]

Yet socialist propaganda, too, was conducted most successfully during the long train journeys, and many a man unwittingly brought a pamphlet along into the dug-out that a newspaper vendor at some station had slipped into his coat sleeve. Why, at the station in Harbin no less than *sixteen poods* of such pamphlets were discovered one day!

Many assert that *socialist propaganda* was to blame for the misfortune of the Russian army; I *do not believe this*, for the Russian soldier seemed to me intellectually too undeveloped for such ideas to have taken root with him.

When we rode away from Gouluozi, we kept to the military road that ran parallel to the foremost lines opposing the First Army—towards Huangshan. From the Japanese side a white cloud of smoke rose here and there, and soon the dull thud of their siege artillery came rolling over. The Russians replied, but rarely did the cannonade last long. Serious damage has not thus far been caused by the heavy Japanese guns; they seemed to be too far away to observe the effect of their fire. If the field guns chimed in from time to time, one could more easily guess their positions from the simultaneously exploding shrapnel of the battery salvos. A beautiful—one might almost say—manoeuvre scene.

Along the way I met a poruchik—a reserve lieutenant whom I knew from the journey. His one-week stay in the section trench seemed to have agreed {p. 119} with him rather badly. He could not tolerate the fare out there, he said. Instead of cooking forward in the battle line itself, the field kitchens drove up as far as the nearest village, from where the fatty cabbage soup with meat was then carried into the defensive line by individual men. Naturally, the food arrived there half-frozen. “Thank God that one only had to choke down this grub once a day—I would rather have done without it entirely,” remarked the poor fellow, who showed distinct signs of the dysentery that the men, in their vexation with the whole country, called “Manchurska,” “but after all, one cannot live on chocolate and frozen bread alone!” Now, as far as bread is concerned, a loaf was rarely to be found in the troop area that had not frozen on its way from the field bakery. It soon thawed out again, to be sure, but retained a strange, unpleasant taste. —

As we rode on, we were able to study the technical installations of the positions. These were laid out with circumspection and with ample means. Over the Hun River five bridges were built—partly on fixed, partly on floating supports; an equal number of military roads led radially from Mukden to the front.

Extensive use was made of the light field railway, which on several stretches even had small passenger carriages running at regular intervals. From the main line of the standard railway, a branch line ran to the left wing towards Fushun, and another to the right wing towards Dawangjiapu and Xiupu.

Along these standard railways, or connected to them by light field railways, lay the first-line divisional field hospitals. The standard railway towards Xiupu had rendered excellent service in evacuating the wounded during the fighting around Sandepu.

All the higher commands were connected with one another laterally and to the rear by telegraph, and in some cases also by telephone; strong posts guarded the lines of communication, wells, and other important installations. The entire area behind the foremost positions revealed the foresight of a commander who had established here a base for later operations—only this "later" must not be postponed too far. At the end of March the thaw usually sets in here, {p. 120} transforming the entire plain into a bottomless quagmire in which light railways and other technical installations must sink without a trace.

For the time being, however, nothing seemed to indicate that anyone intended to shake themselves out of the operational inactivity into which they had fallen after the Battle of Sandepu anytime soon.

Above Putilov Hill—known from the newspapers as the "Hill with the Three Trees"—the last little white puffs of Japanese shrapnel had vanished into the grey haze that hung leaden over the plain in the evening. From the mountains came the lingering echo of isolated shots, then all around grew quiet. In the fields, individual soldiers from the nearby dugouts were pottering about, weeding out gaoliang roots or gathering dried dung—fuel for their cooking fires. We rode on eastward along the hard-frozen military road toward Huangshan, the headquarters of General-Adjutant Linevich, then commander of the First Russian Manchurian Army.

Huangshan lies at the foot of the heights that push forward from here to the south and east with moderate elevations, yet sharply defined contours. Just beyond the village, a narrow mountain ridge rises steeply, with a temple standing on its summit. Still further south, near Erdagou, visible from afar, a tall stone tower was built by the Chinese—to ward off evil spirits. From up there, the land is visible as far as the Liaohe and even further west; to the southeast lies the valley of the Shahe, here already hemmed in by steep mountains.

[Illustration: Main guardhouse in Huangshan.]

Here, in this space that was by no means overly vast for mass armies, the two adversaries had been facing each other since mid-October. Only a practiced eye with good binoculars could spot from here the Russian positions, which, cleverly concealed in the terrain, extended along the mountains, along the Shahe, across the Mandarin Road {p. 121} and the railway, as far as the Liaohe. Little was to be seen of the Japanese. Anyone looking at the landscape lying before them, calm and peaceful, the fields still well-tended from the autumn sowing, could scarcely believe that there was a war going on here.

Riding through the village of Mazatun, headquarters of the 1st European Army Corps, we halted outside the quarters of the German attaché, Major Runkel. He occupied one half of a *fangzi*; in the other room an office of the corps general staff section was housed. Major Runkel was visibly pleased by Lieutenant-Colonel von Csicserics's visit, pulled out his notebook, and shared in a whisper what he had recently learned about Russian troop movements. His notes indicated a *shifting of forces toward the western wing*. Apart from that, he complained about the more than reserved attitude of the Russian officers toward him, offered us tea, and promised to come to Huangshan soon.

Dusk was already falling when we rode into Huangshan. Outside General Linevich's quarters lay strange equipment. There were various assault implements, such as portable assault bridges made of timber and gaoliang mats, as well as chevaux-de-frise of various sizes and several large, thick rollers made of wooden planks, whose purpose was not clear to us. Everything was lined up neatly side by side, apparently for inspection by the general.

"It does seem as though things will finally get underway," remarked Lieutenant-Colonel von Csicserics; "perhaps they will try it from the left wing this time; it is high time!" Soon we reached the liquor distillery in which those military attachés who had attached themselves to the headquarters of the First Army were quartered.

In principle, foreign military attachés were free to choose a command with which they wished to follow the operations. To be sure, this choice was not always accepted; instead, the army high command would assign the attaché in question a corps whose headquarters he might join. Such cases were rare, however; for the most part, the foreign officers could go wherever they wished. They usually preferred a command whose {p. 122} commander or chief of staff they were on somewhat closer terms with as a result of prior acquaintance.

Thus, in the course of the campaign, most of the attachés had formed close ties with one corps or another, where they were so to speak "at home," whose general staff officers and troop commanders they knew personally, and

[Illustration: Lieutenant-Colonel Rostagno, Argentine military attaché, on the railway overpass in Harbin.]

within whose sphere they could everywhere count on the most collegial reception and support in the performance of their duties.

If a military attaché had not understood how to make friends somewhere, his situation was often quite difficult from the moment he left his railcar; not only was {p. 123} every opportunity for insight made difficult for him, but even in procuring the most essential necessities of life, such as quarters and the like, he encountered such obstacles that he could clearly see how little anyone was pleased by his presence. Sometimes political reasons also played a part, as for instance in the case of the Argentine attaché, for whom it was certainly quite awkward personally that his government had sold warships to Japan during the war. Sometimes “making friends” probably also required more than tactful accommodation—a bit of affability, a handshake in the right place, one or even several little glasses of vodka at the right time—useful secondary traits that seemed superfluous to the straightforward nature of the German Major Runkel, which explained his bad mood in Masatun.

In Huanshan, besides the Austro-Hungarian attaché, there lived the English General Gerard, doyen of the military attachés, and the Prussian Lieutenant Colonel von Lauenstein. Assigned to these three gentlemen was the Russian Staff Captain Kachalov.

[Illustration: Open-air horse depot in Huanshan, in the foreground the chimney of a zemlyanka.]

{p. 124}

The quarters assigned to the attachés in the *chin* distillery consisted of a small anteroom, in which Cossacks and servants lodged, and two rooms, separated from the former by a handsomely painted paper wall. Nothing was lacking for the comfort of these rooms except a cozy warmth, which, with the thin walls and torn parchment windows, could not be attained despite all heating. The gentlemen had settled in between the bare walls as best they could—each had set up his camp bed, a camp chair, and a little three-legged table, on the latter of which they wrote their diaries—crafted military history. The Englishman was accommodated most practically; being the eldest, he occupied one half of the room, while Csicserics and Lauenstein shared the other half. The second room—if one could call it that—housed Kachalov and also served as a dining room—two chairs with a plank laid across them took the place of a table.

When we stepped into the fanza, General Gerard happened to be sitting in his “home,” writing in his diary by the light of two English candles. He was a handsome old gentleman with friendly blue eyes, gaunt and wrinkled, as nearly forty years of service in India and Africa could not fail to make him. He had formerly been military attaché in Petersburg and a personal friend of the Tsar, with whom he had hunted tigers in India. This circumstance, together with his distinguished and engaging manner, made him far more popular with the Russians than the representatives of several other states that then maintained much friendlier relations with Russia than England did. General Gerard was ill. Only a month before had he left the hospital in Gongzhuling, and he was now seeking recovery here in the fresh mountain air. “What news, General?”—“Rats, lots of rats at night!” was his laconic reply. “Ils se proménent toute la nuit travers ma figure!” Lieutenant Colonel von Lauenstein had already learned of my arrival from his wife in Harbin. He was newly married and had brought his wife with him at the beginning of the war, knowing she was in good hands, initially with the family of General Uexküll and later, when all women had to leave the theatre of operations, with the {p. 125} [Illustration: General Sir Gerard, English military attaché, in front of the military attachés’ carriage in Mukden.]

friendly German merchants Tillmann in Harbin. At dinner we sat together quite merrily; only the Englishman was somewhat taciturn. The food, from General Linevich’s kitchen, was excellent. Serving at table was Sanyi, Lieutenant Colonel von Csicserics’s orderly—here in Manchuria he did full credit to his native town of Makó on the Tisza. Deft, nimble, and always clean, he stood out advantageously from the *dull*, ever-greasy Russian servants and was soon the favourite of all the attachés, which earned him not only kind words but also clear-ringing coin.

Thus life here would have been quite pleasant had two unwelcome guests not come to us during the night through the gaps and cracks in the walls: the cold and the rats! For me it was all the harder since, lacking a camp bed, I lay on the cold floor, and the disgusting quadrupeds could stroll back and forth across me all the more easily.

{p. 126}

Yet in time a person gets used to anything. With what stoic calm General Gerard would search every morning for his soap, which the rats had carried off to some corner of the fanza during the night. “Coberas are worse.” The cobras in India are even worse—was his curt self-consolation; he wiped off the soap and washed with it.

An unpleasant coincidence gave me an opportunity to see for myself the quality of the Russian veterinarians. Lieutenant Colonel Csicserics had an excellent horse. Since it had formerly belonged to the Spanish military attaché, Sanyi had christened it “Marquis.” One day it went lame, and the veterinarian summoned from headquarters simply ordered that it be shot immediately. In response to a few professional questions from us, which seemed to surprise him, he finally explained that in his whole life he had actually never had *anything to do with horses*, but that until the outbreak of the war only cows and dogs had been his patients! Poor Marquis later had, when

[Illustration: Russian remount commission.] {p. 127} Huangshan was evacuated by the Japanese, unfortunately to be shot after all, since until then no one had known how to treat him.

Procuring a good horse was quite a difficult matter at that time. All serviceable stock had been bought up by the Russian remount commissions, so that the Chinese dealers were only offering animals afflicted with every hippological defect.

The news that the Swedish military attaché, Captain Edlund, was departing for home and that his horses were for sale decided me to return to Mukden.

On the morning I rode away, the military attachés had put on their full-dress uniforms to attend a mournful ceremony—the field requiem for Grand Duke Sergei, who had been murdered in Moscow. His fate had caught up with him; no one shed a tear for him.

On the way back, I stopped in to see Major Runkel. I found him ready to travel. “Are you going to Mukden as well, Major?” “Thank God, a bit farther,” he replied with a laugh, “to Berlin! After all, I cannot accomplish anything useful here—by straightforward means—with this damned secrecy everywhere, so I asked to be relieved and will await the answer in Mukden.” We were standing in front of the fansa as a detachment marched past us. “Where are you marching to?” Major Runkel asked a non-commissioned officer. “To position,” was the brief reply. “I could have figured that out myself,” Runkel grumbled vexedly, “but you simply cannot get anything more out of these fellows—and one is supposed to write reports based on that!”

“Do you believe, Major, that there will hardly be a great decisive battle now?” “I believe,” was the reply, “*what all the other military attachés also foresee and what the Russian General Staff itself most ardently desires: that there will be another great mutual slaughter here on the Shahe, with each side leaving several thousand dead; neither will yield ground, and both, Japanese as well as Russians, will then have to see that things cannot go on like this, and will make peace.*”

{p. 128}

These were also roughly the words that Major Baron Tettau had spoken to me a few weeks earlier in Mukden, being very indignant at my expressed hope that the war would only now properly begin. “You surely don't imagine that the war will drag on for another year for your sake; as you can see, I am already preparing for the return journey,” he said, pointing to a guidebook, *Travers la Chine* [*Across China*], which he had borrowed from the French attaché, Captain Boucé, in order to put together an interesting route back. “One more big massacre, in which the Russians, *thanks to their current significant numerical superiority*, will apparently succeed in scoring a success, so that at least the honour of their arms is saved, and then we are going home!”

[Illustration: Distribution of rations at headquarters in Huangshan.]

So thought the German officers and nearly all the other foreign officers. Lieutenant Colonel von Csicserics, on the other hand, was of a different opinion. “Now,” he said, “would be the best, but also the last opportunity for Russia to conclude a relatively favourable peace. Now—this was still *before* the Battle of Mukden—the army stands numerically superior and un- {p. 129} *defeated* before the Japanese, and the Russian fleet, too, is approaching Japanese waters—still commanding respect today. These are numerical power factors which Japan would certainly have to take into account in a peace settlement; *but as far as I know the Russians, they will not make peace.* Given the military impotence they have displayed thus far, however, it cannot be assumed, even for the future, that they will ever achieve operational success. Where is all their numerical superiority today? Dissipated along a long, thin line; the Japanese can break through it wherever they please! And what follows then?” At this he used to point to the defile of Tieling north of Mukden marked on the map: “Then there will be a debacle here!” How accurate the assumption of Lieutenant Colonel von Csicserics was—of whom the Russian officers themselves said that he knew their army better than they did—events have confirmed.

The road to Mukden was long, and it would not have been advisable to ride alone after nightfall through the graveyards and settlements outside the city proper, where many a European had already been ambushed and robbed. Thus, one morning, a Russian merchant had been brought to Professor Oettingen, a Red Cross physician, after hunghutzes had dragged him into a fansa in one of these suburbs by night and tortured him in a horrifying manner with small curved razors and red-hot irons. They believed that the man had money lying around somewhere in Mukden, and wanted to force a confession from the poor devil, who had already given his tormentors the few rubles that were all he possessed anyway. To avoid such travel adventures, I preferred to quicken my pace in order to reach Mukden before dusk.

Across the Hunhe plain swept that abominable wind once more, obscuring with its brown clouds of dust even that bit of blue sky which on clear days was the only feast for the eyes in this broad monotony. The valleys, the hills, the fansas, and even the people who lived and walked here—everything was brown, bleak, and monotonous. No forest, not a {p. 130} tree as far as the eye could see, no chirping bird, no murmuring brook; nothing but dusty brown plain before me, bare brown mountain slopes to the east, and to the west a brown, foggy haze from which the tall iron framework of the Shajiatun railway bridge stood out in thick black strokes.

The wind whistles and howls, and vying with it, drunken soldiers howl and sing as they rattle toward me at a furious pace on empty carts from Mukden along the rock-hard frozen road. Perhaps they had delivered wounded men and seized the opportunity to buy a few bottles of *tchin* from the Chinese with pay saved up over months. They whip the small horses, brandish their rifles, and act bellicose, as if they intended to drive straight into the Japanese.

It was evening when I reached the Hunhe bridge; three long lines of wagons stood before it, patiently waiting until their turn to cross came. I had Lieutenant-Colonel von Csicserics's two-wheeled cart with me, and I had to fall in behind the last of the vehicles and wait until my turn arrived! It might well be midnight by then! But here chance intervened—that coincidence which befalls us so often in life, where far out in the wide world, amid unknown people never seen before, we come upon a friend dropped as if from heaven. It was the Russian colonel with whom I had traveled from Moscow to Irkutsk, the commander of transports for the Third Manchurian Army. His bundle of furs would have made him unrecognizable had I not known his friendly voice when he laughingly invited me to take a seat in his elegant troika, which in the next minute crossed the bridge ahead of the wagons waiting there.

The joy of seeing each other again loosened our tongues, and the colonel, too, came out with his view on the war. “We have our great, rich country, we have our glorious past and our history. Look at these fansas here,” and with that he pointed to several wretched Chinese huts, “to wage war over these fansas, is that not foolish, when one has a fatherland richer and mightier than all other European states! Over these wretched Chinese fansas!” He seemed {p. 131} to have had bad experiences in these fansas, so much did the mere sight of them irritate him. He was right, besides. Although his duties would not have taken him to Mukden, he nevertheless brought me to the military attachés' train, in whose dining car the gentlemen were just gathering for dinner.

[Illustration: In front of the Mukden railway station.]

{p. [140]}

## On the Military Attachés' Train.

Being able to observe the colorful pageant of wartime life from the window of a Pullman car is certainly an achievement of the age of the iron horse!

Never before had the railway played so vital a role for an army in the field as it did here for the Russian Manchurian Army, for which the trans-Asian line of rails was not only the lifeline funneling troops and materiel to the army, but to whose individual field armies branch lines also led, at whose railheads—much as we saw with the Army High Command—an elegant train of several carriages was likewise placed at the disposal of each respective army command.

In Mukden and further to the rear in the lines of communication area, railway carriages were used as permanent quarters to the extent that they could be spared for that purpose. Chanceries, senior officers, the editorial office of the *Mandzhursky Vestnik* [Manchurian Herald],¹ the retail stores of the "economic societies" that brought supplies for officers and men from Russia to Manchuria, were all housed in carriages, which ensured their constant readiness to move—unfortunately, almost always only for retreat. It was therefore only natural that the home of the military attachés was likewise on a train, which stood at a discreet distance behind the zone of operations and, in the event of reverses, quickly steamed off northwards to bring the foreign guests, entrusted to the protection of the Russian flag, to safety.

Russian railway tracks are broader than those of all other states, the carriages accordingly larger and more comfortable. {p. 133} If Russia is generally superior to its western neighbors in the practical and elegant appointments of its carriages, this is especially true of the Chinese Eastern Railway,¹ whose sleeping and dining cars are certain to satisfy even the most discerning traveler. Two such carriages, a dining car, and two cars for luggage and servants formed that international island where one could live in comfort amid the hardships and privations of war, where physical well-being was sheltered from Japanese bullets and property from Chinese and other sticky fingers.

::: notes
¹) Newspaper for the Manchurian Army.
:::

A practical man soon learns to make himself at home in the most confined space, and so the compartments and half-compartments assigned to the attachés were certainly the safest and most comfortable quarters for this restless wartime existence. The elegant train in which the foreign agents followed operations in Manchuria may perhaps set a precedent for the future. In a future campaign, the belligerents will surely place such a train at the disposal of their official spectators once again; for the latter, this will be a great convenience, and no less so for the army high commands concerned. Concern for the safety of foreign officers, and no less the precautions against their curiosity, will in future cause the commander-in-chief little headache, provided that in a coming war the two adversaries admit foreign officers—whether official or not—at all! Should the proximity of the enemy become dangerous, or conditions arise in one's own army that make the presence of foreign witnesses undesirable, a nod to the engine driver will suffice to send the distinguished guests traveling off in the opposite direction from operations. Any military thirst for knowledge flaring up on such occasions among the attachés rolling about in the rear lines of communication during major battles must then be suppressed by especially fine cuisine and exquisite drinks in the dining car {p. 134} . The odd ambitious striver who resists such philanthropic arrangements—who is not content to construct military history from a railway car, but, disregarding his own physical well-being, wishes to gain a firsthand view of the firing line—will simply be sent packing home. In a future war, however, one would certainly prefer to exchange even such summary procedures against attachés for a polite and total refusal to receive them, were it not that their presence must be tolerated if only out of considerations of reciprocity, and did cases not frequently arise during a war—such as violations of neutrality, the Geneva Convention, and the like—whose impartial investigation requires the participation of disinterested neutral observers. The presence of foreign official witnesses remains undeniably unpleasant for the losing side; all the greater recogni-

::: notes
¹) In name owned by the Russo-Chinese Bank, but in reality Russian state property.
:::

[Illustration: Mountain ammunition column.]

{p. [143]}

{p. [144]}

{p. 139}

tion must therefore be accorded to the Russians for that sincere, unconstrained hospitality which they extended to all their guests, both the military attachés and other foreign officers. Out on the front lines, it was admittedly impossible to spare the officers staying there all those petty discomforts that are inextricably bound up with life in the field—nor did anyone claim otherwise. Whatever could be done, however, in the way of comfort and for their other

[Illustration: United States Major Montgomery Meigs Macomb, a cowboy saddle on his horse.]

physical comfort could be provided was to be had in the “carriage”.

Just behind the dining car lived Major Baron Tettau of the Great General Staff. His adjoining compartment was mostly empty; it belonged to Major von Runkel, who lived out at the front line. The German staff surgeon Dr. Schäfer also came in only rarely from Gongzhuling, where as head of a Red Cross hospital he was certainly best able to perform his duties while also doing useful work. Major Tettau had chosen the Tenth Army Corps, {p. 140} as mentioned in the previous chapter, as his corps of assignment, whose headquarters lay fairly close to Mukden. The next large compartment belonged to the French staff surgeon Follenfant, who later also had to take in his American colleague, Senior Staff Surgeon Howard—incidentally also a Frenchman. Follenfant had already spent a part of his life in the colonies and in the war against the Chinese in Tonkin, just as the other attachés had already travelled far and wide across the world before this war and had taken part in many of those great events that become known to us humble petty bourgeois in Austria and Hungary only by hearsay.

All those states committed a grave sin of omission that, out of petty stinginess or failing to recognise the equal importance of the military medical service, sent no medical officers to the theatre of war. Among all military representatives in this war, the foreign military surgeons certainly found the broadest field open for positive observations. The physician, lending a hand himself, was able to enrich his experience; in doing so, he stood closer to his profession than the tactical observer and, owing to the directly beneficial use of his skills, was welcomed everywhere. Even Bulgaria, indeed even China, in proper recognition of this necessity, had each sent a doctor to the theatre of war, who were assigned to hospitals in the lines-of-communication area.

The Americans, Artillery Major Montgomery Meigs Macomb and the engineer Captain Judson, had also been assigned a large compartment, but lived permanently in the house of a Chinese general (?) in Mukden, quite far from the carriage, which they had rented together with the two American correspondents and where they also ran their own joint kitchen. The next half-compartment was occupied by a very jovial gentleman whose perpetually cheerful mood and lively jokes had an invigorating effect on the entire company: Camperio, naval lieutenant and “Head of the Royal Italian Mission by Sea and by Land”, as he had playfully written on his visiting card. His headquarters was that of the Third Siberian Corps, where the ever-critical, yet ever-cheerful man was known to and liked by {p. 141} everyone. At the beginning of the campaign he had been in Vladivostok, from where, after the blockade of that fortress, he had joined the field army at Liaoyang. He had quickly settled into the role of a general staff officer, and his remarks revealed a keen power of military observation. He enjoyed the privilege of being allowed to say the bluntest things to the Russians’ faces. “Vous êtes un marin, vous ne comprenez rien!” the Spanish military attaché had once told him, and ever since he loved to quote this saying in excuse of his often quite sarcastic assertions. With his mighty black beard he was an imposing figure and, like all naval men, a passionate rider, always keeping five to six horses in camp as well. An equally characteristic figure was his faithful Chinese servant Balilla, who cooked us many a genuine Italian risotto. A solemn pair occupied the last compartment: Norway and Sweden, peacefully united, while the countries in question in Europe were already at each other’s throats. Captain Edlund knew better than anyone how to handle the Russians. Calm, composed, accustomed from his homeland to strong alcohol, speaking Russian fluently like his mother-

[Illustration: In front of the Mukden railway station.] {p. 142} tongue, he possessed the absolute trust of the Russian officers. Edlund always knew everything several days earlier than the other attachés, with whom, moreover, he always most willingly shared what he had learned.

The carriages resembled, especially at times preceding the opening of major operations, an intelligence bureau in which confidential news was exchanged from compartment to compartment. Edlund’s roommate was the Norwegian Captain Nyquist, the source for the reports on Mishchenko’s raid, which he had taken part in.

While the mood in this carriage was always a cheerful, exuberant one—the mere fact that Camperio lived here would have been sufficient for that—Carriage No. 203 maintained the seriousness prescribed for it by the dignity of its residents—all heads of missions.

In the first double compartment was Lieutenant Colonel of the General Staff Aleksandr Bazarov, whom a fortunate choice had placed in the difficult post of “Attaché to the Attachés,” an office held before him, with less success, by Cavalry Captain Count Ignatiev. Bazarov personified everything in the way of charm, tact, and culture that can be united in a man; his honest, straightforward manner and his German, tinged with a Swabian accent, betrayed the former Stuttgart university student.

This officer’s task was to serve as the liaison between the military attachés on the one hand and the Army High Command in Sayatun as well as Colonel Pestich’s censorship office on the other.

In addition, he was the military superior of all the enlisted personnel assigned to the attachés. Alongside these time-consuming duties, he was entrusted by the Army High Command from time to time with drafting operational studies. The Army High Command was in the habit of issuing identical operational problems to senior General Staff officers to solve, which, similar to our proctored exams, were to be worked out without consulting one another. Comparing the submitted papers at the Army High Command was apparently intended to serve as an aid in planning its own operational measures.

{p. 143}

Next to Bazarov lived the Chilean Major Schönmayer, likewise of German descent. Rarely have I seen anyone master the most diverse languages with such fluency as he did. Artillery Major Cheminon—a Frenchman—was rarely seen in the compartment; he was constantly with the 1st Siberian Corps. By contrast, an all the more faithful resident of the carriage was General Silvestre, head of the French mission, who, together with his adjutant, Captain Boucé, occupied half a compartment each. General Silvestre had been military attaché in Berlin for many years; Captain Boucé had been sent by his government to Graz for an extended period some years earlier to learn the German language. Both, general and adjutant alike, seem to have concerned themselves little with Germanic studies during their stay in German lands—or at least did not wish to give it away. It was remarkable that the French in particular voiced their disapproval of the operational blunders of their great ally quite uninhibitedly. After having been attached for a considerable time to the 17th Army Corps, where there was relatively little to see, General Silvestre had already requested his recall to France the previous year, but it had not been granted. From that time on, he harbored a grievance and declared the Russian position in Manchuria to be untenable.

The Argentine Lieutenant Colonel Rostagno felt somewhat uncomfortable. His appearance, his temperament, and his longing for the warm south betrayed the southern Spaniard, who could not feel at home in this cold, bleak monotony. Argentina had sold ships to Japan during the war, and he was made to feel this at the 6th Army Corps, where he stayed for some time—at least so he thought. Since he did not know Russian—which could hardly be expected of an Argentine anyway—his situation out with the troops was difficult. He was certainly an excellent man, gifted with the keen perception of a well-traveled observer, and had already achieved significant accomplishments in the military field, such as in the organization of the army in Bolivia.

Lieutenant Colonel von Lauenstein, head of the German mission, was at that time at the headquarters of the First Army in {p. 144} Huangshan, as were Lieutenant Colonel von Csicserics and the doyen of the military attachés, the British General Gerard. How painful it was for me to learn later that General Gerard, who despite his age had endured all the privations of war with true English phlegm, succumbed on the journey home in Irkutsk to the illness that had long been gnawing at him. I would call him the “Englishman without fear and without reproach,” so completely did he seem to unite all the good qualities of a soldier grown gray in colonial service with those proud and yet engaging manners that are peculiar only to the noblest sons of Albion.

[Illustration: Chinese jugglers in front of the attachés’ carriage.]

No less agreeable was the British Major Mockler, who had come from India to Mukden during the campaign.

The Romanian military attaché and the Austro-Hungarian Captain Count Szeptycki had preferred from the beginning to attach themselves entirely to a headquarters and forgo accommodation in the already overcrowded carriages. The former was with the 3rd East Siberian Division; the latter was for some time with General Bilderling, but otherwise the loyal companion of General Rennenkampf, at whose headquarters he remained until the conclusion of peace.

The last compartment in carriage No. 203 was occupied by Staff Captain Kachalov. To him the physical well-being of the attachés {p. 145} was entrusted, and he discharged this duty in the most punctual manner. Kachalov, too, possessed those engaging manners of address that distinguish the educated Russian officer so favorably. Later, two more officers were assigned to the attachés—a measure of care that was quite superfluous.

Now that we have become acquainted with all the gentlemen who at that time represented their respective military powers in the theatre of war, let us take a look at their daily routine. It should be briefly mentioned that the two Spanish attachés had already departed by then, as had two of the American attachés. For of the latter—much to the annoyance of Germany, which had sent only three representatives—four had appeared at the beginning of the campaign. A Danish cavalry captain was also still expected—to the horror of Baron Tettau, who saw in this an intimation that the campaign would drag on even longer.

The foreign officers had already become Russianised to such an extent that rising was generally begun very late. Since the waiters in the dining car also did not begin laying the tables until around 8 a.m., any premature diligence would have been entirely out of place. Breakfast consisted of tea or coffee with butter and eggs, all exquisite and good, only the Caucasian waiters were usually still unwashed at this hour. The first glance of every morning guest upon entering was directed at the thermometer, which was maliciously graduated in Fahrenheit, so that the day began at once with a difficult conversion to Réaumur. My skill at mental arithmetic aroused great admiration—until it was discovered that in the second compartment of the dining car, behind a window curtain, hung a Réaumur thermometer that spared me all the division and addition.

During breakfast, the *Mandzhurskiy vestnik* [*Manchurian Herald*] was read, a kind of bulletin on the military events of the preceding day. It reported entirely insignificant matters, and even these were rarely true. The morning was devoted to drafting reports, riding out to the nearest corps, and the like; at half past twelve everyone gathered again for lunch. Particularly plentiful and appetizing {p. 146} were the *zakuskis* served on the first two tables on either side of the entrance door. Even the most pampered palate found satisfaction here. The cuisine was excellent and offered ample variety. How pleasant it was to chat here at the well-appointed table over a bottle of red Crimean wine, which was served in abundance, while outside the windows the most colourful military life surged to and fro across the large station square. While we comfortably sipped our coffee over a green Manila cigar, long columns of wounded moved past outside toward the waiting hospital train, ammunition columns rattled past the dining car, then again a detachment of newly arrived reservists stacked their arms in pyramids right in front of our train and began cooking their rations. Regularly toward the end of luncheon, Chinese jugglers appeared before the carriage. A monkey had to ride on a goat, which seemed to provide the greatest amusement to the simple minds of the soldiers standing around.

[Illustration: Major Macomb taking a photograph.]

At times, the cheerful flow of talk would fall silent, and everyone looked somberly at the sorrowful procession passing slowly by the windows—a funeral, simple and plain, as befits a soldier in wartime.

It would be difficult to say which language deserved pride of place in this Tower of Babel; the French, English, and German groups were numerically roughly equally represented. French was familiar to all the foreign officers, German to almost all, while there were many who did not understand English. Apart from Camperio, only the two South Americans spoke Italian. Flawless Russian was spoken only by the representatives of Austria-Hungary, Sweden and Norway, and Captain Boucé, yet even among the rest there were only few who did not at least understand the language sufficiently.

{p. 147}

[Illustration: In the dining car. In the foreground Generals Gerard and Silvestre.]

The art of photography counted many adherents among the attachés. Baron Tettau, Macomb, Rostagno, Follenfant, Camperio, and above all Captain Boucé, were inseparable from their Kodaks—rewarding subjects presented themselves at every turn, though amidst such abundance many a thing worthy of a photograph was passed over in order to save one's plates for something even better. And when that something better did come—during the Battle of Mukden—it happened to many of them as it did to me: plates or films developed at night and still wet had to be abandoned during a sudden early morning departure.

On that evening, when I returned from Huangshan, all the naval attachés from Vladivostok had unexpectedly arrived in Mukden. What did the naval officers want here in the Hunhe plain? At first, the purpose of their arrival was shrouded in deep secrecy; they were to be received by Kuropatkin the following day. Soon we learned the reason for their presence. One among them, who wore a handsome blond pointed beard, had courted the favour of a lady in Vladivostok—and, so it was said, with success. But this lady was the mistress of the fortress commandant or—I do not recall exactly—of his chief of staff. Now jealousy is a wicked passion, to cure which the fortress commandant found it most expedient to send all the naval attachés home! By order of Kuropatkin, they returned to Vladivostok after a few days.

At dinner, more guests were usually gathered than at midday, as now one, now another attaché had brought along his friends from the positions to this lavish table. Cheerful though the mood was, people rarely stayed together past 9 o'clock. Then a {p. 148} little chat was held in the corridors of the carriages and good night was wished.

Many a man would then take out the letters from home in his quiet retreat, which he had only hurried through during the day, to reread them now word for word in peace and with the quiet joy of remembrance.

[Illustration: Temple near the Imperial Tomb.]

{p. [155]}

## Visit to the Imperial Tombs.

Captain Edlund's horse had already been sold. Binder-Kriegelstein had forestalled me and purchased the bay for a relatively high sum. Binder seemed to have been lavishly endowed by his newspapers, for money was no object to him. For me, only Edlund's Chinese saddle remained, in which, incidentally, it was far more comfortable to sit

[Illustration: A cart on the road]

than in the thickly padded Cossack saddles. There was now also a small Mongolian grey for sale, one of those horses that had been assigned to the Spanish military attachés. This little grey was reputed to have the habit of refusing to be mounted and of bucking. Nobody wanted to ride him; thus the balky animal enjoyed a tranquil life, having grown round and fat, while his four-legged comrades {p. 150} were ridden ragged daily at a brisk trot on the hard roads.

The malice of this Mongol horse was not so severe. His refusal to let anyone mount was more a fear of men, who had certainly treated him roughly, than genuine malice, just as his bucking was merely harmless playfulness, out of joy at finally being allowed out of the stable—if one could call the airy reed-mat structure in which the attachés' horses were quartered by such a name.

An opportunity for a trial ride soon presented itself. Lieutenant Colonel Rostagno and Dr. Follenfant invited me to accompany them on a pleasure ride to the Imperial Tombs. We took advantage of the warm midday hours to ride northwards out of Mukden along the railway; then the railway was left behind to the right, and we headed across the fields, past several tombs, toward the dark grey grove whose tall treetops beckoned pleasantly from the monotonous brown of the plain.

A ride around Mukden gave one an idea of the requirements of an army in the field. Reserve installations were established around the city: field hospitals, field bakeries, ammunition depots, a large artillery ordnance depot where damaged guns and other weapons were repaired, a balloon detachment, and various other depots for reserve personnel and horses.

After an hour's ride, the path led through a copse of low trees, from whose green bushes cracked pillars and shattered animal statues peered out. The first temple we came to lay outside the high perimeter wall that encloses the sacred grove. On this one—whether it also contained a tomb, we did not know—the ravages of time had already done considerable destructive work. The massive stone slabs on the ground were cracked, the arch that had once joined two splendid pillars into a portal had collapsed; only the broad marble staircase, which leads up through a second portal to an open terrace flanked by resting stone dragons, is still well preserved. In the background of the terrace rises a high wall upon which images of animals and deities stand out in relief in {p. 151} vibrant colours. The colonnades that enclose the terrace on both sides have fallen into ruin, and the little gods with benign and malevolent faces sitting here are exposed defenceless to the rain and the scorching sun.

[Illustration: First temple courtyard; on the right, Lieutenant Colonel Rostagno and Staff Surgeon Follenfant.]

What a pity that we had no one who could have explained all these things to us. Thus we stood before the monument of a millennia-old culture; in the solemn silence in which these marble colossi have slumbered since ancient times, we sensed the sanctity with which a cult foreign to us had enveloped this place of peace, without understanding the mystical secret that seemed to conceal itself here in every crevice of the walls.

Onward we now went toward the imperial tomb proper, along a narrow bridle path between undergrowth and thorn hedges—we encountered no one.

In the middle of the thick stone wall that encloses the sacred grove in a rectangle, a moderately high gate leads into a well-tended garden whose paths are paved with white stone slabs {p. 152}. A Chinese man has hurried toward us at the gate; he does not permit leading the horses across the threshold—they must stay outside. A second temple keeper approaches and demands—how profane!—fifty kopecks admission. He will accompany us; what a pity that we do not understand him. Fortunately, Dr. Follenfant has read about this burial place of an emperor of the Manchus—the names of these rulers cannot be kept in mind anyway—and acts as our interpreter. From the garden, whose dark green evokes a somber mood, we enter the first temple courtyard; it is the largest of the three which, separated from one another by high, broad walls, lead to the emperor's burial mound. In this first courtyard, our attention is drawn to magnificent elephant statues standing along its sides and leading toward a high pagoda. On the ground floor of the pagoda rests a tortoise of yellow marble, from whose back a delicate obelisk reaches up to the ceiling of the tower—it is the tortoise of longevity, a sacred symbol that is also commonly found by the wayside.

A heavy iron gate, turning with a grinding creak on its rusted hinges, leads us through a low vaulted passage to the second courtyard; smaller than the first, this one lacks the dark green of trees; its paved floor, the high walls, and the three small temples facing us—all gleam in the grey silver shimmer of a kind of slate stone, which seems especially suited to the fine sculptural work with which the handsome edifices before us are covered from base to cornice.

The contours of the roofs here are much softer, more supple than those we usually find in Chinese architecture; the pillars supporting the cornice are more delicate, and the dragon heads yawning toward us from the plinth are rendered more artistically and with less of a grotesque grimace than usual.

Like jewel cases in a large chest, the three little temples stand here between the high, bare walls. Yet if we open the lid of such a casket, we find that the precious gems they were once built to protect are missing. Was it sacrilegious hands that had carried them off, {p. 153} or are those little heaps of yellow clay rubble covering the floor of the sanctuary their pitiful remains? The Chinese man cannot tell us; he merely points to a large, verdigris-coated cauldron, on the bottom of which lie thin brown votive candles—apparently the meagre offerings still made from time to time to the three bogeys sitting in the dark background with their hands and noses struck off. Behind the rear wall of the central temple, opposite the entrance

[Illustration: Central temple of the second courtyard.]

to the final courtyard, stands the "wall against evil spirits," such as we often find before the entrances to villages and houses. An ordinary brick wall, about five metres wide and two metres high, with Buddhist maxims. The gate to the third courtyard is barred with heavy padlocks. A Chinese man who has followed us from the entrance gate holds the key to one of them; he too demands his obol. His partner in the locking is a third temple guard, who will not draw the key {p. 154} to the second lock from his pocket until he too has received his baksheesh.

Does this extortion also belong to the primal culture of the Manchus, or did these good folk learn it only from us civilised Europeans?

The third courtyard is barely a few metres wide and unadorned; it resembles more a moat lying between the last wall and that high burial mound beneath which the great Manchu emperor rests—he with his three hundred wives, who had to follow him alive into the damp grave. At least so it is said to be written on the great stone tablet standing just opposite the courtyard gate, behind which the emperor's coffin stands, while the bones of the women, even in death, are supposedly to be found at a respectful distance from their ruler, beneath the centre of the tumulus.

A small piece of it can be seen in the left corner—and a pagoda, of which three are erected on each of the massive, broad courtyard walls, in the right corner—in that picture

[Illustration: Stone tablet by the emperor's tomb.] {p. 155} which shows the central temple of the second courtyard. Before I photographed the grave tablet with my Block-Notes, I had placed two Manchu boys, apparently sons of the temple watchmen, against the wall to embellish the picture. These rascals now clamoured, with shouts and gestures, for payment for their services as well, and would not be satisfied with the four-kopeck coins for which we had made them race. In the end their fathers arrived as well and joined in the howling of the brats. Oh, you dear Orientals, whether you are in Egypt, Turkey, Persia, or East Asia, as beggars and grubby rogues you are alike everywhere!... Before leaving, we mounted one of the courtyard walls. Convenient stairs lead up to the top, where a delightful scene presented itself to us. The sun was sinking toward the horizon, bathing with its final rays the reddish-yellow burial mound, which now seemed to glow in evening purple. The few bare little trees covering it stood out oversized against the sky, and between their wide-spreading branches, far behind across the plain, drifted small white puffs of smoke from puffing locomotives that ceaselessly brought fresh troops to the battlefield. In a wide arc, the path of the iron serpent curves around the consecrated ground so as not to disturb the sleep of the "great dragon" said to be buried deep beneath the temple here. The alignment of the railway near the imperial tombs had at the time to be determined in consultation with Chinese priests, who established with convincing certainty just how far the tail-tip of the monster, slumbering for thousands of years, extended, and how far away from here the modern steam horse, with its clatter and rumble, still had to veer so that the dragon, the emperor, and perhaps also his three hundred wives would not awaken any sooner than Buddha in his wisdom had foreordained.

The animal statues in the first courtyard cast their long, dark shadows across the white stone slabs as we prepared to depart. With heavy, dragging steps, a Russian guard moved along the courtyard walls from pagoda to pagoda. The dark silhouettes of their shaggy papakhas and pointed bayonets between the delicately curved lines of the Manchu buildings resembled a walking anachronism!...

{p. 156}

We took our leave of the temples, of the dark grove in which the cypresses were now rustling mysteriously and whose white stone paths lay upon the ground like a skeleton. That it would be a farewell forever, we scarcely imagined then.

Around Mukden there are several other imperial tombs, yet none equals in beauty and solemn serenity the one just described. When people speak of imperial tombs in general, it is this one they mean.

[Illustration: Second temple courtyard.]

{p. [163]}

[Illustration: A part of the temple quarter of Mukden.]

{p. [164]}

{p. [165]}

## War Correspondents and the Russian Censorship.

“Just introduce yourself right away to Colonel Pestich,” Major Baron Tettau told me, having looked after me in the kindest manner during the absence of our officers. “Colonel Pestich is the head of the censorship, with whom you will often have to deal, a charming gentleman who speaks German just as the two of us do.”

The censorship office was housed in building No. 14, not far from the railway station, separated from it by a large, dusty square.

Along this square, arranged in three rows, stood low, gray cottages of identical gray color and identical design.

One among them was two stories high—in this one the former Viceroy Prince Alexeyev had lived. The entire complex of houses, which also included a large, square wooden building—the post office—and several border-guard barracks now converted into hospitals, formed the Russian settlement of Mukden.

Over the dark shingled roofs gazed the “Bénédictine bottle,” as people here were wont playfully to call the dome of a lama temple standing at the entrance to the sacred quarter. Temple courtyards with evergreen dark cypresses, artfully carved gates, and latticework enclosed the consecrated dwellings of the priests there, while here the bare, uniform timber-framed walls of the Russian houses, set side by side on the brown sand like soldiers, made a bleak, dreary impression.

The house numbers were painted on the walls in large, clumsy digits, so that House No. 14 was soon found.

{p. 160}

It was a cold, snowless winter morning; from the Hunhe the wind was whistling across again, swirling dust, straw, and other refuse through the wide lanes of the settlement.

[Illustration: Pagoda, called the Bénédictine bottle.]

Tied to the doorknob of the cottage was a small, shaggy nag from which a tall foreigner—probably a correspondent—had just dismounted and entered the house. For one recognizes a non-Russian from afar as such, especially if he is an Englishman or an American. That the censorship office was not very spacious was already betrayed by the dark, narrow entryway, from which, groping forward at random, one entered a clerk’s room in which stood a huge lithographic press. The next little room was designated for the officers assigned to the colonel. The senior among them was Stabsrittmeister Baron van der Höven; by his side was Lieutenant Fehrmann, both of German origin. “The colonel has already been notified of your arrival; I will announce you to him,” said Fehrmann, offering me in the meantime his vacated chair.

“So, a new correspondent!” Baron Höven laughed after a friendly greeting had been exchanged. “Hopefully you know nothing of military matters and don’t speak Russian either—those are the ones we like best, just like that fellow there,” whereupon he tapped the gentleman who had entered before me familiarly on the shoulder, introducing him to me as the correspondent of the Reuters Office. I was wearing civilian clothes at the time, and Baron van der Höven was somewhat astonished when Lieutenant Fehrmann, with the words, {p. 161} “The colonel requests the Rittmeister’s presence,” invited me to follow him.

Colonel Pestich received me exceedingly kindly. He showed me the telegram that, at the instigation of our ambassador, had arrived at the army high command about a month earlier. “*You have only this support to thank*,” remarked the colonel, pointing to the telegram, “*that you are here, for for some time past*

[Illustration: On the left Colonel Kovanko, chief of the aeronautical department, on the right Colonel Pestich, chief of the censorship.]

*all requests from foreign officers who wished to proceed to the theater of war have been rejected without exception.*”

My papers were then checked; I received a red armband and several passes entitling me to enter Mukden and to take photographs outside the perimeter of the fortifications, the railway, the barracks, and bridges.

Finally, Lieutenant Fehrmann handed me a voluminous lithographed sheet—it contained the regulations, {p. 162} or more accurately, the prohibitions, by which correspondents and other foreigners were to govern their conduct with the army.

“As you see, a great deal is forbidden with us—actually everything, strictly speaking,” the colonel remarked jokingly. “You, however, as a soldier, Captain, will surely know even without these regulations what is permitted and what is inadmissible in war. I am convinced that we will remain good friends—and now we have finished with official business. Now give me the pleasure of having breakfast with me.” That was indeed a most collegial reception, which could not have been better back home!

Lieutenant Colonel Bazarov of the General Staff had also come over for breakfast, with reports and private letters from the attachés, whose correspondence was likewise subject to censorship. “And where are your war correspondents, Colonel?” “Oh, please don’t get me started on the correspondents!” Colonel Pestics sighed dolefully. “Right now they are all sitting here in Mukden, and that is just the way I like it; as soon as one of them steps outside, there is invariably trouble. The earful I got again today from the Army High Command on account of Little—you know about it, don’t you, Lieutenant Colonel Bazarov?” “Certainly, Colonel—though you should have taken a firmer line back then, in my opinion.” “It simply will not do for a correspondent who is granted permission to accompany General Mishchenko on his raid to slip off in the dark to the Japanese in Yingkou just so he can dispatch his cables.” “You gentlemen must make allowances for the irresistible urge that draws the newspaperman even through enemy lines to the telegraph office—that I can well understand; but how did this gentleman get back into the Russian camp—why did he not rather stay with the Japanese?” “Why not with the Japanese?” all the censors together with their chief now interrupted me at once. “Why not with the Japanese? Because nowhere in the whole world do correspondents have it so good as with us! With the Japanese they are kept neatly tucked away—but with us—I ask you, look {p. 163} at our two Englishmen, one of whom you met today; we ourselves, that is Baron van der Höven, who is assigned to the English press, dictates all their telegrams to them, for the fellows cannot speak even a single word to anyone, and when they ride out of Mukden, they lose their way at the next fork in the road; it is we ourselves—the gentlemen of the censorship—who dictate everything to them; could one be any kinder? Yes, such

[Illustration: Portals in front of the Mukden Lama Temple.]

gentlemen we like,” Staff Captain Höven laughed again, refilling the empty glasses.

“To your health, Captain, and forgive me that today, in your civilian clothes, I too took you for a military illiterate; you are one of the few officers who come here on their own initiative to look into conditions among us; mostly they are people who in their previous occupations *have never even come into contact with the military profession*, and now they want to see, understand, and properly judge {p. 164} things in wartime—these are the sources from which the civilized world draws its news! It would never occur to us soldiers to pass judgment on things we have never learned, that we do not understand; yet here come correspondents from all continents, some of whom have never even sat on a horse, and want to correct commanders in the field. *The Germans alone*, one must give them credit, their newspapers have sent only militarily competent people here. There was Colonel Gaedke and Lieutenant von Schwarz, and at present there is still Lieutenant von Ulrich, who is taking part in the entire campaign with General Orlov . . .” “Engaged by General Orlov, so to speak, to immortalize his deeds for posterity,” one of the Russian officers put in rather maliciously. “Binder-Kriegelstein is an officer too.” “Certainly, he even attended the war academy with us,” I added, “and then continued his service in the German army.”

“How is it, though, that apart from the official French military attachés, there are no other French officers in the Russian camp? Given the well-known sympathies of your ally, I would have thought that several French officers would surely be serving in your ranks—much like your coreligionists, the brave Montenegrins? One fine day, a small band of the latter, armed to the teeth, arrived in Mukden to support the Russian-Manchurian army.” — “We did have one French officer,” the colonel explained to me. “After much pleading, he was granted permission to take part in the raid on Yingkou in the ranks of a dragoon regiment. We were all very fond of him, and he was burning with the desire to ride into battle. To take part in a great cavalry engagement was his fondest wish. But the poor fellow had to die without having seen anything of the war; on the first day of the advance, a few shots were fired at the squadron with which the Frenchman was riding—they were probably honghuzi. The first and only man to fall on that occasion was the French officer. You can imagine that this incident did little to encourage our gentlemen war correspondents to risk their expensive hides on similar occasions.” “Why do you say expensive?” “Oh! These people, {p. 165} especially the English and Americans, receive magnificent pay—often purely for being here. At present we have two who would like nothing better than for us to expel them from here. If they left here voluntarily, their newspaper would no longer be obliged to pay them; it would be quite another matter if they were deported *ex officio*, just as we did recently with an Italian correspondent. That gentleman had attempted to forward his dispatches via the Chinese post without first submitting them to censorship.” “Must all letters, even private ones, be submitted to censorship?” “Everything that goes abroad; no Russian post office within the army’s area of operations will accept a letter addressed abroad that does not bear the black censorship stamp; the Chinese post, which runs from here via Xinmintun to Peking and Shanghai, is also bound by this regulation.” “And letters to Russia?” “Those do not need a censorship stamp.” “Then the entire complicated censorship apparatus is actually superfluous,” I ventured to object; “one only needs to have an acquaintance in Russia to forward the letters!” “As far as censorship is concerned, we don't care all that much about letters anyway,” the colonel explained. “By the time they reach Europe, they have long since been overtaken by events; the only things that matter to us are the telegrams. But the Italian also tried to make his way to Peking via Xinmintun, whereupon he was picked up by our border guard patrols.” “Colonel, I must confess frankly that I have that same plan! Xinmintun is a solid day’s journey from here by carriage, and from Xinmintun to Peking is 36 hours by train—to be so close to Peking and not be allowed to see it?” “No, Rittmeister, abandon that idea; there is only one way back, and that is the one by which you came. Even the American military attachés who traveled home from here were not permitted to take that route, for it leads not only to China—but also to Japan!” “Then, Colonel, I drink to the health of the Russian army and to a very swift and victorious advance on Port Arthur—by that route you will surely let me cross over into China?” “By that one, yes! And you {p. 166} will see that we shall soon take that southward route, for God will help us. And now to work, gentlemen. Bring me the latest report from *Novoye Vremya* [*New Times*], Lieutenant Fehrmann—you see, that is a good newspaper; it has honest correspondents who always write nothing but the truth!”

[Illustration: Staff Rittmeister Baron van der Höven as I met him on his return journey from the Gobi Desert.]

Good old colonel, if only you knew that across all of Europe, *Novoye Vremya* is known as nothing other than the Russian *rag of lies*!

Staff Rittmeister Baron van der Höven was a very well-educated man. He had been educated more for diplomatic than for military service, had mastered several languages, including Chinese, and had already lived in {p. 167} East Asia for several years beforehand. I was pleased that he knew Budapest, and in the course of our conversation we even discovered a mutual friend, the Hungarian explorer Count Jenő Zichy, whose name I was later to hear mentioned often in the Caucasus as well. With Höven, one could speak quite candidly about conditions in Russia. The next day he took me to Mukden, where we first paid a visit to the two American correspondents. Together with the American military attachés who had remained with the army, they occupied a handsome, spacious *fangzi*, had a good cook, and smoked excellent long cigars wrapped in tin foil. They could hardly have seen much here in Mukden, but that was not the point at all. The main thing was writing and telegraphing. But whoever was out at the front line, no matter how much he saw there, could never send timely reports to his newspaper. On the one hand, writing there was exceedingly difficult in the first place, for frozen fingers are of little use for hammering out feature articles; on the other hand, there was rarely a post office or telegraph station there, and these were not permitted to transmit correspondents' dispatches any further than back to the censors in Mukden. For newspapers that set greater store by the speed rather than the reliability of their telegrams, it was therefore of little value to have their reporters somewhere on the front line, which after all would not always have enhanced the military value of those reports either.

Even during major manoeuvres, and all the more so in war, it is difficult to gain an overview of tactical situations. The enemy's long-range fire makes it impossible for the spectator to approach the firing line, so that only in quite exceptional cases, and only in the mountains, is it possible to observe troops in combat in relative safety. If it is already difficult to *see* anything, *understanding* is all the harder! Even an educated officer requires much practice to grasp quickly and correctly what he sees in the field. Today we mostly have “empty battlefields,” in which the combatants lie well-concealed behind cover and smokeless powder hardly betrays their presence. For the most part, the location of the foremost lines can only be guessed from the position of the artillery and the advance {p. 168} of the reserves, and among these, distinguishing friendly from hostile troops at greater distances is once again difficult. It requires a thorough knowledge of army organisation to deduce the strength and branch of service of the troops at the front from the markings and types of the rearward ammunition wagons, medical facilities, and train transport wagons. How, then, is the layman, amid the overwhelming spectacle of battle and under the deafening impression of roaring guns and howling bullets, to find his bearings or even be capable of critically judging the course of the engagement? Such an evaluation will never be possible *immediately* at all, but only after mature reflection and careful comparison of the facts as they clarify over time. Yet our sensation-hungry reading public demands immediate news! *Yesterday's original news from East Asia!* The correspondent must report, come what may; and so he grasps at any sensational detail lying by the wayside, which the censor, being utterly inconsequential, allows to pass; back home in the editorial office, the brief dispatch is pieced out at front and back according to the taste and editorial slant of the newspaper in question, and appears the next morning in the paper as an “Original Telegram from Our Special Correspondent.” *Not the press*—but solely the impatient public was to blame for those newspaper lies served up to them at their own request by both sides during the war—for quite fanciful reports often came from the Japanese side as well! This was also roughly the view of the two Americans, who endeavoured to cater fully to the tastes and demands of their readers back home in the sense described above.

From the Americans we went on to the French writer Naudeau. He lived in that cosy temple quarter with a priest, the only place in Mukden where one could live cleanly and without being robbed. For only two days earlier, two horses had been led out of the stable during the night from Binder-Kriegelstein; horse theft had become a regular trade here, at the weekly Mukden {p. 169} horse market almost nothing but stolen nags were sold, and Binder even found one of his own there again.

Curiously enough, all those correspondents of whom I speak here had gathered here during the subsequent fighting around Mukden and, together with the two American military attachés, Staff Surgeon Howard and Engineer Captain Judson, had fallen into Japanese captivity.

The censorship officers were from time to time also entrusted with intelligence duties. Thus, prior to the Battle of Mukden, Staff Captain Baron Höven was sent to Xinminting to ascertain whether there was any Japanese advance along the Chinese railway. Naudeau accompanied him on this journey; he later attempted to return to Mukden, in doing so encountered General Bürger's Russian brigade, and with it was forced northward for two days. Naudeau was certainly witness to interesting fighting on this occasion. Binder-Kriegelstein had stayed in Mukden for only a few days and had gone in Szeptycki's company to General Rennenkampf. I met him at the end of February in Matsundan, on the eastern wing at the general's head-

[Illustration: Chinese troops of General Ma near Xinminting.] {p. 170} quarters, near which he had rented a fanza from an old Chinese woman. By Rennenkampf himself he was treated with every courtesy; the staff officers were less friendly toward him. One morning I informed him that a dispatch had arrived at headquarters during the night, according to which serious fighting with the Japanese had already begun southwest of Mukden. “Then it will probably be best if I ride into Mukden and secure my suitcases and Szeptycki’s, which we left there in a fanza,” he said. In Mukden he fell into Japanese captivity along with the other correspondents and was taken to Japan, where, while cleaning his revolver, he was fatally struck by a bullet.

Thus human life depends on blind chance. Had I not told Binder about that dispatch at the time, he might have stayed with Rennenkampf and would still be enjoying life today.

Regarding the American and European newspapers that were able to afford the luxury of their own correspondent in the theatre of war, as well as the names of the latter, there was a list on file with the censorship, a copy of which follows:
“Daily Mail” — Hands.
“Associated Press” — Mac Cormick.
“N. Herold” — M. Cullagh.
“Le Journal” — Naudeau.
“Le Temps” — Recouly.
“National Tidende” — Fr. v. Jessen.
“The Morning Post” — Behring.
“Reuter” — Lord Brook.
“Gil Blas” — Prévignaud.
“Chicago Daily News” — Little.
“Manchester Guardian” — Hamilton.
“Berliner Lokalanzeiger” — v. Schwarz and Binder.
“Havas” — George de la Salle.
“Echo de Chine”\
“Courier du Tientsin” } Howen.\
“Ostasiatischer Lloyd”

{p. 171}

“Kölnische Zeitung” — v. Ulrich.
“Le Matin” — Jean Rodes.
“Berliner Tageblatt” — Gaedke.
“Photographer” (Amer.) — Lutkewić.
“Illustrated L. News” — Preiß.

And now a few words about the Russian war correspondents. Of these there was an immense number; they brought the relatively small number of foreign correspondents up to two hundred and sixty. In terms of quality, however, with few exceptions they were quite inferior. The *Novoye Vremya* [*New Times*] had an officer who had been slightly wounded at Liaoyang and who served, so to speak, as its official correspondent; in addition to him, it had a professional journalist in the theatre of war—both very pleasant, amiable companions who were equal to their task. As for the rest who went about under the title of Russian correspondents, they could hardly have had anything to do with the writing profession before the war. I remember finding myself one morning—it was on the return journey from Gongzhuling to Harbin—sharing a compartment, to my little delight, with an individual who was bristling with dirt and weeks of unwashedness—one could hardly credit his bulbous, greasy fingers with any acquaintance with a penholder—and yet the man identified himself as a correspondent for the *Birzhevye Vedomosti* [*Stock Exchange Gazette*] (Exchange Courier), a paper whose telegrams Hungarian newspapers in particular were fond of reprinting.

From such people, of course, a considered judgment, a report reflecting the situation as a whole, was not to be expected. Mostly they hung around station restaurants, staging posts, or brothels, and whatever bad things they saw there, they reported to their paper for lack of anything better. A drunken officer, a worthless reservist who had exchanged his government-issue clothes for a bottle of spirits, a few dissolute women whom he used to visit in their regular haunts—such were the subjects he knew how to write about, and from which he drew conclusions about the whole. And in this very respect there was {p. 172} hardly anything to reproach in the army. The *soldiers were superbly fed and clothed, their appearance was excellent*, and the fact that further back along the line of communication people sometimes drank more than thirst required, and that obscene scenes took place in brothels, could hardly be a yardstick for the ethical and moral worth of the army!

Such spiteful accounts flowed far more from Russian than from foreign pens, however. For the most part, people at home wished thereby to arouse pity and spur charitable giving, without considering how severely the reputation of the Russian army was damaged by it.

[Illustration: Maimachin on the Mongolian border.]

In addition to the censorship in Mukden, there were also censorship offices in the principal towns of the staging area and in Vladivostok. That the distribution of the material to be reviewed was poorly regulated is shown by the following example: {p. 173} About a month after the Battle of Mukden, I visited Colonel Pestics, who had relocated with the censorship to Gongzhuling.

Of my acquaintances, I met only Lieutenant Fehrmann; Staff Captain Baron van der Höven had been cut off by the Japanese at Xinmintun, Count Orloff was on leave, and a fourth officer, who had been tasked with reviewing the French reports, had been obliged to return to his regiment. To my astonishment, I saw letters from the firms of Tillmann and Albert in Vladivostok, addressed to Shanghai, being opened here in Gongzhuling, even though the route to Shanghai led via Harbin, Petrovsk-Zavod, and then through the Gobi Desert via Urga and Peking. Sending the letters down to Gongzhuling (when there were censorship offices in Vladivostok, Harbin, and the Manchuria border station) dated back to the time when Port Arthur still belonged to the Russians, and it now meant that this correspondence arrived at its destination ten to twenty days later, while for the field censorship itself it was an utterly unnecessary burden.

“That is nothing at all,” said Colonel Pestics, “just look at this.” They were three picture postcards—“to the victorious commanders Oyama and Kuroki”! One from Aarau, the second from Basel, the third from Vienna (Mariahilfergürtel 29). The writers congratulated the Japanese commanders on their victories and modestly asked, each and every one of them, for an autograph at the end.

“You see,” Colonel Pestics added with a sour expression, “even that the Russian censorship has to let through.”

{p. [180]}

## The Catholic Mission. The New Year’s Festival in the Lama Temple.

In war, Sundays and holidays lose all right to a special status. Of the latter, it had only been Christmas whose pious celebration the Russians did not forget, even in threatening proximity to the enemy. Wherever Russians lived, in the mud huts, in the *fanzis*, even in the shrapnel-proof dugouts of the positions, the *yolka*, the Christmas tree, burned on Christmas Eve. The troops arriving from home had brought their comrades little spruce trunks or at least green twigs from the forests of the Urals, whose resinous fragrance wafted through the camps like a greeting from the homeland.

Later, no time remained for such celebrations, and the priest had to be glad if he could recite a short Mass for his men on Sunday morning, which among the Russians—by our standards—still lasts long enough.

The attachés attended these religious services only on special solemn occasions. The French, on the other hand, seemed to be more devout than the rest; they went every Sunday to Mass, which a French bishop celebrated in the mission church.

The way to the mission led from our railway car along the southern city wall past the Chinese cemetery, an uneven, neglected place made as repulsive as possible by heaps of rubble, scattered refuse, and carrion. Here lie the dead in free-standing stone or earthen coffins, placed alongside one another in disorder, many of them overturned by the dogs sniffing for prey that lounge here in packs. The stench of carrion was so overpowering that we left our curiosity to inspect some of the seemingly quite handsomely painted coffins {p. 175} unsatisfied. Through crooked, filthy lanes, in which *arbas* laden with sacks of flour frequently blocked our way, we went on until a halt was made before a stone perimeter wall. We entered. A broad open space, where the ruins of a tall church portal stand before us. As if seeking to warn, the mighty pillars of the wall tower toward the sky; they tell us that the ancient conqueror of the world, the Christian Church, having sent one of her children here, must still halt before this soil for a good while yet. The church had been built by the French in the nineties and fell victim to the fury of the Boxers in 1900.
