The Christian community, Europeans and converts, had withstood the onslaught of the besiegers at the outer stone wall for days, but then Chinese regular troops arrived, before whose artillery fire the Christian community fled into the interior of the church, where they were slaughtered to the last man by the Boxers storming in after them. The rubble of the breached church walls covered the bodies.

[Illustration: The Catholic mission church destroyed by the Boxers in 1900.]

{p. 176}

Today the bones of the victims, so far as they could still be found, rest in a grave against the churchyard wall; the interior of the ruin has been cleanly cleared of debris, but for divine service a bell invites the faithful, tolling from the roof of that plain little house that stands low and modest beside the tall, cracked pillars. The congregation is already gathered upon our arrival. The Mass has begun. A hymn resounds from within; it is meant to be a Christian church hymn, yet foreign, almost incomprehensible is its tune. We enter. The bishop, with the golden tiara on his head, stands at the altar; two yellow-skinned altar boys swing the censers. The space is densely packed with believers; they sing in chorus, they sing the same hymn that the missionary sings to them, and yet it sounds so completely different! A Catholic Mass hymn with Chinese rhythm! Yet that signifies nothing; the children of the mission are said to be good, upright Christians who love their faith and have embraced it out of conviction. So the missionaries told us, who lead a life of renunciation here in the service of their calling. One may think what one will about the usefulness or harmfulness of missions; one may also hold the view that missions, in their striving to make proselytes, have often caused discord and grave misfortune in the countries concerned—yet this one thing all of us who have encountered these champions of the Christian faith on their thorny path must admit: that not worldly reward, but only faith in its most sublime form can move a man to devote himself to such a life, which brings only toil and danger, but never earthly gratitude.

Here in Mukden, too, opinion about the mission was divided. Most people said, and there will certainly be much truth in it, that many a Chinese becomes a Christian merely to be protected by the mission against the avenging arm of Chinese justice.

After Mass had ended, the mission bishop delivered an address in Chinese, which he seemed to speak fluently, whereupon the faithful lined up along the walls for Confirmation, which the bishop now {p. 177} began to administer. Two Chinese mission priests accompanied him and distributed holy pictures to the children, which the little rascals came to show us with boisterous delight.

Now we took our stand before the church door—we had not yet forgotten this custom from Europe—to gaze at the pious beauties, who, as good Christians, allowed us to look into their narrow, slanted eyes far more unselfconsciously than did their heathen sisters. The Chinese woman feels a certain shyness before the European; she avoids him if possible, yet she never veils her face, nor is she so uncivil as to turn her back to us and face the wall upon meeting, as is the custom among Mohammedan women.

The colourful floral ornaments and decorative hairpins that Buddhist women as well as the female followers of Confucius wear in their hair, and the garish white powder and red rouge with which they paint their lips and cheeks, are absent in the Christian Chinese woman, who has also renounced colourful silks even for her Sunday dress. Yet for all these modesties she has been richly rewarded ever since birth. While girls loyal to the old faith and old custom mince along on artificially bound feet scarcely a span in length, keeping their balance with a stick, the Christian woman steps out freely; her foot has been spared the dreadful custom of crippling.

Girls and women, men and boys passed by us, laughing and joking. “The mission counts over four hundred converts,” the priests told us, “but it will likely be a long time before we can rebuild the church, for the jealousy of the Russian priests alone would prevent us. And if the Russians should have to leave here one day, we will be all the more at the mercy of the mandarins' arbitrary rule. Thus we can only trust in the help that comes to us from above.”

In Mukden there was much to see. It was a pity that the way into the city was so long and inconvenient. One usually took a rickshaw, a two-wheeled vehicle drawn by a Chinese, long rows of which stood before the railway station. They were able to weave their way through the throng of pedestrians, {p. 178} carts, and riders far more skilfully than was possible on horseback. The road to the western gate leads past Mongolian temples and ceremonial archways; all are shaded by large trees with wide-spreading branches that have not lost their green even during the winter. There, behind the grey walls of the temple courtyards, the lamas lead their contemplative existence. On the street is the most colourful life. Chinese two-wheeled carts, with four to five horses and donkeys harnessed partly abreast, partly in tandem, columns of *dvukolkas*, the Russian light army cart, laden with provisions of all kinds, between them

[Illustration: Fourtunka of a wealthy Manchu.]

an elegant troika now and then, officers and Chinese on horseback. My companion this time is an officer of the Russian censorship, who wants to show me the eve of tomorrow's Chinese New Year—and something of Mukden's nightlife. The wretched jog-trot of our two rickshaws finally brings us to the baked-clay city gate, before which stands a Chinese guard. To him the foreigner must present his *propusk*. All at once, a purely Chinese street reveals itself. From the curving roofs hang golden dragons, colourful birds, and various kinds of wooden monsters. In front of each house stands a tall post with carved cross-arms protruding from it, bearing gold or red {p. 179} characters—Chinese signboards. In front of the houses or in their courtyards stand small altars with incense; the shops were closed in honour of the New Year festival. The third New Year that I have witnessed in a short span of time! Everyone wears holiday attire, the dignitaries dressed in silk; black and blue seem to be the favourite colours. Right in the middle of a crossroads stands a tall wooden pagoda; one has to drive completely around it, for ropes are stretched across its gateways; they are probably too sacred for ordinary mortals to be permitted to pass beneath them. Constantly something new, constantly something strange! All the angular figures that we have seen at home on teapots, fans, and Chinese folding screens have come to life here; we know these long queues, gold-embroidered caps, silk robes, painted women with tiny feet, all these images of dragons and birds from pictures and books, and yet here everything is new to us—unfamiliar. The Chinese seem so good-natured and gentle, curious as children and always helpful towards the foreigner. How can there be so much cruelty in these good people? Whoever wishes can convince himself of it; almost daily one can attend the revolting spectacle of a penal court session with mild torture.

The rickshaw finally came to a halt in front of a mud shack above whose unpromising entrance was written “Nowa Gostinica”. Along with “Dalni Wostok”—a dive of similar quality—it was the most reputable inn. The door leads into the dining hall, which, never ventilated according to Russian custom, reveals by its very atmosphere the culinary pleasures offered for sale here. But the company that gathers here during the day is likewise hardly inviting to linger in; so we prefer to stroll around the streets until evening. First, we pay a visit to the Russo-Chinese Bank. It is housed in a Chinese building constructed of solid stone. At the counters stand Russian officers and officials depositing the savings they have made during the war. Mr Frisk, a German, receives us, as indeed most of the officials of this large institution are Germans. We met again several times later on.

{p. 180}

As chance would have it, after leaving the bank we witnessed a destructive fire. Evidently during the preparations for the great illumination in which the streets of Mukden were to blaze that evening, one of those airy little wooden houses had caught fire and was now blazing brightly toward the sky. In an instant, several hundred Chinese had gathered at the scene of the fire. Everyone was shouting and gesturing; it was a tremendous uproar, but no one was willing to lift a hand against the crackling element that was already threatening the neighbouring houses. Not a trace of a fire brigade, of a fire engine, or even of a few water buckets! Fortunately, a Russian

[Illustration]

officer pushed his way through the crowd with several soldiers, and their resolute intervention succeeded in preventing the fire from spreading any further. To me, the passivity of the otherwise so bustling Chinese in the face of the devastating fire remained an enigma.

Meanwhile, evening had come. Lanterns in the most colourful hues, painted with fierce dragons, hung from the eaves of the houses. The colourful street life, now joined by processions in the most extravagant masquerades—reminiscent of our carnival revelries—was bathed in the shifting tinted glow of Bengal fire, while rockets banged and crackled in between.

{p. 181}

From the pagodas flickered transparencies bearing the images of Chinese deities, while on the cornices wax candles flickered in coloured glasses—just like at home on similar occasions!

All this splendour did not last long. Toward nine o'clock in the evening the last lantern had gone out, and the city once again lay in that nocturnal silence peculiar to the settlements of this land. “By night the evil spirits fly over the roofs,” says the Manchu, and shuts himself into his *fansa* after sunset.

Yet of the wild, riotous life of the Russian soldiery, of which one could read so much in European newspapers, scarcely anything was to be seen.

That here and there people drank well over their thirst, and mostly strong liquor at that, cannot be denied; yet nowhere was the peace disturbed; on the contrary, the

[Illustration: New Year's Festival in the Lama Temple.] {p. 182} vodka made their heads heavy—so heavy until they sank, tired and mute, down onto the table.

We looked into one more teahouse—our Chinese rickshaw pullers even claimed to know more amusing establishments—yet I preferred the way home, which I covered running alongside the rickshaw on account of the icy cold.

The next morning, dense crowds streamed from Mukden and the surrounding villages toward the Lama Temple, which lay close to the road leading from the railway station to the western city gate. But people had also come from further afield, from Northern Manchuria and even from across Mongolia.

When I arrived with Lieutenant Fehrmann at the artfully carved wooden archway leading to the sprawling complex of the Lama Temple, a whole wagon-camp of *fourtunkas*—the light Chinese cart—had already driven up. The very exterior of these carts, their bodies covered with blue fabrics and their back panels decorated with distinctive poker-work, pointed to the high standing of their occupants.

As cold as it had been during the night, the sun now beat down just as hotly upon the sandy square, so that the Chinese, in whom I had on every occasion observed great care and affection for animals, drew sunshades over the various quadrupeds harnessed to the *fourtunkas*. How gratifying it is, even here in the Far East, among peoples of heathen faith, to find feeling and heart for the defenceless beast, whereas at home the most Christian countries of all, such as Spain and Italy, arouse the revulsion of every finer-feeling human being by the acts of animal cruelty encountered there at every turn!

This time we had the opportunity to observe the little ladies in their festive finery at our leisure. Seated at the front edge of the wagon box, their tiny feet drawn in and their hands gesturing vigorously, they sat chatting and giggling with their neighbours. Merrily they swayed their little heads from side to side, never resting, always in motion, while their narrow, slanting, yet vivacious black eyes sized us up in turn. Unfortunately, we seemed to find little favour with the beauties, for all attempts to strike up a most respectable acquaintance with them were often cut short by quite vulgar pantomimes. {p. 183} Even the timid attempt to photograph a baby that its pretty sister was carrying in her arms was thwarted by a pack of ugly old women, who, with every sign of indignation, held their bony yellow hands before the child’s face. Here we seemed to have no luck at all, and so we turned toward the great temple courtyard, where the clamorous beating of tin drums drowned out the cries of a crowd of thousands. A festival as colourful and magnificent, as bright and joyous, as only the easy-going humour of the Orient can produce. And in the midst of it

[Illustration: Buddhist idol statues.]

among these small, mischievous, nimble figures, amid the shimmering silk garments whose predominant color was dark blue here as well, stand Russian soldiers with large, shaggy papakhas, gray fur coats, and heavy felt boots, like polar bears that have strayed into southern currents.

It is a pity that I never engaged in comparative studies of religious customs, for here there was much that recalled our own Christian worship. Above all, there also seems to be a divine trinity in the religion of the lamas; the frequent occurrence of three gods, {p. 184} which always stand together in similar form in the temples, suggests as much. Processions with banners, just as with us; similarly to our bishops, the chief priest here also wears a golden tiara, and when, at the conclusion of his prayers, he turns toward the crowd, it almost seems as though he wished to bestow a blessing upon them with the sign of the cross.

Yet the entire ceremony is neither devout nor uplifting; everyone shouts, laughs, jostles, and when, during the prayer of the priests, four dancers disguised in animal masks accompany their wild leaps before the altar with the deafening din of metal cymbals, the merriment of the spectators reaches its climax. Carried along in the dense stream of people, we then proceed to another, smaller temple, from whose gate a line of young girls comes tripping out, all in blue silk, with yellow flowers in their hair and on their garments.

How gladly I would have stayed here longer to delight in this vivid scene, had I not wanted to head back out to the positions in the afternoon, where the guns had been more audible since yesterday than before.

Colonel Pestić had told me to report to the censorship office that afternoon, where the papers were to be handed over to me that were to serve as my safe-conduct to the Second Chita Cossack Regiment. That I was to see Mukden for the last time that day, I certainly would not have thought. Thus, at least, a cheerful, warm picture concluded my memories of that city, which for so long had been the destination of my desire to travel.

[Illustration: Confirmation.]

{p. [191]}

# The Battle of Mukden.

{p. [192]}

{p. [193]}

## Preliminaries to the Battle.

### The Successful Japanese Raid on Gongzhuling.

One morning, shortly before I rode away from Mukden for the last time, the entire large square in front of my railcar window down to the post office building was occupied by troops. They were battalions that had returned to Mukden from the positions. What was this supposed to mean? No one knew. At noon, as we sat at lunch, the Don Cossack Brigade crossed the square, likewise heading north. Among the Cossack regiments, those from the Don are perhaps the best. They have noble, medium-sized horses and, like the Orenburg Cossacks, carry long lances. Again, people asked why so many troops were moving north—shrugs and silence were the answer; even Captain Edlund, who usually knew everything, returned from his corps toward evening without news. It was quite remarkable how little one learned in this war of what had occurred in one's immediate vicinity. Those who possessed official reports wrapped themselves in profound silence, and the other sources, especially the reports of the *Mandzhursky vestnik* [*Manchurian Herald*] or the Harbin newspapers, were so full of lies that one no longer believed them at all.

So one lived in the midst of war and could know only what one saw with one's own eyes.

The next day, the infantry battalions and the Cossacks were dispatched to the north, and a sotnia of border guards stood in front of the station ready to entrain. Only now did one learn, slowly and vaguely, that something unpleasant had happened to the Russians in the rear near Gongzhuling and that {p. 188} measures also had to be taken for the better security of Vladivostok.

On this occasion I was able for the first time to observe that unfortunate mania of the Russian army high command, with which it endeavored during the later events as well to create conglomerates of new formations. This time it was a *composite division*—pieced together battalion by battalion from various corps—which was sent back to Nikolsk to reinforce the Vladivostok district, and a composite brigade put together company by company—the Russians called it the *Harlequin Brigade*—which went back to Gongzhuling. “This move to the north means peace,” said Lieutenant Colonel Csicserics, who had come down from Huangshan in the course of the day and likewise knew only in general terms of a Japanese success at Fanjiatun. Only in the afternoon, as we watched the border guard sotnia entraining at the Mukden station, did General Staff Colonel Dragomirov, the son of the famous general, tell us the cause of these troop movements to the rear.

“The Japanese,” he said, “did not hesitate long to respond to the raid by our General Mishchenko. He showed them the way to pass through neutral territory, and so on the night of February 12 they appeared unexpectedly before Gongzhuling, and north of it, at Fanjiatun, a

[Illustration: Border guard sotnia.] {p. 189} raided and sabred down a border guard sotnia, drove off the bridge guard, and succeeded in damaging a stone bridge pier. The Japanese were indeed driven back by reinforcements that had meanwhile arrived from Gongzhuling, but on 14 February the pursuing Russians encountered significant Japanese forces some 20 versts *northeast* of Gongzhuling—that is, *directly in the rear of the Russian operational army*—and were beaten back with severe losses, the Japanese also carrying off a gun.”

“How strong might the Japanese have been?” “*On that point*,” said Colonel Dragomirov, “we are *completely* in the dark, for the reports received on the matter contradict one another. I believe that the Japanese were not nearly so strong, and that only the commander of the Harbin district, whose ambition strives for a larger sphere of activity, is painting the matter so black so that more troops will be placed under his command. To be sure, the bridge was already repaired the following day, but, as you see, this Japanese cavalry feat has bad indirect consequences for us. Not only have we been standing here idle for months, but now, just when things are finally supposed to move forward, we must pull troops from the front and send them north; how, then, is confidence in victory ever to awaken in our men!”

The good fellows, however, seemed not to occupy themselves with such grave thoughts at all. On an open flatcar before us, between loaded carts and crates, a platoon of a sotnia had just made itself as comfortable as possible. The night promised to be cold, and for this airy journey the soldiers had nothing more than their warm fur coats. Yet already the kettle was going round, and one of them had brought out his accordion and was playing a little Cossack song, to which the others beat time with their heavy boots dangling down the sides of the flatcar. The train started up, a hearty hurrah roared through the cold air—scarcely did they know in which direction of the compass they were traveling. A blessing for you, Russian generals, that you have such good soldiers! Thus good core troops moved to the rear from the front, {p. 190} perhaps merely to indulge the vanity of a general, and to gloss over the matter, figures and events are inflated or minimized at will. But that is possible only where the ground is paved for *falsehood* by a system of unaccountability, where the *servants of a corrupt bureaucratic regime* crush the people’s striving to know the truth.

Thus it is that through this dense web of lies even the commander-in-chief cannot see what is happening behind his back, and allows himself to be led into measures that actual conditions would never have warranted.

Yet in *military history* one often hears little of such things; that is then “constructed” after the fact at the conference table—or much like here in the railway carriage, while the campaign is still under way!

### The Russian Plan to Resume the Offensive.

The news that General Rennenkampf had assumed command of the cavalry corps on the Russian right wing had an invigorating effect. That Rennenkampf would do his job better than Mishchenko was certain.

As could be gathered from the overall situation, *General Rennenkampf had the task of using his cavalry to harass the Japanese rear in the direction of Liaoyang, while Adjutant General Kuropatkin, similarly to late January, concentrated strong forces on the western wing in order to open the general offensive once again with an attack on Sandepu.* Depending on the progressing successes of the Second Army, the command of which had been taken over by General of Cavalry Baron Kaulbars following the departure of General Grippenberg, the Third Army—General Bilderling—and the First Army—General Linevich—were then to take the offensive.

Guiding idea for the attack: *Rolling up the Japanese front from the western wing.*

{p. [197]}

[Illustration: Entraining of the frontier guard sotnia.]

{p. [198]}

{p. 193}

In the past October, Kuropatkin had announced by means of an army order—which was reproduced in all the newspapers of the world—that he felt strong enough to attack the enemy—and throw him back. Such an announcement of one's own operational intentions—one might almost say in the manner of an advertisement—which, besides its inexpediency, cannot be spared the accusation of boastfulness, was certainly something entirely new in the realm of command procedure and also had its ill consequences in every direction.

This time all plans were kept strictly secret, and only the sharp reconnaissances already begun by Rennenkampf against the Japanese left flank, as well as heavy troop movements toward our own right wing, pointed to the eventual resumption of the Russian offensive.

On the Russian side, as we learned from a reliable source, it would have been preferred had the Japanese attacked, and this waiting also seemed to correspond to such an expectation. "If only one could put it into the Japanese' heads to attack us now," Lieutenant Colonel Bazarov had also said once on returning home from army headquarters.

Despite all the secrecy, however, it was in the air that we stood at the threshold of great events. A great battle makes itself felt in the atmosphere like an approaching thunderstorm, only we did not know from which quarter the first lightning bolt would strike. Where should I go now in order to be an eyewitness to the expected great events? An individual observer must not imagine that in a great battle he will gain any kind of overview of the whole—for this, even the vast apparatus of the army commander, equipped with all the resources of the reporting service and modern technology, is not always sufficient.

The threads connecting the commander with his troops and keeping him informed of their combat situation converge farther behind the battle line the wider that line is. Back there, the battlefield spectator would hear nothing but the roar of the guns, see nothing but the coming and going of ammunition columns and transport of the wounded, the deployment of army reserves, the establishment of dressing stations {p. 194} and the like. The psychology of war and the sight of combat remain unknown to him back there.

Yet even he who ventures farther forward should avoid the plain! Here the eerie whistling of bullets and the crash of bursting shells will force him to turn back before he has even caught sight of the reserves of the engaged troops. Whoever has the opportunity should try to take to the hills. The observer is hardly bulletproof there either, but the opportunity is offered to follow the course of an action under cover behind a knoll, in a gully, or elsewhere.

On the plain, moreover, a thaw was in sight, during which one could sink into the soft loam of the fields. Thus I preferred to return first to Huangshan to await the further development of events there.

In Huangshan things had become lively toward the end of February; in place of the customary noon shots, the guns boomed over into our fanza from morning until evening. During the night, rifle fire could occasionally be heard from the nearest positions. Three Japanese columns, it was said, were marching against the left wing and had attacked the Eastern Group of General Alekseyev. The news issued every morning by the General Staff section on a lithographed sheet was quite meager; mostly it ran: village A or sopka B was fought over throughout the night and the following day, changing hands repeatedly. The last hand into which it passed was usually that of the Japanese. Several times I saw General *Linevich*.

A good, worthy old gentleman in whom the soldiers had confidence. "The Japanese, they are wild Asiatics," he had once said after dinner; "we must take them by the scruff of the neck and show them civilization!" "Well, hardly civilization; they already have that!" one of his generals remarked modestly. "Fine; power, then, is what we must show them—our power!"

{p. [201]}

## Fighting with the Second Siberian Corps.

### Kandolisan.

On February 20, the fire on the positions was particularly heavy; it lasted through the entire night. The Chinese, too, began sending their women to the rear in whole cartloads. That was always a sure sign that the Japanese were planning an attack.

The next day, on a cold, bright morning, my patron, who had helped me so much during these often difficult days, escorted me to the military road leading to Shandalinsan to the Third Siberian Corps; this was the immediate destination, from which I was to proceed further to the extreme left flank to a Cossack regiment. “God keep you and take care of yourself—come back to Mukden again soon; in a fortnight or so, I might be there too!”—and then I rode on alone into the sunny day, into the mountains whose northern slopes shimmered in white snow, while the southern exposures, thanks to the warm midday sun, displayed the monotonous brown that gives the entire country its dull coloration. From the Shahe valley, the cannons rumbled; beside me hummed and chimed the telegraph wire, the surest signpost to the next headquarters.

My destination lay far away, far over on the army’s extreme left wing. I was to report to the commander of the *Second Chita Cossack Regiment*. As to where it currently was, I would learn more precise details only at General Rennenkampf’s headquarters.

I felt so easy and free; at last I could go wherever I wanted, bounded by neither time nor space; as far as the rations in my saddlebags and the {p. 196} Japanese bullets permitted, the way was open to me in every direction.

As for the rations, by the way, that was not to be taken so literally. In this war, the Russian officer had to live quite frugally, and often the barest necessities were lacking; but what he had, he was always glad to share with strangers. Without this *hospitality, which was nowhere lacking*, it would also have been impossible to make any headway. Yet just as courteous as the Russian is as a private individual, just as much vexation and difficulty arise the moment one has dealings with the cumbersome administrative apparatus, sunk in perpetual disorder and ignorance. Toward this “sloppiness,” the Russian displays a patience bordering on lethargy. The officer takes it for granted that the commissariat does not bother in the least with procuring his subsistence, and he pays the Chinese heavy sums for the adulterated food and drink brought in via Shanghai–Xinmin. There are no Russian merchants here; they are too indolent to pick up the money lying, so to speak, in the street—though perhaps the railway may also be to blame, which, it is said, carries no private freight from Russia, or at the very least assumes no guarantee for it.

The road was deserted and lonely, so much so that I thought I had lost my way. At last, I met several horsemen.

“Am I on the right road to Shandalinsan?” I asked them—pointing out the route I had taken on my map.

“Just don’t ride by the map, sir!” the soldiers said almost simultaneously—“put that stuff in your pocket, or you’ll certainly get lost!”

Well, the Russian four-verst map hardly deserved such mistrust—rather, perhaps, those who had once led these good fellows “by the map”!

The road I was riding was, in fact, the right one; it ran a few kilometers north of the positions of the Second Siberian Corps, above whose lines little silver clouds {p. 197} of bursting shrapnel hovered high in the air, while massive geysers of churned-up earth, standing out sharply against the blue sky, betrayed the effect of the shimose.

From time to time I halted to listen to the rumble of the artillery and the crackle of rifle fire. Over there things seemed to be getting serious—yet what was stopping me from going over to have a look? Was I not at last—at long last free—could I not go adventuring wherever I pleased?

As if to assure myself fully of my freedom of movement, I turned off immediately cross-country to the south, toward the heights of the Second Corps.

Sound and distance are sometimes deceptive. After nearly an hour’s ride, I realized that the Russian positions lay yet another ridge further to the south.

The sun stood high at noon; the villages through which I rode were devastated and deserted, with no one to be seen far and wide, so that I was glad when I spotted a group of horsemen and headed toward them.

It was an artillery colonel, a veterinary surgeon, and a few soldiers. With the amiability of Russian officers that has already been highlighted so often, the colonel invited me to accompany him—he was on an inspection ride. Whatever he might be inspecting, I thought to myself, here too superiors will surely be welcomed with a good meal; and so I rode along.

In a quiet valley, well away from shrapnel and shimose, a mortar ammunition park led an idyllic existence. With the good artillerymen, anyone is in good hands; and so the temptation was great when they suggested I abandon my further wartime travels in order to listen here, in peace and quiet, to the rattle of musketry and other battle clamor drifting over from the redoubts, all over a well-spread table. During the day, from a not too distant sopka, one could comfortably watch one’s own battery and a Japanese one duel back and forth. Toward evening, the wounded were carried past our fanza to the division hospital; they told us what had occurred in the positions during the day.

During the afternoon hours and the previous night, the Japanese had attacked the Russian positions south of Kandolisan {p. 198} at Wanyepuzi and Sunupudza, but had been repulsed with heavy losses. Many of the wounded who were led past us carried, in addition to their own, a Japanese bayonet that they had taken from a fallen opponent.

Yet the Russians, too, had attempted to take the offensive that night, again of course with only a small fraction of their strong forces. Thus, a small but daring band—the scout detachment of the First Siberian Reserve Division—had crossed the Shahe after nightfall,

[Illustration: Small Buddhist temple.]

taken by surprise the Japanese outposts, who on this bitterly cold night did not anticipate an attack by the Russians, and after a brief bayonet fight reached Nanshanpu—a village on the southern bank of the Shahe—entrenching itself in its northwestern half. At the same time, as mentioned earlier, the Japanese attack against the Russian positions had been repulsed, so that our scout detachment was caught between the Japanese forces falling back across the Shahe and those still holding Nanshanpu. Two of its men had succeeded in reaching their own positions {p. 199} via Sunupudza and reporting on the desperate situation of their cut-off detachment.

How much blood had flowed once again, how many had stood in mortal terror or were perhaps even now facing the strangler Death, who had already mercilessly drawn his circle around the small band on the other bank of the river, while we, barely an hour’s march away, enjoyed the bright sunshine. Heavy rapid fire from the mountains had indeed awakened us around one in the morning—yet the kang on which we slept was warmly heated, we were tired as well, so we turned over onto the other ear and went on sleeping until the sun stood high in the sky.

At the mortar ammunition park, besides the commander, three reserve officers and the veterinarian we already know were stationed. He was a good photographer and had brought along excellent large cameras.

Anything one might call duty I did not observe at this ammunition park, yet everything proceeded in the best of order. Empty ammunition wagons returned, full ones went up into the mountains, then an ammunition column from the army park arrived, everything received and directed by the long-bearded Ivan Ivanovich, the ordnance sergeant. Only once did an official discord ripple through this peaceful atmosphere. The cow, which was carried on the strength of the draft horses, had failed to yield the morning milk needed for our breakfast, and for some reason not entirely clear to me, Ivan Ivanovich was supposed to be held responsible for this. On that occasion a sharp reprimand was administered to him by his commander from bed. Whether it bore fruit I do not know, for the next morning already found me up with the mortars. In the evening, the officers from the battery had arrived; one of them had a magnificent voice. There was singing, guitar playing, and throats did not remain dry either. The officers had come to say goodbye, for the Second East Siberian Mortar Battery was to go into action tomorrow for the first time. For three months they had lain idle in the zemlyankas near Kandolisan, until at last, tired of idly looking on, they had asked General Zasulich, commander of the Second Siberian Corps, {p. 200} for permission to lead the battery into the positions from which their high-angle fire was to bring under bombardment the part of the elongated village of Nanshanpu occupied by the Japanese.

The joy at facing the enemy the next day was sincere and unconstrained—it grew ever wilder the closer morning approached.

Soon it was decided that I would go with them.

### With the Second East Siberian Mortar Battery.

At daybreak we left Xiaoyangxintun—a name hitherto completely unknown in military history—and walked to the battery, where the horses were just being harnessed. “Another glass of vodka, comrade?” the captain invited me into his dugout. “No vodka for me, thank you, but a glass of tea would be most gratefully accepted.” It was a small, narrow space, partly dug into the earth, its inner walls neatly scraped down and hung with color prints and photographs. In one corner hung a guitar with a blue silk ribbon, between whose dainty bows the picture of a girl was pinned. In the other corner, an icon of the Mother of God with a little red lamp before it.

My head had grown heavy from the clamorous night. The senior lieutenant showed me stereoscopic photographs he had taken while serving with a battery to which he had previously belonged. They showed the dead laid out in rows, or men mutilated by shell hits, whose contorted faces seemed to come alive beneath the stereoscope lenses.

“Before going into action, one shouldn’t look at these things!” the captain said, taking the pictures away from us. “It gives you a hollow feeling rising up from the stomach!” He had hit upon the right words: a hollow feeling rising up from the stomach—how often had I felt this at the sight of the wounded, without being able to define the unease I experienced.

A non-commissioned officer came to report that the commander of the {p. 201} battery—I did not know him yet—had ridden ahead, and that the captain should bring up the battery.

An hour later the mortars were in position to set Nanshanpu, on the opposite bank of the Shahe, on fire. At first the affair seemed like target practice; the ground was frozen bone-hard, so that the impact of the high-explosive shells was difficult to detect; on the enemy’s side nothing stirred. Later on, however, they too began to fire from across the river, and that was uncomfortable. Anyone who could press himself against an earthen parapet had little to fear, and the ear soon grew accustomed to the whistling and whining in the air—not so, however, the officers and the crews serving the guns. The infantry officer and the cavalryman are swept forward in the tumult of battle—forwards or backwards, as circumstances dictate; they have no time to reflect, often barely to recognize the danger. Not so the artillery officer. He can determine to the minute when the enemy projectile will strike his battery; he sees the Shimose shells exploding closer and closer in front of his guns, and all the while he must calmly observe their effect, determine sight corrections, and keep the enemy target in view. And how much more devastating is the effect of a hit in the battery than in the ranks of the other arms! How much more disheartening are the severe, almost invariably fatal wounds caused by shell splinters compared to the clean perforations of infantry bullets. I believe *that in modern combat, of all officers, those of the artillery require the greatest moral fortitude* to be equal to their arduous duties under enemy fire.

The battery was fortunate; all the shells the Japanese sent flew many meters above our heads, throwing up the dirt far to the rear.

Around midday the men of the first echelon hauled up ammunition, but also reported that near the horses of two ammunition wagons, which stood unharnessed further to the rear with their drivers, a shell had struck, injuring two of the animals and killing one man. This was {p. 202} the brother of a gunner serving here in the battery; they brought him a prayer book and a crumpled letter they had found in the fallen man’s pocket.

Again a shell whirred over our heads; the heavy shells sing quite differently from their lighter sisters, the slender shrapnels. This time the men crossed themselves, for it had passed just over their heads.

The telephone connecting us with the observation post reported that Nanshanpu was beginning to burn.

The battery stood behind a sopka on which stood an enclosed redoubt that, incorporated into the line of positions, formed one of its main strongpoints. I had accepted all the more readily the invitation of its commander to drink a glass of tea in his secure dugouts, as the battery officers had asked me to leave the battery, since Japanese shells were expected to strike at any moment. The cold, too, had become biting as I lay motionless behind the earthen embankment, so that inwardly I was glad to crawl on all fours with some semblance of dignity out of this—at the very least not comfortable—situation.

### At Redoubt No. 9.

At the gorge—the only exit from the redoubt facing away from the enemy—Nikolai Semyonovich, a captain in the 3rd Siberian Nerchinsk Regiment, was waiting for me and led me into his quarters, which, apart from offering a certain degree of protection against shrapnel balls, had few other virtues. A small door lined with torn sacking, reached via a narrow communication trench, led into a completely dark space; it had to be very small, for with every movement one bumped against something above or to the side. At last Nikolai Semyonovich found the matches. A board for a table, some sort of makeshift contraption that served for sitting and sleeping, a small tin stove with a tea kettle hanging above it. Here in this hole the officer had lived for four long winter months. Yet even this hiding place {p. 203} he had to share with someone, upon whose feet I had sat down in the darkness and who now started up from his sleep in fright. Second Lieutenant of the Reserve Pyotr Mikhailovich, his captain introduced him to me. He had kept watch through the night—thus sharing duty with the commander.

“When will there be peace?” was the lieutenant’s first question. “You see, I have a family, small children at home,” he added, as if by way of apology for his question, indicating with his hand the approximate height his offspring might have reached during his thirteen-month absence. I could well understand that his home over in Siberia had a greater appeal for him than Redoubt No. 9 with all its trenches and dugouts.

If I had fancied that it would be more pleasant here in the redoubt than outside by the battery, I had been sorely mistaken.

I was just trying to sip the rather hot tea when a peculiar slapping noise began above our heads, and immediately afterwards earth fell from the ceiling. Semyonovich cast a glance at the lieutenant—nothing more. “Can’t we step outside for a bit?” I finally ventured to break the expectant silence that had followed this sinister clapping. “No, no one may go out now, it’s about to start,” said the captain, “but later on you can take some pictures for me with that gadget there,” he pointed to my camera. We did not have to wait long for it to “start.” Here in the semidarkness of the dugout, it seemed as if outside a terrible hailstorm with stones the size of pigeon eggs had broken loose. Above us, beside us, everywhere it rattled, and from the ceiling, through the joints of the thick timbers, fell a veritable rain of earth. Mikhailovich offered me a second glass of tea. “It never lasts long,” he added, pointing upward with his finger. “Don't you care for tea?” “Certainly, but yours is still too hot for me—I’ll wait until it cools down.” “And what will happen,” I continued, “if the Japanese send over some Shimose shells instead of shrapnel?” “They’ve done that too, but they didn’t hit—God protects us from that, for He surely wants me to see my {p. 204} children again!” Under other circumstances such naive trust would have delighted me, but now, when the frail ceiling above our heads seemed barely able to withstand the shrapnel balls rattling down ever more fiercely, I cursed the hour in which I had resolved to enter this mousetrap. Here a single heavy shell splinter would suffice to smash through the none-too-thick timbers—and then, good night!

The reserve lieutenant—surely he had never heard of the penetrating power of projectiles—sat quietly through this calamity-threatening spectacle, drew a worn photograph from his pocket, and showed it to me by the dim light of the candle: it was his wife with two children in her arms.

Captain Nikolai Semyonovich had no children—but he had a toothache. He took advantage of the time when he could not go out anyway to brush his gums with ether, whose pungent smell only hastened the “empty feeling in the pit of the stomach” that was beginning to assert itself in me despite all resistance. “For four long months,” he explained to me, “he had asked every week for the regiment to send him a doctor; once one had indeed come, but he had brought no forceps with him. Since then, he had simply grown accustomed to the toothache as well.”

Thus we sat in silence side by side for perhaps another hour, until finally—as at the end of a thunderstorm—the slapping above us slowly ceased. “Now it’s over, let’s go outside!” said Semyonovich and opened the door, through which, like a deliverance, the bright, warm sunlight flooded in. “And how do you know that it’s over now?” “It’s the same story every day—the firing from across the way always begins at midday—that’s why I had you brought in to my quarters.” Our real duty only begins at night—then one of us must always keep watch—so they don’t take us by surprise. At times, however, both officers, along with their entire garrison, had to be on their feet day and night. None of them was permitted to leave the fort. When the sun shone bright and cheerful, they could sit on the gorge parapet and look down into the Shahe valley or, just {p. 205} like today, take pleasure in the flight of the mortar bombs that our mortars fired in high arcs across the redoubt. This time, however, it was better to keep just behind the sheltering crest line. From here there was a magnificent view into the valley, toward the Russian redoubt lying before us, whose abatis stood out sharp and thorny against the snow-glistening opposite slope of the “Two-Horned Mountain,” from which flashed the fire of Japanese batteries.

[Illustration: Shahe Valley with the Two-Horned Peak.]

Toward evening it grew quiet; a peaceful stillness rested over the wide valley, from which the silver ribbon of ice of the Shahe stretched far into the plain, visible from afar. The flames in Nanshanpu seemed to have been extinguished; only thick black smoke betrayed the successful day's work of the Russian mortar battery.

What had become of the scout detachment down there, we did not know.

As I was leaving, Nikolai Semyonovich asked me to send him toothache drops from Mukden when I had the chance. Does he still need them? His redoubt was taken by the Japanese two nights later.

{p. 206}

As I rode down the mountain slope in the evening stillness toward my ammunition park, I thought to myself: What is *courage* and what is *cowardice*? Could I call it courage that the night before, out of curiosity, I decided to attend the combat of a battery? And should I now consider myself a coward because there in the battery, where, chattering with cold, I had to press myself flat against the earthen slope, the thought came to me that it was really quite pointless to have come up here just to have Shimose shells and shrapnel flying over my head? Was it my fault that during this time I could not help thinking of the stereoscopic photographs the lieutenant had shown me early that morning? Did I have less courage than those Russian officers who, in the zeal of their duty, seemed to pay no heed to the looming danger? Certainly not! Courage is the *disregard of danger*; this can come from *ignorance* of it, in which case it only *seems* to be courage. The man storms courageously against the enemy rampart—if he knew, as his officer does, that the enemy has laid mines around the rampart that could destroy him at any moment, he might turn back! Furthermore, a man can be courageous who *does not notice* the danger because he is too absorbed in his own duties—in his manual tasks; that is what the mechanical training, the *drill*, of the soldier must strive for, which, precisely for reasons of fire discipline, is not as reprehensible as is often asserted. In the impact of enemy shells, the artilleryman sees the approach of deadly lead, but the mechanically drilled operation of his gun—and, for the officer, the strenuous observation of the results of his own shots—leaves no time for other thoughts. I saw men in the battery make the sign of the cross on their foreheads with their right hand, while with their left they mechanically reached into the wheel spokes of the gun recoiling backward!

From what has just been said, one might conclude that through a minimum of education and a maximum of drill, that—I would say *mechanical*—courage can be instilled which the Russian army as a whole possessed, and which would indeed have sufficed for a skillfully conducted defense.

{p. 207}

Who, then, is courageous in the most unrestricted sense of the word? He who sees the danger, recognizes it, and is nevertheless ready to sacrifice his life for a higher goal. With the terrible effect of modern weapons, danger reveals itself with shattering force to anyone who leaves his sheltering cover to advance; to carry one's body into the rain of bullets, mechanical courage no longer suffices for this; today, the courage of enthusiasm must lead the attacker against the enemy—against a real, hated enemy. Short-sightedness and drilled routines, which are certainly an excellent means up to a certain level of moral demand, are never sufficient in an attack against a modern-armed and morally capable opponent—just as they did not suffice for the Russians, despite all their numerical superiority. For this we need more: enthusiasm, enthusiasm for a great idea shared by the entire people. And in our intellectually advanced age, such an idea can today be awakened only by the concept of nation or religion, as we have seen with the Boers and the Japanese, and as everyone learns to comprehend who has seen an earth-smoking, death-sown assault field of the recent battles.

Yet since I happen to be speaking of such things, for the sake of the completeness of the train of thought I will anticipate events somewhat and touch upon a further psychological factor; it is the

## Contempt for Death.

It was before the Battle of Mukden, on the eastern wing, deep in the mountains. The place where I spent the night back then was called Fanshen, if I am not mistaken. South of it lay the Russian positions on the steep heights flanking the Shahe; across the river, which was frozen solid at the time, stood the Japanese.

One gets used to shooting in war just as one does to the clatter of a mill. When an onlooker at that {p. 208} time found a warm place to sleep, he was grateful and glad of it. If he was awakened in the morning by brisk rapid fire rattling up in the mountains, he rolled over onto his other side to sleep on, glad of his safe little spot. After all, it had been the same thing for weeks! By day, from our side and theirs, the dull, rumbling roar of the guns rolled over the mountains at long intervals; at nightfall—as if to call it a day—quiet settled over both sides until morning, when now one side, now the other attempted raids on the opposing villages. This time the crackle of rifles had been closer to our *fanza* than usual, but as the artillery remained silent, it could hardly be any major Japanese attack worth getting up so early for. My sleeping companions, two orderly officers from Rennenkampf's corps, must have calculated along the same lines under their warm blankets, for they showed no sign of getting up anytime soon when I left the *fanza* a few hours later. Right beside our house, on the hillside, stood a dilapidated temple with a spacious courtyard and a well-preserved priest's dwelling that had been converted into a divisional field hospital. The first wounded from the nighttime engagement were just being brought in, among them a senior lieutenant whom I had met in Mukden a few weeks earlier. He had taken a bullet through his right upper arm, but spoke enthusiastically of the fight he had just come from. “Yes, my dear sir,” he said, turning to me, “you mustn't think that we Russians always come off second best—this time we really gave the Japanese what for! Only yesterday evening our Chinese told us that the yellows intended to raid the village tonight, the one lying right in front of the lower redoubt, where the only wells we can draw water from are still located. Two scout detachments of our own were supposed to cross to the other bank of the Shahe tonight. They are held back to wait for the Japanese. Our Chinese hadn't lied—for once—around 3 a.m. the Japs were there; our men lurking in the trenches had by then almost frozen to the ground, so it became all the hotter {p. 209} for them later. In the end, we too had to go down from our position, which is where I caught this lead pill—but not one of them gave us the slip this time! The men from the 10th East Siberian Corps cut off their retreat from behind, and then one after the other was put to the blade ¹). But they fought like devils. When dawn broke toward morning, we saw that there weren't so many of them after all—two or three dozen.” The victory-flushed senior lieutenant would gladly have gone on talking to the listeners gathered around him, had the doctor not called on him to come inside the hospital. “Wait a moment,” he shouted to us as he went, “they're bringing a few Japanese over too, three or four wounded men we took prisoner.” He said this with an affectedly indifferent air, as if taking Japanese prisoner were the most ordinary thing in the world!

Meanwhile, several curious onlookers and doctors had gathered and were waiting for the escort of the wounded, which was now coming down the path from the hillside. Black papakhas, bayonets glinting in the red morning sun, and two white stretchers—that was all we could make out in the procession, which disappeared from our view at the next bend. “People always say that the Japanese never surrender—and now you see for yourself that we take prisoners,” remarked an officer. “Surely those being brought here are severely wounded, and,” I added politely, “as I have heard, Japanese prisoners are treated so well among you that it is hard to see why they should prefer a useless death to captivity. I am glad that I finally have the opportunity to see Japanese up close.” My wish was soon fulfilled. From the gate opposite, the procession of wounded entered the courtyard. The escort formed a wide double file, in the middle of which a small Japanese limped along, leaning heavily on a cane. Behind him followed two more stretchers. The Japanese may have been about thirty paces away from us. He held his head proudly erect, his eyes fixed firmly upon us; his gaze was mesmerizing. No one spoke; only the {p. 210} dragging footsteps of the escort could be heard. The Japanese began to smile. His eyes seemed to grow larger. His mouth opened as though he were about to speak to us; his whole face was so joyful, and yet so sublime. Slowly he came closer, his left hand resting on his cane—that his right hand seemed to be searching for something in the folds of his loose robe we hardly noticed, so captivated were we by his gaze. Now his eyes grow even larger—he laughs out loud—or perhaps he shouted something—a sharp crack, a violent blast of air that nearly throws us to the ground, white gunsmoke swirling before our eyes. It had happened so fast that no one knew what had actually occurred. The front ranks of the escort and the front stretcher with the wounded man and his bearers lay on the ground. The bright-eyed, laughing Japanese? A cloud of smoke still hovered over him—then one saw feet in a pool of blood, something pulpy from which entrails hung, and a torso from which part of the skin and clothing had been pulled back over the head.

::: notes
¹) Bayonet.
:::

I can find no words to describe what I felt at that moment. The appalling spectacle shook us all; in silence we gazed at the horror our eyes had seen happen with such bewildering speed, yet it was not the blood before us, but the torn corpse and the thought that we ourselves had escaped death only by sheer chance, that made our nerves tremble. Every one of us felt that in this moment he had seen a revelation in which he had previously not believed: *that even today there is still a people that knows how to die laughing, that prefers death even to an honorable captivity!*

This was the first Japanese I had seen at close quarters during the war.

And now one might ask: why were there no Russians capable of such an act?

To such a question it is surely enough merely to mention the names of all those Russian generals and colonels who fell in the ranks of their soldiers, flinching not from the hail of bullets—making, to be sure, a useless sacrifice. If we {p. [217]} [Illustration: Japanese spies before Lieutenant Colonel Rottstein.]

{p. [218]}

{p. 213}

seek, however, a counterpart to the fanaticism and contempt for death of that Japanese, then let us ask not only among the Russians, but also among the rest of us, the white civilized nations, and we shall see that our nerves and our ideals are already far more worn out than those of the East Asians—I say East Asians—the Chinese and Manchus included, as far as nerves are concerned.

What is the psychological obstacle to bringing troops forward under enemy fire? The fear of death and of painful injury! Now, the East Asian possesses the precious quality of bearing pain more easily than we do, of being more indifferent than we are to the pain of his fellow man and the sight of blood, and of having no fear whatsoever of a swift, painless death. In modern combat, where the mass effect of modern weapons produces a shattering impression even on the distant observer, these are decisive advantages that already secure the East Asian a certain superiority over us for years to come—until, that is, by absorbing our culture, they stand on the same level with us physically and morally.

To this indifference to the physical pain of death, however, there is added in the Japanese an ideal element. *Death for the Fatherland* is regarded by him as the highest honor; for his fatherland he goes to his death not merely willingly, but joyfully; his religion and his ancestor worship have instilled these feelings into his breast from childhood on.

We know that every Japanese soldier carried with him a postcard for his relatives, informing them that he had had the good fortune to die for the fatherland. If he was actually killed, this postcard was sent by his comrades to Japan, and the Russians, too, never denied the fallen enemy this act of piety.

In order, however, to better characterize that physical indifference to a painless death, which is common to all East Asians, and the “consecration of oneself to death,” which is an ideal trait of the Japanese, I wish to mention the following examples.

{p. 214}

Even before my journey to East Asia, I had read a great deal about the ingeniously cruel death penalties that Chinese justice makes its victims suffer. Consequently, I imagined the Chinese to be a crude, hard-hearted people, and was not a little astonished to find in them good-natured, affable folk. Notwithstanding this, however, I had the opportunity to convince myself that the Chinese's inventive genius for tormenting his fellow man surpasses all the tortures of our Middle Ages.

In Mukden there was a large courtyard enclosed by high walls, in the middle of which stood a fantastical wooden dragon. This was the execution ground of the Chinese court. Floggings, the tearing out of fingernails, the hacking off of limbs, and the like could be seen here once a week, though I had no desire to witness them.

Accompanied by a Russian officer, I visited this place of torment one day. Among the various instruments of torture, an iron pot caught my eye, resembling a flowerpot, with a small hole in its bottom. The Chinese man accompanying us—he was probably some executioner's assistant—explained to us the purpose of this vessel. The condemned criminal is stripped, tied face down to a table, and the iron urn is strapped opening-down over his buttocks, so tightly that a starved rat, which had previously been placed inside the urn, can find no way out. The condemned man is then left for a day or two to the rat gnawing at his living flesh. What follows, however, would have surpassed the imagination of the bloodthirstiest Grand Inquisitor: the rat, satiated with the flesh of its victim, begins to grow sluggish. Now the executioner comes with a red-hot iron rod, inserts it through the small opening in the bottom of the pot, and tortures the rat with it until, to escape the glowing iron, it creeps into the rectum of its victim. Yet the red iron rod pursues it even in there, burning and prodding it until it has bitten its way forward, not into the belly, but into the intestines of the man, who by now has been tortured into laughing madness! A skilled executioner, through careful selection of the {p. 215} rat, is said to be able to keep the condemned man alive for three to four days despite all the agony!

"The people here are like earthworms," my Russian companion explained to me; "they hardly feel the cutting off of a limb, and such tortures are necessary to arouse in them the fear of punishment; simple hanging or beheading excites neither the spectators nor even the condemned man particularly."

A few months later, I had the opportunity to convince myself of the correctness of the latter assertion.

It was some time after the Battle of Mukden, in Kuanchengzi, the second-largest city in Manchuria. Here, too, there was such a court of justice, and I was present there at the execution of 13 honghuzi. The executioner, recognisable by a gleaming curved sword whose blade he tapped affectionately with his crooked finger and whose clear ring seemed to delight him, had a fat, genial face. His assistants were smoking from small opium pipes; no preparations whatever indicated that a bloody spectacle would take place here in a few minutes.

At last, a pack of people came along from the opposite gate. They were the 13 condemned men, their hands tied behind their backs, dragging broad iron shackles with chains on their feet. No more than two Chinese, seemingly chatting intimately with the condemned, were their escorts. The whole scene was so peaceful that I could hardly believe in the impending beheadings. Since these were bandits whom the Russian border guard had handed over to the Chinese authorities for sentencing, they were still waiting for a Russian officer who was to attend the execution in an official capacity. Naturally, he kept everyone waiting. Meanwhile, one of the executioner's assistants stuffed wax into the ears of the honghuzi—"so that they would not hear the whir of the sword and flinch with their heads"! That the executioner had to strike precisely a certain spot on the vertebra with his blade was evident to me from the fact that another Chinese drew a line with red paint on the nape of each condemned man's neck, having first expertly {p. 216} palpated his spine with his index finger until he had found the right spot. The honghuzi seemed to recognise the expediency of this philanthropic precaution; today I laugh at it, but at the time an icy shudder ran through me whenever the condemned man indicated with an affirmative grin that the man with the red paint had found the right cartilage on the nape of the neck where the executioner's sword was to fall.

At last, the execution could begin. It seemed as though the condemned men had already attended such affairs. Without being asked, they knelt down in a row at wide intervals from one another; not a single expression, not a single gesture betrayed fear of impending death.

[Illustration: Captured honghuzi.]

The executioner stepped up to the first; a barely perceptible movement of his wrist, and a thick, red jet of blood shot straight forward from the headless torso. It seemed to me as if it were still standing upright on its knees, and already the fat Chinese was at the second. Once more I saw the short sword flash upwards, saw again the red, horizontal jet stream over the head rolling on the ground, {p. 217} then disgust and horror, which I had hitherto steadfastly fought against, seized me. The steam of the warm blood took my breath away—I felt ashamed of my weakness, turned to leave, but after a few paces returned. Now I saw one of the assistants struggling to remove the iron shackles from the feet of the beheaded men. To be sure, he tried to open the irons on the first one with a key or some similar tool, but this did not seem to accomplish the task quickly enough. Thereupon he took a knife and whittled the foot off the carcass, much as one sharpens a pencil. Now, however, I had had enough. In vain I reasoned with my nerves that, as a bystander, I had no need to be more upset than those whose heads were being chopped off here. In vain I pointed out to them the cheerfully grinning faces of the other Chinese spectators—it was no use, they refused to function; I had to obey them and leave the ghastly place.

And so it will fare with us Europeans in war as well; we shall reach a certain limit where the strength of our will and of our physique will succumb to the weakness of our nerves—and indeed even more quickly than happened with the Russians, who are far better off regarding their nerves than the members of those armies in which nervousness is methodically nurtured.

[Illustration: ]

{p. [224]}

## Fighting with the Third Siberian Corps.

The tranquil life at the 2nd East Siberian Mortar Ammunition Park in Xiaoyanxintun had been disrupted by a Shimose shell. It had struck early in the morning; the men came running together to look at the enormous brown cloud of earth that, whirled up tower-high, stood over the ground. Slowly the dust settled, and the soldiers went to scatter gaoliang before their horses. Ivan Ivanovich, the ordnance sergeant, was just having the loaded ammunition carts brought down from the hill; now they stood right next to our fanza. Yet life in it was far too cozy to abandon it at the very first shot—besides, it had not been aimed at the park at all, but at the battery up on the mountain. At midday, word went round that the situation before the Third Corps, which stood to the east of the Second, had improved; the Japanese had taken possession of three heights there during the night, but the third had been recaptured by the Russians. That the good people never want to admit their failures straight out! An improvement in the situation, when instead of three sopkas only two have been lost!

With the Second Siberian, everything still remained fairly quiet on 26 February. It was believed that the great offensive would begin in a few days. General Linevich had promised to cut off the three Japanese divisions advancing under Kuroki against the Russian left flank from the center of the Japanese army by a bold thrust. As we learned, on this day he had driven with his inner staff from Huangshan to Shihuicheng.

That such a thrust always begins with a step backward! I thought to myself, since Shihuicheng lies further north than Huangshan.

{p. 219}

The First Siberian Corps as well, which only a few days before had stood on the western flank, was said to have arrived in Shihuicheng on this day—elements of the Sixth Corps, too, had marched eastward from the center the day before; so it was now determined that the Russian offensive this time would begin not with a strong right flank, as had initially been thought, but with a strong left.

To be sure of the matter, I went at noon to the headquarters of the Second Siberian Corps, where General Pappengut, the corps chief of staff, received me very cordially. He introduced me to Lieutenant General Zasulich, a somewhat corpulent gentleman who at the time on the Yalu had opened the campaign none too auspiciously. Here I learned that General Rennenkampf, too, had returned from the extreme right flank back to the extreme left in order to take back command from his successor, General Alekseyev. One of the gentlemen here had the day before been an earwitness to a telephone conversation that Lieutenant General Linevich had held with Alekseyev. Linevich was said to have been furious over the headlong abandonment of the positions that Alekseyev had yielded to the Japanese at their first assault, and not to have minced his words on the telephone. In conclusion, he had advised Alekseyev to look for a position with the Red Cross and had stripped him of the command of the Western Detachment.

“Would you not like me to send a few Cossacks with you on your way?” General Pappengut asked me courteously. “Thank you, General, I hope to reach Shandalingshan by tomorrow evening, and there I have acquaintances who will help me further.”

In a wretched snowstorm, I left the hospitable house of the brave bombardiers the following day. Since then I have never seen them again. In the turmoil of the great retreat, some two weeks later, I saw mortars driving among other wagons, shrouded in dust. Perhaps they were the East Siberian ones. In the tangle of men and wagons that surged past me then, they were soon lost to sight.

Along the positions that stretched eastward from Kangdalingshan from sopka {p. 220} to sopka, it was almost completely quiet. I rode down in the valley and encountered here only one battery, which was in position for indirect fire. Its commander sent an officer after me who invited me to linger with them for a while, and then provided me with two Cossacks who escorted me to the 2nd Siberian Infantry Brigade.

Again we rode alongside the telegraph line, whose wires whirred and sang in the wind. Had I understood the language of the wire, I would also have been able to explain to myself why supply train wagons of the First Siberian Corps were coming toward me, marching westward.

As we already know, the sorely tried First Siberian Corps had on 24 February again stood ready to attack Sandepu. On the same day, however, the Japanese had attacked in the mountains themselves—and the Russian commander-in-chief yielded the initiative to them; the attack against Sandepu was called off, the First Siberian sent to Shihuicheng on the *extreme left flank*, only to be *reassigned back to Mukden* a few days later. But of all this I knew nothing at the time! In firm confidence in old Linevich’s “display of might,” I rode on eastward and after half an hour arrived at the 2nd Siberian Infantry Brigade. This time it was a fine, spacious dugout in which the staff officers were housed. The regimental adjutant, who here simultaneously performed the duties of brigade general staff officer, led me to the bedside of his severely wounded father, commander of one of the two regiments whose dugouts stood in the valley.

“We can’t stay here much longer,” said the colonel. “Either forward or back—just look at these chunks of iron they’ve been hurling into our camp for the last two days”—and with that he pointed to a collection of fragments from twelve-centimetre shells that his son had arranged against the wall. “I don’t even know which one it was that smashed my hip bone—I’m only sorry that I won’t get to see with my own eyes how Father Linevich finally shows those yellows over there what’s what!” Yes, that much was clear to me: Linevich enjoyed great trust; {p. 221} *and he was strong now, too—he must have had nearly eighty-three battalions—why then had he gone back to Shihhuicheng—why had he not come here to lead forward the attack all the troops were expecting?* Here again two regiments stood idle, letting themselves be bombarded by the Japanese!

The commander of the 2nd Rifle Brigade had kindly given me a mounted rifleman as an escort; at Fanshen, with the 3rd Siberian Division, I was to be provided with another. The general staff section there was just then busy packing up. One of the officers said: “We are falling back to a more favorable position.” Another was less diplomatic, admitting that the Japanese had taken three redoubts overnight and that several shells had already flown into the village during the morning. “One of the three *sopkas*,” I asked, “you took back again, didn’t you?” “Not at all,” came the reply. “It merely changed hands back and forth—in the end, the Japanese kept it after all.”

The warm tea had done a world of good; my new escort stood ready, and now we could head back out into the snow and wind.

Up hill and down dale the path led, often quite wretched. By a ruined temple, against whose front gateway a regimental standard leaned, several battalions were bivouacking. The men stood in clusters around the campfires, which the biting wind whipped flat along the ground. They were troops of the 72nd Siberian Division, easily recognized by their numbered shoulder straps. How did they come to be here? After all, they belonged to the 6th Corps, which stood in the center with the Third Army! “Where are you marching?” I asked a soldier needlessly. “To the position,” came the short reply. More than that he could not possibly know. It was just as well, in any case, to break the habit of asking. *“Ne mogu znat!”* (I cannot know!)—that was the ever-recurring, stereotypical reply. Often a convenient way out for mental indolence, but mostly the plain truth. Where one stood oneself today was still known; {p. 222} but who was to the left and to the right, to which general one actually belonged, where tomorrow’s supply train was to be sent—assuming it was not already lost altogether—changed not from day to day, but from hour to hour. For that reason, with the best will in the world, no one could possibly know. Thus General Zasulich had also believed the commander of the neighboring 3rd Siberian Corps to still be in Xiandalinshan, whereas he was already much farther south at Panjiazi,

[Illustration: Wounded on the way to Panjiazi.]

north of the Gaotuling Pass, which had been fought over since February 25.

The sun was sinking toward the mountains, and Panjiazi was still far away. The headquarters of the 3rd Siberian Corps was indeed still in Xiandalinshan, but I hoped along this route to reach the Eastern Corps sooner.

It must have been around six o'clock in the evening when my guide turned south off the path we had been following. Over two more low passes—the Russians call them *pereval*—and we rode down into a narrow valley that led to Panjiazi.

{p. 223}

As if invisible portals had opened, the din of a distant struggle suddenly came sounding between the steep mountain walls, borne on the wind. As yet it resembled a slowly vibrating, dull rumble, which the thunder of battery volleys drowned out from time to time. On the narrow path running along the ice of a stream, soldiers marched closely packed; their heads were bent forward, for the wind drove the snow into their eyes. Now and then empty ammunition carts came galloping toward them from the opposite direction; then they had to make way and step onto the ice, which frequently broke through. Sullenly they would pull their soaked felt boots out of the water. Heavy two-wheeled *arbas* also came toward us, perhaps a hundred or even two hundred of them. Those lying on them were covered up over their heads with gray wool blankets. They came from the field hospital situated by a long wall at the entrance to the next village. Behind this wall people seemed to be digging, for from time to time the heads of iron picks showed above it. They are probably digging trenches again, I thought, and rode over to the wall to look across. Far from it! Graves were being dug here. Several had already been filled in; on these stood crosses of white wood without names. Beside them a priest was pacing up and down. Perhaps he was waiting for the next guest, for whom the coffin ropes were already lying ready . . .

In the village, people and wagons were jammed together on the muddy road; before the bridge stood a line of stretchers with wounded that could not get across. But no one was loud or impatient.

At last, one managed to get out of the village. The sun had set, and only indistinctly could two opposing steep slopes be made out, between which the road continued toward the Gaotuling Pass. From there came the noise, in which the crackle of rapid rifle fire could now clearly be distinguished alongside the crash of artillery. It sounded very near; high in the air there was a howling and rushing, as though bullets were already whizzing overhead. In the twilight, flashes now came from the batteries stationed to the rear on the mountains, and rifle volleys also lit up now and then from the opposite mountainside. Pajiazi was full of officers.

{p. 224}

The commander of the Third Corps, Lieutenant General Ivanov himself, had come here with his immediate staff to follow the course of the engagement. Several battalions were stationed beside the village; one of them was just moving into the mountains. The newly arrived column occupied the abandoned campsite.

### The Fighting in the Gaotuling Pass.

South of Panjiazi the valley widens, only to close into a narrow pass between the two aforementioned steep slopes. Behind this, fighting was underway. It had grown almost dark; the firing lines of the infantry occupying the positions resembled trembling, glowing ribbons that fringed the crests of the five heights, which stood out black against the grayish-white firmament. Right by the sunken road, a Russian battery seemed to be stationed and delivering rapid-fire salvos.

The wounded were being brought back from there. In the darkness, not much could be distinguished: two-wheeled carts on which figures crouched huddled together, or stretchers carried by four men. “Look at this one,” Naval Lieutenant Camperio told me, whose tall figure I had soon found, holding his hand lantern over a stretcher that the bearers, stopping to rest, had set down on the ground beside us. On it was a man doubled up; he had been shot in the abdomen. Beside him, however, lay something completely misshapen: a corpse lacking the head and the entire back—“Shimose,” Camperio added by way of explanation. “Wouldn’t it be simpler to send the priest and the gravediggers further forward, rather than transport the corpses all the long way back? Whether he is buried here or there is all the same to the poor devil after all.”

For five days now the struggle for the Gaotuling Pass had been going on. Of the eight heights that lay to the south, the Japanese had already taken five, and indeed the highest ones. On the other three, however, the Russians held on with tenacity.

I could not tear myself away from the terribly beautiful spectacle. For the first time in my life, I saw and heard {p. [231]} [Illustration: Prayer of the Krasnoyarsk Battalion before the battle.
Gaotuling Pass.]

{p. [232]}

{p. 227}

here at closest quarters the nerve-shattering surge of a battle in the mountains. As if from the chasms of hell, the rolling echo of the volleys thundered from the dark valleys, only to die away immediately afterward in the rapid-fire bursts of the nearby batteries. It seemed to me as though, through the crackle of the rifles, I could hear the voices of the combatants from the black mountain ridges. The air trembled in one great, wildly vibrating tone. And now the moon, smiling amiably as ever, rose behind the mountains. The fire of the skirmish lines paled in its wan light; all the more distinct against the greenish-yellow sky were the little clouds of exploding shrapnel and the swirling columns of Shimose smoke.

“We shall see this quite often yet,” Camperio told me, and led me to a nearby *zemlyanka*.

General Schwerin, who had most graciously invited us into his dugout, where he was staying with his officers during the battle, had just returned from the

[Illustration: Killed by a Shimose shell.] {p. 228} batteries to his dugout. It was an officers' *zemlyanka*, meaning that the entrance led through a small anteroom intended for the servant; this time, however, officers were glad if they could find room even here.
