In the *zemlyanka* it was cold; the wind whistled through the repeatedly opening door and the hole in the wall opposite, which, formerly covered with parchment paper, had served as a window. Straw had been scattered on the ground, but too little to prevent the chill of the frozen earth from being felt. On a crate stood tin mugs of tea, from which thin wisps curled up into the cold air. The soldiers had brought a bowl of cabbage soup, and General Schwerin invited us to join in. “*À la guerre, comme à la guerre*,” he remarked, giving us his silver cutlery while he himself tucked in heartily with a wooden soldier’s spoon. Throughout the night, the rattling of rifles continued without interruption. In the *zemlyanka*, too, sleep was hardly to be thought of. Reports arrived, orders had to be written, and then it was necessary again to drink a glass of hot tea to drive the chill from one's bones. How the soldiers must have frozen in their positions that night! I could not help thinking of the poor fellows who had splashed into the water that evening and were now drying their wet feet by a campfire somewhere in the open. In the morning, fresh troops arrived in Pajiazi. It was the 72nd Division of the Sixth Corps, which the day before had bivouacked north of Fanshen. But the Vyborg Regiment had also joined the Third Corps, and a regiment from the 1st Siberian had remained here, so that once again, happily, troops from four different corps were jumbled together in one place!

One battalion after another came along the valley road that I had ridden yesterday, entering Padsjaza, stacked arms here in pyramids, and waited until they should be sent into the mountains. In this manner there arrived one after another two battalions of the 72nd Division and one battalion each of the 71st Division and the 10th East Siberian Regiment, as well as detachments of the 286th Kirsanov, 288th Kulikov, 7th Krasnoyarsk, and 287th Tarusa Regiments.

The field kitchens came driving up, and the men, quietly {p. 229} and in the finest order, formed up around them in radiating ranks. When they were all gathered, they sang a brief grace, then the man standing atop the field kitchen lifted the lid from the copper and, with a large ladle, filled each outstretched mess tin. Standing, the soldiers slurped down the hot cabbage soup, for which they then thanked the good Lord with three crossings of themselves.

[Illustration: In the Gaotuling Pass]

There can hardly be a soldier in the world more undemanding and enduring than the Russian. What a pity about this splendid material!

Past the battalions moved the long column of stretchers and wounded being taken to the field hospital. On the way, the dressings were beginning to stain red, and on the canvas of the stretchers were large patches of blood.

Soldiers were now standing around one stretcher. The man lying on it was dead, his hands hanging limp. The men seemed to have known him and were searching through his {p. 230} pockets for letters. He was from a reserve division. The bullet had struck him square in the forehead. I wanted to photograph the group, but the men noticed me and dutifully snapped to attention. “What are you looking for in his pockets?” “His wife’s name—I want to write to her that she shouldn’t wait for her husband any longer; she’ll never hear it from the ‘orderly room’ anyway!”

Among the 72nd Division, the men began winding the woolen scarf around the black papakha. Why this monstrosity was put on the poor fellows’ heads? A fur cap with ear flaps, such as the Buryats and Chinese wear, is far more practical than this tall grenadier cap. But the Japanese are as little impressed by this absurdity as by the daggers, sewn-on cartridge pouches, and other frippery belonging to the theatrical costume of the Caucasians—who, for the rest, are better brigands than good soldiers. When moving against the enemy, the black cap had to be wrapped with the grey bashlyk so as not to stand out too conspicuously against the white snow.

In the mountains it crackled and rattled again with renewed violence; overhead in the sky shrapnel shells wailed and hissed, their little white clouds hanging over the Russian batteries.

Toward noon, a battalion of the 72nd Division, with blue shoulder straps, went forward as a reinforcement to the position south of Padsjaza.

The battalion stood ready to march. The commander had the companies form a square and addressed the men simply and briefly: they should think of God and the Tsar, then they would have nothing to fear from shrapnel and shimose—everyone had to die sometime, after all.

In conclusion, he took off his papakha and began to recite the Lord’s Prayer. The men prayed with him. Then arms were shouldered and they marched off. Every single man, before taking the first step, took off his cap once more and crossed himself.

I followed as well. How dispiriting it is for troops marching into battle when the dead and dying are carried {p. 231} past them to the rear! How anxiously the men’s eyes followed the groaning wounded or mutilated corpses carried right past their ranks. Where was the ringing, metallic singing innate to the throats of these men now? Their throats were constricted—just like their hearts—the men felt that they were going to their deaths.

From the observation post of a battery, whose officer I had met through the artillery brigadier, I was able to follow the battalion’s further fate. It had to occupy a long trench—a rallying position—into which Russian troops from farther ahead were just then surging back.

The Japanese seemed to have succeeded in emplacing guns here that enfiladed the forward Russian fortified lines, for clouds of white smoke and black earth billowing up along them showed the terrible effect of the Japanese batteries, which rendered any further resistance by the infantry impossible. But the ground up to the next rallying position, and a good distance behind it, was like a hot stone slab onto which cold water is poured, so thickly did the earth billow and swirl up from the densely falling shells!

And are there really men, troops, who will walk into this fire, storming forward in this hail of bullets? “Yes! The Japanese—they can endure it!” the artillery officer told me—“but not by day either, only by night. By day one moves, just as our battalion did just now, through a prepared communication trench into the fortified line and pours fire into one another from here until evening; *to attempt to push closed formations forward across this ground swept by enemy fire would be madness—even the Japanese bring up their reserves only in loose swarms.*”

And we Europeans, much-tested tacticians, still delight in that fine spectacle of maneuvers when our reserves advance into the front battle line *tambour battant*, in lockstep and neatly dressed! We think of everything on such occasions except two things: first, that {p. 232} we have no men who would walk into this storm of bullets—which is what it is in reality—for us; and second, that if they did, nothing would be left of them after a few minutes!

In such a case, one must simply push forward a *living wall*, as the Japanese do—sacrificing battalions, even whole regiments, as a living wall, behind whose corpses the actual battle line only then establishes itself; *yet we have no such living walls.*

Toward evening the fire began to slacken on both sides. A brief lull in the fighting occurred, during which the severely wounded and the dead were carried out of the trench. To replace them, half a battalion was brought up, which, like the first, was led into the position under cover of communication trenches.

That same day I rode on. The Russians had so far lost two thousand men in the sector of the 3rd Siberian Corps, but they held out bravely on the three heights still left to them; the Japanese were only able to seize these when the 3rd Corps received the order to retreat from the 1st Army Command.

It was not thanks to the higher command that this stubborn resistance was offered, but solely and exclusively to the brave Russian soldiers, whose virtues and failings I intend to describe in greater detail.

{p. [239]}

## The Russian Soldier, His Clothing and Food.

“General, sir, our Little Father sends me to report most obediently that our men have been in action for five days; scarcely more than three men remain for each gun.” So reported the old artillery sergeant, who had come late at night from the Gaotuling Pass to Panjiazi to see the artillery brigade commander, General Schwerin.

“Tell your colonel that your men must hold out and wait. God will help. Go!”

“We must hold out and wait,” repeated the old non-commissioned officer, from whose shaggy mustache glittering icicles hung in long cones. “God will help!” With that he saluted and walked slowly, his head bowed, out of the low, smoky dugout into the dark night.

How few words had been spoken, and yet in them lay an infinite wealth of faith, hope, and resignation. But one had to have heard the voice in which the old man had carefully, deliberately repeated each word—the simple, convinced tone that sees help already in the solace of patience and God alone.

Just as he had come, he made his way back, heavily yet carefully. The thick fur coat and the heavy felt boots permit no brisk striding. It is a hard road in the dark night. He walked it today for the first time; he had never been in this region before, yet he did not lose his way; without a map—he cannot read one anyway, he cannot read at all—guided only by that innate sense of direction peculiar to the children of the vast steppes. The way back is easier. Far from high up {p. 234} over the mountains, the howling and whistling begins again; six white flashes shoot up into the black sky—those are bursting shrapnel shells that the Japanese are sending at the Russian batteries in salvos. Six, and another six, and in such rapid succession eight salvos. “That is eight batteries against our four,” the artillery sergeant mutters. Well, what does it matter; after all, the general said: “Hold out and wait.” The old man thinks no further—he is not really old at all, but only looks so gray and weathered; he goes on his way, repeating his general’s words so as not to forget them: “Hold out and wait!”

Wounded men are carried past him. They lie beneath thick woolen blankets and do not stir; perhaps they are already dead.

Vasili Stepanovich—such is our artillery sergeant’s name—lets them be carried past him without concern; he must keep his memory together so that not a single word escapes him of what he has to report to Little Father Polkovnik ¹).

Now he reaches the mountain slope along whose ridge his batteries stand. A white light, with a red cross upon it, points out the way. Here, close against the slope in the courtyard of a ruined temple, is the advanced dressing station. He steps inside to ask for the colonel. Wailing and groaning. Against the wall, right beside the gate, the returned stretcher-bearers have set their canvas litters. Ugly red bloodstains can be seen on them; some litters are as if soaked through with blood. These are stiff as leather, for the blood is frozen.

“Looking for one of these red beds for yourself, are you?” the artillery sergeant’s friend, the senior feldsher, asked him. “Hardly likely to be needed,” the latter chuckled; “I am coming from Panjiazi; but tell me, where is the polkovnik? You feldshers always know more than we do up front facing the enemy.” “Our polkovnik was here an hour ago. The shimose drove dirt into his eyes; but he came off better than the new commander of the 2nd Battery, the lieutenant colonel of the {p. [241]} [Illustration: Types of Russian soldiers.]

::: notes
¹) Colonel.
:::

{p. [242]}

{p. 237}

Guards, had his left leg taken off, and . . .” “Don’t chatter so much, I want to know where the colonel is.” — “Right where he was before, at redoubt number two.”

From the dressing station to the redoubt, one could still cover a good distance in complete safety. The howling high above was now drowned out by the nearby thunder of the cannons, mingled with the sharp crackle of the skirmish line. The infantry still lay far from the batteries, but the icy air stood above the earth like a mighty pillar of crystal, vibrating every sound with metallic clarity into the distance. Here it was far colder than down in Pajiazi; had the artillery sergeant known what a thermometer was, he would have estimated the cold at twenty-five degrees. It would not have made him any warmer, and his thoughts remained single-mindedly fixed on “endure and wait—God will help!” He now began to mutter these words incessantly to himself—he needed them. Tch—tch—striking now to the right, now to the left of him, then again the whirring hum of a ricochet, and the bursting of the shrapnel above the batteries could now also be heard. Vasily

[Illustration: Bayonet thrust.] {p. 238} Stepanovich crawled on all fours. He could barely make headway; the heavy clothes hindered his movement. To take off his heavy fur coat, or at least unbutton it, in order to get out of the range of the incoming bullets more quickly and reach the cover of the redoubt—such a thought never entered his head. Cold hurts, and only a warm fur coat protects against it—so he had always heard and seen. Walking fast or running, for that matter, was not his style—he preferred to hold out slowly over this last stretch. What if he were hit by a bullet here? Left lying helpless in the dark night to freeze to death! He began to pray aloud and crept slowly forward on all fours. “And to think I hoped,” ran through his head, “that down there at the general’s they’d give you a small glass of vodka—and the little brothers probably think I’m bringing back a whole flask; but there was nothing, absolutely nothing to be had down there anymore—and there isn't even snow in this cursed land to rub your nose with when it begins to freeze.” — Then he began to sing a hymn aloud.

The colonel was sitting in the gorge shelter of the redoubt. His face was bandaged, only his left eye looking out between the white linens. Shot after shot struck the fortification, but it had so far been spared from the heavy shimose shells. The Japanese attacking batteries had changed their positions during the night, so that the Russians could determine the approximate direction from the flashes of fire, but could not judge the effect of their shots in the darkness. Zeroing in on the enemy's possible positions while it was still daylight was something no one had thought of. Thus, fire on the Russian redoubt had been ceased—being futile; officers and men sat in the covered dugouts and let the bullets patter above their heads into the protective frozen layer of earth. Most of the men were asleep. Vasily Stepanovich stepped into the dugout. His bashlyk was frozen to his beard; mouth and nose were covered with ice. He was gasping for breath, for the march on all fours had not agreed with him. He took the shaggy papakha from his head and walked toward the corner of the dugout, where a small icon of the Virgin Mary was wedged between massive wooden posts {p. 239}—before it burned a little red lamp. It was the only light in the dugout. Firewood had long since ceased to exist here, only dried dung for boiling tea.

The artillery sergeant bowed to the ground before the icon of the Virgin Mary, crossed himself three times, then went to his colonel and reported what General Schwerin had ordered him to report. “Very well, we shall wait. Now go, Vasily, to the forward works as well and tell our people there. Hold out until tomorrow, then God will provide further help.” This time Vasily took a man with him who guided him to the batteries on the sopkas located farther forward—whether he reached them, I do not know. In the following days, these positions too were taken by the Japanese, and the Third Corps retreated toward Shihuicheng.

Yet these brave people did hold out and wait until they were ordered to retreat. Why God did not help them is another question entirely!

We fast-paced Europeans find it hard to enter into such ways of thinking. We lack the vast, flat, monotonous milieu in which these children of the steppe grow up, the endless snowfields in which space loses its value and whose barrenness would scarcely make it worthwhile to utilize the long summer day, let alone the few daylight hours in winter, for any intensive labor. These are the geographic factors acting upon the spirit of the man; alongside them, we lack the naive outlook of the Russian peasant, cultivated through the deliberate stultification of the people, who imagines the source of all that exists and all that is to come to lie in the omnipotence of God and the Tsar.

Morale and endurance, indispensable though they are, do not suffice in the field when military training is lacking, and in the Russian army this stood at a low level. Above all, marksmanship training was completely inadequate. The Transbaikal Cossacks, the Don, Ural, and Orenburg Cossacks, and at times also the Caucasian foot Cossacks were excellent marksmen, since like the Boers they are peoples of hunters. The Russian infantry as a whole, however, shot poorly. The soldier is uneducated; he can scarcely read the numbers on the rear sight of his rifle, which {p. 240} in his clumsy hand is far too complicated a machine; I have met men who did not know what the rear sight notch and front sight on the barrel were for!¹) That the Russian is an uncomprehending rider and a poor horse groom, and that it is only thanks to the excellent {p. 241} and hardened horse stock that the good beasts held out to the end, has been discussed in greater detail elsewhere.

::: notes
¹) As is well known, shooting results in actual combat have hitherto been very meager in all campaigns, and the expenditure of ammunition in any major engagement has never stood in anything even approaching a proper proportion to the extent of the losses inflicted on the enemy. This is attributable to the fact that, as experience shows, troops in wartime almost always shoot high. In earlier times this could come as no surprise, since musketry was then treated very neglectfully in all armies; but from virtually untrained riflemen, who even in peacetime failed to meet the then so modest requirements, one could absolutely not expect them to achieve satisfactory shooting results under the difficult conditions of actual combat. After all armies, however, having drawn on the lessons of the South African campaign, had decided to devote appropriate attention to musketry and to regard this previously so neglected branch of military training as the most important, it might well have been expected that the recent campaign would show better shooting results. Yet this was not the case, for enormous quantities of ammunition were always required to achieve the objective of an engagement, the resupply and replacement of which caused the greatest difficulties.

As an example of what great sacrifices of ammunition had to be made for very meager results, one may cite Rennenkampf's firefight at Dapu, in which the Russians, with 2,000 rounds, inflicted on the enemy a loss of three horses.

The phenomenon of *shooting high*, which had hitherto always been observed, also emerged in striking fashion in this campaign, the cause of which can be seen solely and exclusively in *faulty aiming*. Now, it would be quite incorrect to conclude from the constant shooting over the enemy that the soldier, owing to the varied psychological influences of combat, does not aim at all, but merely brings his rifle to his shoulder mechanically and pulls the trigger; for in that case, the dispersion of projectiles in massed fire would have to be distributed equally on average in all directions. The consistent deviation of the projectiles in *one and the same direction*, upwards, shows rather that aiming definitely takes place, though incorrectly, namely with a coarse sight.

This deficiency can be traced solely and exclusively to the sighting device then used on army rifles, the wedge front sight, taking a fine sight on which, due to its pointed shape, is quite difficult even under the most favorable conditions, but in the excitement of combat and
:::

How difficult it must have been, given the generally low level of education, to obtain suitable personnel for the technical troops is clear enough. The artillery, with regard to both officers and enlisted men, is the elite force. I also encountered very skilled and resourceful men in the border guard. The Russian's slight sense of order reveals itself first of all in the fact that he cannot accustom himself to march discipline in our sense. Every detachment marches with stragglers, yet all reach their destination.

Just as with order, the Russian also has little sense of thorough cleanliness. His clothing, including that of the officers, was often greasy and stained; a burst seam or even just a missing button escapes his notice.

A bad trait of Russian soldiers is their penchant for begging. At all the railway stations, I was begged from by loitering soldiers. My travelling companion on {p. 242} the outward journey, Engineer Sch., told me that he had given alms to a beggar in Omsk. When a passing officer saw this, he called out to Sch. that he had been wounded at Liaoyang, pointed to his Cross of St. George, and likewise asked for alms! When I rode through Tieling during the retreat from Mukden, I was beset by whole swarms of begging soldiers. On the return journey, the railway trains on those stretches where bridges or other installations had to be crossed were manned by Landsturm men (*druzhina*). They stood with fixed bayonets in the corridors and at the door of the dining car, and had to ensure that none of the fellow passengers dropped a bomb onto the tracks. These men, too, begged almost without exception. At first they would usually ask for a newspaper. "What am I to do with the newspaper now, sir? After all, I can't read; give me ten kopecks instead!" was then the second request.

::: notes
which, with the fatigue of the eye that naturally sets in during hours-long firefights, becomes downright impossible.

The greatest importance must unquestionably be attached to any new sighting device that facilitates aiming for the marksman and, above all, prevents that so dangerous overshooting of the enemy by offering the man the ability to align the front sight quickly and reliably level.

Numerous inventors have already occupied themselves with designing such a sight; yet these projects, often quite ingenious, suffered for the most part from the defect of being too complicated, and therefore entirely unusable for military service.

The Royal Hungarian Hussar Captain Domobran Kokotović has now earned the credit of solving this problem in splendid fashion. The *universal front sight* designed by him must, on account of its *simplicity* and immense advantages, be described as a truly battle-worthy sighting device which, particularly against modern combat targets, brings about an enormous improvement in hit accuracy.

The new sight has admittedly not yet had an opportunity to prove its merits in actual combat; in numerous foreign armies, however, practical trials are currently under way, the results of which to date fully confirm the universal front sight's high utility in the field.
:::

[Illustration: Changing of the guard.]

Money for vodka—that tempted many a man to sell his only fur coat, his boots, even his army-issue trousers. To be sure, the number of these men derelict in their duty was not large, but it was precisely these scoundrels who loitered around the stations and staging posts {p. 243} and tried to arouse the pity of travellers and strangers. Not infrequently do we find among Russian soldiers a propensity for theft. This, too, seems to be a consequence of his faulty upbringing. In a country where a Grand Duke Sergei embezzles the felt blankets donated to the wounded, and where, despite all piety, it is customary to steal the jewels from altarpieces, where officials live largely by theft—for the autocratic system permits no oversight of the public purse—it can hardly come as a surprise if the muzhik, too, steals without seeing anything particularly evil in doing so! Yet in this regard as well, much may have been painted in blacker colours than it really was. I travelled and marched with Russian soldiers, bivouacked with them—*nothing was ever stolen from me by these people.* During the retreat from Mukden, I entrusted my horse to a Cossack of Prince Dolgorukov’s sotnia so that he might take his chances and try to deliver it to Captain Count Szeptycki at General Rennenkampf’s headquarters. The man could be certain of never seeing me again in his life; in the appalling chaos of that time, any check on whether the horse had perished during the retreat or not would have been impossible—and yet the man faithfully delivered my property. I had bad experiences, on the other hand, with the Caucasians. The people from these regions are not only thieves, but—*if it can be done without any particular danger*—robbers as well. During the retreat from Mukden, Caucasian Cossacks plundered worse than the Honghuzi; it was Caucasian Cossacks under General Kozakovsky who attempted to loot the wagons of the Russo-Chinese Bank at Puhe, and it was mostly Caucasians whom General Linevich had executed by firing squad in Tieling immediately after taking supreme command of the army. Yet in resourcefulness and resilience the Caucasian is far superior to the Russian, and far cleaner in his appearance and dress as well.

When judging the clothing of the Russian soldier in the field, one must keep in mind above all a fact that will perhaps surprise the reader: the Russian {p. 244} cannot tolerate the cold or, better said, he fears the cold more than the southerner does, and strives and knows how to protect himself against it better than the latter. “They are no cold weather people,” an American quite rightly remarked of them. It seems as though we still had something left to draw upon from the rays of the sun stored within us, while the Russian has already surrendered his little bit of warmth to the perpetual frost and winter. He spends the harsh winters mostly in an overheated room whose windows are never opened. The fear of fresh air goes so far that even the dining car of the Trans-Siberian express train—which, it may be noted, served as a dormitory for the waiters—was never aired out. Washing or even bathing outdoors during the cold months—as the Japanese did—is something the Russian shrinks from, which is again a sign of a lack of willpower. The army was clothed in a manner corresponding to this aversion to the cold and the unusually harsh weather conditions. On his head the soldier wore his fur cap, the papakha, pulled down over his

[Illustration: Wounded men with the Cross of St. George.] {p. 245} ears; his neck, chin, and nose were protected by that excellent woollen scarf, the bashlyk; his body down to the knees was enveloped in a warm leather fur coat without shiny buttons or other frippery; his feet were stuck into the only footwear that truly protects against cold—the valenki—felt boots that contain not a single piece of leather. On his hands he wore large lined mittens. All the photographs accompanying this book clearly show, I think, that the army was *superbly equipped against the cold*, and that all the talk and writing about half-naked, frozen Russian soldiers was either malicious fabrication or stemmed from letters that deceitful reservists sent home to arouse pity and wheedle out money.

For military purposes, this clothing had several disadvantages. Above all, it was too heavy and hindered the man in moving freely, in aiming properly, and in pulling the trigger. As advantageous as the absence of any shiny metal on uniform and weapons was, the colour and shape of the misshapen, large white or black fur caps were equally unfavourable. These offered an excellent target to the enemy and had to be wrapped around with the grey bashlyk during combat, which only made the entire headgear even more clumsy.

I had frequent opportunities to see captured Japanese. In their yellow caps, little yellow overcoats, and yellow gaiters—though admittedly with an excellent snow hood pulled over their heads—they seemed, beside the fur-swathed Russians, to be marching along as if in summer overcoats.

Just as suitably as against the cold, the Russian dresses in the hot summer, when white linen caps and linen blouses replace the heavy winter furs. In place of the valenki he wears high boots, which may well be a Russian national tradition, but is less practical from a military standpoint. The high leather boot, even for the cavalryman, is not a battle-worthy type of footwear. If the boot gets wet, the man is often unable to pull it off his foot for weeks. It is strange that army administrations so rarely take as an example the experiences of tourists regarding suitable footwear and clothing, but still {p. 246} cling to traditional ways! Today the whole world already walks, travels, rides, and engages in sports in natural leather shoes and natural leather gaiters, and yet there are still poor soldiers who freeze in winter and languish in summer in brilliantly polished boots! There is surely no sportsman who would wear the same outfit for ice sports and rowing, yet there are dress regulations that know only one cap, one coat, one pair of trousers, etc., for winter and summer, for south and north!

[Illustration: Captured Japanese at the railway station in Harbin.]

The reality of war soon does away with all unnecessary and impractical frippery, forcing the soldier's clothing to adapt to the geographical conditions of the country in question. Kuropatkin had not only permitted soldiers and officers to dress in whatever way best protected them from the cold, but even had local footwear and padded Chinese garments with long sleeves purchased from Chinese contractors and distributed to his soldiers.

All of this shows that the Russian high command {p. 247} did everything possible to clothe the army in the field irreproachably and beyond complaint.

An interesting comparison was afforded by the various uniforms of the military attachés. Here it was clearly evident that the representatives of young armies, such as the United States, Chile, Argentina, Sweden, Norway, and others, were uniformed far more practically and in a manner better suited to military purposes than those of the so-called old military powers, who cannot part with the paintbox, yet when it comes to the most important thing:

[Illustration: Captured Koreans at the railway station in Gongzhuling.]

forget the “field serviceability of the uniform in winter as in summer”!

*The provisioning of the Russian army was excellent.* One needed only to look at the well-fed faces of the men to be convinced that all the calamitous reports of starving and destitute soldiers were sheer fabrication. A further proof of the good and regular nourishment was the state of health of the army. That there are certain times in war when regular provisioning {p. 248} becomes impossible is self-evident. The conditions for provisioning the army were not unfavourable despite the considerable distance from the mother country. Manchuria is a highly cultivated, fertile land; barley, gaoliang, and bean cake are its export articles, the rivers abound with fish, and in the north of the country as well as in neighbouring Mongolia there are numerous herds of cattle. German contractors supplied cattle for slaughter from China. From Russia, mainly cabbage and sugar had to be brought forward. Supplying the cabbage caused the greatest difficulty, as Quartermaster General Guber told me, since the soldier is accustomed to this vegetable, but it was not to be had in Manchuria.

From an operational standpoint, the supply situation was likewise not unfavorable. An army on the retreat is easier to provision than an advancing one, since it continually draws closer to the stores stockpiled to the rear. Furthermore, the entire campaign was composed of a series of operational pauses, during which provisioning was all the easier. During battles and other operations, however, the commissariat’s supply arrangements very often failed as a result of the army’s poorly organized supply train. At Mukden, the *one-month supply of provisions for the entire army had to be burned*; after such catastrophes there were, to be sure, days of hunger as well. The Russian soldier is a hearty eater, yet he does not grumble when he must go without food for days on end. His midday meal, humble though it may be, the Russian soldier never takes without thanking God for it. How often did this make me think of our Hungarian soldiers, who throw their midday meal into the refuse if it does not happen to suit their spoiled palates. Often it was already late in the evening before the men reached their march objective or returned from their positions. Patiently they then waited until the unlimberable kitchen wagon—an excellent, commendable arrangement—came driving up. They passed the time until then with innocent banter; one or another would pull out his harmonica, and there were always men who were not yet too tired to dance to it—perhaps they did so merely to keep warm. Once {p. 249} the steaming little field kitchen had finally arrived, then—without jostling or shoving—the men formed up radially around the wagon; no officer was present, no non-commissioned officer shouted about, everything proceeded quietly and patriarchally. When at last the final man in turn had been served, they took off their caps as if on command and sang a brief prayer.

Yet even with meager fare they never forgot God; they thanked Him just the same for a midday meal that consisted only of tea and black hardtack. Never was I witness to a quarrel among them, nor did I ever hear a coarse word from these good-natured men. Not so the Caucasians. The proprietor as well as the waiters of the military attaches’ dining car were Georgians—in their appearance and speech bearing the closest resemblance to Israelites. Not a night passed without a brawl breaking out among these people, and even in the most peaceful interaction they shouted and clamored at one another. At the railway station in Harbin there was never such a commotion as when Caucasian infantry arrived.

As for the role that tea plays in the Russian army, both as a refreshing beverage and as a preventive measure against various infectious diseases, I have already discussed this at the beginning of this book.

It remains now only to say a few words about the all too well-known and frequently discussed drunkenness of the Russian, and here too let it be said straightaway that in this respect as well the muzhik is better than his reputation—the dangerous drunkards are to be sought not in the ranks of the common soldiers, but *much further up*! That the Russian soldier, when he gets liquor, drinks it and, being unaccustomed to it, quickly becomes intoxicated, is certain; but that happens elsewhere as well. Cases of drunken soldiers in the area of the operational army were rare. After exhausting marches and engagements, when one or more jugs of *tschinn* fell into the men’s hands, they quenched their thirst with this fiendish beverage and soon lay on the ground as if senseless. When during the retreat from Mukden the military attaches proceeded farther north with their train from Tieling {p. 250}, the train had to stop suddenly at Kaiyuan. The Chunchuses were said to have cut the line north of the station, and an attack by them on the train was to be expected. Weapons were now sought out, and the Russian escort soldiers in the baggage car were also to be fetched. But they were so drunk that they could scarcely be awakened. Such and similar instances certainly occurred, but they permit no conclusions to be drawn about the troops on duty facing the enemy. Unfortunately, the Russian officer is rarely suited to set the enlisted man a good example in abstinence from alcohol. While today scarcely anyone remains in doubt that even moderate consumption of alcohol impairs the physical performance of the body, I repeatedly heard Russian officers claim that the Japanese always get drunk before their attacks in order to more easily overcome cold and exertion!

The whole country over which the war down there was fought was of no concern at all to the poor muzhik. Never in his life had he longed for Manchuria or for sovereign rights over Korea; the Japanese was unknown to him. That this man, leaving his homeland and kin, was supposed to have gone off to war with enthusiasm could hardly be expected of him. That his military training had been neglected was not his fault either; but what was demanded of him, he did. Willingly and faithfully he held out—and waited until God would help. That God did not help was not his fault either—for he, the muzhik, had prayed enough.

{p. [257]}

## Fighting with the Eastern Corps.

### To the Headquarters of General Rennenkampf.

The direct road from Bajiazi to Machuntan—the headquarters of General Rennenkampf—was already threatened by the Japanese on the evening of the 3rd, and since the higher commands changed their locations almost daily during this period, it was in any case advisable to ride first to the First Army Command at Shihouicheng to obtain more precise information about the Eastern Corps there.

Thousands of campfires burned in and around the village, which was covered by a reddish, shimmering cloud of smoke billowing high into the firmament—a dependable guide for a rider coming out of the mountains in the dark of night. No camp guard stopped me as I rode into Shihouicheng. The First Army Command was already sitting on packed trunks. The mood was depressed. Once again they were to retreat; part of the train had already been sent to Fushun on the Hun River.

I was still able to meet Rennenkampf in Machuntan the following day. Early in the morning I rode on. The path led to the southeast, deeper and deeper into the mountains, along the positions that were still stubbornly held by General Bachinsky and Prince Tumanov's foot Cossacks.

I met General Bachinsky in Xiluolingzi, a small place wedged between the steep walls of a side valley of the Hun River. Under his command were: two battalions of the 36th East Siberian Rifle Regiment, three battalions of the Kulikovsky Infantry Regiment (a battalion of which we saw over in Bajiazi!), and the Ninth Mountain Battery.

{p. 252}

As we were standing in front of the general's fanza, the battery rattled past—in the direction of Shihouicheng! Why was the battery being pulled out of the battle now, leaving their own infantry defenseless against the fire of the Japanese batteries?

Our battery had received orders from army command to withdraw to Bajiazi—to replace it, two field batteries were supposed to come to us from Shihouicheng! And so three batteries were marching back and forth behind the positions—thanks to the headlessness of a general staff section, while the troops in the positions were bleeding to death.

General Bachinsky's outermost outposts were on the Xidalin Pass that day; southwest of them, on the Gulingzi Pass, were those of Prince Tumanov—several companies of foot Cossacks.

Bending back in a hook to the north from the Bachinsky group as far as Beipingtai stood Colonel Aleyev with roughly a regiment.

General Bachinsky himself did not know exactly on March 3 whether he still belonged to General Rennenkampf's group or had already been subordinated to the Third Corps, with which a part of the troops organizationally under his command was already located.

Thus here too we see once again the battalion-by-battalion dissipation across a long, thin line that showed neither the character of a planned offensive nor the will for a stubborn defense—of group or general reserves nothing was to be seen here, and to the rear in Shihouicheng I had nowhere encountered stronger forces that could have been used as an "army reserve" to support these exposed forward groups.

My confidence in old Linevich's "demonstration of strength" began to fade more and more, the more opportunity I had to gain insight into the operational orders of his general staff section.

The further way to Rennenkampf led over hill and dale, cross-country; from hour to hour, "flying posts"—*letuchie*—were stationed. They each consisted of a Cossack officer and ten to fifteen horsemen; their task was to secure the {p. 253} communication among the commands. Everywhere I was warmly received, for each of the officers—there were also some among them who had previously served in the Guards—was glad to hear something new. Nor could I tell them very much, but for them, who had not left the mountains for more than five months and hardly received their letters from Mukden once a month, even what I had heard and seen in Petersburg before my departure was news. That barely twenty kilometers away as the crow flies bitter fighting was raging at the Gaotuling Pass—that they did not know!

Each of them was kind enough to escort me to the next post. But each of them also wanted to show off his prowess as a horseman on this occasion—by setting off at the most unreasonable hard trot and not pulling up until the next station.

With the first I went along; the subsequent ones I calmly let ride ahead and then explained to them that riding at a hard trot on the road was a cavalry vice that even our battalion adjutants were slowly beginning to abandon.

It must have been six o'clock in the evening when we reached Machuntan. The village lies in a narrow gorge; it seemed to me much more solidly built than the Manchurian villages I had seen so far. The walls were made of that dark grey stone which here, the further east one goes, replaces the brown loam. The houses enclosed large courtyards in which troops were bivouacking. On the roads was once again the familiar sight of ammunition and wounded transports. An autocannon was just being brought down from the mountain; it had shot off all its rounds—there was no more ammunition for it. Rennenkampf's headquarters was easy to recognize. A large temple compound with front and side courtyards—in the second courtyard a large fanza on a stone terrace. On the right lived the general with his chief of staff; on the left was the general staff section.

Szeptycki was likewise here. He had ridden over with Rennenkampf from the right to the left wing, where he witnessed the critical situation at the Dalin Pass.

{p. 254}

Surprised in front by the enemy, Alekseyev had evacuated the pass, but the retreat was already threatened as well. The troops began crowding back along the narrow road, where a few versts further to the rear trains of all kinds were jammed together. Rennenkampf had arrived just in time to avert a worse disaster. General Alekseyev was sent away, several battalion commanders were relieved of their command, and a bullet was promised to anyone who dared move from his post. The heights north of the road were occupied. The very same troops who had given way under their former leaders stood firm under Rennenkampf and held their own against the numerically superior Japanese. The losses, however, were terrible; some rifle companies had dwindled to twenty or thirty men, and almost every battery had lost several guns in the fighting.

General Rennenkampf greeted me most cordially and personally led me into the general staff section opposite, commending me there to the care of Lieutenant-Colonel Baron Korff.

At staff headquarters, whose chief, Colonel Gurko, was the son of the famous general, there was still complete confidence at the time, and high hopes were placed in General Linevich, who surely would soon attempt an offensive thrust with his strong reserves. That the decisive struggle of the battle was no longer being fought here on the left wing, but over by the Liaohe, was not learned by the Eastern Corps until the night of 4 March! Although exhausted, I was unable to fall asleep, being far too agitated by the shifting impressions of the day. Around midnight the officer on duty was awakened. A telegram was brought—he woke his neighbours to the right and left and read in a low voice:

“Japanese attacking from the west; battle underway for three days seven versts southwest of Mukden.” What, then, were the losses for, the bloody sacrifices of the rifle regiments—I heard the officers exclaim indignantly—if we are to be beaten on the right wing after all!

Beaten? There was nothing about that in the telegram! “Were we winning, they would have told us sooner, not three days later!” That is the Russian principle!

{p. 255}

*At a time when details about the fighting around Mukden were perhaps already known in Europe, we, a hundred-odd kilometres to the east of it, still knew nothing at all.*

That I met Binder-Kriegelstein here once again—and unfortunately for the last time—I have already mentioned earlier.

In the morning, before I rode on, I also made the acquaintance of the “town commandant” at Rennenkampf’s headquarters.

He was a retired Cossack chieftain—his greatest pleasure seemed to be beheading Chinese, whom he hated in their entirety. Anyone stopped in the mountains was searched to see whether he had any “flags” hidden in his garments. If such were found on him, it was taken as proof that he had been signalling to the Japanese over on the other side. He was brought to Machuntan, where the executioner was already waiting for him. Cossacks had volunteered for these executions, which our Cossack chieftain directed with visible relish—save that in handling the sharp sabre they fell far short of their Chinese colleagues. Often the Cossack had already swung for a blow three or more times, and the spy’s head was still hanging from his torso.

With a letter of recommendation to Colonel Zakrzewski—commander of the 2nd Chita Cossack Regiment—which Lieutenant-General Rennenkampf had given me, I was to ride on in the afternoon.

[Illustration: Observation post of the frontier guards.]

{p. [262]}

## Fighting on the Eastern Flank.

### To the Chita Cossacks.

On the night leading into March 4, the Japanese had once again pushed back the left wing of the Eastern Detachment by a contour line. Our Chinese even claimed to have seen Japanese on the mountains near Xiaoshangou, thus almost in the rear of headquarters. East of the Eastern Detachment were still the separated groups of Generals Lyubavin, Maslov, and Danilov, likewise subordinated to General Rennenkampf; there was imminent danger that they would be completely cut off from the Eastern Detachment.

“If you still want to get across to the Chita Cossacks, then make haste,” Rennenkampf told me. “Today you will still find the staff in Xidihezi—where it will be tomorrow, I might not even know myself. This afternoon a platoon of Argun Cossacks is crossing the mountains to General Lyubavin; your best course is to ride along with them!”

The officer leading the Cossacks was known to me from Mukden: he wore the Cross of St. George and seemed to be an able man. We also had a Chinese guide with us to show us the way over the mountains. Along a steep bridle path we climbed from Machuntan up toward the heights. When we reached the top, that abominable wind was once again sweeping across the stony plateau, a wind that in this country never seems to run out of breath. General Lyubavin was not far off—at the Tongshiling Pass, whose deeply depressed saddle lay about seven versts ahead of us. A wild, rugged region. From Lyubavin’s batteries we could distinctly count the guns, and we soon discovered their opponent on the heights to the south. Our Chinese guide urged haste. Once again we went down {p. 257} a stony passage and up another, through scrub and thorn hedges, the only vegetation covering these bare mountains.

We must already have been close to the Tongshiling Pass; we could distinguish the reports of the guns, with only a small deciduous wood, eking out a sparse, solitary existence here, concealing the view from us. Suddenly one of the horses—we were leading them dismounted by the reins—leaps to the side, dragging along the Cossack, who stumbles over a stone and falls full length. Immediately afterward a second bullet smacks against the yellow sandstone, shattering it into dust. We

[Illustration: Landscape west of the Tongshiling Pass.]

do not know where the shots are coming from, and turn back. Now we see that our thirty riders, whom their commander had paid little heed to during the march, had dissolved into a long line, the tail end of which was just descending from the opposite height. Yet over there, too, bullets seemed to be striking; about three or four of the men abandoned their horses and ran toward the small wood in which we were still standing. Where are they firing from? The wind howled from the east, carrying over to us only the dull boom of the artillery from the Tongshiling. Blood ran from the wounded nag’s right shoulder; it could go no farther; we left {p. 258} it behind and ran, as fast as we could make headway on the stony ground with our tired horses, down into the next valley, which we would have had to cross anyway. Now, however, we stayed down on the valley floor until we reached a tributary valley of the Hun River, where in a small village—Tongshigou, if I noted it down correctly in the haste—we met General Lyubavin. We had lost two of the horses. Toward evening, their riders went back to fetch the saddles, which are, after all, the property of the Cossacks.

No one cared any longer who had fired the shots or where they had come from. “Honghuzi!” was the simple answer to that.

General Lyubavin, who had the reputation of being a cavalry general, was not a very dashing cavalry figure. Short, fat, and somewhat greasy. The officer had a large, thick roll to deliver to him, which he now carefully opened before us. “At last they are sending us maps,” he said, already pleased, but soon his face flushed crimson with righteous anger, and with a fury I would never have suspected in the genial gentleman, he slammed the whole bundle onto the table, sending the map sheets flying across the floor of the fanza. Then he went out. We picked up the sheets; there were a well-counted thirty maps of—Sandepu, where a month earlier the great battle had taken place, seventy versts from here on the opposite flank!

Now only one stage separated me from the Second Chita Regiment, with which I was to see out the remainder of the campaign. This last stretch I rode in the pleasant company of a Guards officer, a yesaul and commander of the 2nd Sotnia of the said regiment, which was tasked with maintaining the flying post between Tongshigou and General Danilov—northeast of the latter was my destination for today: Changdahezi, the station of the regimental staff. We might have been riding for barely half an hour when two Cossacks came toward us. “Where are you heading?” “To Yaogou, to the 3rd Sotnia, Prince Alexander Dolgorukov. We come from General Danilov and are carrying the orders for tomorrow.” “We are going to attack Qingjiapuzi,” the second added proudly. “And {p. 259} the regiment?” I asked. “That I don’t know,” the Cossack replied, “I haven’t been there.” “Do you know what,” I said to my companion, “I will ride with these two Cossacks to the 3rd Sotnia. I am free, I can go where I please, my propusk is made out for the Second Chita Regiment, and besides, I carry a letter of recommendation from General Rennenkampf with me.” “In that case, though, you still have a good stretch of road ahead of you today—Yaogou is far, you won’t get there before nightfall, and tomorrow morning things will move right along.” “Then I would have a favor to ask of you, Yesaul; would you permit one of your men to take the heavy bag from my horse’s saddle, and my camera will also be unnecessary to me. Could both remain with the regimental staff until I happen to arrive there?” “With pleasure, but make sure to stuff your pockets thoroughly with chocolate!”—“Do you have good food there?” he interjected, asking one of the Cossacks. “Oh, food, sir, there’d be enough of that, it’s only the ‘little water’ that’s missing!” The repacking was quickly finished; I had no map of the area I was now entering—yet worth ten times more than a map was the natural sense of direction of the Cossack, who, like a homing pigeon, always found the right way straight ahead across unfamiliar valleys and heights.

Once again I was heading into the unknown, toward what lay ahead in anticipation. I felt as easy and free as I had back then when I rode away from Huangshan. Soon I began to chat with my two new companions. They did not have much to tell; both had only arrived a few weeks earlier from the 1st sotnia, which had been stationed to the rear near Tieling as line-of-communication troops since December. To my surprise, one of them asked me if I knew German—and then began to stammer out a broken jargon that he claimed to have learned in a “German town.” His geographical lexicon was so poorly stocked, however, that despite all my efforts I could not figure out where this “German town” was located—and what its name was, he had forgotten himself.

We had probably been riding for two hours in the dark night when we reached Yaogou. A sentry challenged us and led me to the officers, who were sitting together over tea in a {p. 260} fanza. My arrival aroused surprise—which struck me almost as suspicion. My papers were soon inspected—the precaution of carrying my railway pass with its affixed photograph also proved its worth here—and warm handshakes soon showed me that I was welcome.

### Attack of Prince Dolgorukov's Detachment

The sun had not yet risen above the dark, jagged heights when the 3rd sotnia assembled in the narrow lanes of Yaogou.

Dolgorukov did not fail to send a few men ahead to send back reports to us about the enemy; it was the first time that I had seen a Cossack detachment march off in good order and properly screened, and with the dispatch service not neglected; this inspired confidence in me toward the detachment and its commander. Behind us marched the sotnia of the 2nd Argun Cossack Regiment, and to our flank half a company of the 24th Infantry Regiment. Its scout detachment had already set out before us; we caught up with it along the way, but let it proceed ahead again onto the heights flanking the road, while we remained down in the valley.

Our route led along a river that flowed now against one rock face, now against the other, so that we had to cross it frequently.

The ice was smooth and hard, so that the wind, which was raging once again, drove the little horses before it across the slick surface. This time we still envied the infantrymen, who with sure footing in their rough felt boots marched across the slippery expanse, in which the fiery-red ball of the sun, now rising in the east above the heights, reflected its first warming rays.

It had been very cold in the fanza; the chill was still deep in my bones.

In Dolgorukov I observed that diligence and keen interest in the task which, unfortunately, I found lacking only too often in most other {p. [267]} [Illustration: Fallen Cossack.]

{p. [268]}

{p. 263}

Russian officers. Repeatedly he rode forward to the vanguard and gave instructions to its commander; not a single Chinese did he let pass without questioning—one of our Cossacks acted as interpreter; he was constantly studying the map and explaining the situation to me as far as it was known to him. On our right, that is to the west of us, General Danilov stood with six battalions; we had been hearing his artillery since sunrise. Behind us to the north stood General Maslov with four battalions, a few guns, and the 3rd and 6th sotnias of the Chita Cossack Regiment. His task was to block the road to Yingpan.

Thorough military education by our standards Esaul Dolgorukov probably did not possess either; he had previously served in the Guards in Petersburg and had later been attached to the embassy in Constantinople to learn Oriental languages. Yet what he needed to be a capable cavalry officer his active, open mind had soon acquired in the course of the campaign. *A clear head and steady nerves* in a cavalry officer will always make up for a certain lack of formal military schooling, whereas a horseman made short-sighted and nervous by rigid routine and the nitpicking inspection drill is utterly useless before the enemy. Thus among the former Guards officers assigned here to the Cossack regiments there were many whose military knowledge was just as full of gaps as that of the original Cossack officers, and yet they were far more useful than the latter. They were men of the world with quick perception, whereas the others had lost all elasticity of thought and action through the monotonous life of barracks and taverns.

Around 7 o'clock in the morning we thought we heard Japanese artillery fire from the south—and still the horsemen sent ahead had brought us no word of the enemy. I say horsemen sent ahead deliberately here, and not “reconnaissance patrol,” for such things did not really exist among the Cossacks. Dolgorukov had the right instinct; annoyed that he had still received no report about the enemy, whom he was bound to be very close to by now, he rode forward to the vanguard himself. “You see,” he said to me, “that is where our Cossacks fall short; they are dashing, daring fellows, they would {p. 264} go through fire for me—but of dispatch service as handled in modern cavalries, they haven't the foggiest notion! I sent the best men of my sotnia ahead, but knowing the Cossacks, they've spotted a few Japanese somewhere and are now riding after them until they've cut off their heads or ended up lying there themselves with a bullet in the belly—that they are supposed to send me reports seems secondary to them—and nobody ever taught them, either!”

Just north of Tesindsa, the Cossack Dzhenik came toward us and reported that south of the village of Tesindsa there was a “pereval”—pass—held by the Japanese. At the same time, two other Cossacks arrived and reported that two Japanese companies were advancing toward the heights southwest of the said locality. “And this village here, Tesinda?” “It is still clear of the Japanese—but we had better not go in,” the Cossacks said.

The pereval, clearing the Japanese from which was our immediate objective, lay a little more than a thousand paces south ahead of us; the path there led up a broad, gently rising slope. To our left were steep heights covered with scrub; to our right, the advance of two companies had been reported. We had to finish off the Japanese at the pereval before they struck our right flank.

Any other Cossack commander would certainly have tried his luck again with the famous *lava*—an attack by cavalry detachments dispersed in swarms—only to realize, after several unsuccessful charges, that cavalry, surprise attacks aside, is powerless against intact infantry detachments, and especially so when they have dug themselves into a defensive position.

Dolgorukov had drawn lessons from several such botched cavalry charges and decided that the infantry should bypass the pereval. With the cavalry, which could not have made any headway on the steep heights anyway, he intended to dismount for a firefight and keep firing at the enemy up on the pereval until shots from our own infantry could be heard, whereupon he intended to have them mount and charge.

{p. 265}

It was truly gratifying to find someone at last who devised a battle plan; after all, one had grown accustomed to seeing only aimless advances that bounced off the first—naturally always “unforeseen”—obstacle and came to nothing.

It was a wonderful morning, the sun shone in southern splendor, for the first time in ages a few green trees could be seen on the mountain slopes, and the wind had also finally ceased blowing. The guns to the right in the mountains and from the Japanese over there did boom more sharply than the blank rounds of peacetime exercises, yet the day was so beautiful, so peaceful, the men were so calm and indifferent, that I kept thinking I had a maneuver before me in which Esaul Dolgorukov had to demonstrate his fitness to become a staff officer. From Tesinda, too, the Chinese were now coming out, curious to see what would happen.

In a tone more intimate than commanding, Dolgorukov communicated his plan to the commander of the Argun Cossacks and to the infantry captain, who also seemed to agree with it—that Dolgorukov was the cleverest among them they had to feel for themselves; soon the infantry had vanished into the scrub of the heights to our left.

Now, however, from the commander to his men, the Cossacks! I myself have disparaged them much in this book already and will do so again later—and yet I must acknowledge that these small, daring horsemen have in them the makings of the finest soldiers! Once again I saw how the men burned with desire to come to grips with the enemy at last—an enemy whose position they knew; not like yesterday, when the carelessness of their commander had exposed them to the bullets of an unseen foe.

No one had paid any attention to the sotnias standing a few hundred paces behind us, no one had given them an order, and already they scented the enemy on the heights and were trying to get a clear picture of him. Right and left, individual horsemen galloped forward, skillfully taking cover along the steep valley wall or in depressions in the ground, but in front of the village, too, there was suddenly shouting. Four Cossacks {p. 266} had crept around the Chinese and now held in their midst a stout man with a long queue, who was shouting and gesticulating violently.

Why had I sent away both of my cameras yesterday! This morning would have compensated me for all the dreary days during which my cameras had had to lie idle. The fat Chinese whom the four Cossacks were now dragging back amid the screaming and howling of the whole village rabble would have made an excellent picture! Our Cossacks know their way around the Chinese here; the fat fellow must serve them

[Illustration: My white horse.]

as a hostage to ensure that no Hunhutzes fall upon our flank from the village.

“Two Japanese squadrons, Esaul, are stationed up on the mountain,” the Cossacks reported to their commander, which was what they had learned from the Chinese and what the horsemen who had ridden ahead confirmed soon afterward.

In a broad line we now advanced at a sharp trot, off the road across stubble fields. I gazed intently at the pereval, but could see no enemy.

Suddenly a thin white cloud rises along the crest, followed immediately by the sharp crack of a rifle volley. {p. 267} -- Halt! -- No further command was given. The horses were left behind in groups. Those holding them struck them on the shins with the handles of their knouts, and the animals lay down. I tried the same with my white horse, which, however, instead of lying down, began striking out at me with its forelegs. Stupid beast! If you want to get shot, stay standing and show your white coat to the Japanese, I will not keep you company! and, holding the white horse by the lengthened reins, I lay down between the furrows of the field.

Regular volleys now flashed from the mountain, to which our Cossacks replied with lively skirmish fire; each man fired on his own, for there was no fire control among them. I was lying rather far off, near the village, and could observe well. Up to this point only our third sotnia had been under fire, but now the Argun Sotnia also began to shoot, having skillfully moved forward along the hollow and caught the enemy on the Pereval in a crossfire from there. "Hurrah!" now rang out from the third sotnia—almost simultaneously the men leaped up and rushed forward—yet only a few paces! I could clearly see how, after the very first steps, two men flung their arms wide, dropped their rifles, and collapsed with heads thrust forward. The Japanese seemed to have been waiting for this moment; a terrible rapid fire rattled down from the heights. As was later determined, no more than seven men from the third sotnia were left lying, seriously and lightly wounded. The range was still too great for rapid fire, but the moral effect was unmistakable. The sotnia had advanced no more than fifty paces, while individual Cossacks, faced with the bullets striking thickly around them, had already earlier thrown themselves down into the sheltering furrows.

I had never before seen combat from so close. My heart pounded with excitement; my thoughts, my feelings, my nerves, everything in me seemed to vibrate; something inside me cheered when the fire of our men, who had now slowly gathered again in a line along the field ridge, intensified into a deafening rapid fire, and I grew anxious {p. 268} whenever it began to die down here or there. And that is how it is with the men as well—the more shooting there is to their right and left, the safer they feel!

My grey had used these gripping moments to tear himself free with a sudden jerk, and was now galloping after the other horses, which I likewise saw running to the rear in wild flight! Bullets had surely struck among the held horses; they had bolted up and were now—more sensible than the humans—seeking a safe spot farther to the rear. Our two squadrons now remained lying in the position described above for almost two hours! Indeed, in war things move far more slowly than on maneuvers! The Argun Sotnia did manage to advance across one more fold in the ground, but for the time being no one thought of advancing any further. If the cavalryman is to advance to the attack with his carbine, then give him a bayonet to go with it as well—it has little practical value in combat, to be sure, but all the greater moral value!

The ordinary Russian always regards the fire fight as merely a preparation for finally charging with the "shtuk," as he calls the bayonet.

At last—I noted the time on my watch immediately—there was a crack from the mountain above, just above the Japanese. Our infantry company! A lively hurrah from our Cossacks, who now, without waiting for another command, considered their work as infantry at an end and crawled back to their horses. The runaway bunch that my grey had joined was back again—the horses here are just as phlegmatic as the people!

Dolgorukov had assigned me a Cossack that morning as an orderly—he was now standing before me with my horse, and pointing up at the heights, he drew my attention to the fact that the Japanese were retreating.

These sons of nature have enviably good eyes! I had always fancied that I had good eyesight, but to my annoyance I had to admit that with field glasses I often could not find what the Cossacks spotted with the naked eye. But here there was no more time to take out the glasses; already we were off {p. 269} at a gallop—that is, as fast as the short-legged Cossack horses could manage—toward the Pereval.

The fire of our infantry seemed to have taken the enemy completely by surprise, for I did not hear a single bullet whistle over our heads.

How magnificent this charge would have been on swift, powerful horses, over level ground, with a few fine obstacles along the way—just as a cavalryman is accustomed to dream of battle! Here the ground was wretched, hard-frozen plowed field, where the little horses soon lost any desire to gallop. From the heights of the pass only sporadic shots rang out; our infantry on the opposite height was still firing after the retreating Japanese. The Argun Sotnia had been closer to the pass and had reached the crest on the right ahead of us; when we reached the enemy's abandoned position half a minute later, the only sign of them was a few wounded horses running down the hollow of the saddle, limping and bleeding.

But the Argun Cossacks had made a good catch! "Yapontsy, Yapontsy!" Prince Dolgorukov called to me. He had personally led the Argun men and had been one of the first on the Pereval, where his Cossacks succeeded in cutting off two Japanese who had been slow to mount. Now the two little yellow men stood among the jubilant Cossacks. They readily surrendered their weapons. When cartridges were also demanded of them, they pointed to the empty casings lying on the ground. They were said to be two officers of the 7th Japanese Dragoon Regiment. I had barely seen them when Cossacks came riding up and reported that very close on our right flank, two Japanese infantry companies were advancing.

Our two sotnias would not have been enough against these, and the support of our infantry, which stood on the eastern heights separated from us by a deep ravine, was not to be counted on; thus the most sensible course was to withdraw to Tesinda, where we met our wounded. We had no doctor with us, so that the condition of these poor people was truly pitiable. Some were still able to proceed on horseback, {p. 270} while for those who had bullet wounds in their legs or abdomen, two arbas were already waiting; their owners, however, were still holding out for a gratuity, which Prince Dolgorukov gladly gave them just to get his men to safety. At a brisk trot we returned along the way we had come. The two arbas with the wounded drove ahead of us; there had been no time left to prepare a bed for them on the hard wagons. What agony the poor men must have suffered during that fast ride over the rough, stony track! To be sure, the feldsher had hastily bandaged their wounds, but after a short time we, riding right behind the arbas, saw the white linen turn red with seeping blood and heard the groaning—and praying—of the unfortunates.

After half an hour's trot we slowed to a walk; the path branched off to the northeast and northwest, toward Mamangou and Jaugoa. Prince Dolgorukov also thought it would be better

[Illustration: Colonel Leontin Zakrschewski, commander of the Second Chita Cossack Regiment.] {p. [277]} [Illustration: Sketch of the engagements of Yesaul Prince Dolgorukov's detachment on 5 March.]

{p. 272}

for me to present myself first to the regimental commander, Colonel Zakrschewski. Thus our paths parted here. The two squadrons withdrew to the northeast toward Mamangou, while I rode with the wounded to General Danilov's positions, whence an officer accompanied me to Chandakheza.

### With Lieutenant Colonel Slatin's Reconnaissance Detachment.

*5 March.*

The sun had long since disappeared behind the mountains when, on 5 March, I entered the low fanzah where Colonel Leontin Antonovich Zakrschewski was quartered with his adjutant. He knew of my arrival, and when I delivered General Rennenkampf's greetings to the Second Chita Cossack Regiment, he gave a military bow as though the gallant cavalry general were standing right before him.

“How and in what way can I best be of service to you?” he asked, warmly shaking my hand.

“Colonel, I should like to witness further combat action by your regiment.”

“You will have an immediate opportunity for that, Rittmeister. In about an hour, Lieutenant Colonel Slatin will be here; he is setting out tonight with the Fourth and Fifth Sotnias against the Japanese right wing. Do you have reliable weapons, Rittmeister?” I showed the colonel my small pocket revolver, which I had bought in Moscow. “That is no good at all.” — “Have the gentleman given a shashka and a good revolver!” he commanded, turning to his adjutant. “Or would you prefer a carbine instead of the revolver?” I asked for a revolver. Matters are taking a warlike turn, I thought to myself, and went to see that my grey got some oats. The animal had now been under the saddle every day from morning to evening for nearly a week, had stood outdoors every night in twenty degrees of frost, had received dry straw or at best a little gaoliang for feed, was not even shoed—and yet, even after the hard rides {p. 273} of today, was completely sound. When he saw me, he opened his mouth wide—which he always did when he was hungry. He was not handsome: small, with a short neck and large head, and a coat like a bear—but it kept him warm! How miserably our neatly groomed, smooth-coated horses would freeze in a winter campaign!

When I returned to the fanzah, Colonel Zakrschewski had finished official business with his adjutant, a long-bearded staff captain, and was just about to dress for dinner. He was a handsome, stately man, displaying that impeccable cleanliness and neatness in dress and person which those brought up to it from childhood never neglect, even under the most difficult circumstances. And it truly does not take much time to put oneself into a condition tolerable both to oneself and to one's fellows. Some officers, however, consider it especially martial to sit down to dinner greasy and dusty straight off their horse after a manoeuvre, perhaps even tossing their sweaty cap onto the table—that way, at least not a single minute of the cherished post-dinner nap is lost!

The colonel, who spoke German and French fluently, told me that he had had the opportunity in Vienna to become acquainted with the Austrian and Hungarian cavalry. “Yes, what one sees *there*, that is what I call *cavalry*!” he said, turning to his adjutant and a lieutenant colonel who, having just woken up, came over to join us from a corner of the room.

“Yet *Russian cavalry* is also better than what you see of it here in the theatre of war! For almost my entire service I was stationed on the German frontier with Russian army dragoons. Only at the beginning of the campaign did I take command of my present Cossack regiment, but I cannot reconcile myself to this Cossack horse, and *change* the Cossacks—that I cannot do either! You, Captain, will certainly see much here that will not please you. The Cossacks would, in and of themselves, be quite excellent material for the ranks, {p. 274} enduring, daring, and gifted with an admirable, instinctive sense of direction. They have deficient notions of right and wrong when it comes to mine and thine. Their obsolete organisation as well as insufficient military training during active service in the first line are to blame for their *lacking any military value that can be taken seriously*. The second-line officers are on leave for most of their service; in the meantime, they hold posts in the civil administration. In the districts of Amur,

[Illustration: Cossack patrol.]

Argun, and Nerchinsk, the officers for the second-line regiments came at the outbreak of the war from the Guards and the army cavalry, which, considering the quality of some of the original Cossack officers—you will get to know them!—could only be beneficial. Yet even among the newly arrived officers, the military education and cavalry proficiency are *not always without exception* such that they could help the Cossacks overcome their deficient training.

{p. 275}

The Cossacks bring their horses, clothing, and sabres themselves; only their rifles and ammunition are issued to them by the regiment. Like the men, their horses are excellent in their way; there can hardly be a tougher, less demanding animal than these little Siberians. Straw and a little green fodder scraped up from beneath the snow is their nourishment; they spend the freezing nights in the open, with nothing on their backs but the fully packed saddle, which the Cossack never removes from the beast. How often have I been vexed by this, yet our officers have no eye for such things. As a rider, the Cossack certainly shows a certain dash, but absolutely no understanding; senseless, purposeless running of horses into the ground, preferably at a fast trot, he considers a cavalry feat. Knowledge of horses, care, and love for the animal are lacking in all of them. As long as a nag can still hobble on three legs, it is considered fit for service, and since the poor four-legged beasts cannot scream, they are blithely ridden on despite galled backs. In spite of all this, the good beasts still hold out, even when they can barely drag their hind legs behind them. *They are no good for a charge*; they are too slow for that and have too little weight. Yet by virtue of their endurance, they would render excellent service in reconnaissance; but for that, their riders—officers included—are once again *too poorly trained!*”

During his description of the Cossacks, the colonel had become so animated that only now did he notice Lieutenant-Colonel Slatin, who had already been standing beside us for some time.

“While I explain his assignment to the lieutenant-colonel and discuss a few other matters with him, you, Captain, can sleep for a few hours,” said the colonel, pointing to a bed of gaoliang straw in the corner. Even during supper I could barely keep my eyes open, having been in the saddle almost uninterruptedly since early morning. The bed was soon prepared; as always, the tunic served as a pillow, the greatcoat as a blanket. Over by the table they sat bent over the map, tracing the lines of march; similar, ugly-sounding names of Manchurian streams and villages, which the adjutant read out in a monotonous voice from {p. 276} General Danilov’s orders. With it, the dim, flickering light of a clamped pine splinter, soporific even for someone less weary than I! And yet the deep, nerve-soothing sleep refused to come.

The images of the past days had affected me too powerfully and in too rapid succession; at the time I could take in only the glaring colours and deepest shadows; the eye was never granted time to examine intermediate tones and nuances more closely.

Yet the images are not blurred for all that; after some time they emerge again in their unfaded colouring from the back room of memory before the mind’s eye; most often when our body, tired and weary, has yielded full dominion over our being to the spirit, which is still busily preoccupied with the recent past. From the right ear to the left, and then back to the right, the sleeper tosses, pressing his eyes tightly shut as if to bind the dark god of sleep, yet in bright sunlight familiar figures already rise up in motley succession behind his lids. There I must look once more into the eyes of the Japanese soldier, who then thrusts the bomb into his abdomen; they carry past me the bleeding torso from which the Shimose tore away head and back; then Cossacks run forward again, faster and faster, raising their arms, tumbling and pitching headfirst into the brown furrows, one after another, ever wilder—then comes dark, quiet night—suddenly a jolt through my entire body, from crown to toe—my grey broke free of me with a sudden leap, and I am still lying in the furrow—Manjiapuzi, Ximapuzi, and on to Haoweizhen, the adjutant reads in an unvarying, monotone voice. Sleep, sleep! You have only a few hours left, then you must move on again. And again I try to sleep, for I am so tired!—Why did the colonel even want to give me a carbine? So we will be in action tomorrow.—Probably tomorrow night, he said.—I wonder if there are letters for me at the Mukden post office? Really I ought to write too, just a {p. 277} few lines home—but there will still be time for that tomorrow before riding off—now sleep, at last! Yes, at last I fell asleep, heavy and leaden, until Lieutenant-Colonel Slatin woke me at dawn.
