Colonel Zakrzewski had likewise woken up. As we shook hands, he told me that when leaving I should not forget to cross myself before the icon of the Mother of God on the regimental standard, which was leaning against the wall outside and beside which a Cossack stood guard.

*March 6.*

On the way to Xujiazi, where Lieutenant Colonel Slatin's two sotnias were stationed, he explained his mission to me. The forces confronting Dolgorukov's detachment were to be attacked from the south, and in addition Lieutenant Colonel Slatin was to determine how far east the Japanese flank extended.

As far as I had seen on the previous day, Prince Dolgorukov had not remained facing the enemy at all, but had withdrawn to Mamagou, which, however, was not yet known to General Danilov at the time he issued the above order.

Far more important than supporting Esaul Dolgorukov's sotnias, however, was the second part of the mission—ascertaining the easternmost Japanese advance column.

For modern cavalry on reconnaissance duty, combat can only be a means to an end! The main thing is that it approach the enemy, gain insight into his dispositions, and report what has been observed to the rear as quickly as possible so that the necessary measures can still be taken there in good time. If this insight into the enemy's dispositions can be obtained without fighting, so much the better, for combat means *loss of time*, whereas the reporting service aims at *gain of time*.

From this point of view, it must be considered inappropriate that Lieutenant Colonel Slatin's reconnaissance detachment, in addition to the vital task of "ascertaining {p. 278} the easternmost Japanese column," was also assigned a secondary task—a combat engagement to achieve a completely inconsequential success—which could not fail to distract its attention from its most important activity: reconnaissance.

That among the Cossacks reconnaissance activity was something unknown altogether, so that no attention could be distracted from it in the first place, was something I was soon to see!

Around 7 a.m. we arrived in Xujiazi, where the officers of the sotnias were awaiting us. Commander of the 4th Sotnia was Esaul Belyaev; he had been garrisoned on the Galician border many years before, and had now been serving with Cossack regiments in Siberia and Manchuria for eight years—which one could tell just by looking at him.

Commander of the 5th Sotnia was Esaul Pavel Skoropadsky; I had made the acquaintance of this charming Guard officer a month earlier in Mukden, and only regretted that he was not present here at the time, but was on leave in Harbin to visit his wife.

Besides the commander, two more officers were assigned to each sotnia. The sotnias were around a hundred horsemen strong and carried no baggage train with them apart from a pack horse with ammunition crates.

The regimental doctor was bored at headquarters, while we did not have even a feldsher at our disposal.

At 8 a.m. we set out. In order to bypass the Japanese flank, we had to swing far to the east—our destination for that day was Dapu, several dozen versts east of Dexinda. There were Russian troops neither south nor east of us, and yet we marched quite carefree into the fine, bright morning, toward the gloriously shining sun that already stood high in the east. It felt so good to see cheerful spring light again after the long, dreary winter days—even if it was bitterly cold.

The two sotnias had sent ahead a platoon as vanguard, which rode four hundred paces ahead of us—that was all we did for our security—and so it remained later on!

{p. 279}

There were no reconnaissance patrols. No one was nervous.

Thus, toward midday, we arrived at Bapanlou, where we took a one-hour rest in the courtyard of a Chinese, during which no security posts were posted. When the march resumed, another platoon went ahead as vanguard. From then on our route led south along the valley, whose river line we had to cross several times.

The area was lonely and deserted; fewer settlements than on the plain, but here they were far better built than there. If we met Chinese along the way, they came up to us and readily answered our questions. Their last words were always: "Give me rubles!"

It was already evening when, eight kilometers north of Dapu, we met a platoon of retreating Argun Cossacks—probably from the half-sotnia that had spent the night before in Dapu—though we did not ask them about it. In passing, the commander told us that an infantry column with artillery had marched into that village in the afternoon—its strength he did not know! In an hour at most, therefore, we were bound to run into the enemy—and glad of the beautiful evening descending upon us, we marched on. Even now no reconnaissance patrols were sent out, and no one became nervous. Soon afterward we encountered an infantry company marching back. It was the one that had been attached to Prince Dolgorukov the day before. It marched past us without saluting, and neither did anyone among us ask where it was coming from or whether it knew anything of the enemy!

How much further we would have marched on with this stoic calm, I do not know; fortunately, at six o'clock in the evening Dolgorukov came toward us with the third sotnia. He told us that the previous day in Tapu he had met half a sotnia of the 2nd Argun Cossack Regiment and urged its commander to advance today jointly via Qingjiapuzi and attack the enemy remaining at Tesinda from the south. The half-sotnia, however, did not comply with this request (why not an order?!), so that Dolgorukov {p. 280} without them advanced once again toward Tesinda this morning with his two sotnias and the infantry company. Arriving toward noon north of the village—roughly where we had dismounted for a firefight yesterday—it was established that the enemy had placed two guns on the height southwest of the village in addition to the two infantry companies, so that Dolgorukov was forced, without accepting battle, to beat a retreat via Mamangou. The infantry then separated from the detachment and reported back to General Maslov.¹)

We had halted in the meantime. Our commander, Lieutenant Colonel Slatin, decided to call off the further advance to Tapu—where we would have run into superior Japanese forces—and to branch off further to the east.

After a short rest in a nearby fanza, in which Dolgorukov's officers had already quartered themselves, we continued toward the northeast. We wanted to reach the next parallel valley to the east that very day, in order to attempt reconnaissance toward the south from there on the morrow. The third sotnia remained behind.

Our march became arduous. By seven o'clock it was dark in the mountains, and the small horses struggled onward with difficulty through the deep snow that covered the narrow floor of the mountain gorge, often leaving barely enough room for a single rider.

The advance guard often became stuck, so that we closed right up behind them and then had to wait again until they had gained some ground ahead. Toward nine o'clock in the evening we were finally on the other side of the ridge and had a wide valley before us. We could not see the advance guard, and the track, too, was barely discernible under the freshly fallen snow that blanketed slope and valley alike.

So we rode on, slowly feeling our way forward step by step in the darkness, until suddenly we stood right before a steep slope—it was the bluffy bank of a river. But now {p. 281} we could already hear men from the advance guard shouting for us to turn right, as a village lay barely a hundred paces to our right.

::: notes
¹) Even this weak detachment was combined from the units of two groups, those of Generals Danilov and Maslov!
:::

Soon we were in the narrow lanes. The gates were firmly closed. No one showed himself. I had dismounted from my horse because my feet were aching from the cold. We were just halting in front of a large gate, on which we had begun to knock, when suddenly a shot rings out in a side lane—and immediately after it a second, a third, hard and sharp through the silent night! For a second, as if paralyzed by fright, everything stands still, then everyone reaches for his rifle—but toward where? from where was the firing coming? It is so dark that we cannot see how high the wall beside us is; I recognize Lieutenant Colonel Slatin only by the silhouette of his well-shaped, bristly papakha.—Like him, everyone else on foot quickly vaults into the saddle so as not to be ridden down in the dark by the scattering Cossacks. A few more shots crack through the air—whether our own or the enemy's, nobody could say—then off they go in a wild dash out of the village, through the nearest lanes where men had just been standing. Thank God, the exit is unbarred, and soon we are out again on the wide snowfield. We? Barely fifty riders are behind us—all the rest are scattered to the four winds. Again we are down by the steep riverbank; a few men dismount to find a crossing. All around us everything is quiet once more—from the village nothing stirs. Cautiously we ride upriver—now here, now there, a dark group approaches us, against which each time we ready our carbines to fire—yet time and again they are our own men. But now the Cossacks themselves have surrounded the village; they know this kind of petty warfare from the last campaign against the Chinese, in which almost all of them took part.

In twos and threes they rode back toward the exits of the village and guarded them until the sotnias had assembled in the valley. It was well past midnight when the non-commissioned officers reported that no one was missing or wounded.

During these turbulent hours, the helplessness of a cavalry detachment in the dead of night and in enemy territory had become {p. 282} even clearer to me than I had already been convinced of before. To be sure, the Cossacks had disregarded the simplest security precautions, yet even these would have been of little use under the circumstances. In the dark, a horseman is defenseless; a stray shot brings the advance guard to a halt, a rope stretched across the road cuts off the squadron's retreat! And yet Lieutenant Colonel Slatin acted entirely correctly in not having us bivouac in the open that night, but instead moving with his sotnias into the next village—it was Zabei, which we reached around one o'clock in the morning. In village streets, the effect of enemy bullets is limited, and once one has quartered oneself in the houses with the necessary caution and barricaded the entrances to the village, the enemy will hardly be able to surprise the resting troops. It is otherwise in an open bivouac—where we traditionally expect a higher state of readiness. In an open bivouac at night, a few shots striking among the horses are enough to turn everything into a pell-mell, in which everyone merely tries not to be trampled by horses tearing about in wild stampede.

When one arrives in a village in the pitch-dark night, it is almost impossible to reconnoitre it thoroughly. So it was in Zabei; our only concern had to be to get some rest at last. The Chinese, whom we had awakened from their sleep, proved friendly and helpful. Soon the fire was burning under the kang, and my moustache slowly detached itself from the upturned long-haired fur coat, to which it had been frozen fast since evening. Our host had also thrown some gaoliang straw onto the kang, on which, stretched out comfortably, we now sipped the hot tea with which the Chinese served us.

Some men who had been in Qingjiapuzi that day were brought before us and stated that this village was occupied only by a weak force of Japanese infantry. Thereupon we lay down to sleep. Had outposts been posted outside? After all, we were close to the enemy—Japanese had been reported barely a few hours away from us—yet I had not noticed any of the officers attending to outposts. I went outside and saw that the Cossacks had seen to this themselves. Two men at a time, their carbines ready to fire, stood before the entrances to the village.

{p. 283}

*7 March.*

Around eight o'clock in the morning we had set out from Zabei, and at eleven o'clock—marching south—we arrived in Nanjiahe. Here we found a sotnia of Amur Cossacks belonging to General Maslov's group; their commander knew no more about the enemy than we did, and was sullen and taciturn. Living for thirteen months in Manchuria without a permanent abode,

[Illustration: Russian border guard officer with Chinese spies.]

enduring the tropical heat in summer, nearly sinking in the mud during the rainy season, and wintering in fanzas with paper walls—that is a life which may perhaps still seem bearable to the Asiatic Cossack, but inevitably dulls the European officer. The Amur Cossacks had sent out spies and were waiting here for their return. Thus Lieutenant Colonel Slatin also decided to rest here, all the more so {p. 284} as the fanza in which the Amur Cossack officers had already settled was very inviting. It seemed to belong to a wealthy Manchu, for the kitchen and servants' quarters lay opposite the main building, on either side of the large, handsomely painted entrance gate. The Cossacks whom we took into the courtyard of the fanza with us quickly found their way to the kitchen, just as they generally displayed a special aptitude for camp life. However well the Chinese might conceal his pigs and chickens, the Cossack soon found them, and shortly thereafter they were already stewing over the campfire, for which he ruthlessly used rafters, doorposts, wagon shafts, and the like. For the rest, such damage was always handsomely compensated to the inhabitants, and since the officers maintained discipline among their men, the Chinese were always well disposed toward us—or so at least it seemed.

That the Russian soldiers took little fancy to the diminutive Chinese wives could only be pleasing to the latter; this was probably also the main reason why they preferred to see Russian troops enter their villages rather than their racial kin, the Japanese, who, upon arriving in a settlement, were accustomed to requisitioning not only the usual foodstuffs, but also—women. Hence the great haste with which the Manchus sent off their "Madame" whenever they caught wind of Japanese.

This time, however, one of the Cossacks did set out in search of adventure; we had barely entered the fanza when a party of Chinese arrived, gesturing vehemently, before the commander of the Amur sotnia. The aggrieved Chinese held in his hand the papakha which he had pulled off the intruder's head in order to identify him. Naturally, it was the clerk's cap! A brief interrogation, resounding slaps left and right across the culprit's cheeks, and the surrounding Chinese bowed down to the ground in satisfaction, praising the master's justice; the avenged husband acknowledged the vindication of Babushka's honour by falling to his knees into the bargain.

Toward midday the spies returned and reported that the perevals south and southwest of the village of Chebaipo (about {p. 285} ten versts south of us) were occupied by the enemy. At three o'clock in the afternoon an officer with his platoon was dispatched toward each of the aforementioned pass heights. Both platoons found the passes occupied by Japanese infantry, came under fire, suffered wounded, and returned toward evening to Nanjiahe, where Lieutenant Colonel Slatin was just dispatching spies, for which service a whole pack of Chinese offered themselves. In such cases the craftiest faces had to be chosen at random; usually, once they had received their assignment, the men also demanded an advance of money—whether they would return, or whether they went off at all and did not find it more convenient simply to make something up out of their own imagination the next day, of that one had no certainty whatsoever. They were paid thirty to fifty roubles per man.

We spent the night in the fine fanza in Nanjiahe. It was so large that Cossacks were also billeted together with us in the same room.

8 March.

Early in the morning, before our spies sent out the previous evening had returned, Lieutenant-Colonel Slatin sent the regimental commander the following report:

“Japanese infantry (approx. 500) in Hanjiapuzi and Tupdabusa. In the Kosadu valley there are still further Japanese.

On the line Gundjason—Matande and on the second line (?) Ulingon—Shidajiazi—Madansadu as well as in Totikotsen there is likewise Japanese infantry; their patrols have reached as far as the village of Liujiabuzi.

Spies were sent by me to Dapu, Qingbiapuzi, and to the south.”

Such was the wording of the report, without further commentary.

Personally, Lieutenant-Colonel Slatin was quite an excellent comrade. Educated, amiable, and quick of comprehension, he stood far above the intellectual level of the officers subordinate to him. In years he was younger than they, which perhaps had an adverse effect on his assertiveness toward them. Surely we would have been on our way today as well, had not the sluggishness of the two Cossack yesauls put on the brakes always and everywhere.

{p. 286}

While we thus spent the morning quite pointlessly in Nanjiahe, I used the time to inspect our host's fanza a little more closely. I noticed beautiful ink drawings pasted to the wall. I asked the Chinese man for one of them and offered him money for it, but in vain. The Amur Cossack chieftain happened to join us and asked me what I desired. “You want this drawing here? — Well, that is simple!” — and with that he pulled his knife from his pocket and cut the paper down from the wall for me. The host put a good face on it — knowing full well, after all, that he would not lose out in the final settlement with the Russians.¹)

Finally, towards noon, after a hearty breakfast, we set out to clear our way over the heights south of Chebaipo with united forces.

Around three o'clock in the afternoon we arrived at this village, where the spies sent ahead that morning were awaiting us. The passes were still occupied by the enemy, the heights further east too steep to make a wider detour in that direction — we had to attack the enemy.

Our two sotnias dismounted for the firefight; the Amur Cossacks remained in reserve. To the west of us, around General Danilov, heavy fighting seemed to be taking place; artillery salvos from friend and foe echoed over to us in ever more rapid succession. But soon things came alive on the pereval before us, against which the Cossacks had advanced in a skirmish line. Between the dark bushes, little white clouds rose up, soon followed by the sharp crack of rifles. Yet the enemy greeted us with only slow, scattered skirmish fire, as if wishing to encourage us to advance. In good time, Lieutenant-Colonel Slatin recognized the danger that could threaten us from the heights on our right flank. If these were occupied by the enemy, our retreat could be cut off from there if the dismounted men advanced further.

::: notes
¹) The characters in the upper left corner are Tibetan and could not be explained to me later at the Chinese legation in Vienna.
:::

{p. [293]}

[Illustration: Ink drawing with Tibetan characters.]

{p. [294]}

{p. 289}

The sotnias advancing to the attack were ordered to halt. They fired several volleys against the pereval, but their effect could not be observed because of the tall brush covering the slopes. Individual horsemen now rode to the right toward the heights on which we likewise suspected Japanese.

Sending forward such reconnaissance patrols early in the morning, or at least at the beginning of the march, had been regularly neglected. Thus the detachment now had to wait another two to three hours until the horsemen had climbed the high mountain opposite and returned with the news that, apart from a few armed Chinese, nothing was to be seen of the enemy. The two sotnias were just about to follow the vanguard, which had approached the pass from the side through brush and young timber, when an order arrived from regimental headquarters to fall back immediately to Yingpan. Why fall back? This order came unexpectedly! Had not the clerk who returned yesterday from staff headquarters recounted that General Kuropatkin was already on the way to Liaoyang! From the south—that was General Danilov—our own guns were still booming—why fall back, then? Some officers thought the regiment might now be sent to the *right wing* to march with Kuropatkin to Liaoyang; others began to recount episodes from the fighting in August and October, in which the *general retreat* had always begun in a similar manner.

That the Japanese on the right wing were already on the Xinmintun road northwest of Mukden, and their own center had already been forced back to the Hun River, the sotnias here in the mountains could hardly know.

Slowly we retraced the same path by which we had come. Exhausted by the exertions of the last few days, the horses dragged their hind legs sluggishly and stiffly under the heavily packed Cossack saddles. Two were wounded; these were led along by the reins. In the village that the sotnias had left that morning, everyone was already asleep; the Cossacks banged and clamoured at the heavy wooden gates until the Chinese opened them. We found our fanza just {p. 290} as we had left it; we intended to stay here only an hour or two, after which we were to move on again. The Chinese had placed a lantern of coloured paper on the low table. Leaning against the walls in silence, smoking their little opium pipes, they watched us as we sat even more silently around the large iron kettle, from which Yesaul Belyaev filled our glasses from time to time.

The order from the regimental command stated that the sotnias were to withdraw to Yingpan as quickly as possible in order to rejoin the rest of the regiment's sotnias there. That amounted to saying that the Eastern Corps, and with it perhaps the First Army, had been thrown back!

Lieutenant Colonel Slatin urged our departure; we had to try to get past Zabei so that the Japanese coming from Mamagou would not cut off our route! “Better early tomorrow before sunrise than now in the dark of night,” the two chiefs countered, “our horses

[Illustration: Chinese beggar.] {p. 291} are tired and the men have eaten hardly anything today.” So it was decided to spend the night here. We had posted no outposts, yet no one feared that the enemy might take us by surprise. “What do you suppose would happen to you if we were suddenly surrounded by the Japanese?” one of the officers asked, pointing at me. “They might even take you for a spy and hang you!”

“I have my papers with me, after all,” I replied. “Nonsense! Papers! Who looks at papers in wartime! The Japanese would certainly hang you—still better than if Honghuzi take us by surprise, for then all our heads would be adorning the alleys of Nanjiahe tomorrow!”

Jesting thus, we emptied a few more cups of tea, and then the officers threw themselves down on the straw pallet. Through the window of the fanza pierced the glow of a flickering blaze. I went outside into the courtyard, where the Cossacks had stoked a roaring fire over which a plump little pig was roasting. Leaning against the doorpost, I gazed into the leaping flames and listened to the soft singing of the soldiers camped around the fire. Someone tapped me gently on the shoulder; it was our host. He gestured for me to follow and led me to his wives’ room. Astonished, I followed him and entered the dimly lit room—it was empty. Madame, along with all her friends and servants, was gone! “You may sleep here undisturbed until tomorrow morning,” seemed to be the message conveyed by the gesture of the Chinese man, who had prepared a warm bed of soft blankets for me in the corner.

Yet I was not alone here—from the corner of the fanza, behind a paper screen, came faint groaning. There the Chinese man had prepared pallets for our wounded from yesterday; the feldsher of the Amur Cossacks had dressed their wounds.

*9 March.*

We had marched off from Zabei early in the morning—though without the Amur Cossack sotnia. Its commander was sleeping too soundly! “Post sentries on the sopkas around the village, and if the enemy comes, wake me!” he had, {p. 292} as we took our leave of him, ordered his *starshik* (senior non-commissioned officer), and then pulled his coat back over his head. A few weeks later I met one of the officers from this sotnia. They had not been ambushed at the time, but that very morning, just as we were, they were cut off by the Japanese, and were only able to find their own army again after wandering for weeks through the Korean mountains. Thus, through the sleepiness of its commander, this sotnia was crippled for the remainder of the campaign.

At a walk, we fell back toward Zabei. On the way, the Cossacks brought in a Chinese man from the next village. The interpreter translated that Japanese troops in the next valley to the west were on the march toward Zabei. Was this true or not? As usual, we had sent out no patrols. But since the retreat had begun, the Cossacks, too, had grown indifferent and cared little about the enemy now. In retreat they are entirely different soldiers from when they are advancing.

“Why don’t we trot?” I asked the commander. — “Don’t you know that all the horses’ backs are saddle-sore?” was the reply; “after all, their saddles haven’t been taken off for four months!” We continued on at the most deliberate walk. Just before Zabei, a rider from the vanguard approaches, calling out from afar: “Over there,” and points with his arm toward the village, “the Japanese are advancing!” How many? Where they are going? No one even asks about that anymore.

The horses were quickly turned, and we continued riding at a trot around the village. It went on like this for a while, when suddenly it began to sing and whistle overhead. The men already knew this music; “They’re firing!” one heard them shout, even before the crackle of Japanese infantry fire could be heard from the opposite mountainside. The enemy was shooting too high, the bullets striking ahead and to the flank of the sotnias. Without waiting for a command, following their own instinct for self-preservation, the riders turned once more to the right, toward a mountain spur that finally shielded the Cossacks from the bullets striking closer and closer. Down the valley from here, another Cossack detachment could be seen trotting back. It seemed to have been fighting, {p. 293} for its vanguard was just running back to the horse lines. The enemy had to be strong there, for across the broad valley plain, little clouds of sand swirled up close beside one another from the falling rounds. The soldier who in attack rushes forward willingly and undaunted across this hot ground hardly stands firm under fire in retreat, especially when he has *given up covering it with his own weapon*. Our two sotnias tried

[Illustration: Lieutenant Colonel Slatin and officers of the Second Chita Cossack Regiment.]

now, trusting to luck, to reach the next valley to the east over the steep mountain ridge. Whether there were Japanese over there as well we could not know, for we had not sent patrols in that direction either. So we proceeded in single file up the steep slope, leading the tired horses behind us by the reins. Soon the column was strung out over several thousand paces—if the Japanese pursued, they could pick off every single Cossack.

{p. 294}

In this critical situation, we at the front were suddenly startled by several shots that had been fired within our own column! “Nichevo!” said the men following behind with a laugh, pointing to a covey of pheasants that had flushed on the mountainside! It was not even a quarter of an hour since we ourselves had been fired upon; we were still within range of the enemy rifles—and already the Cossacks were no longer thinking of the danger they had just endured and found time to hunt pheasants! As huntsmen, on the other hand, they deserve full praise! Three shots had been fired, and three pheasants lay hit by bullets despite the considerable distance. “They are poachers without exception, our Transbaikal Cossacks,” said Lieutenant Colonel Slatin, excusing his men.

After about an hour, the mountain had been climbed. A tourist’s heart would have leaped at this magnificent view! Towering in massive tiers behind one another appeared the mountains of the Khingan, whose snow-covered peaks, rising above each other, seemed to billow like silver waves in the radiance of the midday sun. But the Cossacks were thinking of no such comparisons; with exhausted horses whose flanks heaved up and down, they now stood before a steep precipice whose scree gave way with every step. Along the narrow ridge, over boulders and through thorn thickets, we now proceeded, searching for a better way down. Here the small Siberian and Mongolian horses proved their full worth; with any others, this route would have been utterly impossible.

Down in the valley, all was quiet. The Chinese claimed to have seen no Japanese here. We marched on for a short distance, but then the tired horses had to be given a rest. A halt was called in a small village. It had become late afternoon; if possible, the night was to be spent here. The Cossacks began looking around in the *fanzas*; soon the chickens were fluttering anxiously about the courtyards and the piglets began squealing in mortal terror.

Half an hour later, they were already scalding in the cooking pots. Yet today we were not to enjoy their catch; the officers were just about to help themselves to their midday meal when a man burst in with the report: “The Japanese are firing on our picket!”

{p. 295}

No one, to be sure, had heard any shots, but everyone ran to the horses, which were quite reluctant to let their heads be pulled from the fodder straw ... “Mount! Move out!” But at the picket all was quiet—in all, three or four shots had been fired!

Yingpan could no longer be reached by the detachment on that day. But I felt pressed to return to regimental headquarters. Here, with the two sotnias, there was likely not much more to experience; they had as little striking power left as they were *unwilling to sacrifice themselves for the sister arms, which in retreat becomes the duty of the cavalry!*

Yet with what shall I continue my narrative? Straight away with the end? With the depiction of that beaten army streaming back, which I encountered in the days that followed, in which the bonds of order and discipline were beginning to dissolve; which, resembling a masterless mob more than marching columns, moved northwards on both sides of the railway, on the Mandarin Road, alongside it, and cross-country, leaving thousands of stragglers, wounded and dead horses, and other broken-down detritus along the way—an easy prey for the swarms of Honghuzi pressing in from the flanks and rear?

Or with the childlike, naive hopes that the army then once again placed in the old General Linevich, who, after all, already as commander of the First Army, had known how to exploit the numerical superiority of troops at his disposal for nothing better than a lackluster defensive! More of all that later; for now, let us cast a glance at the events that unfolded from the beginning to the end of the battle on the western flank and in the center.

The relevant data I gathered after the battle in Harbin, or in part had recounted to me by eyewitnesses along the way. If the purely objective account of the fighting that now follows holds little appeal for the non-military reader, he may skip the next few pages and only rejoin me on my journey from Tieling to Harbin!

{p. [302]}

## The Fighting on the Western Flank and in the Center from 25 February to 9 March.¹)

### The Planned Resumption of the Offensive.

On 19 February, following a conference at Army Headquarters in Sujiatun, a renewed attack on Sandepu had been decided upon. The capture of this place was to mark the first stage of the general offensive upon which Kuropatkin had now resolved for the third time since the retreat from Liaoyang.

The initial measures for this were the reinforcement of the 1st Siberian Corps, which formed the reserve of the Second Army, by half of the 8th East Siberian Rifle Division.

The attack on Sandepu was to have begun with the *Rifle Corps* and the *8th Army Corps* on *25 February at 6:00 a.m.*

The 1st Siberian [Corps] had already marched south from Shakhezi. General Rennenkampf was to *advance southwestward* with the cavalry on the *25th*.

*The participation of the other two armies was again planned to be merely demonstrative for this day*—thus a repetition of the mistake responsible for the first failure at Sandepu!

This time, however, it never even came to that!

On the evening of the 24th, all these offensive movements were halted—*the attack on Sandepu was canceled!!*

::: notes
¹) All place and river names correspond to the spelling of the Russian two- or four-verst maps that were in use by the staffs and troops.
:::

{p. 297}

From the extreme left flank, from the Qinghecheng Detachment, unfavorable telegraphic reports had arrived; Japanese forces advancing in the mountains had, as we already know, forced General Alekseyev with the Eastern Detachment to evacuate the positions pushed forward there toward the southeast, which created the impression at Army Headquarters of a major Japanese offensive that, with progressing success, could have as its objective the defile of Tieling in the rear of the Russian army.

Given all the vulnerability of their own line of communications to the north, the best security for it in this case would surely have consisted in vigorously carrying out the already planned and prepared attack on Sandepu, in which, of course, the Second and First Armies should not have looked on idly either!

For this, however, the Army High Command lacked the necessary energy! Always it was only concern for a secure retreat, and never the firm resolve to attack the enemy with all available forces!

*Thus, once again, at the enemy's first move, the initiative was conceded to him.*

If the commander lacked the will for decisive action on this day, his chief of staff demonstrated his complete operational ineptitude at the very same time: *The 1st Siberian Corps was halted on its march toward Sandepu and routed via Zhangsamutun to Shihuicheng, thus shifted from the right to the left flank of the battle front.* Sandepu–Shihuicheng, sixty kilometers as the crow flies! Likewise, the 72nd Division was pulled out of the fighting line from the 6th Army Corps (center), along with still other troops, in order to shift them eastwards—to the 3rd Corps!

Thus the eastern sector was significantly reinforced while the western flank and the center were weakened.

Lieutenant General Linevich, commander of the First Army, whose headquarters fell back further eastward from Huangshan to Shihuicheng on 26 February, had, after uniting the forces already under his command and those newly arrived {p. 298} (exclusive of the 4th and 2nd Siberian Corps), more than eighty battalions:
1st Siberian Corps . . . . . . . . . . . 24 bns.
71st and 72nd Infantry Divisions . . . . . . . . . . 32 "
3rd and 6th East Siberian Rifle Divisions . . . . . . 24 "
Gen. Maslov's and Prince Tumanov's foot Cossacks 6 "
totaled 86 bns.
(in addition to this, temporarily, the 20th East Siberian Rifle Regiment of General *Putilov's* brigade), which were opposed—so it was said—by *at most three Japanese divisions* under General Kuroki. The expectation that our popular general would use this crushing numerical superiority for a counteroffensive remained unfulfilled; he allowed himself to be restricted by the enemy to the defensive, in which he also remained until the end. The good gentleman! He was, after all, the one who had once said: “The Japanese are *Asiatics*; we must take them by the forelock and teach them culture!” Now he would have had the opportunity!

### The Threat to the Russian Right Wing.

*25–28 February.*

Unlike the Russian recipe for attack, the Japanese did not seem to wait for the outcome of their eastern attacks first, but attacked vigorously along the entire front. At the same time that over in the mountains the Dalin Pass, as well as five redoubts south of the Gaotuling Pass and three south of Fanshen, fell into Japanese hands, fighting was raging in the centre around *Putilov Hill*, and reports of the advance of strong columns along the Liaohe had already reached headquarters on the 25th.

How thoroughly the army high command was dominated by the spectre of being cut off, how little confidence it had in its own power of resistance, and furthermore how lacking was the will to commit its entire forces to an energetic resistance, is shown by the fact that *as early as 25 February, work began on dismantling and sending back the 300 heavy siege guns installed in the centre*, {p. 299} which had been transported here and emplaced a few months earlier with so much trouble and expense.

To ascertain the Japanese advance from the south-west, the Dagestan Dragoon regiments were sent southwards along the Liaohe. The reports sent by this cavalry up to 28 February were bound to convince the army high command that it had acted *hastily* in deploying such significant forces against its own left wing!

On 28 February, strong Japanese turning columns were reported from the Liaohe!

To counter this movement threatening the Russian Second Army, recourse had to be taken to the *general army reserve*—the Sixteenth Army Corps at Baitapu—since the reserve of the Second Army, the First Siberian Corps, was indeed already at Shihoucheng.

Yet even of the Sixteenth Corps, only the 25th Division remained intact; the 2nd Brigade of the 41st Division had departed to Gongzhuling for railway protection (a successful Japanese raid on Fanjiatun).

That brigade had at the time been replaced by the Tsaritsyn Infantry Regiment No. 146, which on 25 February had likewise been sent to the Third Siberian Corps. Thus began the scattering and mixing together of units that was bound to end ultimately in chaos!

Now, on the 28th, from the Sixteenth Corps, which was stationed south of the Hunhe at Baitapu, the 25th Division under Lieutenant General Topornin was directed to the north-west, and the 1st Brigade under the commander of the 41st Division, Lieutenant General Birger, to the north on the Xinmintun road. The latter detachment was deemed necessary because our own cavalry had reported the approach from Xinmintun of four enemy columns—without giving any details as to their strength. In order to create a new general reserve, the army high command now resorted once again to its favourite expedient of cobbling together a composite division, one of the primary causes of the loosening of all organic formations.

In the centre stood, enumerated from the east, the Second {p. 300} and Fourth Siberian, First Army, Sixth Siberian, Seventeenth Army, Fifth Siberian, and Tenth Army Corps. Of these, the Fourth Siberian, First Army, and Sixth Siberian Corps held their positions on the Shahe, while the Seventeenth and Fifth Corps fell back under enemy pressure—*influenced, to be sure, by the events on the western wing.*

Meanwhile, the Japanese attacks from the west became increasingly energetic. The Rifle Corps and the detachment stationed at Sifangtai had been fiercely attacked on 28 February. The Rifle Corps had requested reinforcements, but these could by no means be given to it, for, as mentioned, the army high command had itself to think of *forming a new general reserve*.

#### 1 March.

Thus, a *combined division* from the Tenth Army Corps (2nd Brigade of the 9th and 2nd Brigade of the 31st Division, with the 35th, 36th, 123rd, and 124th Infantry Regiments and artillery) was placed in readiness at General Headquarters near the railway.

General of Cavalry Baron *Kaulbars* stood west of Mukden with almost the entire Third Army: to reinforce him, Lieutenant General *Topornin* with the remainder of the Sixteenth Army Corps and, in addition, the new general army reserve, the combined division of the 10th Army Corps under General *Shatilov*, had to be deployed on 1 March in the direction of Salinpu.

On this day, Sifantai and the position on the Hunhe were attacked anew, with the enemy envelopment in the rear already making itself felt against Sifantai.

On the evening of the 1st, army headquarters decided to *pull back* the right wing on the Hunhe: specifically, the *Eighth Army Corps* in its position on the left bank of the Hunhe was to be *relieved by the Rifle Corps* and itself likewise march to Salinpu.

#### 2 March.

Besides "combining," the "*relieving*" of troops engaged in combat was also very popular with the Russian General Staff. {p. 301} It failed *regularly*—and so, of course, this time as well: the riflemen evacuated their position, the Eighth marched off *before* being relieved, the enemy pressed forward, and the entire sector on the Hunhe opposite Sandepu was lost. The Rifle Corps could only be saved through the intervention of the 241st and 215th Regiments near Lijiapu on 2 March.

On the same day, the *general reserve* that had arrived under Generals Topornin and Shatilov also intervened in the fighting. General Shatilov, with the combined division of the Tenth Corps, found Salinpu already occupied by the Japanese and came under artillery fire. To the east of him marched General Topornin; he attacked and brought to a halt the enemy advancing against Shatilov.

*Situation on the evening of 2 March:* On the Hunhe near Lijiapu, the *Rifle Corps*, then to the north of it the *Eighth Army Corps*, both with *badly intermingled units*. Opposite Salinpu, the columns of Shatilov and Topornin.

*The enemy* not only stood opposite this front, but by 2 March had already crossed the Xinmintun road; beyond it, in Xishelizhi, three Japanese regiments were reported.

The *Birger Brigade*, whose mission was to secure Mukden against enemy forces coming from the direction of the Xinmintun road, had been pushed northward and cut off after costly fighting near Dafangshen.

#### March 3.

In the morning, the 25th Infantry Division, stationed east of Shalingpu, and General Shatilov's column were attacked by the Japanese. The 25th Division fell back as far as Yuhuantun; fugitives reached Lanjiatun, which is southwest of the Mukden railway station.

Shatilov's division held out near Zhanggutun, but that village was evacuated that same day or the next. General Shatilov suffered a concussion in the fighting.

Following the dispatch of Topornin's and Shatilov's columns, *there was once again no general reserve available*. For lack of available troops, the army high command {p. 302} had already on March 1 withdrawn units from other formations; on March 3, of the troops of this kind that had arrived during the night, the following were at the Mukden railway station: *a composite division of the 17th Army Corps* (Infantry Regiments Nos. 12, 138, and 140 with artillery, plus three battalions of the 147th Samara [Regiment] from the 1st Army Corps!). On March 3, this new reserve was sent to Houchai on the Xinmintun road, so that on the evening of that day, the Russian western group stood in a fairly continuous line from the latter locality down to Erzhaizi on the right bank of the Hun River, opposing the direct attacks on Mukden.

Now, however, there would once again have been no general reserve available, had there not arrived on this day, marching in from the east shrouded in dust and exhausted, a column that then bivouacked south of Mukden on both sides of the railway: it was the 1st Siberian Corps! Having been ordered from the extreme right to the extreme left wing, scarcely had it arrived in Shihoucheng when it was ordered back again; and to ensure that here too organic unity was by no means preserved, one of its regiments had remained with the Rennenkampf group and one with the 3rd Corps, in which latter corps, besides the Vyborg Regiment, troops from the 6th and 1st Siberian Corps were now mingled! And the 1st Siberian Corps in particular would have needed to be spared; back when it was still under General Stackelberg's command, it had always been the corps called upon to pull the chestnuts out of the fire. At Sandepu alone it had lost 8,000 men, so that its total casualties since the beginning of the campaign amounted to *one hundred and twenty-five percent* of its original strength.

#### March 4 and 5.

Little fighting took place on March 4. On this day, attempts were made to reorganize the units, which had already become heavily intermingled. South of the Hun River, the 10th Army Corps and 5th Siberian Corps evacuated their positions of March 3. The heavy guns of the 10th Corps had already been sent away by rail on March 2.

On March 4, the Second Army command had issued an attack disposition {p. 303} for March 5, which, however, was never carried out. But this disposition of March 4 provides a rough overview of the grouping of the troops on the western wing on that day.

On March 4, General Gerschelmann was supposed to hold the Sujiapu sector with Infantry Regiments Nos. 33, 121, and 122, along with six foot and two mortar batteries; however, the army disposition already lists these regiments on that day on the right, northern bank of the Hun River, so holding Sujiapu had to be abandoned.

In summary, on the evening of March 4 south of the Hun River under General von der Launitz, we find the following belonging to the Second Army: the 5th Siberian [Corps] (from the Third Army) minus Infantry Regiments Nos. 215 and 241, but plus Infantry Regiment No. 34, which had engaged in an action near Yinerpu. Furthermore, those parts of the 8th Army Corps and the riflemen not mentioned in the army disposition for March 5 (Infantry Regiments Nos. 53, 54, 57, and 60, Rifle Regiment No. 6, and the 1st Brigade—excluding detachments?).

The army high command was already finding it difficult to keep track of the troop distribution, especially where the enemy was pressing, that is, on both sides of the Hun River.

North of the Hun River from Majiapu to Changxintun, the following were grouped for attack according to the disposition for March 5: Lieutenant General Tserpitsky (commander of the 10th Army Corps) with General Gerschelmann's group near Majiapu, then seven rifle regiments, further Infantry Regiment No. 56 (from the 8th Army Corps), Infantry Regiment No. 215 (from the 5th Siberian [Corps]); and adjoining them, the 25th Infantry Division in the Yuhuantun–Niuxintun area.

It is worth noting that at noon on March 4, Lieutenant General Tserpitsky appeared in the military attachés' dining car and declared that at that moment he had no troops whatever at his disposal, as they had all been reassigned away from him by the army high command! It was not until around 2:00 p.m. that he was summoned to the army high command, where he was entrusted with the command of the troops designated in the disposition for March 5.

{p. 304}

The *composite division of the 10th Corps* was withdrawn from the battle front and in the disposition for March 5 was listed among General Gerngross's attack groups.

As *army reserve*, the Second Army's disposition for March 5 designates Infantry Regiment No. 55 of the 8th Corps and Infantry Regiment No. 41 of the 5th Siberian [Corps] at Lanjiatun.

The *Combined Division of the 17th Army Corps* (the former general reserve), which had marched out from Mukden on March 3, was to cover the Sinminting road near Houzha; the 147th Infantry Regiment was to provide flank protection north of it near the Imperial Tombs; the 1st Siberian Corps remains in Mukden. *The Second Army Headquarters and the Army High Command fall back to the Mukden railway station.*

The *supply trains of the Second Army begin to withdraw northward*; they were thoroughly intermingled. March 4 had passed without any serious fighting on the army's western front, and can therefore, in view of the enemy's turning movements to the north, which were assuming an ever more threatening form, be described as a *lost day*.

The *Third Army* on the Sha River was still holding its positions. On the left wing, *Linevich*, despite his numerical superiority, had abandoned all thoughts of an offensive and had fallen back with his headquarters to Fushun on March 4. The *Rennenkampf* Corps held out in the mountains with heroic resistance. Led by a general like Rennenkampf, the Russian soldier performs miracles of bravery and self-sacrifice. The center of gravity of the situation on that day, however, lay unmistakably on the right wing. Here, having now united the Second Army and parts of the Third Army on the northern bank of the Hun River, the Second Army Headquarters resolved upon an *attack* for March 5. According to the

### Army Disposition for March 5

issued on the evening of March 4, the enemy's main force had not advanced beyond the Sinminting road on March 3; in Sanpuchao there is an infantry regiment with cavalry. {p. 305} *Advancing to the attack:* The *cavalry* from the Sheliche—railway area, in order to harass the enemy in flank and rear.

*Right Column under General Gerngross:*
1st Siberian Corps . . . . . . . . . . . 18 battalions
Combined Division of the 10th Army Corps . . . . . 16 "
Combined Division of the 17th Army Corps . . . . . 12 "
147th Samara Infantry Regiment . . . . . . . 3 "
Total . . 49 battalions
and 115 guns — is to assemble at 8:00 a.m. in the Houzha—Xiashentun area, and then attack the enemy's left wing via Dashiqiao (on the Sinminting road) and force it westward.

*Center Column under Lieutenant General Topornin:*
25th Infantry Division . . . . . . . . . . . . 14 battalions
and 48 guns — begins its advance in the Majiazan—Changxingtun area "*when the enemy has fallen back to Dahuang—Yaojiatun*."

*Left Column under Lieutenant General Tserpitsky:*
56th Zhitomir Infantry Regiment . . . . . . . . 4 battalions
33rd, 121st, and 122nd Infantry Regiments . . . . . 12 "
5th, 7th, and 8th Rifle Regiments . . . . . . . . 6 "
5th Rifle Brigade . . . . . . . . . . . 8 "
215th Infantry Regiment . . . . . . . . . . 4 "
Total . . 34 battalions
72 quick-firing guns, 16 old-pattern guns, and 12 mortars — pivoting on Majiapu, begins its movement simultaneously with the 25th Division against the old railway embankment between the Hun River and Gujiazi.

*Army Reserve under General Hahnenfeld:*
55th Infantry Regiment . . . . . . . . . . . 4 battalions
241st Infantry Regiment . . . . . . . . . . 4 "
Total . . 8 battalions

*Second Army Headquarters* in Houzha.

{p. 306}

It is not difficult to recognize that this disposition suffers once again from the typical flaw already discussed: the flanking column is assigned an objective, and the other groups are not to begin their advance until this has been attained by the flanking group. Yet this time, too, the Japanese were not kind enough to fall back to the Dahuang—Yaojiatun line assigned to them by the Russian Second Army Headquarters—and the entire Russian attack consequently ground to a halt.

In just the same way, on August 2 of the previous year, no coordinated general attack came about because Major General Orlov suffered a reverse at the Yantai coal station; in October, the evacuation of the Banyupu position threw the whole problem of the attack into confusion, and here the *waiting for the capture* of the Japanese positions by the Eastern Army Group led to disastrous inactivity. In February of this year, as we have seen, the impregnability of Sandepu paralyzed the entire offensive.

At 10:00 a.m. on March 5, General Gerngross's attack group was not yet in the ordered area. The blame was once again shifted onto the poor 1st Siberian Corps, which was alleged to have received the disposition only at 12:00 midnight on March 4 and consequently to have arrived late at the assembly area; this, however, is improbable, since at that time the 1st Siberian Corps stood united in bivouac, roughly eight versts from the assigned position. It is more probable that operational reasons—namely, the Japanese offensive against Majiapu manifesting itself in the *south* on March 4—caused the execution of the at long last adopted *decision to attack to be postponed*. For this reason, the *Combined Division of the 10th Corps* also seems never to have passed under General Gerngross's command, but rather to have been retained as a reserve.

On the morning of 5 March, Japanese patrols were already appearing north of Mukden; on that day, the partial evacuation of the Mukden railway station and the dispatch to the rear of the Mukden post office began, the latter subsequently establishing itself in Kuanchengzi.

{p. 307}

Once again—*for the fourth time in a short period*—a *general reserve* had to be formed; Colonel Tsekhovich, chief of the topographical section which had now become idle, rode around to the higher headquarters to take away from them as many dispensable (?) troops as possible!

South of the Hun River, between it and the Sha River, stood General von der Launitz with elements of the 5th Siberian, 8th Army, and elements of the Rifle Corps, together with artillery.

The center and the First Army generally held their ground in their positions.

#### 6 and 7 March.

During the night of the 5th/6th, the 31st Brigade of the combined division of the 10th Corps was alerted and marched at 3:00 a.m. to the south bank of the Hun River, where it arrived at 7:00 a.m. At 9:00 a.m. it received orders to march back to Mukden, where it arrived, exhausted, at the railway station in the afternoon! Why this senseless marching back and forth took place, no one could say!

[Illustration: Mailbags of the Mukden post office dispatched to the rear to Kuanchengzi.]

{p. 308}

On 5 March, the Second Army headquarters had once again issued an

*attack disposition for 6 March*, which read in excerpt:

Enemy with three divisions is positioned with infantry and artillery between the Hun River and the old railway embankment as far as the Xinmintun road; with cavalry northeast of it.

The *envelopment* (*zakhoshdeniye*) is to be continued (?) and the Shalingpu–Tiaojiansuo–Lanjiapu sand hill line is to be gained.

Advancing for this purpose:

1. *Colonel Zapolsky*
with Infantry Regiment No. 147 . . . . . . . . . 3 bns.
with Rifle Regiment No. 9 and two draft detachments 4 „
---
7 bns.
moves at daybreak from Santaizi to Dashiqiao, where he guards the Xinmintun road.

2. *General Gerngross*
*a)* with the *combined division of the 17th Army Corps* . . . . . . . . . 12 bns.
Two regiments advance to Dashiqiao, from there, after *relief* (once again!) by Colonel Zapolsky, toward Dahong–Yangjianan–Yangjiachuan–Langshantai.
The third regiment from Fanshitun toward Houhuntai–Chamintun.
*b)* *With the 1st Siberian Corps* at daybreak *nine battalions* toward Changshitun, *nine battalions* toward Ningguantun.
The *sand hills* are to be fortified after capture.

3. *Lieutenant General Tserpitsky* supports General Gerngross with fire. Following the latter's successful attack (typical!), Wulipu and the old railway embankment are to be taken.

4. *General Gershelman* was to remain in his previous position, while

5. *General Topornin* with the 25th Division and the 13th Brigade *remains in place* as "army reserve" *and is to support the at-* {p. 309} tack of the 1st Siberian Corps with fire. (What was meant by this, however, is hard to understand!)

6. This point deals with the cooperation of the troops.

7. General Birger, who had again succeeded in fighting his way south with the 2nd Brigade of the 41st Division, was to protect Mukden to the north near Houchitai Station.

8. Second Army headquarters in Houhu.

This disposition proves that the attacks ordered the previous day for the 5th were not carried out, and results in a shift of forces to the disadvantage of the attacking wing.

The mission of the reserve is incomprehensible.

The actual events of the day on the *western* wing, briefly summarized, were: on the Xinmintun Mandarin Road,¹) Xiujiachuan, about two kilometers from Dashiqiao, cannot be taken and the overall attack grinds to a halt.

For 7 March, either no attack disposition had been issued, or, on the basis of bad experiences with the two previous ones, it was preferred not to make one known to the foreign officers. On this day, fighting takes place west of Mukden near Niuxintun, Yangshitun, and Yuhongtun. The Russian forces employed here, employed in a unified manner, achieve local successes. Colonel Zapolsky falls at Bajiazi. General von der Launitz's group crosses to the northern bank of the Hun River in order to protect Mukden against the threat manifesting itself ever more forcefully from the north.

To characterize the chaos, here is the composition of a column encountered by one of the military attachés while riding away from Houhu: {p. 310} *two companies of Rifle Regiment No. 5* (hitherto with Lieutenant General Tserpitsky's group); between them, *Batteries Nos. 6 and 29* (8th Army Corps), and *a combined regiment* of the 4th Siberian Corps (soldiers questioned claimed to belong to the Irkutsk, Omsk, Tomsk, Tobolsk, and Barnaul regiments!).

::: notes
¹) There are in fact several roads designated as the Mandarin Road.
:::

This was part of the newly formed large army reserve, which now joined General Gerngross, to whom Rifle Regiment No. 10 had also been assigned (newly arrived).

To characterize the *infantry tactics*, the attack of the 5th and 10th Rifle Regiments will be outlined.

At 1:45 p.m., the 123rd Kozlov Infantry Regiment passes Luguantun in combat formation, then turns toward the southern edge of Yuhuantun and carries out the attack.

At 2:15 p.m., south of Yuhuantun, the 5th and 10th Rifle Regiments arriving from Xieguantun deploy according to the following diagram:

[Illustration: 10th Rifle Regiment. 5th Rifle Regiment.]

At 2:45 p.m., *General Kuropatkin* arrives at the riflemen with a large staff, greets them, and remains with them for some time.

At 3:00 p.m., the *5th Rifle Regiment* moves to the left wing of the *10th Rifle Regiment* (in {p. [317]} [Illustration: Effect of several shrapnel rounds in a retreating column of General Eck's division.]

{p. [318]}

{p. 313}

part through it) and assumes the same formation there as the latter.

General *Kuropatkin* proceeds toward Yuhuantun; shells strike near his staff, which now moves into open formation.

Yuhuantun—the Japanese appeared to have had only a little infantry and one battery here—is taken in the afternoon, whereupon the *5th and 10th Rifle Regiments march off to the north again as a reserve*.

Throughout the day (7 March), *five batteries stood idle in reserve* near Lanjiatun and Luguantun, namely two from the 25th and three from the 51st Artillery Brigade.

In the evening, six battalions from the 6th Siberian Corps arrived at Mukden. Caohetun and Bajiazi are attacked by the enemy and fall during the night. At the latter place, General Staff Colonel Zapolsky is mortally wounded.

General *Bürger* had fallen back from the north as far as the Imperial Tombs.

The *railway and telegraph communication* to the north was cut, though only temporarily. No destruction of the railway took place. (Lack of Japanese cavalry!)

### The Evacuation of the Shahe Front on 7 and 8 March.

Adjutant General Kuropatkin had already intended on 6 March to evacuate the Shahe front, but refrained from doing so at the request of Generals Bilderling and Kaulbars.

Under the influence of the events of the 7th, however, the commander-in-chief saw himself forced to evacuate the Shahe front on the night of 7–8 March and withdraw the southern front into the fortified position of Mukden–Fushun.

Dating from September 1904, the fortified sectors here consisted of: the Mukden bridgehead on both sides of the railway and on the Mandarin Road; furthermore, a flanking work for this bridgehead above the Mandarin Road bridge; furthermore, the positions at Fuling (steep bank), Jinshan, and Fushun. The southern front began its withdrawal on the evening of the 7th, with the general direction of march toward the northeast.

{p. 314}

Lieutenant General von der Launitz was entrusted with the defense of Mukden to the north (chief of staff Major General Eichholz). His post on the 8th was at the northern edge of the Imperial Tombs.

### The Position of the Russian-Manchurian Army on 8 March.

The *First Army* under General of Infantry *Linevich* in the east, from Yingpan to Fushun; Lieutenant General *Rennenkampf*, with the Eastern Corps on the left wing of the First Army; the easternmost groups under Generals *Danilov* and *Maslov*; cavalry pushed forward into the southern side valleys of the Hunhe. Colonel *Madritov*, who had been detached toward the Korean border with several Cossack detachments and a *honghuzi* detachment in Russian pay, was likely already cut off from the army by this time.

The *Third Army* under Lieutenant General *Bilderling*. The direct protection of Mukden from the south fell to the Third Army; standing from east to west with the VI and XVII Army, V Siberian, and VIII Army Corps, and those parts of the Rifle Corps that had still remained south of the Hunhe, it extended from Fuling—the Mukden bridgehead, consisting of five large redoubts—to Machiapu, where it linked up with General *Herschelman's* group. In the army's rear, all terrain sectors had already been prepared in the autumn of last year as potential rallying positions.

The *Second Army* under General of Cavalry Baron *Kaulbars*, forming the western group and at the same time the focal point of events, even though the decisive action was not to take place here. Extending from General Herschelman's group northward from the Hunhe toward the Xinmintun road, there stood: General *Tserpitsky* at Yangshitun, the 25th Division under General *Topornin* at Yuhuantun, General *Gerngross* at Houha. For the protection of Mukden to the north, in the woods near the Imperial Tombs, stood General von der Launitz; subordinated to him were the 41st Brigade under the divisional commander General *Bürger*, which had now regained contact with the army, the {p. 315} 1st and 10th Rifle Regiments, and a composite regiment from the 4th Siberian Corps.

For the direct protection of the railway, the 2nd Dragoon and 1st Verkhneudinsk Cossack Regiments assembled on the afternoon of the 8th near the Mukden railway station. The wounded General Mishchenko addressed these troops, after which the brigade rode northward along the railway.

The *Army High Command was in Mukden.*

The Army High Command had in no way—at least noticeably—exerted its influence on the commander of the First Army, General of Infantry *Linevich*, to induce him to exploit the numerical superiority placed at his disposal against the enemy for an *energetic offensive*; it may therefore well be said that—far removed from any thought of an offensive—even the resistance at Mukden amounted merely to a fight to gain time.

On the 8th, stubborn fighting was already taking place to the *north of Mukden*; south of the Hunhe, the Japanese pressed hard behind the columns withdrawing to the Hunhe line.

It seems as though this critical situation was required to awaken the energy of the commander-in-chief.

Now, as the enemy's iron ring from the west, south, and east closes tighter from hour to hour, Adjutant General *Kuropatkin* arrives at a resolute—yet *belated*—decision.

### Kuropatkin's Counter-Offensive on 9 March.

Kuropatkin's plan was to extricate himself from the threatening encirclement from the north-west and, to this end, to advance toward Ci'ertun on 9 March with approximately *eighty battalions*. Of the forces positioned at and west of Mukden, the commander could place scarcely more than thirty battalions under his direct command for this task. The remainder required was to concentrate under General *Zarubaev* at Dawa, that is north-east of Mukden, in the course of 8 and 9 March.

{p. 316}

For this purpose, fifteen to twenty battalions each were detached from the corps that were just retreating northward from the Shahe to the Hunhe, and these were now to continue their march toward Dawa, that is, toward the north-west. Yet this concentration order, issued on 7 March, had failed entirely to reckon with the *imperative conditions of time and space*! The corps that were to detach battalions had already begun their retreat from the Shahe on 8 March; the new order did not reach them until the evening of 8 March or even early on the morning of 9 March, on and north of the Hunhe; carrying out this order meant, however, that the battalions concerned, ordered to Dawa, had to make detours—with the exception of those of the 1st Army Corps—which entailed a considerable loss of time. Those of the 4th Army and 2nd Siberian Corps arrived late, while the twenty battalions expected from the 3rd Siberian Corps did not reach Dawa at all.

General-Adjutant Kuropatkin had frequently demonstrated his skill in concentrating and deploying forces during the campaign. This time, his effort at the eleventh hour to form a powerful strike force for a decisive attack from elements of the 3rd and 1st Armies—which by then were already in retreat—was to prove his undoing. The detachment of the aforementioned troops to Dawa had the direct consequence of *weakening the centre*, which enabled the *enemy to achieve a strategic breakthrough*!

It is difficult to deduce what motives may have guided army headquarters in concentrating numerically such a powerful force at the eleventh hour, which could scarcely be done without impairing the cohesion of the affected corps, already loosened by the various group combinations. The strength of such a reserve would indeed suggest an offensive attempt, as it were a final exertion of all forces to turn what was still not an entirely hopeless fate in favour of their own arms. Praiseworthy as such a resolve would have been several days earlier, in the actual event it came {p. 317} decidedly too late; it was impracticable and was carried out only partially and belatedly.

Just as little as the timing were the conditions of space in which this offensive was planned favourably chosen, for two reasons: Dawa, where the group was to assemble, was too far removed from those parts of the 3rd and 1st Armies from which the various battalions were drawn for it to be reached in time, with the exception of the battalions of the 1st Army Corps. The direction in which the attack was launched, that is, generally from Ci'ertun toward the south-west, was neither sensitive for the Japanese—at best it could have brought the enemy's turning attempt to a halt, for which, however, fewer forces than those assigned would have sufficed—nor would an attack with eighty battalions have encountered a numerically worthy adversary here; it was bound, therefore, to be a blow in the air!

#### March 9.

March 9 began with a hurricane-like *dust storm*. The fine brown dust-sand covering the vast plain from the Hunhe valley northwards beyond Mukden was swirled aloft in thick, massive clouds. In places, the force of the wind forced men to cling to the nearest trees, walls, or whatever else could be found; the dust settled in thick ridges on the eyelids and prevented seeing. Trains were still running on the railway line, at least to evacuate the hospitals; yet only the roar of the locomotive and the shrill sound of the steam whistle gave away the approach of a train—everything was shrouded in an opaque, indeed almost impenetrable yellowish-brown cloud of earth.

At 8 o'clock in the morning, the first Japanese shrapnel rounds burst over the station north of Mukden. The trains had to keep running in order to transport at least the most indispensable part of the war materiel. In the preceding days, large quantities of artillery ammunition had been detonated in deep trenches near Mukden, evidently that for the heavy guns. South of Mukden, for several days already, {p. 318} tall clouds of smoke had been rising from the immense *firewood dumps* that had been stacked near Gajiazi and had to be burned. For March 8, as already mentioned, orders had been issued whose execution was intended to place a strong reserve—approximately *eighty battalions*—under General *Zarubaev* at Dawa at the disposal of Army Headquarters in the course of that day.

In its execution, the attack of March 9, which General Kuropatkin directed in person, fell far short of its conception. For the reasons discussed earlier, barely more than some twenty battalions were at the direct disposal of the Commander-in-Chief on the morning of March 9, who had arrived with his suite at Cuertun during the early hours of the forenoon. At that time, Japanese artillery shells were still falling near Army Headquarters, so that it was compelled to disperse over a wider area—in the manner of a swarm—in order to present a less favorable target. To provide a linear sketch of the fighting west of Mukden for the ninth would be difficult, as far as the situation could then be surveyed from the Russian side. The units, which had already been heavily intermingled since the beginning of the fighting through the continuous combining of new formations, had become even more mixed and disrupted under enemy pressure over the past day. Thus, for example, on the forenoon of March 9, detachments of the 217th Regiment, belonging to the 6th Siberian Corps, were retreating across the railway north of Mukden, its men saying, "We are scattered," while almost at the same time a composite battalion of the 54th Division, belonging to the 5th Siberian Corps, was advancing westward not far to the south, the individual companies in single-rank line at intervals of about 300 paces from one another.

Coordination among the individual groups seemed to have been little regulated, so that the battle on the western front dissolved into a series of *isolated engagements*, the general tendency of which was that the individual groups, seeking to gain ground toward the west, attacked the villages situated immediately west of the places mentioned at the outset. To {p. 319} all this was now added the dreadful dust storm, which made any overall view, and perhaps also unified direction within the groups, impossible.

The Commander-in-Chief personally led his approximately thirty battalions against the enemy, and this *attack, directed uniformly toward Cuertun*, gained ground to the west; the *enemy here was far weaker* than expected and fell back.

The enemy artillery fire grew noticeably weaker toward noon; the Imperial Tombs had been spared altogether by the Japanese—seemingly intentionally. Without encountering significant resistance, General Kuropatkin's offensive group advanced from Cuertun via Yuhuantun toward the southwest, and the attacks of the other groups were likewise attended by success. These local successes already caused the fortunes of the day to tilt in favor of the Russians.

### The Breakthrough.

Toward evening, however, Japanese columns appeared in the north, in Daxintun, and what was far worse—*in the southeast of Mukden: the Japanese had broken through the center, which was falling back to the Hunhe and had been weakened still further by the transfer of troops to Dawa*. Mukden was thereby lost. At the beginning of the battle, precautions had been taken against a Japanese turning movement around their own left flank; then seeing their right flank threatened, the center of gravity was shifted to the latter, in the course of which the center was so severely weakened that the enemy finally succeeded in achieving a *breakthrough*.

At 8 o'clock in the evening, army headquarters was forced to order the *immediate evacuation* of Mukden.

A complete debacle of the army was prevented partly by the bridgehead fortifications south of Mukden and partly by the fact that the *evacuation of Mukden* had already begun quietly on March 2.

The Third Army was still able to hold out in the bridgehead south of Mukden; the First Army naturally now had to abandon the Hunhe {p. 320} and withdraw to the Fanhe line in order to secure *Tieling*, the main staging and railway station in the army's rear.

General *Rennenkampf*, whose energy in these battles had helped the left wing through many a difficult situation, had fallen back to Sainpanj. With him was the division of General *Eck*, then to the east the Danilov and Maslov groups, and on the extreme left wing at Wangaro General Lyubavin with the 2nd Chita and 2nd Argun Cossack Regiments and five sotnias of the Amur and Nerchinsk Cossack Regiments.

[Illustration: Locomotive hit by Japanese shrapnel.]

{p. [327]}

# After the Battle.

{p. [328]}

{p. [329]}

## Yingpan and the Hunhe Are Abandoned.

With four Cossacks whose horses still had strength left, I rode on to the regimental commander to obtain orders for the detachment. As early as the morning of the 9th, that dreadful Manchurian wind had started up again in the mountains, and when we emerged into the Hunhe plain, it was raging here as a horrific dust storm. The air was saturated with brown sand, which the hurricane, gathering itself from time to time for a tremendous gust, swept across the plain in thick clouds. Tree branches, thatched roofs, and whatever else it found along the way were swept along in its raging vortex, and anyone who did not want to be dragged along held on to a tree, the corner of a house, or lay flat in a sheltering hollow. The horses would scarcely move forward. At every fresh gust of wind, they turned their croups to the howling elements and hid their heads between their forelegs.

On the way to the Hunhe, we encountered a retreating scouting detachment; in a village stood a rifle battalion, which knew nothing of the enemy. From the direction of Fushun rolled the thunder of cannon. Late in the afternoon, snow began to fall and the roar of the storm subsided, but all the louder now was the booming of the guns and the crackle of rifles carried over by the west wind from the broad Hunhe line.

A dense fog lay over the river, so that Yingpan, on the other bank, was difficult to find. The Cossacks tried several times to ride onto the ice, but the crust broke through each time, and the water was already deeper than the stirrups right near the bank. Riding upstream, we finally found a track that led to the eastern end of Yingpan. The place seemed large. Inside the lanes, a strange sight presented itself: a crowd of {p. 324} soldiers and wagons, all heavily laden with clothing, bread, canned food, and sacks of oats. One rider and his small nag were literally covered by a pile of padded drill smocks. Felt boots hung down on both sides of the horse's neck, and coats were tied behind the saddle, hanging down to the ground. This one was followed by many others who were similarly laden.

“Where is General Maslov’s staff headquarters?” “Here in the large fangzi, but the general already rode away in the afternoon.” In the courtyard of the abandoned house, the same superabundance reigned as in the street; a crowd of horses stood ankle-deep in oats, gorging themselves to bursting, tin cans lay strewn about the ground, and a pack of dogs was gnawing on a wheel of cheese. The regimental staff was a few versts north of Yingpan in Yaopu, so I was told by Colonel Radetsky, who was marching through Yingpan with the 7th Krasnoyarsk Independent Siberian Reserve Battalion.

After half an hour's rest, during which our horses had thoroughly gorged themselves, we rode on toward the north. From the southern edge of the town tall flames rose up, in which the thatched roofs of the houses crackled. The fangzis in the next lane were just being set on fire as we rode through.

Toward the northern exit of the town, the last arbas were driving out, heavily laden with sacks of oats and flour. In the village, however, many thousands of poods of supplies that could no longer be moved away had to be set on fire so that they would not fall into the hands of the enemy.

Just north of the village, on a rise, the Krasnoyarsk Battalion was encamped. Colonel Radetsky invited me to drink a cup of tea with his officers. Cheerful, we sat around an upturned crate and followed with our binoculars the engagement of a scouting detachment on the other bank of the Hunhe, east of us, which seemed to be firing at Japanese infantry on the opposite mountain slope. From the other bank of the Hunhe, from a southerly direction, close rifle fire could also be heard. “Those are our men!” said some. “No, those are Japanese!” said others. And while they were arguing whether it was friend or foe, bullets suddenly struck right in front of our tea crate.

{p. 325}

With his bayonet, a soldier quickly dug out the nearest bullet—it had the reddish-brown jacket of a Japanese infantry projectile. But before the debate on this could continue, a violent explosion in the village drove us to a hasty retreat. Whether it was a Japanese shell that had struck there, or whether the Russians had left ammunition behind in the village that was now exploding in the fire—we did not know; after a violent detonation, we saw a massive column of smoke, mixed with

[Illustration: Yingpan.]

earth and rubble, rising before us, similar to those that Japanese Shimose used to throw up.

When I looked back toward the south half an hour later, the heavens, as far as the eye could reach, were fiery red. High flames blazed up from Yingpan; above the blaze, sparks danced and showered in the black smoke. Down the Hun River, the villages were burning as well.

This was roughly the time when the Russians, after a bloody two-week struggle in which they had left twenty thousand dead and sixty thousand wounded, were forced to abandon Mukden. The decision had fallen far away {p. 326} in the west, but disaster had overtaken us too in wild haste. From Mukden to Yingpan, the flames flickering toward the sky, now blazing together in the darkness into a vast sea of fire, showed that Russia's rule here had come to an end.

The regimental staff was situated a few versts north of Yingpan. Late in the evening, the commander had been summoned to the brigade staff. The officers of the third and sixth sotnias, who had marched in during the day, had just laid down to sleep when the regimental adjutant called into the room: "We are falling back!" Up again, back to the horses, standing for an hour in the courtyard, waiting mounted on the road for an hour—then the regiment marched off. The night was dark; along the road, infantry bivouacked by campfires. Behind us, villages were burning again—no wonder! No one put out the campfires upon marching off, and the dry gaoliang easily catches fire in the wind. But no one cared about that now; after all, there was no going back, and the land in which the war was being waged was not their own! The path could not be seen in the darkness; only the tread of the horses indicated whether one was still marching along the wheel ruts cut deep into the clay by the arbas, across a field, or over a frozen body of water. The troops had no maps for the region north of the Hun River—who would have thought that one would have to head north instead of south!

Often it was difficult to get through the campfires with the horses; when one had finally wound one's way between the blazing pyres and pyramids of rifles, with the horses often stepping into the still-glowing coals of a burnt-out fire, one's eyes were so dazzled that in the darkness the man next to him could not be seen.
