A broad body of water crossed the line of march. Barely in the middle, the ice gave way. We pulled our feet up, but the horses are small, and soon the saddle too is underwater. From behind they press forward, and the riders in front cannot get out of the water; the horses strike their chests against the sharp-edged sheet of ice, which they cannot climb onto. Several Cossacks attempt to smash the ice with their rifle butts {p. 327}—in vain! We must turn around and return to the bank to attempt the crossing elsewhere. The sotnias split up and now each seek their way individually in the dark night. One Cossack after another; in this way, the ice holds.

Over on the other bank, the regiment assembled; but soon there is another delay. Up ahead, somewhere in the sunken road, an overloaded wagon has broken down; the entire column halts and cannot move on. Dismount—wait. If only there had been a campfire nearby to dry our soaked clothes and boots! In the night frost, the wet garments had quickly become coated with a layer of ice, and our legs could scarcely bear the pain of the cold any longer; men hopped from one foot to the other to keep from freezing.

At last the lantern hanging from the last wagon of the baggage train began to move; the way was clear—Mount!—Walk!—March! Those who had stood in the water, however, soon dismounted again and ran alongside their horses.

It may have been one o'clock in the morning when the regiment reached Xiaohadao, where General Danilov's troops were bivouacked.

[Illustration: The Krasnoyarsk Battalion before Yingpan.]

{p. 328}

My feet were numb with cold, as was my left hand, which held the reins. To make matters worse, during the sudden departure from Yaopu I had been unable to find my left glove in the dark fangzi. That too had now become war booty for the Japanese.

In a small, grimy fangzi, Colonel Zakrzhevsky was awaiting the general. I pushed the hard leather case holding my camera under my head and fell asleep instantly—after all, I had ridden more than ninety versts that day!

At two o'clock in the morning, General Maslov arrived, to whom we were henceforth subordinated along with the staff and the third and fifth ¹) sotnias, while the second, fourth, and sixth sotnias of the regiment were assigned to General Lyubavin in Tsanta, west of General Danilov's group.

We received orders to march to Wangao, and around three o'clock in the morning arrived at a village that was densely packed with troops.

We waited here for an hour; men thrown together from the most diverse regiments crowded around the fires in the narrow village lanes. Some had been standing here since yesterday, waiting for orders. Many did not know from whom they would receive orders, for the organization of the troops had changed almost every day of late.

The regiment resumed its march to the northeast at four o'clock in the morning.

On the extreme left wing, General Lyubavin had to secure the flank and wing of the army with nine squadrons. He knew that the Japanese at Inpan could not be strong, and attempted to push south with his brigade—but in vain! The decision had already fallen in the west.

On March 10, we on the extreme left wing were still far from knowing that Mukden had already fallen the previous evening. Even the generals only suspected that over in the west a heavy blow must have struck {p. 329} the army, but where the army high command might be at this time remained in doubt—though it was certain that there would never be any going "straight on to Liaoyang"!

::: notes
¹) This had joined us in the course of the night at Imarshan north of Yaopu.
:::

At Shanzalintsu I left the regiment in order to learn something more definite in Tieling. Should it no longer be possible for me to return, I was at least to write to the officers with exact information about the past events on the western wing; they wanted at least to know the cause of the general retreat. Esaul Dolgorukov sent three of his best Cossacks with me. To spare my horse, which after much difficulty had finally allowed itself to be shod, I also bought the horse of a wounded man through whose knee a bullet had passed the day before.

[Illustration: Brief rest with General Maslov's brigade.]

My route led from Shanzalintsu to General Maslov at Maabentsa. I found his troops resting on the retreat at that place. From here the way continued over the {p. 330} Fuantun Pass, which was occupied by General Danilov's 23rd Rifle Regiment. In the evening, I arrived at Sainpan, at General Rennenkampf's headquarters.

Even Szeptycki could tell me nothing definite about the loss of Mukden at that time—one was simply always and everywhere in the dark about what had happened just a few versts away!

In the evening, in the general staff section, the casualty lists of the subordinate troops were compiled. The figures I heard here spoke terrible truths. Forty—thirty—sixteen—yes, even *twelve* men was the remaining strength of the companies!

"Which company has only twelve men left?" I asked the staff captain who was reading out the regimental reports. . . . "The 13th Company of the 283rd Regiment!"

After dinner, I chatted with Szeptycki for a while about the events of the last few days; Szeptycki was full of praise for Rennenkampf and the troops under his command. "I believe," he said, "we would do our fatherland a disservice if we told people at home that this army is bad! Relying too much on its weakness could perhaps lead to bitter disappointment for us in the future!"—There is certainly much truth in these words. In the Russian people lies unmistakably the seed of an excellent—if not the best—army; someone would only have to come along and make that seed sprout!

The next morning, long before the sun rose, I was already on my way so as to reach Tieling before noon. I had no map and veered too far to the south, back in the direction of Fuantun, whereby I became witness to an infantry engagement one last time. It was General Eck's division that was indirectly covering the retreat of the Third Siberian Corps as it fell back toward the northwest here. Yet it too was soon forced to withdraw to the steep northern banks of the Fanhe River. The Japanese did not press, and the columns marched in the best of order. Without our even having heard enemy artillery fire, {p. 331} little white clouds were suddenly visible above us, whose rocket-like puffs only too soon revealed themselves as exploding shrapnel. To be sure, they were only stray shots, but as ill luck would have it, they too found their mark! The picture on page 311 shows the effect of a cone of dispersion that had struck the retreating column along its length.

On the northern bank of the Fanhe, along which I rode past the Third and Second Siberian Corps, entrenchments were hastily thrown up. In the eastern sector assigned to the First Army, the river formed a very favorable defensive line. While the valley plain extends broadly to the south, the northern flanking heights push close up to it and, through the sheer steepness of their slopes, already form a formidable obstacle in their own right.

At last, toward noon, I caught sight of the tall tower standing on a steep mountain east of Tieling.

[Illustration: Chinese farriers.]

{p. [338]}

## The Retreat of the Third and Second Armies.

### Tieling.

As if at a single stroke, the military character of the scene now changed. While in the mountains with the First Army there had everywhere been calm and relative order, even among the retreating troops, the entire plain, as far as it stretched before me from the heights, resembled a broad highway upon which swarms of ants struggled forward side by side and over one another in long black columns. At one moment horsemen galloped past me in a wild pack; at another, artillery, ammunition wagons, and other vehicles came rattling from the south, pressing on northward, here along the road, there across country; then again I passed bivouacking troops; and so it went all the way to Tieling. By our notions, one might have thought it was quarterly moving day, for all the good Chinese stood on the streets ready to drive on, with all their belongings packed onto carts. Here, for the first time, I saw soldiers looting. They appeared to be Caucasians raiding the wine shop of a German merchant. I had no time to concern myself with this, ordered my four Cossacks—one more had joined us from Sainpan—to form up around me, and rode toward the railway station, where I hoped to find the military attachés' train. The confusion here was unprecedented. I could recognize Kuropatkin’s train by the sentries posted around it in a wide arc; asking or searching here would have been futile! The sun had barely begun to shine a little warmer, and already the entire ground was turned into deep morass. In a long file, {p. 333} artillery teams moved past me—surely they must have had to abandon their guns and ammunition wagons! Slowly we wound our way toward the station through charred campfires, filth, and Chinese carts—it was with promises of the station that I had kept reassuring my Cossacks throughout the journey, telling them that there they would find shchi, tea, and gaoliang for the horses!

Yet what boundless confusion we found here, what repulsive scenes, such as I had never before witnessed in the war, were playing out in front of the station building and inside the buffet room! The glass cabinet in which the various beverages were usually stacked had been smashed to pieces; what it had contained could be guessed from the red, bloated faces of the officers, who lay about heavily intoxicated on tables, chairs—and on the floor as well—some in deep sleep, others howling and shouting. *Yet let us not forget, before this revolting sight, that these were men who had perhaps spent eight days in the open and fighting, who had scarcely partaken of food or drink during that time, who had greedily thrown themselves upon the first refreshment to be had, and who now could not but fall victim all too quickly to alcohol!*

[Illustration: The tower east of Tieling.]

{p. 334}

Where is the station commandant? A useless question; no one knows! At last I find him. He had taken off the red armband that identified him as such—so as not to be asked so many questions!

Where are the military attachés? He does not know; perhaps the general will know! The latter advises me to ride to Gongzhuling. How far might that be from here? He does not know—I should just keep following the railway, and eventually I will get there!—Any chance of eating, drinking, or feeding the horses was out of the question.

I want to ride on along the railway, but this is impossible. Hundreds of carts block my way, begging soldiers press close around me, off the road troops are encamped or waiting. Dead horses and fallen cattle lie on the ground, abandoned rifles, cartridge belts—even instruments from regimental bands were to be found there!

To make faster headway, I branch off to the west and reach the Mandarin Road again, which I had already crossed once that morning. Here I now encountered the entire train of carts and riders, still bearing the traces of that panic which last night, at the crossing of the Puhe, had dissolved its last semblance of order.

### The Evacuation of Mukden.

I have already mentioned that the evacuation of Mukden had begun in complete secrecy as early as 2 March. Yet that from the very beginning of the battle there was a lack of any energetic resolve to hold the city under all circumstances was amply demonstrated by the hasty removal of the three hundred fortress guns, which were evacuated to Harbin for safety at the first Japanese attacks. Concerning the destruction of the ammunition belonging to these guns, which could not be loaded so quickly and safely, Major Schönmayer recounted that a huge pit was dug near Mukden station and filled with fuel; into this were placed the dismantled projectiles. The heated {p. [341]} [Illustration: Supply trains of the Third Army in retreat.]

{p. [342]}

{p. 337}

bursting charges of the bombs and shells, which were more than a metre high, then blew apart their steel casings with a terrible roar—it is said to have been a truly hellish spectacle that lasted for more than two days.

This terrible concert was soon followed by a mournful yet dreadfully beautiful illumination: the seemingly endless timber yards stacked at Gajiazi for the operation of the Russian railways were set on fire with naphtha from 3 to 5 March—a burning torch, mightier perhaps than any that had ever blazed on earth, visible across the entire battlefield by night. Far to the east, the soldiers watched in astonishment as dense showers of sparks sprayed up into the sky through the black night; the billowy black smoke was carried by the west wind as far as the mountains to the First Army. But the Japanese, too, saw the pillar of fire—to them it was a bonfire that their enemy had kindled as a sign of his weakness.

Even though the evacuation of Mukden thus seemed a settled matter already at the start of the battle, the order for it was issued only at the eleventh hour, when the enemy broke through the army's centre on the evening of 9 March.

The military attachés had already left the train in the first days of March, having been quartered first in a house near the railway station and later in a *fangzi* in the interior of the city of Mukden. In the courtyard of this same *fangzi*, part of the baggage train belonging to army headquarters was also camped. Not far from here was the Russo-Chinese Bank, which during the battle had received no fewer than thirteen orders—at one moment to pack up and depart, at the next to unpack and remain.

At 10 o'clock in the evening, the order to march reached the attachés.

Of the four exits from Mukden—whose city wall has a high tower facing each cardinal direction, and whose angled gateways and small intermediate gates formed an obstacle to movement even under normal circumstances—all, with the exception of the southern one, were soon jammed with wagons, troops, slaughter cattle, and whatever else sought to force its way hastily out into the open. {p. 338} When the military attachés reached the city gate, men were just then labouring to batter a breach in the mud wall, through which the crowd of shoving and shouting people then poured into the narrow alleys of the suburb, finally reaching the Mandarin Road from there towards midnight.

The censorship bureau left for Tieling on the last passenger train to depart Mukden on the evening of the 9th.

### The Retreat on the Mandarin Road.

In a broad line, three or even more wagons abreast, the baggage train pushed forward toward the north in the deeply rutted tracks of the Mandarin Road. Mounted men and infantry marched beside and between the wagons, among which were also the thirteen dvukolkas in which the Russo-Chinese Bank was packed. It would have been difficult to say where the head of this column began, for isolated stragglers—"individual travellers," as they were called—had already been moving north for several days, and many of Mukden's inhabitants had likewise already sent their families and belongings ahead toward Tieling during the day, so that the entire road was occupied by an unbroken, densely packed column in which every blockage made itself felt all the way to the rear.

At the time the wagons of the Russo-Chinese Bank approached the Pu River, an accident had occurred to the wagons in front while crossing the steep-banked river—there was no bridge—which brought the advancing column to a halt. Thus wagons and men now stood in the dark, cold night and waited, freezing and exhausted from fatigue, for the morning that would perhaps bring order to the chaos of men, animals, and wagons that were hopelessly tangled and jammed together here.

Yet suddenly, it might have been around three o'clock in the morning, rifles begin to rattle several thousand paces to the west from the fields, and already in the darkness one can also hear the clatter of heavy wagons racing in a pounding gallop across the ploughed fields toward their own column. Ever {p. 339} closer comes the rumbling din, already the snorting of the horses can be heard, when the men rip their rifles from their backs and fire—firing forward and firing to the right and to the left, for already the wild stampede is crashing over their own horses and wagons. It was a terrible impact when the heavy six-horse ammunition wagons slammed with full force into the tangle standing there! Yet their drivers had not seen the column on the Mandarin Road at all in the deep darkness of the night and lashed their exhausted horses all the more desperately, thus crushing men, wagons, horse teams—in short, everything that stood in their way. And those being run over here did not know whether Russians or Japanese were racing over their bodies! As long as there were cartridges in their rifle magazines, they fired away, even after the fleeing column had long since disappeared into the darkness of the night.

Mr Frisk, the official of the Russo-Chinese Bank in Mukden mentioned earlier, had been present during this scene. At the first onset his horse had reared and tumbled over, and when he picked himself up again, it had disappeared in the darkness. Frisk now leapt quickly onto a wagon so as not to be knocked down by the approaching unknown danger. But immediately thereafter the wild shooting back and forth began, so that he crawled under the wagon as fast as he could to be safe from the bullets. When all finally grew quiet, he sat down on the wagon again to await the Japanese here, who in the people's opinion were bound to arrive soon. The dawning day spread its grey glimmer over a horrifying scene. Dying horses, their bodies pierced by the wagon poles of the ammunition carts, dragged themselves miserably onward, their entrails trailing on the ground; here lay a cart, wheels in the air, there a wretched nag with crushed legs; over there the horse teams of several wagons that had collided in the nocturnal panic were jammed inextricably together, the drivers had run off, and the animals yanked and pulled at the traces without being able to get free of one another. Of the men, only a few remained. Some were lying on the ground; were they asleep or {p. 340} were they dead? nobody cared; most had abandoned their teams, and only a few loyal souls still kept watch beside them.

As it grew lighter, Frisk noticed a compact body of men cautiously approaching the bank's wagons; he took them for Japanese, but the guard that had held out beside the bank's wagons soon recognized that they were Caucasian Cossacks from General Kosakovsky's brigade. "Where are the wagons with the silver?" asked the leader of the Caucasians. "Why?" "You'll see in a moment," came the reply, and the mutinous horde was already beginning to rummage through the nearest wagon. At that, the guard opened fire. The bullets probably went only into the air, but the Cossacks thereupon preferred to loot the Red Cross wagons, where they at least encountered no resistance.

[Illustration: "Individual travellers."]

Frisk was soon relieved of his unpleasant company; one of his colleagues came riding along, and Frisk swung up behind him onto the little nag, which carried both gentlemen to Tieling—for bank officials, this night had certainly been eventful enough!

The total loss which the bank had suffered during the hasty evacuation of Mukden amounted to no more than some {p. 341} thirty thousand rubles. The wagons on which the silver bullion had been loaded had gotten stuck in the swamp near Puhe—everything else slowly turned up in Harbin.

Colonel Kovanko, the head of the Russian aeronautical section, had also witnessed the confusion at the Puhe in the morning hours of 10 March. Among the wagon trains, as he told me, the provision wagons of the army high command had also fallen victim to the panic during the night. Crates of champagne bottles, tin cans, zwieback, and the like, which had rolled from the overturned wagons, lay scattered here across the field. All the misery was not without its comic interludes: Cossacks struggling to uncork champagne bottles, then bravely raising the painstakingly opened bottle to their lips to pour the sparkling drink down their throats, only for it to come foaming back out through their mouth and nose! Others had better luck with cognac bottles, while tin cans that were finally pried open with sabres and contained nothing other than condensed cream were tossed aside in disappointment and disgust!

Naturally, this blockage here also had a delaying effect on the departure of the subsequent wagon trains from Mukden. *Thus it happened that the military attachés' baggage trains came under fire from Japanese artillery at 10 a.m. near Dawa, ten versts north of Mukden (they had progressed no farther than that in twelve hours!)*

The attachés' wagons, on which their bulkier baggage had been loaded, scattered in all directions as the Japanese shrapnel rained down; as luck would have it, I found them again later.

The attachés themselves were by this time already close to Tieling, where their train, which had left Mukden several days earlier, was awaiting them. Yet not all of them were together this time; some, including Szeptycki, as we know, had remained forward with the troops. Of the Americans, Surgeon Howard and Captain Judson of the Engineers had remained behind in Japanese captivity in Mukden, while Major {p. 342} Macomb, like myself, had looked for the train in Tieling and was said to have ridden right past it, but, failing to hear Lieutenant Colonel Rostagno's shouts, had soon lost himself again in the general turmoil.

The plain, roughly four versts wide, between the Mandarin Road and the railway was densely blanketed with soldiers. They were either columns hoping to make faster progress off the road, or once again those individual travelers tramping north quietly and peacefully, as though out for a stroll! Of panic there was really nothing left to be seen; the waves of excitement that the hasty evacuation of Mukden had stirred up during the night were already smoothed over, and everyone and everything went their own way, singly, in packs, in columns, just as it happened.

“Where is the road to Harbin?” a Jewish reservist asked Colonel Kovanko. — “What do you want in Harbin?” — “Everyone is heading for Harbin, so I’m going too!” — “And where is your rifle?” — “I lost it.” — “And did you lose your cartridge belt too?” — “What good are cartridges to me without a rifle? I threw them away.” — “And what are you carrying in that sack on your back?” With that, the soldier threw the sack to the ground and ran off. In the sack, however, were found more than two dozen pocket watches!

Among the columns marching north, I identified north of Tieling on 13 March troops of the 61st Division, the 3rd Division in its entirety, further troops of the 35th, 4th, 22nd, and 55th Divisions, two batteries, a military bridging train, the 4th Siberian Landsturm Battalion, several machine guns, and mortars.

### The Retreat Along the Railway Line.

Of my two horses, one had become completely unfit for service, and for my good grey, too, the exertions of recent weeks had been too much; he was beginning to go noticeably lame in the right hind leg. The prospect of having to continue on foot with the entire baggage train along the Mandarin Road was hardly alluring, so with my Cossacks I struck out across the plain once more to reach the railway.

{p. 343}

Here too, now scattered, now densely packed and jumbled together, the masses pushed forward on both sides of the tracks. Whenever a train coming from the south pulled into a station or halt, enlisted men and officers rushed at it in wild haste to secure a place on the running board, up on the roof, or even on the wagon buffers; this was not out of fear of the enemy, who was following only slowly, but because it was more comfortable than marching. At Kaiyuan, the crammed train was just pulling in when I reached the station. It

[Illustration:]

was already late in the evening, and once again the roofs, the footboards, the open flatcars—in short, anywhere an individual could cling to—were covered with soldiers. How would these people spend the cold night? And how much worse for those who, straddling the iron buffers, between the cars or even out on the catwalk around the locomotive, had chosen their perilous positions?

At the station in Kaiyuan there was even greater disorder than in Tieling. Naturally, I no longer asked anyone anything. Here my Cossacks were finally able to dispatch their telegrams, which the colonel and Dolgorukov {p. 344} had entrusted to them; I dismissed them here and commended my lame grey to their care. The good fellows faithfully brought the fine beast back to Szeptycki.

But what now? One train after another pulls in—yet there is no hope of finding even the humblest little spot. Thus it grows darker and darker; all around me are drunks, sleeping men—and dead men too! Yes, dead men as well; they lie beside the wooden fence, laid out side by side like those covered with straw mats. Last night a train, such as those arriving and departing here, had been packed full of people from running board to roof. Whether from sudden braking or rapid starting, no one knew any longer; in short, several hundred people had fallen from the train, the wheels had passed over them, and twenty of them had died—they were lying here!

Fortunately, I became acquainted with an amiable border guard officer—van der Beiningen. We finally managed to tear the seal off a freight car—inside were flour sacks from the Tieling station. Quickly we crawled in and shut the door. The flour in the sacks was frozen, and that night it was still –16° R. Beiningen and I wrapped ourselves together in our blankets, huddled back to back; we thought it would be warmer that way—yet poor Beiningen shuddered so violently with the cold that I too was unable to sleep.

Four long days and nights we travelled in this way, often waiting ten to twelve hours at the halts.

Ever-changing though the scene was, it was always mournful! Troops upon troops filed past us; a transport of Honghuzi and captured Japanese were also marched past a halt. There was almost nothing to eat; Beiningen and I helped each other out with canned food.

On the third day we were finally able to purchase a bottle of rum for seven rubles and a loaf of bread for two.

Finally, at noon on the fourth day, our train stopped nine versts before Gongzhuling. And yet we had covered no more than 120 versts in four days!

{p. 345}

Behind us came Kuropatkin's train. What nameless anguish must have thrilled through the commander's soul when at night he looked out of his carriage window across the fields and everywhere, unceasingly, saw the campfires flickering, around which the beaten, scattered, and exhausted soldiers were slowly gathering once more. All the misery that reached him through the silent night from the hospital trains, from the ambulance columns, from those dark masses of the unfortunate army pressing northward—was he *alone* to blame for it?

[Illustration: Fleeing Chinese.]

{p. [352]}

## After the Retreat.

In our boxcar, upon the frozen sacks of flour—uninviting as they were—Beiningen and I did not remain alone for long. Already by the following morning, four other officers had discovered that there was still room here to get forward, and by the fourth day there were already twenty of us sitting beside and on top of one another. At the last stop before Gongzhuling—the engine driver did not know whether we would proceed today or perhaps not until tomorrow—I packed my saddlebags and horse blankets onto my back in order to cover the few versts on foot. The others, among whom were some who claimed to be billeting officers of the Third Army Headquarters, preferred to wait perhaps another twenty hours in the greasy boxcar rather than walk the short distance.

So I moved on northwards alongside the railway line. The field on either side was still strewn with solitary travellers, wagons, and troops of all arms, yet here some semblance of order did seem, after all, to be slowly returning to the detachments.

The sun was burning hot again, my fur coat was warm, and the horse blanket, the rucksack, the saddlebag, and on top of that the two cameras amounted, after only the first quarter of an hour, to a most uncomfortable burden. And what on earth would I do if the attachés' train had already left Gongzhuling as well?

Suddenly, to my astonishment, I hear my name being called from behind! A soldier leaps from a wagon and hurries after me. It is the driver of Lieutenant-Colonel von Csicserics! "Where is my master?" he asks. "In Gongzhuling, hopefully, just ahead of us here!" Thus, in an unexpected {p. 347} manner, I had found a conveyance, upon which, in good spirits, I now trotted towards future events.

On the way, the soldier told me how near Dawan they had all first been shelled with shrapnel by the Japanese, and later he claimed to have seen Japanese cavalry as well; since then he had been driving straight northwards, having had just enough money to support himself and the horse until now, but this morning his last kopeck had run out—and then, to his great delight, among the many wandering "homeward," he had spotted me. He thought that it must now lead straight to Russia, which surely could no longer be so very far!

At Gongzhuling station I was told that the military attachés' train had already departed for Kuanchengzi, which is 60 versts further north. I had already climbed into the dvukolka and was about to move on when, after all, I got down once more, went to the stationmaster, and asked him again most urgently whether the attachés were really in Kuanchengzi. "Please wait a moment," said the station commandant, a German, and after an hour he returned with the answer that the attachés' train was still here in Gongzhuling, on track eight down by the bridge. Now I drive over to track eight by the bridge, and on the way I happen to run into Major Macomb! "Where to?" "Looking for the attachés on track eight!" "They aren't here," said Macomb, "I've been looking for them myself for three days." Now we go to the station together, and there we were finally informed in the most definite terms (?) that the attachés' train was already in Harbin.

Macomb is staying at the staging point. A large, spacious dugout, stoutly and well built, with broad plank beds. About *two hundred* officers are lying here! What they are waiting for here and where they belong, they perhaps do not even know themselves. Here Macomb proves himself once again a practical American; in one corner of the dugout—on precisely as many square feet as fall to his share—he has pitched his tent, set up his table and camp bed, and sat here for three days without speaking to anyone. For me there was at last something to eat as well! For four days I had lived almost exclusively on chocolate and hardtack.

{p. 348}

Scarcely had I been there an hour when, one after another, the drivers of the other attachés presented themselves, brought together here by sheer chance. Only the baggage of the British and French generals and of the German Lieutenant-Colonel von Lauenstein had been lost; that of General Silvestre was found after some time in Harbin.

In the afternoon, Kuropatkin's train arrived in Gongzhuling. Behind the cordon formed by the guard, the crowd stood waiting for the general—no longer commander-in-chief—who stepped down from the carriage. No cheer rang out, everything remained silent—it was truly sorrowful.

I stood off to the side with two German-speaking field telegraphists, to whom I was recounting the story of the lost and recovered baggage of the attachés, for which I was now looking for an empty boxcar to convey it, as well as Major Macomb and myself, to Harbin.

A Cossack colonel, whom I saw alighting from Kuropatkin's carriage, had listened to our conversation and asked me—likewise in German—what had been lost. In reply to my request whether he might perhaps be able to help me secure an empty boxcar for our baggage—which, after all, simply had to be brought to Harbin eventually—he remarked: "I am afraid I cannot help you, but I will give you a good piece of advice: ask no one! For either people will lie to you, or they will tell you: 'Ya ne mogu znat'—'I cannot know'—for in this . . .," he added, "no human being can make head or tail of anything anymore!"

Thus spoke a colonel from the commander-in-chief’s immediate entourage!

Kuropatkin was at that time on his way to St. Petersburg; only in Harbin did he receive his recall to the army from the Tsar.

“I am an old soldier and must not leave the army in this sad situation,” he said during a brief conversation at the Harbin railway station with which he honoured Lieutenant-Colonel von Csicserics. “I requested from His Majesty the command of the Fourth Siberian or the First Army Corps, and His Majesty was so gracious as to grant me command of the First Army. Tell all the gentlemen attachés, {p. [355]} [Illustration: Japanese prisoners. In the background, railway trains with Russian soldiers and artillery.]

{p. [356]}

{p. 351}

who were formerly with the First Army, that I invite them to return there very soon!”

Supreme command over the army had been assumed while still in Tieling by Lieutenant-General Linevich.

A certain energy which he displayed in restoring order certainly cannot be denied him. Thus, shortly after his appointment as commander-in-chief, he had over a hundred mutinous soldiers shot in Tieling.

### The Military Significance of Harbin.

The pressure of the pursuing army had eased near Kaiyuan. The Japanese pursuit was somewhat more vigorous this time than after the preceding battles—yet a lack of cavalry made exploiting the success impossible this time as well. Anyone who saw the Russian Second and Third Armies in retreat during the days from 10 to 14 March had to admit to himself that here an intact modern enemy cavalry division would have sufficed to dictate unconditional peace to the Russians! It was precisely here that the absence of powerful *battle cavalry* proved its indisputable right to exist!

Just as after all its failures, this time too the Russian army came to a halt wherever enemy pressure ceased.

Since mid-March, the Russian army had been stationed with army headquarters one station south of Gongzhuling, the First and Second Armies pushed forward to Sipingjie and Maimakai, and the Third Army in reserve behind the First and Second. Of fresh troops from Europe, the IV Army Corps and two rifle brigades had arrived. General Linevich’s somewhat naive demand to mobilise six army corps in Europe and send them to the theatre of war by echelon traffic—that is, one train after another without return trains—which would have required in all no fewer than 1,000 trains of 40 wagons each, was flatly rejected by St. Petersburg and earned the new commander-in-chief {p. 352} a very sarcastic remark from his supreme warlord. Apart from replacement transports and some troops from the Caucasus, few military forces were en route to the east. Of the lines of advance available to the enemy, three came into consideration: the direct route along the railway to Harbin; that via Jilin to Ninguta, which cuts Harbin off from Vladivostok; and that via Boduna to Qiqihar, which cuts Harbin off from its previous line of communications to the west. To be sure, the Songhua cuts across the three aforementioned lines of attack, but its southern bank dominates the northern, and its course bends southwards near Jilin, so that stubborn resistance with the forces available at the time was out of the question.

The evacuation of Harbin was only a question of time—provided that the Japanese continued their offensive. But what then? From where and with what to feed the army? Besides the many thousands of wounded who were in and around Harbin, removing the army’s supply

[Illustration: Kuanchengzi, city gate.] {p. 353} provisions, calculated to last for months, would have been just as difficult as in Mukden, although this time preparations for a possible evacuation of the city—such as burning the war materiel that could not be transported away, the timely evacuation of the bank, the removal of private individuals, etc.—had already been made.

Yet what was to become of the army without Harbin? Mukden as a place had only a moral value, but it was quite different with Harbin, which had the only seven steam mills supplying the army with flour, where the only large railway repair shops were established, and which, as the junction of the railway to Vladivostok and the south, was of vital strategic importance! And yet everything pointed to the fact that they were determined to evacuate Harbin!

Indeed, some regiments—such as the Kuanchengzi Frontier Guard Regiment, with which I stayed for some time after the battle—had sent their heavy baggage to Hailar, on the border of Transbaikalia! Indeed, Colonel Thurn-Taxis, commander of the frontier guard regiment in Gongzhuling, had told me himself that all preparations had already been made to blow up the barracks there.

What the German Prince Heinrich was still doing in the theatre of war at this time, when everyone was already thinking of the end of the campaign, was inexplicable to all. At the end of April, he had arrived in Harbin by a detour across the Gobi, since the Japanese would not let him pass through their outposts from Beijing!

Since in spring the army was tied to its supplies and to reinforcements from Europe—from which, moreover, one could not expect too much—its only further line of retreat remained the one leading west from Harbin along the railway, where it might perhaps find better chances for resistance at a *second Sungari position* than south of Harbin.

Despite some faint-hearted talk that could be heard especially from some Jewish physicians and other non-combatants, the *confidence of the Russian* {p. 354} *army on the whole was still not lost even after the Battle of Mukden!* “The war must go on for another two years, and then we will surely be victorious!” Thus spoke people who had a grueling, bloody seventeen-month campaign behind them! That is precisely the advantageous reverse side of the tedious phlegm of these people—that they *never despair!*

The losses of the Russian-Manchurian army in the period from 26 February to mid-March were in round numbers: 60,000 wounded, 20,000 dead, 45,000 captured. Far more significant than the Russian reports indicated must have been the losses in field guns, whose empty teams we saw by the hundreds in Harbin; the loss in ammunition and war materiel, including the installed field railway and bridge material, was approximately one billion rubles, though it was less the actual monetary value than the *temporary irreplaceability* of what was lost that was keenly felt. Besides various regimental dressing stations, the St. George Hospital remained behind in Mukden with approximately 3,000 wounded. The doctors, medical orderlies, and Red Cross sisters were sent back under escort by the Japanese to the Russian outpost lines.

It would be ungrateful to forget in this book a fellow campaigner who endured so much and so patiently during these hard days, who did so infinitely much good and alleviated so much pain—the Russian woman, who bravely and unpretentiously stood by her brothers in this war.

[Illustration]

{p. [361]}

## Woman in War.

Whoever has never understood in woman that noble, self-sacrificing love which is her unique, innate domain wherein she surpasses us men, has surely never seen woman in the simple dress of the Samaritan, in the hospital, on the battlefield. Now by the stretcher, now at the operating table, but always with only pain and misery before her eyes, the “Sister of Mercy” performs her arduous service; no other reward awaits her than the consciousness of having alleviated the suffering of her charges.

Here in the war, it was perhaps once again the racial trait of the Russian woman which, when placed in the service of voluntary care for the wounded, proved worthy of *unreserved* admiration. Not that women of other nations would lack similar self-sacrificing courage; yet I doubt that elsewhere, *from all strata* of the female population—from the poor student up to the pampered princess—such a relatively *large number* would have submitted to this calling full of privation and endured it *over the long duration of the war with such unflinching, phlegmatic devotion* as we found in the operational and rear-echelon areas of the Russian army.

The whole war was certainly never popular, to be sure, and it was hard to demand that “patriotism” be inscribed on the banners of a military campaign whose vague objective was the preservation of state prestige in a country that to the great masses was initially unknown and later detested. But that patriotism—and indeed a great deal of patriotism—dwells in the Russian people was amply proven by the Russian woman in this war. Like the soldier, she too went without sleep, food, and {p. 356} shelter without complaint; yet she, the woman, suffered want and endured hardships *gladly and with self-sacrifice*, for she dedicated her labor to the best part of the fatherland: its sons! The soldiers, however, did not know why and for what they had to fight and die!

Of what the love of women back home in Russia created for the wounded and sick, I have spoken at the beginning. Later on the journey, I saw what hardships and trials the young girls who had dedicated themselves to hospital service had to endure. A hospital train brings two hundred or more sick from the Harbin hospitals back to Chita. Let us assume that this is not one of those comfortable trains fitted out specially for transporting the wounded, such as had been donated by the generosity of certain grand duchesses, charitable societies, or cities, but rather a hospital train improvised from ordinary freight cars. The journey from Harbin to Chita takes around ten days. This time the nurse spends virtually without interruption among the sick; so few places to rest are allocated to the sisters that they can only be used by turns.

[Illustration: Sisters of the Nobility Hospital in Harbin.]

{p. 357}

Meals are irregular, depending on when one pulls into the feeding stations for the sick. At last Chita is reached; the hospitals there are already overcrowded. Together with the accompanying doctors, the sisters hand over their patients, and then the hospital train ceases to be one and returns as a freight or troop transport. “By which train will we return home to our hospital in Harbin?” doctors and sisters ask. “Go to the station and wait!” is the curt answer. Waiting! And waiting yet again!

No order, no guiding hand to *employ the good and willing material, available in abundance, expediently and sparingly of strength*. And so the poor nurses wander back to the railway station that very evening. They ask the stationmaster when the next train will leave for them. “Wait!” he too tells them, and the girls, often barely eighteen years old, sit down around a round table in the smoky refreshment room, which has not been aired out since the onset of winter, rest their heads on their arms, and sleep sitting up until morning. Thus they lie, sit, and stand in the waiting room for another day or two until a train takes them along. If all goes well, crammed into a third-class Pullman, the journey takes ten days back to Harbin, where in the hospital wards the groaning and rattling sufferers already await their care. Yet it was not only the hospitals; almost every regiment had its nurses. Although these women were probably more exposed to the dangers and rigours of the weather than their colleagues in the institutions, this arrangement was nevertheless more advantageous, as it was permanent and the nurse felt more at home here than in the constant transfer from hospital to hospital.

Later, in Gongzhuling, I made the acquaintance of Princess O., who had served through the entire campaign as a nurse with a regiment. After the Battle of Mukden, the few belongings she carried with her had been stolen by Caucasian Cossacks. “Our Cossacks are worse than the Chinese brigands,” she said indignantly; “I myself saw pieces of my linen in the hands of the thieves, but my regiment was gone and there was no one to help me recover my property!” The poor woman had {p. 358} nothing left except what she wore on her back, and the campaign was far from over.

At that time, I also met nurses who were returning from the St. George Hospital, which had fallen into Japanese hands at Mukden.

“How conscientious the Japanese doctors are in comparison to ours! When the Japanese doctor took over our ward, he asked for the number and names of the Russian wounded in it. ‘We do not know the names,’ our doctor answered, ‘there are exactly a hundred of them.’ After a good while the Japanese returned; he had noted down the names and regiments of the captive patients—‘but there are not a hundred, only ninety-nine.’—‘Well, yes, I told you *a hundred*,’ our doctor remarked. ‘One hundred—and ninety-nine, Staff Surgeon, is a great difference,’ the Japanese said solemnly—‘especially when it is a question of *human beings*!’

“Such lessons must we, the bearers of culture”—the nurse remarked ironically—“receive from the Asians! What is one human being to our doctors, what are hundreds to them! For we well know that when the first transports of wounded arrived from Mukden in Harbin, our doctors had those who had died along the way laid at dawn in the doorways of the nearest houses, in order to spare themselves drawing up the official death reports.”

The truth of this account, incidentally, was later confirmed to me by German merchants in Harbin; this was said to have occurred on the morning of 12 March.

Imagine now a feeling woman’s heart that had to witness all this misery!

The nurses were young and the war lasted seventeen months. Whether this long period passed for every one of them entirely without piquant memories, I am inclined to doubt—and yet, why not? Certainly the novelist would have found in the hospitals and field infirmaries plenty of tender material for short love stories. For instance, in a hospital not far from Mukden there was a young doctor to whom a nurse was devoted. His superior, who was likewise infatuated with the beautiful nurse, {p. 359} sent the doctor back to his regiment in the positions so that he might remain cock of the walk alone. A few months later, after Mukden—the regiment had stood near the Imperial Tombs during the final days of the battle—we met him enveloped in dust on the retreat. Bringing up the rear was the doctor—and beside him on horseback the beautiful nurse!

Even General Sakharov, the commander-in-chief’s chief of the general staff, had found time to fall in love with a lady of the Red Cross and to marry her—three days before the Battle of Liaoyang!

Among the thousands who wore the red cross, there were certainly some for whom this emblem of mercy served merely as a passe-partout to the theatre of war.

Thus there was a strikingly large number of officers’ wives—including those of senior officers and generals—who had followed their husbands here to the theatre of war.

While in Mukden hardly any other women were to be found apart from those truly engaged in caring for the wounded, the Harbin station and the lower port city offered a rich assortment of the gentle sex—of all categories. Yet how

[Illustration: Sisters of the Cross and doctors of the Red Cross.] {p. 360} could it be otherwise! Manchuria had already been permanently inhabited by Europeans before the war; Vladivostok, Port Arthur, Dalny, and Yingkou were large port and commercial cities whose inhabitants were gradually sent to the rear by the Russian high command as the Japanese advanced—what wonder that dubious rabble of both sexes had accumulated back in the rear staging areas! Now, there were people who feasted their eyes only on these marsh flowers and were blind to the high, noble calling that the Russian woman was fulfilling in the theatre of war. There were also those who believed they saw something wicked in the fact that the nurses carried out their service, which brought them into such close relations with men, mostly without the protective moral wings of mothers, aunts, or matrons! But these gentlemen forgot that the Russian woman had long since broken free from the petty-bourgeois views of our female education, and that here of all places the fertile seed of women’s emancipation, which seeks to qualify woman as man’s fully equal comrade and supporter, had borne its most consummate fruit!

[Illustration]

{p. [367]}

[Illustration: Mongols in the Gobi Desert.]

{p. [368]}

{p. [369]}

## Afterword.

Mighty Russia was cut off from the Pacific Ocean. Its cherished dream, which the labor of many centuries had brought so close to realization—to rule freely over a warm sea in the Far East—had been cruelly shattered. Not a ship and not a man could pass beyond the iron ring that the Japanese had drawn in a wide arc from Vladivostok along the coast and then up the Tumen toward Jilin, Kujujan, and in the west into Mongolia, around the shaken symbols of Russian power.

Yet my own favorite plan, to return home across the cheerful blue sea and still see Japan and India, had likewise come to naught.

Disheartened, I had returned from the Gobi Desert to Transbaikalia; all the exertions and privations there had been in vain. The difficulties of travelling on from Urga via Kalgan to Beijing exceeded my means, and slowly, day and night, the jolting tarantass made its way toward Lake Baikal.

It was a May night with snow and ice. The carriage halted before the post station to change horses; I peeled myself out of my furs and stepped into the small, cramped room. An Orthodox priest sat here with his five children, who had curled up together on the floor and fallen asleep.

So we sat here for a good while by the dim candlelight—taciturn and drowsy.

Slowly the priest's postilion came in through the door, bowed and crossed himself before the icon lamp of the Virgin in the corner, and announced to his master that the tarantass was ready. When the door opened, cold air had rushed in; we {p. 364} shivered. It seemed as though the priest had not heard the driver; motionless, his head resting on his right arm, he looked at his children. The other man remained standing and looked at the priest. At last, weary and slow, the latter turned around and said: “Wait!”—“I shall wait!” the simple man repeated deliberately, and walked with dragging steps out of the room—into the cold night to his horses.

Wait!—I shall wait!—Did it not seem to me as if I were hearing the general and the sergeant of Padzyaza once again!

*Wait, and wait again and again*—that is the great watchword here in Russia, turning days into months and months into years!

The entire war had begun under the auspices of *waiting*; indeed, at its outset, Commander-in-Chief Kuropatkin had demanded *patience*, *patience again, and a third time patience* from the Russian fatherland. Perhaps, without knowing it, he had thereby translated into Russian the well-known words of Montecuccoli, who demanded money, money, and yet again money for waging war! For money—power—is there; the Russians need only *time*—but *a great deal of time*—in order, despite all the sluggishness and corruption of the obsolete autocratic system, to bring this power to bear. They have entirely different views on the value of *time* from us fast-living Europeans, and the gigantic dimensions of their empire make the *value of space* seem far less there than among us, who must live on a narrower plot of earth, yet have been able, free and unhampered by the whims of an autocratic government, to develop intellectually and economically.

War, as the supreme exertion of a nation's capacity, is at the same time the best yardstick of its efficiency; it is the bloody harvest festival of the good—or bad—seed sown in the people over decades. One must not seek the explanation for the Russian defeats purely in military considerations. These alone would leave many questions unanswered: it is in the people themselves, in {p. 365} their religious and state institutions, in the national flaws that have embodied themselves over centuries into a specifically “Russian system,” and in bureaucratic malpractice that one must seek the primal sources of those evils, which naturally carry over into the army as well and prove the untenability of this system there.

Whoever travels for days and weeks through the endless plains of the Russian Empire, whoever waits there for weeks and months for the simplest matters to be attended to, whoever is lied to there deliberately or unintentionally at least once every day, soon comes to the realization that time, space, and truth play a vastly different role in the great Tsarist Empire than among the peoples of the rest of Europe, where corruption—the mother of lies—is held in check by the system of public control. Rigid autocracy had sealed the empire off from the rapid historical development of its western neighbors; the despotic rule of some two hundred families, who, with the aid of an ossified and corrupt officialdom, must keep the people in apathy and indolence for the sake of their own self-preservation, has hampered the free intellectual development of the indi-

[Illustration: Through the Gobi.] {p. 366} vidual. Out of fear of losing control over the masses, they are raised not to think and to work, but to wait and obey with dull apathy. Nowhere in the world is there so much and such patient waiting as in Russia, for where one does not strive and does not work, time has no value after all! The immediate consequences of this failure to recognize the value of time are *slowness*, *unpunctuality*, and *lack of energy*—qualities that one encounters at every turn, as though fundamental characteristics, in the Russian

[Illustration: Yurt village in the Gobi.]

people. And now, if one imagines this slowness, unpunctuality, and lack of energy, inoculated into the people by all the instruments of state power, *transferred to the military sphere*, one finds these hereditary evils of the Russian army clearly illustrated once again in every phase of the recent war, in every single operation from a battle right down to the undertaking of a raiding detachment. While fully acknowledging the difficulties rooted in the vastness of the distance and the inadequacy of communications, Russia cannot, {p. 367} this time as in all its earlier wars, be spared the reproach of having prepared its strategic deployment insufficiently and carried it out slowly.

Despite the numerical superiority finally achieved after several months, the *initiative of attack*—setting aside the ill-fated attempts on the Sha Ho and at Sandepu—was left exclusively to the Japanese. The *value of time* was never appreciated on the Russian side; that is to say, the moment for a promising offensive was consistently missed. Thus, after the fall of Port Arthur, Kuropatkin's army stood idle on the Sha Ho and tolerated the Japanese forces released in the south joining the field army. Further inactivity: roughly three hundred and eighty Russian battalions stood motionless opposite an adversary now reinforced to some two hundred and sixty-odd battalions, and had the Japanese not attacked at Mukden, the Russians, after their initial failure, would once again have halted the attack and quietly awaited the onset of the thaw, which would have prevented operations for months to come. In the execution of the operations themselves, the stereotypical Russian *unpunctuality* reveals itself.

On the 4th and 5th of March, dispositions were issued in the Second Manchurian Army west of Mukden, none of which was carried out. The orders arrived too late or not at all, were only partially obeyed or—having arrived too late—no longer obeyed at all; under the pressure of the enemy, the general unpunctuality had soon led to chaos in which keeping track of the troops was impossible. Formations were broken up and units taken wherever they happened to be at hand; thus it happened that on March 4th General Tserpitsky, commander of the 10th Army Corps, found himself entirely without troops, as now army headquarters, now the high command had assigned them in the most diverse directions. And during these long fourteen days, Linevich stood in the mountains with eighty-four battalions and quietly waited until the centre and the right wing in the plain were beaten! To concentrate this considerable force and commit it with an energetic thrust to extricate the Second and Third Armies {p. 368}—for this, he, like most of the others, lacked the energy and a proper understanding of the value of time.

Yet the gigantic dimensions of the Russian Empire and the resulting lesser sensitivity to the loss of space also give Russia's wars a peculiar character. In the Russo-Japanese War, there is the further circumstance, virtually unprecedented in military history, that throughout its entire duration the campaign took place in the territory of a third party, which placidly tolerated the devastation of its lands and the destruction of its villages—a fact that only further tempted the Russians to portray their forced retreats as voluntary shifts of position to the north.

The moral impression that the fall of Port Arthur made on the Russian army at Mukden was extremely slight; for a day or two it was talked about, then the loss of the fortress was gotten over just as easily as the entire territory conquered by the Japanese, in which the Russians had already made themselves completely at home for years and where millions of rubles had been invested in railway construction, barracks, hospitals, fortifications, and the like. *The consciousness that it was still not Russian soil from which they were being driven produced a feeling of indifference and insensitivity to the value of the terrain* on which they were fighting, which unfavourably influenced the will to offer energetic resistance. Thus, during the days from the 26th to the 28th of February—that is, just after the first serious Japanese attacks against the positions south of Mukden—the Russians immediately sent back to Harbin those three hundred fortress guns which had been placed with great difficulty in the centre of the Sha Ho position and had formed a formidable defensive front there. “The Japanese can have the Sha Ho, but they won't get our cannons!”

Thus it was also possible that, in the face of all the misery presented by the defeated army surging back northward from Mukden, a Russian General Staff colonel explained to us in the most convinced tone that “Mukden had been evacuated voluntarily and they were now returning to the north because southern Manchuria, sucked dry by the war, had no value left anyway!” And the good {p. 369} man believed it himself; he said it with the same self-assurance with which the Tsar, for instance, in his telegram of thanks to General Linevich, spoke of a “stubborn repulse of all the enemy's attacks”: *Tenete ridere*—but that is how it is; people truly believe in such things.

Through his endless “keeping people waiting”, the official educates the people in wasting time. Through the system of corruption in all offices and courts, faith in earthly justice cannot but vanish. Religion and trust in God were certainly a mighty support for the Russian army in this war, but since the priest, too, does not teach fulfillment of duty and love of truth, but instead ensures that his flock does not awake from its thoughtless cult of icons, and does not consider evil to be evil so long as it goes unpunished, the lie cannot be eradicated. Yes, the lie! It is nurtured in the Russian Empire, among high and low, poor and rich! How could it fail to have taken root in the army as well? All too often it struts through orders, dispatches, and reports! At one moment timidly modest in the patrol report of an officer who reports as “seen with his own eyes” what a Chinese told him for a few rubles; at another joyfully jubilant in the hurrahs of detrained soldiers in Harbin, who were informed in the order of the day that Rozhestvensky had blasted the Japanese to the bottom of the sea; at another haughtily proud when General Linevich telegraphs of offensive intentions—and here too it is neither malice nor the intention to speak untruth, but merely *the lack of any feeling for the value of truth.*

Yet it must also be admitted that in these harmful psychological factors discussed so far there lies, on the other hand, such a sustaining power for the Russian army—to which it is thanks that, despite nineteen months of failures, it was not yet demoralized (for this, contrary to many accounts on the subject, should likewise be stated here)—which made it possible that, despite the critical situation in which the Manchurian army already found itself, the *conclusion of peace for Russia*, viewed from a *purely military standpoint*, was still {p. 370} by no means a *compelling necessity*. Much was said of the great panics following the Battle of Mukden—panic never lasted long among the Russians, for they are too apathetic for that. On the night of March 9–10, when Mukden was evacuated under the impact of Japanese shrapnel and enemy cavalry broke into the baggage trains in the pitch-dark night, there was indeed a scene of the most dreadful confusion and all formations dissolved. After a few days, however, tempers had already calmed and order was perhaps not restored only because little store had ever been set by it in the first place.

The greatest disappointment in this war was surely prepared for us by the cavalry—the famous Russian cavalry. To be sure, of its regular army cavalry Russia had no more than three dragoon regiments at the theatre of war; all the rest were Cossacks—though these, after all, also belong in part to the regular cavalry; but even with the irregular second-line regiments more could have been achieved than actually was.

On account of the poor performance of both the Japanese and the Russian cavalry, in the course of this cam-

[Illustration: Circum-Baikal Railway.] {p. 371} paign, voices were often raised that sought to deny this arm any value at all. Without going into this question more closely here, it suffices for the present to point out that it was precisely the failure of the cavalry reconnaissance and reporting service among the Russians that led to the most mistaken dispositions in both the strategic and tactical spheres, and that the lack of adequate battle cavalry among the Japanese made it impossible for them to exploit their success in all their victories.

What would have become of the Russian Manchurian army if one or even several well-organized divisions of modern cavalry had been at the disposal of the Japanese after the Battle of Mukden? Yet cavalry divisions would not even have been necessary here—intact cavalry regiments on March 10 and 11 would have sufficed to drive entire Russian corps in any direction they pleased; the war would have been over.

It is not the lack of necessity, but on the contrary the eminent importance and indispensability of cavalry that this war has proved—while also showing, however, that even masses of cavalry are useless if their commanders are not inspired by drive and energy, and their officers not tactically trained.

Besides military training, however, the Russian cavalry above all lacked the firm will *to sacrifice itself*—and that was perhaps its greatest failing!

Discipline had been maintained in the ranks of the men right to the end. Yet the discipline of the Russian soldier is less the result of his military education—his officer cares little for him—than of the innate religious feeling of devotion inherent to the man. The only sustaining element common to the entire army, preserving it even in the darkest days from moral disintegration, was religion! And if one were today to take away the faith of the Russian people before providing them with a substitute in education and enlightenment, little indeed would remain of the moral worth of the Russian army! In this war {p. 372} it was faith in a better future that unshakeably animated officer and man.

"The war must last two more years, then in the end we will prevail after all," the officers said after Mukden; "what is the little bit of land from Port Arthur to Gongzhuling that we have evacuated compared to our Russia, which stretches from the Baltic Sea to the Pacific Ocean! And are we beaten, destroyed? No, after all, we are still here! And when we are no more, other hundreds of thousands will come!" To be sure, such a way of thinking does not conform to modern concepts of warfare. Yet it imparts to the whole people that passive tenacity to which the Russians ultimately owed a great success: that of the conclusion of peace.

Yet there will be no peace in the land of the Russians until the people have shaken off the fetters that hinder their intellectual and economic development.—And these fetters will fall! For no system and no law is strong enough to sever the vital artery of a healthy people. When the autocratic regime, together with the falsehood, corruption, and patronage in the Tsarist Empire, has outlived itself, then a modern citizen army will arise there as well—for the protection of its ruler, its country, and its laws, strong and formidable, as befits a mighty people.
